UNIT TWO LIVING UNDER A DEMOCRATIC FORM OF GOVERNMENT 

In our democracy where the people rule it is necessary that they should understand the structure and forms of our government. Unit 

Two describes our national, state, and local governments. 

Chapters Four and Five describe our national government. The 

United States Constitution contains the basic outline of our federal government. It also contains a Bill of Rights for all the people in our democracy. Our national laws are made by Congress, enforced by the President, and interpreted by the federal courts. 

Each of our forty-eight states also has a constitution outlining the form of government and guaranteeing certain rights to the people. Chapter Six in a general way describes the forms of our state governments. Laws are made by the legislatures, enforced by the governors, and interpreted by the state courts. 

Self-government in our local communities is an important part of our democracy. Chapter Six describes the forms of our local government as found in counties, towns, townships, villages, and cities.

Sessions of the Senate and of the House of Representatives are held in the Capitol building, Washington, D. C.

Chapter IV  A. THE CONSTITUTION 

Beginnings of Our Union.-During the colonial period the thirteen American colonies were not united. 

Each had its own government and was entirely independent. When England tried to tax the colonists without their consent, the colonies began to join together to protect their liberties. In 1776 the Continental Congress, which consisted of delegates from each colony, adopted the Declaration of Independence. This declared "That these 

United Colonies are, and of Right ought to be free and Independent States." After a long struggle the colonies won their freedom, but they were not yet established as a united nation.

The Articles of Confederation.-At the same time. that they were thinking about independence the members of the Continental Congress considered plans for bringing all the colonies under one regular, permanent government. 

By 1781 all the states had united under a plan of government which was called, "The Articles of Confederation." 

This plan was very weak and its glaring defects soon became evident. Before long our national government became an object of contempt both at home and abroad. 

Congress had very little power; it could not enforce treaties with foreign nations; it could not keep order; and it could not raise money for the running expenses of the government. The states gave little attention to the re- 55

quests and recommendations of Congress, and quarreled among themselves over tariffs, navigation, and boundaries. 

Several states actually took up arms against each other. 

Conditions like these caused thoughtful people to realize that something had to be done to bring about a better union and a stronger government. 

The Constitutional Convention.-After several years of agitation and discussion, Congress finally called a national convention to revise the Articles of Confederation. 

Although the convention was called to meet on the second 

Monday in May, 1787, the proceedings did not get under way until two weeks later. Gradually the delegates straggled in and the convention was officially opened. George 

Washington was chosen president—a happy choice, indeed. The convention decided to vote by states, and ordered secrecy for all its proceedings. 

Many leaders realized that mere revision of the Articles of Confederation would not be enough. Wise heads saw that nothing short of a new constitution would meet the situation. The decision to draw up a constitution was one reason for the secrecy of the convention's proceedings, for, if news of such action should leak out, the opposition would form rapidly, and the convention might come to an end before it had done its work. 

Sixty-two delegates were selected by the states to attend the convention, but only fifty-five of this number were present. Only thirty-nine delegates signed the Constitution after it was completed. Young men were prominent in the work of the convention. The average age of the delegates was a little over forty. Hamilton was only thirty years old when the convention opened and Madison thirty-six. But two of the leaders, Washington and Franklin, were older men, the former being fifty-five and the latter eighty-one. 

The convention was made up of all kinds of men. There

Ewing Galloway

Thirty-nine of the delegates to the Convention signed the Constitution in Philadelphia in 1787. were rich men like Robert Morris and George Washington and men of moderate circumstances like Roger Sherman of Connecticut and William Few of Georgia. There were men of great wisdom like Washington, Madison, Franklin, and Hamilton, as well as men of moderate ability. One of the best-educated men in the convention was Alexander Hamilton.

Probably the most original person in this assembly was James Madison of Virginia. He has often been called the "Father of the Constitution." Madison was the outstanding scholar of the convention. He was well informed in matters of government and history. Certainly Madison was more responsible than any other man for the ideas which went into the Constitution. 

Compromises in the Constitution.-Many conflicts arose in the convention. At times the conflicts were so serious that had it not been for the presence of men like Washington and Franklin the convention would have come

to an inglorious end. Many of the great differences of opinion were settled by compromises. For example, the small states insisted on each state having the same number of persons in the new Congress, while the large states wanted the number to be based on population. A compromise was finally adopted whereby each state has the same number of Senators, but the number of Representatives from each state is based on population. There are several other compromises in the Constitution. 

Ratification of the Constitution.-The final draft of the Constitution was submitted to the Convention on September 17, 1787. The proposed Constitution was then sent to the states for ratification. Bitter opposition developed in some of the states because it was feared that the new government would take away the liberties of the people. It was agreed, therefore, that amendments or additions which would guarantee the rights of the people, would be made to the Constitution immediately after its ratification. The necessary number of states (nine) had ratified the Constitution by the middle of 1788. Washington was elected President and went into office April 30, 1789.

Greatness of the Constitution.—The English states man Gladstone declared that the Constitution is "the greatest work ever struck off at a given time by the brain and purpose of man." For 150 years it has stood the test of time as the basis of our government. It has been widely copied with modifications by many foreign lands. It is the fundamental law of our country and contains the outline of our form of government. No state or national law can be contrary to the Constitution. All public officials must take an oath to support it. No one can understand our government and its activities without a knowledge of the Constitution.

Comparison of English and American Constitu tions. The Constitution of the United States is a written one. It consists of seven articles and twenty-one amendments. The Constitution of England is not so compact as ours. England has what is known as an unwritten constitution. It is made up of great documents like the Magna 

Charta, the Petition of Rights, the Bill of Rights, laws passed by Parliament, and customs and traditions. 

The American Constitution in the narrow sense is a rather rigid document, while the English Constitution is flexible. Our Constitution is very difficult to change. In a period of 150 years the American Constitution has been, amended only twenty-one times. The English Constitution can be amended by Parliament at any time. 

But the American Constitution is not so rigid when we speak of it in the broader sense. Let us look at some of the ways in which the Constitution has been expanded and changed.

Expanding the Constitution. The framers of the 

Constitution made a broad document. Many of the details about our government remained to be filled in by Congress. For example, the Constitution provides directly for the establishment of the Supreme Court only; other courts have been established by Congress. The Constitution does not set forth the procedure for law-making. It remained for Congress to make its own rules of procedure. 

Many times the Constitution has been expanded because the courts have given a broad interpretation to certain parts of it. These interpretations have given Congress greater powers than it had before. Our Constitution has usually kept pace with the times because of liberal interpretation by the courts. John Marshall, an early 

Supreme Court Chief Justice, said the Constitution was sufficiently broad to meet any emergency.

Another method of expanding the Constitution is by usage. Usage develops when one person starts doing a thing a certain way and others continue to do the same thing. For example, the first President established a Cabinet and other Presidents followed the same procedure until it became a usage. 

Amending the Constitution.-There are two steps in the process of amending the Constitution: An amendment must be proposed; then before it becomes a part of the 

Constitution, it must be ratified. 

There are two ways of proposing an amendment: (1)

Congress may propose it by a two-thirds vote of both houses. (2) If the legislatures of two-thirds of the states request it, Congress may call a special convention for proposing amendments. Thus far, only the first of these methods of proposing amendments has been used. 

After the amendment has been proposed it must be ratified or approved by three-fourths of the states before it goes into effect. When Congress submits an amendment to the states, it may provide that it shall be ratified by the state legislatures or by special conventions called in each state for that purpose. In nearly all cases thus far, amendments have been ratified by the state legislatures, and not by conventions.

Twenty-one amendments have been added to the Constitution. The first ten were added soon after the Constitution went into effect. They constitute a Bill of Rights. 

The eleventh amendment, passed in 1798, provides that no person may sue a state in the national courts. 

The twelfth amendment came as a result of the mix-up in the presidential election of 1800. It provides that presidential electors must vote for President and Vice-President separately. It was adopted in 1804. 

Three amendments, the thirteenth, fourteenth, and

fifteenth, are known as the war amendments, as they were passed during and just after the war between the northern and the southern states. The thirteenth abolished slavery. 

The fourteenth defines citizenship and guarantees certain fundamental rights of persons against state action. The fifteenth prohibits discrimination against the rights of citizens to vote because of race, color, or previous condition of servitude.

The sixteenth gives Congress the power to levy an income tax.

The seventeenth provides for the election of United States Senators by the direct vote of the people.. 

The eighteenth, known as the prohibition amendment, was adopted in 1919. It prohibited the sale and manufacture of liquor, wine, and beer. It is no longer in effect. 

The nineteenth was added in 1920 and gives women the right to vote.

The twentieth was adopted in 1933. It provides that 

Congress shall begin its sessions on January 3, and that the President shall take the oath of office on January 20. 

It is known as the "lame duck" amendment.

The twenty-first was adopted in 1933. It repeals the eighteenth amendment.

Our Federal System of Government.-The Amer ican nation is now made up of forty-eight states, the District of Columbia, the territories of Alaska, Hawaii, and Puerto Rico, and other outlying possessions. Our government, under the Constitution, is a union of states with a strong national government and is called a federation. The union of the states is a permanent one. No state now has the right to secede or withdraw from the union. 

Most persons in our country really live under two different governments-national and state. Each makes its own laws and has its own government officials. But if the

2 authority of the state and the nation conflict, the state must recognize the higher authority of the nation. 

The national government guarantees to every state a republican form of government. If in any state some person should try to establish a dictatorship, the national government would have to suppress it. 

The national government must also protect each state from foreign invasion. When it becomes necessary, the governor or legislature of a state may appeal to federal authorities for help in maintaining order. 

Division of Powers Under Our National Constitu tion. The national and state governments have each certain powers with which the other dares not interfere. 

In some cases, such as taxation, they exercise similar powers. 

1. Powers of the National Government. The national government has power over those things which affect the entire nation. These powers are specifically outlined in the 

Constitution. Such powers are (1) the power to tax for federal purposes and to borrow money on the nation's credit; (2) to regulate interstate and foreign commerce; (3) control over foreign relations; (4) control over the coinage of money; (5) regulation of the postal service; (6) control over the army and navy; (7) admission of new states; and (8) regulation of weights and measures and of patents and copyrights. Throughout our history the authority of Congress has been extended because the powers listed in the Constitution have been given broad interpretations. A broad construction of the powers of Congress is sometimes based on the famous "elastic clause" in the Constitution: "To make all laws which shall be necessary and proper for carrying into execution the foregoing powers. 

2. Powers Reserved to the States. According to the

tenth amendment, all powers which have not been given to the national government by the Constitution and which have not been prohibited to the states, are reserved to the states or to the people. This leaves almost an endless list of activities which are carried on by the states and their local governments. Among these are: the promotion of education, regulation of marriage and divorce, protection of public health, and granting of the right to vote. Under our plan of government the larger number of powers has been reserved for the states. Whenever any dispute arises about powers that are on the border line, it is settled by the courts. In many cases today the states and national government cooperate. 

3. Concurrent Powers. Concurrent powers are those which are exercised by both national and state governments. Thus, both governments need money and have the power to borrow or levy taxes. 

4. Forbidden Powers. Some powers of government are forbidden to the states, others are denied to both the states and the national government. 

States are forbidden to enter into any treaty, alliance, or confederation, to coin money, to grant any title of nobility, or to pass any bill of attainder or ex post facto law. A bill of attainder is a law condemning a person to punishment without giving him a trial in court. An ex post facto law is one under which a person might be punished for doing something which was not a crime at the time it was done. 

The rights of the people are insured against both national and state governments because the first nine amendments are duplicated in the state constitutions. The thirteenth amendment forbids both to permit slavery. The nineteenth amendment forbids them to deny the right to vote on account of sex.

Division of Government into Three Departments. -The government of the United States, under the Constitution, is divided into three departments. (1) The legislative department consists of Congress which makes the laws. (2) The executive department consists of the President, his Cabinet, and various independent agencies, which enforce the laws. (3) The judicial department consists of the courts which interpret the laws. The makers of the 

Constitution were afraid that one department might become so strong and powerful as to endanger the liberties of the people. Therefore, they worked out a system of checks and balances by means of which the different departments may restrain one another. Congress may be checked by the President when he vetoes a bill that has been passed; the Supreme Court can check Congress by declaring a law unconstitutional. Congress has a possible check on the President through its power to impeach and remove him from office; the Senate may refuse to ratify treaties which have been made by the President. 

B. LAW-MAKING IN  

Congress.-Congress, which makes the laws for our national government, is made up of two houses, the Senate and the House of Representatives. Because our national law-making body has two houses, it is said to be bicameral. 

The makers of our Constitution believed that having two houses would prevent hasty and ill-considered laws from being passed. The argument was that if a bill had to pass through two houses the chances were that it would be sound.

The example for a two-house law-making body was found in the Parliament of the English government. All of the original thirteen states, except Georgia, had bicameral legislatures. Georgia had a unicameral system.

Members of Congress are chosen by the people at regular elections held in November every two years. 

In accordance with the twentieth amendment to the Constitution, their term of office begins on the following January 3. Since Representatives are elected for only two years, the life of a regular Congress is two years. This includes two regular sessions, each beginning on January 3 and lasting from three to eight months. The President may also call special sessions of the entire Congress or of either house alone. 

The salary of Senators and Representatives is $10,000 a year. In addition they have the privilege of sending official mail free (franking), receive an allowance of twenty cents per mile for travel from their homes to Washington and return, and are furnished an office and a secretarial staff. The mileage allowance is intended to cover the traveling expenses of the member of Congress and his family.

According to the Constitution, each house is the judge of the qualifications of its members and has a right to refuse to receive a person into membership even though he has been elected by the people. In 1927 the Senate refused to admit Smith from Illinois and in 1929 Vare from Pennsylvania, because they were said to have used money corruptly to secure their nominations. As a rule, however, an elected person is admitted without question. 

The Constitution provides that members of Congress shall not be arrested, except for committing crime, during their attendance at the sessions of Congress, and in going to and from the same; and for any speech or debate in either house they cannot be questioned in any other place. 

Proceedings of both houses of Congress are recorded in the Congressional Record. It is published each day while 

Congress is in session and includes a stenographic report

of every word that is spoken in each house, but it also contains a great deal of material which is never uttered in the houses. Members of Congress are permitted to insert speeches which they may prepare but do not deliver, and even lengthy quotations from newspapers and magazines. 

Such materials may be printed in the Record under the heading, "Extensions of Remarks." They put these into the Record so that they may secure copies and send them out under their franking privilege to the voters back home. 

The House of Representatives.-The House of 

Representatives is composed of four hundred and thirtyfive members elected by the people for a term of two years. 

A person in order to qualify for the House of Representatives must be twenty-five years of age and “be an inhabitant of that state in which he shall be chosen." 

The number of representatives which the people of each state elect is based on population. At the present time each member represents about 300,000 people. Each state, however, is permitted to elect one representative, regardless of its population. Nevada has one member even though its population is just slightly over 100,000. New York, which is the largest state in population, has 43 members. Every ten years after the official census, Congress redistributes the number of representatives among the states. This is called reapportionment. Since 1910 the total number of representatives, however, has not been increased. After each census some states gain members, while others lose. 

The gain or loss depends upon changes in population. 

In each state the legislature divides the state into as many districts as it has representatives. One representative is elected by the voters of each district. If any state is entitled to an additional representative under a new apportionment, it need not defer his election until a new district is created, but may elect him from the state at large.

Ewing Galloway

The president is delivering a message at a joint session of the House of Representatives and the Senate. 

A representative so chosen is called a Congressman-atlarge. Sometimes state legislatures make these districts of queer shapes in order to give one political party control over more districts than it deserves. This is called "gerrymandering," and is so named for Elbridge Gerry, an early governor of Massachusetts, who was accused of encouraging the practice in that state. 

The presiding officer of the House of Representatives is the Speaker who is elected by the members. He is a member of the political party which commands a majority of the votes in the House. The Speaker presides over sessions of the House, and recognizes members when they wish to speak. He applies and interprets the rules of procedure. In spite of rigid rules the visitors will find the House very noisy. Members read newspapers, carry on conversation,

and to the visitor appear to pay little attention to what is going on.

In addition to the general duty of law-making, which the Senate and House of Representatives share equally, the Constitution has given each house special powers. (1) The House of Representatives has the power to bring formal charges in impeachment proceedings against a federal civil official. (2) All bills for raising revenue must originate in the House of Representatives. (3) When no candidate for President obtains a majority of the electoral votes, the House of Representatives, voting by states, elects the President.

The Senate.-The Senate is composed of ninety-six members or two from each state. According to Article V of the Constitution the states must always be equally represented in the upper House. Because the Senate is a smaller body, its members have more opportunity to become well-known to the whole country. It is generally admitted that the Senate has a larger number of experienced members than the House. Thus, the Senate has acquired somewhat greater prestige than the House. 

In order to qualify to be a Senator one must have attained to the age of thirty and have been nine years a citizen of the United States. It is true, however, that 

Henry Clay was admitted to membership in the Senate before he was thirty years of age.

The term of a Senator is six years. One-third of the 

Senate or thirty-two members have to be elected every two years. Thus, the Senate is a continuously organized body since two-thirds of the membership hold over after each election.

The Constitution originally provided that Senators should be elected by the state legislatures, but in accordance with the seventeenth amendment adopted in 1913

Ewing Galloway

This picture shows the interior of the Senate chamber in the Capitol building. Photographing the Senate in session is prohibited. they are now elected directly by the people of each state. 

Senators are elected at large from each state, but it is customary in some states for one Senator to come from one section of the state, and the other from another section.

The Vice-President of the United States is automatically President of the Senate. He presides over the Senate's sessions and has the power of making rulings. He can make only two speeches-one when he begins his term of office and the second when he goes out as Vice-President. 

He has no vote except in the event of a tie. The senate also has a President pro tem. He is elected by the members and serves when the Vice-President is absent or in the event that official becomes President of the United States.

In addition to being practically equal in law-making power with the House, the Senate has certain special powers. (1) It acts as a court to try all federal officers who

have been impeached by the House. A majority vote is required to remove an impeached official. (2) The Senate has a share in the control of foreign affairs. The President negotiates treaties and the Senate approves or disapproves them by a two-thirds vote. (3) The Senate is called upon to confirm all major appointments made by the President. 

This is done by a majority vote. (4) When no candidate for Vice-President has a majority of the electoral votes, the Senate elects the Vice-President of the United States. 

Political Parties in Congress. In many ways the members of Congress are influenced by their political party connections. One party is usually in the "majority." 

This means that more than half the members of Congress belong to it. The other party is then known as the "minority." Members who belong to the same political party have meetings called "caucuses," at which they decide how they will vote on important matters that will come up for consideration on the floor of the house. They agree on how they will vote on certain bills and they decide what members they will nominate and support for the various positions, such as Speaker of the House. Both the majority and the minority parties have caucuses.

Each political party also has a "floor leader" who advises the members of his own party how to vote and directs his party's strategy. The "party whip" makes sure that each member of his group is present to vote. 

Committees in Congress.-Proposed laws which are brought before Congress are called bills. Thousands of bills are introduced at every session. In order to select those bills which are important and deserve to be voted on by the members, each house has committees. There are forty-seven standing committees in the House and thirtyfive in the Senate. The membership of these committees is selected by the political parties of each house. Each party

is allowed membership on committees in the proportion that its membership bears to the entire number of the house. Thus, if sixty per cent of the members of the House of Representatives belong to the Democratic party, sixty per cent of the committee members will be Democrats. 

Every bill or resolution which is introduced into either house is referred to the proper committee of the house in which it is introduced. Bills having to do with national taxes are referred to the Ways and Means Committee in the House; when they get to the Senate they are considered by the Finance Committee there. Bills which are concerned with the army are referred to the Military Affairs Committee in the House and to the committee of the same name in the Senate. 

How Laws Are Made in Congress.-Bills may be introduced by any member of either house, but all revenue bills must come from the House of Representatives. 

Let us imagine that a bill is introduced in the House of Representatives. After it is written, a member of the House writes his name on it and deposits it in a box at the clerk's desk where it is numbered and the presiding officer refers it to the proper committee.

At this point the great majority of bills are quietly put to death. The committee does not take any action on them and they are never heard of again. However, if it is an important bill the committee may have public hearings when people who are for and against it are given an opportunity to be heard. The bill may be killed in committee, it may be amended, it may be rewritten, or it may be adopted in its original form. 

After the bill is reported favorably to the House, it is put on the calendar to await its turn to be considered. 

When a bill is considered by the House, it is read section by section. It is debated and may be amended.

President Roosevelt is signing a bill requested by the Department of Justice to aid the federal government in combatting crime. 

If a majority of the members vote in favor of its passage, the bill is signed by the presiding officer. 

It is then sent to the Senate where it goes through essentially the same procedure. If the Senate should change the bill from the form in which it passed the House, it will have to be returned to the House for its agreement. If the 

House does not agree to the changes, the bill is sent to the conference committee composed of members from both houses, where the differences may be adjusted. 

After a bill has been passed by both houses in exactly the same form, it is then sent to the President. He may deal with it in any one of three ways: (1) He may sign it, in which event it becomes a law. (2) If he disapproves it, he vetoes it and returns it with his objection to the house where it originated. In this case the bill does not become a law unless it passes both houses by a two-thirds majority.

133 (3) He may keep it ten days without taking any action on it. In this case it becomes a law without his signature if 

Congress is in session during these ten days. Those bills which remain unsigned on the President's desk after a session of Congress has come to an end are considered dead. 

Putting an end to a bill in this way is called pocket veto. 

Debate in Congress.-After a bill once reaches the floor of the House of Representatives, debate begins. But a membership of 435 is too large for effective debate and usually speeches are very much limited in time. 

In the Senate, however, there are usually no limits on debate. Only when two-thirds of the Senators are willing, is it possible to limit each speaker on a bill to one hour. 

This rule has been applied only a few times. Sometimes members try to "talk a bill to death." This is called filibustering. A small group of members can hold up the 

Senate for weeks and prevent it from doing anything. A 

Senator once talked sixteen hours without stopping. Filibustering can be a nuisance, but the Senate should continue to be a forum for free speech. In a democracy it is necessary to have free discussion.

Lobbying in Congress.-Many of the bills in Congress are introduced and passed because they will benefit various groups of people and organizations in the country. 

Those who are especially interested in certain laws frequently employ agents called lobbyists, who bring pressure upon the members of Congress to get what they want. The work of these agents who try to get certain laws passed and prevent the passage of others is called lobbying. Thus, soldiers' bonus laws have been passed because of the pressure brought to bear by ex-soldiers and their organizations. Many bills are passed because of the influence of lobbyists. There are more than five hundred lobbying organizations, or pressure groups, in Washington.

Congressional Investigations. For a number of years Congress, usually the Senate, has conducted special investigations. These are usually carried on by a special committee which has the power to bring persons to the hearings who tell what they know about the subject under investigation. During World War II Senator Harry S. 

Truman, of Missouri, was chairman of the Senate Committee to Investigate the War Program. Startling facts were revealed. Other famous investigations have dealt with naval oil leases, banking practices, and civil liberties. 

These investigations often lead to constructive legislation and at least bring to light things the public should know. 

Congress and the People.-At every session of Congress each member receives many letters, telegrams, telephone calls, and personal visits from individual citizens from his home state. This is as it should be in a democracy. 

Members of Congress are the elected representatives of the people and are expected to carry out their wishes. 

The people themselves should at all times study the activities of Congress and the problems which our nation is facing. In this way they will know what kind of laws will be best for our country. Congress should be encouraged to ignore selfish groups of persons who want laws only for their own profit and gain; it should be encouraged to make laws for the welfare of the country as a whole.

Chapter V  

A. LAW ENFORCEMENT IN OUR NATIONAL GOVERNMENT 

The President.-Congress makes the laws of our national government, but the enforcement of these laws is the work of the executive department, headed by the President. He is the official head of our government. Few persons either in this country or abroad are as prominent and attract as much attention as the President of the United States. Through the newspapers and the radio his activities and speeches are constantly kept before the people. Strong and capable Presidents usually set forth plans and policies for our government and influence the making as well as the enforcement of laws. During the first year of Franklin D. Roosevelt's term, which was a time of emergency, Congress did almost everything that he asked them to do.

The Constitution provides that the President must be a native-born citizen of the United States at least thirty-five years of age. He must have been a resident at least fourteen years in the United States. Most Presidents have been considerably older than thirty-five. Theodore Roosevelt, our youngest President, was not quite forty-three when he took the oath of office.

The President is elected indirectly by the people for a term of four years. The Constitution does not say how many terms a President may serve, but ever since Washington declined a third election, only one man, Franklin D. 79

Roosevelt, has had more than two terms. Friends of President Grant wanted him to run for a third term but he did not. Theodore Roosevelt tried for a third term but failed.

The President is elected in November and takes office on the following January 20. His inauguration, or formal introduction to office, is held in Washington and is usually attended by many thousands of people. 

The salary of the President is $75,000 a year. He is also furnished with a home-the White House clerks, four secretaries, and six executive assistants. For traveling expenses and official entertainment, he is allowed $25,000 each year. In comparison with what the heads of most countries receive, the total amount of the President's salary and expenses is small. 

How the President Is Chosen.-Candidates for Presi dent and Vice-President are nominated by the political parties at national conventions held in June or July every four years. Delegates to these conventions are chosen in each state according to the laws or party regulations of that state. A number of different candidates for President and Vice-President are usually proposed at each convention. A majority (more than half) of the votes of the delegates is required for nomination. An official statement of party principles called a platform is also adopted. 

The makers of the Constitution wanted the President to be elected by a group of capable men carefully chosen from each state. These men, called electors, were supposed to use their own judgment and choose the President. But this plan does not work in the way originally intended. 

Today the electors do not use their own judgment in voting for President and Vice-President; they are “rubber stamps" who vote for the candidates who have been nominated by their political party. 

Each state has as many electors as there are Repre-

bibogo ali bus mojposts 

Ewing Galloway

The President of the United States and his family live in the White 

House. 

sentatives and Senators from that state. Thus, no state has less than three; the largest state in population, New York, has forty-seven. The total number is 531. We refer to all the electors collectively as the "electoral college." 

On the first Tuesday after the first Monday in November in every year divisible by four, each state holds an election in which the presidential electors for that state are chosen by the people. A voter does not vote for individual electors but for the entire list presented by a political party for that state. A voter who favors the election of the 

Democratic candidates for President and Vice-President will, therefore, vote for the entire list of Democratic electors. The political party which gets the most votes of the people in a particular state wins all the electors of that state. The political party which gets a majority of the elec-

tors from all the states wins the election and its candidates become President and Vice-President. In actual practice, of course, the voters do not think about the electors but about the candidates for President and Vice-President. 

If no candidate for President receives a majority of the electoral votes, the election is referred to the House of Representatives which chooses one of the three highest candidates. In this case each state is allowed one vote. 

The Representatives from each state voting as a unit cast this ballot. On two occasions, in 1801 and in 1825, Presidential elections have been decided by the House. Thomas 

Jefferson and John Quincy Adams were elected in this manner. In case no candidate for Vice-President has a majority of the electoral votes, the Senate makes the choice. 

This happened only in 1837. 

Vacancies in the Presidency.—If the President dies, resigns, is removed, or is unable to perform the duties of his office, the Vice-President becomes President. The Vice- 

President should have the same qualifications as the President, but usually the political parties nominate men for this office largely to represent a certain state or section of the country or a disappointed faction of the party. The 

Vice-President is not a busy man, since his only official duty is to preside over the Senate. 

If the Vice-President should become President and then die while he is in office, or if he should become unable to carry on the duties of that office, the members of the Cabinet succeed to the presidency in the following order: 

Secretary of State, Secretary of the Treasury, Secretary of

War, Attorney General, Postmaster General, Secretary of the Navy, Secretary of the Interior. This is in accordance with a law passed by Congress in 1886. 

Powers of the President.-The powers of the Presi dent are numerous.

They may be listed as (1) executive,

Our national government is divided into three departments: legislative, executive, and judicial. The members of Congress, elected by the people, make the laws of our country. The executive department, headed by the president, enforces the laws. The judicial department interprets the laws. 83

(2) legislative, (3) military, (4) pardon, (5) power over foreign affairs. 

1. Executive Powers. Strictly speaking, this means control over law enforcement, which is the chief duty of the executive department. Before taking up his duties as President the following oath is administered to him by the Chief Justice of the Supreme Court: "I do solemnly swear (or affirm) that I will faithfully execute the office of President of the United States, and will to the best of my ability, preserve, protect, and defend the Constitution of the United States." To enforce the Constitution and laws of the United States, if need be, the President has the army and navy at his disposal.

The President has extensive powers of appointment and removal. He appoints many important officials, including judges, Cabinet members, diplomatic officials, members of independent boards and commissions, and postmasters of large cities. All of these appointments have to be approved by the Senate. In addition the President appoints a vast number of minor officials who do not have to be approved by the Senate. In 1933, it was estimated that there were 150,000 political jobs in the federal service. Members of Congress recommend many people for these positions. Each Representative is consulted about one hundred or more jobs and each Senator approximately one thousand. With the exception of judges of the federal courts, the President has the power to remove any official whom he has appointed. This power of the President to appoint and remove officials gives him control and supervision over them. 

2. Legislative Powers. A strong and capable President has much influence over law-making. The Constitution provides that the President shall from time to time send messages to Congress. From the time of Jefferson to that of

Wilson these messages were always sent to Congress in written form, but Woodrow Wilson revived the custom begun by Washington of appearing personally before Congress and delivering his message. In these messages to 

Congress the President frequently makes recommendations for laws. In some cases he sends complete bills for consideration.

Congress is not obliged to pass these laws suggested by the President, but his recommendations usually carry great weight. Because some members of Congress want the President to appoint their friends and political supporters to some of the many government positions, they are sometimes willing to return the favor by voting for the President's bills. When the President has definite recommendations for law-making, he may call Congress into extra session. Another reason for the President's influence is his power to veto bills. They are very rarely passed over his veto. Sometimes bills are changed to suit the President so that he will not veto them. In recent times the President has become the most important leader of Congress. 

3. Military Powers. The Constitution says "the President shall be Commander-in-chief of the Army and Navy of the United States, and of the militia of the several States, when called into the actual service of the United States." 

While the President does not actually take the field during war, he might do so if he wished. He may have much to do with the selection of the actual field head of the armies. 

During war Congress usually gives the President many additional powers. President Wilson had the power to fix the price of wheat during the World War I. Many emergency powers were given to President Roosevelt during 

World War II. 

4. Power Over Pardons.

The Constitution provides that

the President shall have power to grant pardons for certain offenses against the United States. 

5. Power Over Foreign Affairs. The President is very powerful in the field of foreign affairs. He appoints ambassadors, ministers, and special representatives to foreign countries. He appoints the Secretary of State who looks after our relations with foreign countries. In normal times the President may be content to let his Secretary of State handle foreign affairs. But in a crisis the President usually assumes direction.

The President has the sole power to make treaties with foreign countries. But a treaty cannot go into effect without the consent of the Senate. This must be by a twothirds vote.

The President cannot declare war. This power is in the hands of Congress, but the President can make a conflict practically unavoidable by his conduct of foreign relations or by his orders to the army or navy. 

The People and the President.-Sometimes political parties have deliberately refused to nominate for President some of the best-known and best-qualified men, because these men had definite opinions on public questions and had made many enemies because of the opinions. 

A candidate is often chosen from a big state, or from some particular section of the country, just to win a large number of electoral votes from that area.

This is not as it should be. Each political party should try to pick out its wisest and ablest man as candidate for this great office. In nominating and electing the President the people in the American democracy should keep in mind the work the President has to do. 

In a democracy the people are loyal to their President because he is the elected leader of their nation, but they will never fail to criticize him when they do not agree with

him. Freedom of speech and press are important essentials of a democracy.

The Cabinet.-The Constitution refers to heads of departments, but it does not definitely provide for a Cabinet. 

President Washington began the custom of having a Cabinet and of calling meetings of that body and his successors have kept it up. 

When our government under the Constitution first began in 1789 Congress established three departments— 

State, Treasury, and War. An Attorney General and a 

Postmaster General were also created the same year and these two offices were made departments. Gradually others were added. The Navy Department was set up in 1798, Interior in 1849, Agriculture in 1889, Commerce in 1903, and Labor in 1913. There are ten departments today. 

Each department has at its head an official, usually called a secretary. Taken as a body, these department heads make up the Cabinet. Members of the Cabinet are appointed by the President, subject to approval of the Senate. Very rarely does the Senate refuse to approve the 

President's choice. The President, then, has practically a free hand in choosing his Cabinet. This body is responsible to him and each member holds office at his pleasure. 

Cabinet members have two general duties: (1) Each member is head of one of the departments and is responsible to the President for its management. (2) The members of the Cabinet, as individuals or as a group, act as advisers to the President. The Cabinet meets with the 

President twice a week for discussion of important matters about the government. All department heads go to these meetings and lately it has become customary for the Vice-President to attend. The President is not compelled to accept advice given. It is said that a President once asked his Cabinet to vote on an important matter. The

results showed that all the members of the Cabinet favored a certain action which he opposed, so the President announced " 'ayes' seven, 'noes' one-the noes have it."

The American and the English Cabinets.-Governments are sometimes classified as presidential and cabinet. 

The American government is the presidential type; the 

English government is the cabinet type. Most European democracies have the cabinet type of government. 

Members of the English cabinet are also members of Parliament where they are responsible for introducing all important laws. They belong to the majority party in the 

House of Commons and when they lose control of this body they must resign. Thus, the English cabinet is always responsible to the elected representatives of the people. 

In the French government cabinets change very frequently in order to keep satisfying the law-making body. 

The American cabinet members cannot be a part of Congress; they can only recommend bills or have them introduced by some member of Congress. Our Cabinet members are directly responsible to the President and hold their offices as long as they please him. The fact that the 

American President and his Cabinet may occasionally belong to a different party from the majority members of Congress causes the two groups sometimes to pull in different directions. The American system works best when the 

President and his Cabinet are of the same political party as the majority of the members of Congress. 

The Work of the Departments. Each department head has many assistants, and in some cases several thousand employees who work under his direction. 

Department of State. This is the oldest of the departments and its head is consequently the senior member of the Cabinet. It has charge of the relations of the United 

States with foreign countries. It makes treaties with for-

Courtesy Bureau of Engraving and Printing, U. S. Treasury Department 

The currency printing presses are under the supervision of the Treasury Department. eign governments and sends instructions to American diplomatic officials, and does many other things. This department also keeps the original copies of all laws and treaties, and keeps the great seal of the United States which must appear on many public documents. It also communicates with the state government on all official matters.

Treasury Department. This department has general charge of the finances of the government. It collects the money which is owed to the national government. It has charge of all federal funds and pays government bills. It also supervises the coining and printing of money and oversees the national banks. 

In addition to its financial activities this department has to do with the construction of public buildings, the secret service, and the control of the coast guard and life-saving service.

Official photograph, U. S. Army Air Corps 

Airplanes are an important factor in American defense. 

Department of War. The main task of this department is the organization of the army. It is charged with enlisting and equipping men and has the responsibility for training officers. It has general supervision over the United 

States Military Academy at West Point. The engineering corps of this department dredges harbors and improves waterways. The Panama Canal Zone is also governed by the Department of War.

The Navy Department. The chief tasks of this department are the construction and equipment of ships, enlistment of men, training of officers, and supervision of the armed forces on the high seas and navigable waters. 

Department of Justice. The Attorney General who heads this department is the legal adviser of the President and of the other members of the Cabinet. He or his assistants represent the United States in all cases to which the

91 

Official U. S. Navy photograph 

United States cruisers are named after cities. The U. S. S. "Chicago" is shown in maneuvers off the West Coast. government is a party before the courts. The now famous 

G-men come under this department. They have done much to protect the people from organized crime. 

The Post Office Department. The Postmaster General is in charge of the postal service of the United States. This department employs more than 300,000 people. Every postmaster, clerk, or mail carrier is an employee of the 

United States government.

The Interior Department. This department has many and varied duties. It has under its control many millions of acres of public lands and national parks; it looks after the Indian tribes still living on reservations; it has charge of irrigation dams and reservoirs constructed by the national government; it supervises the government of 

Alaska, Hawaii, Puerto Rico, and the Virgin Islands. These possessions of the United States are known as territories.

Each one has a governor appointed by the President, and a legislature consisting of two houses. Most laws for the territories are made by their own legislatures. 

Department of Agriculture. The main task of this department is to aid and encourage the farmer. It estab- 

Ewing Galloway lishes and maintains agricultural experiment stations, makes scientific studies of soils, plants, and livestock, distributes seed, enforces the pure food and drug laws, and inspects markets. One of its most important tasks is to enforce those laws which are planned to conserve the soil, control the size of farm crops, and raise prices of farm products. 

Department of Com- A complete population census is taken every ten years. merce. One of the most important tasks of this department is the encouragement and expansion of American business and trade. Other responsibilities of the department are the inspection of ships, the maintenance of lighthouses, control over fisheries, and regulation of commercial aviation, including the licensing of pilots and planes. 

Every ten years one of its divisions, the census bureau, makes a complete census of the population of the United States.

Another important division is the Patent Office, which does an enormous business. A patent gives an inventor exclusive control over his invention for seventeen years. 

The Department of Labor. This department gathers and publishes statistics on wages and working conditions in various industries; it promotes the welfare of wage earners and enforces some of the national labor laws. It often helps to settle serious labor disputes. 

This department also has charge of the enforcement of immigration and naturalization laws; it examines foreigners who seek to enter the country, and helps in the work of Americanizing foreigners who have been admitted. 

Independent Federal Agencies.-A number of additional boards and commissions have been set up by the federal government. Many have been created since 1933. 

Only the more important ones can be described here. 

Commissioners for the District of Columbia. The city of Washington, the nation's capital, is located in the area known as the District of Columbia. All the laws for this city are made directly by Congress, and the government is carried on under the direction of three commissioners who are appointed by the President. Persons living in the 

District of Columbia cannot vote for President, members of Congress, nor for local officers.

The Interstate Commerce Commission was created by Congress in 1887 and now consists of eleven members. Its chief work is to make rates for railroads, steamships, and other forms of interstate transportation. 

The Civil Service Commission was created by Congress in 1883. It has three members and not more than two can belong to one political party. The work of the commission is to examine applicants for government positions which are under the merit system. Examinations are conducted in the larger post offices. At present about 500,000, or

more than half of the federal employees, are under civil service regulations. Once in office these persons cannot be discharged, except for cause shown in a public hearing. 

The Federal Trade Commission was established in 1914 and is composed of five members. It investigates the business of large companies so as to prevent the use of unfair methods.

The Federal Reserve Board was established in 1914 and has seven members. It supervises our national banking and finance.

The Federal Communications Commission was established in 1934 and consists of seven members. The largest work of the commission is the regulation of radio broadcasting. It licenses stations, fixes hours of operation, decides on wave lengths, and determines sending power limits.

The Securities and Exchange Commission was established in 1934 and consists of five members. Its task is to guard investors against needless losses in the stock market.

Stocks have to be registered and approved by the Commission. It is also believed that unwholesome speculation on the stock market can be prevented by this commission. 

The Tariff Commission was reorganized in 1930 and is composed of six members. It makes investigations for the 

President and Congress concerning the tariff and recommends changes.

The Federal Power Commission was created in 1920 and consists of five members. Its work is concerned with the conservation of water power and it can regulate interstate rates for power.

The Veterans' Administration was created in 1930 and was placed in control of pensions and other aid to persons who have served in the army or the navy. After World 

War II the work of this government agency greatly in-

creased. Pensions and vocational rehabilitation are provided for veterans with service-connected disabilities. This agency makes provision for loans to veterans; it also provides help for any who wish to attend schools or colleges. 

Reorganization of the Executive Department.-For a long time it was felt that the various federal agencies were too numerous and too complicated. For this reason 

Congress in 1939 gave the President the power to reorganize and consolidate the work of some of these government bureaus and commissions. However, he is not permitted to establish any new departments or to abolish any that now exist. The President's reorganization plans have to be submitted to Congress which has some veto power over them. President Roosevelt consolidated the work of a number of different government agencies under three headings: 

The Federal Security Agency, The Federal Works Agency, and The Federal Loan Agency. This plan should make for greater efficiency in our government. 

B. OUR FEDERAL COURTS The Work of the Federal Courts.-The authority of our national courts is defined in the Constitution and extends to the following types of cases: (1) when the Constitution of the United States is concerned, (2) when any laws of Congress are violated, (3) when a treaty made by the national government is concerned, (4) when United States ambassadors, other public ministers, or consuls are involved, (5) when there is a dispute between a state and a foreign country, (6) when a crime is committed on the waters outside any state, (7) when there are lawsuits between citizens of different states, (8) when there is a dispute between states, (9) when there is any dispute in which the government of the United States is involved, (10) when a state wishes to bring suit against a citizen

96 of another state. It was necessary to give the national courts power over all such cases because they are concerned with national and international questions, and with the relations between the states. 

The courts of few other countries have as much power as those in the United States. Both state and national courts have the power to declare laws which are in conflict with the Constitution to be null and void, and of no effect. Any federal court can declare a law of Congress unconstitutional, but such cases may be appealed to the Supreme Court whose decisions are final. The Supreme 

Court may also declare a state law null and void if it finds that law to be contrary to the Constitution of the 

United States.

How Our Federal Courts Are Organized.—The 

Constitution says "the judicial power of the United States shall be vested in one Supreme Court, and in such inferior courts as the Congress may from time to time establish." The number of members of the Supreme Court, their salaries, the number of other courts, and all other details were left to Congress to work out. 

Congress has established three grades of regular federal courts: district courts, circuit courts of appeals, and the

Supreme Court. 

District Courts. There are eighty-four district courts. 

Some districts have more than one judge; one district in 

New York has five judges. The total number of district judges is about 150. There is at least one district in each state and some states have as many as eight. Most federal cases begin in the district courts. 

The district court has two or more sessions a year. 

In every district there is a prosecuting attorney who represents the government in cases before the court. Also each district court has a marshall who acts as the executive

The regular federal courts include the Supreme Court, which is the highest court in the country, ten circuit courts of appeals, and eightyfour district courts. The organization of state courts varies somewhat in different states. State courts are discussed in Chapter VI, pages 117 and 118. officer of the court and arrests offenders against federal laws.

The Circuit Courts of Appeals. The states are divided into ten circuits, each of which has a circuit court of appeals. These courts hold sessions in various parts of their circuits. Each court has from two to six judges. The purpose of these courts is to hear appeals from the district courts. They make final decisions when no question about

the Constitution arises. They thus prevent many cases from reaching and overloading the Supreme Court. 

The Supreme Court. The Supreme Court is the highest federal court. Its opinions in all cases brought before it are final and every other branch of our government is required to abide by its decisions. 

Cases which affect ambassadors, other public ministers, and consuls, and those in which a state is a party may begin in the Supreme Court. All other cases must be appealed to it from a lower court. In some cases there may be appeals from the highest state courts as well as from lower federal courts.

In the beginning of our history the Supreme Court was composed of a chief justice and five associate justices. At one time there were ten members. Today the Court has one chief justice and eight associate judges. In 1935 the 

Court moved into the new Supreme Court Building. This building contains a court chamber, offices for each of the justices, and a large law library. It is one of the most beautiful public buildings in Washington.

It is interesting to watch the Court in action. At the opening of a daily session the judges march from their robing rooms and take their seats amid much pomp and ceremony. The judges sit on a raised platform. The Chief 

Justice sits in the center and next on his right comes the senior associate justice in service and to the left comes the second in seniority and so on through the list. Seniority is not determined by age, but by years of service. 

Cases are argued before the Court by lawyers for both sides. Judges may interrupt lawyers and ask questions at any time. After hearing the arguments, the judges take the cases for decision. During the regular session the judges meet every Saturday to discuss and decide cases. At least six of the judges must be present to hear cases. The Chief

adt 30 sto  

The new Supreme Court building is one of the finest examples of classical architecture in the United States. 

Justice calls upon each judge to discuss the case under consideration and then a vote is taken. A majority vote decides a case one way or the other. The Chief Justice, if he happens to be of the majority, may write the opinion; otherwise he appoints another judge to write it. Those who disagree may also write dissenting opinions. Monday is usually the day when opinions are delivered in the Court. The utmost secrecy surrounds opinions and decisions until they are read.

There is always special interest in the work of the Supreme Court because it has final authority in all matters that directly concern the meaning of the Constitution. 

When the Supreme Court declares a law unconstitutional,

that law becomes ineffective and no person is required to obey it. During the early years of our national history very few laws of Congress were declared unconstitutional, but since 1920 more than forty such laws have been declared null and void.

The Supreme Court does not give advisory opinions on the constitutionality of any law; it makes decisions only when particular cases of laws which have been violated come before it. It is not possible, therefore, for Congress to find out in advance what the Court thinks about the constitutionality of a proposed law. 

Many people respect the Supreme Court for the impartiality and soundness of its decisions. They think of it as the means by which proper balance between the state and national government is maintained, so that neither will trespass upon the rights of the other. It is also thought of as the guardian of the liberties of the people under the Constitution, because it will not allow Congress or the state legislatures to take away the rights of the people. 

At various times in our history, however, the Supreme Court has become the subject of much controversy. Presidents Jefferson, Jackson, and Lincoln attacked the Court. 

During the depression years of the nineteen thirties critics of the Court, including President Roosevelt, claimed that the Court did not allow Congress enough power to make the kind of laws which were needed to solve some of our national problems, among others being low wages and low farm prices.

Federal Judges. All federal judges are appointed by the President with the approval of the Senate. They serve for life or until they resign or are removed by impeachment.

The present salaries of federal judges are: chief justice of the Supreme Court, $20,500 a year; associate justices

of the Supreme Court, $20,000; judges of the Circuit Court of Appeals $12,500; judges of the district courts, $10,000. 

These salaries are low compared with the salaries of judges in England. Most federal judges could earn more money in private practice as attorneys. In 1937 Congress passed a law for voluntary retirement of United States Supreme Court justices at the age of seventy at full pay provided the judge has served ten years or more.

Chapter VI STATE GOVERNMENT A. STATES AND THEIR RELATION TO EACH OTHER How Our States Were Made. All of the original thirteen colonies which declared their freedom from England in 1776, became states in the American union. Thus, at first there were thirteen stars in the United States flag. 

Soon after independence had been won other states were admitted into the Union. The number of states has continued to grow until today there are forty-eight stars in the flag representing that many states in the Union. 

With the exception of the thirteen original states and two others, all the states of the Union were at one time territories. The Constitution of the United States provides for the admission of new states into the Union. When the people of a territory wish to become a state, Congress may pass an enabling act. This gives the people of the prospective state the right to choose delegates to a convention to draw up a state constitution. After this constitution has been approved by a vote of the people of the territory, it is sent to Congress and the President. On their consent, the new state is admitted into the Union and in a short time a new star appears in the flag. 

No new states may be created from land wholly or partly from states already in the Union, unless the legislatures of those states that are concerned give their consent. All of our continental area has now been divided and admitted as states, but there is some possibility that some 105

Courtesy Colonial Williamsburg, Incorporated 

This structure, closely identified with the political life of colonial Williamsburg, Virginia, has been rebuilt on original foundations as the earlier of two buildings that stood here during the eighteenth century. 

Ewing Galloway

This beautiful state capitol at Richmond, Virginia, was designed by Thomas Jefferson. It is the seat of the oldest continuous legislative body in the United States.

STATE GOVERNMENT 

107 of our territorial possessions, Hawaii, Alaska, and Puerto Rico, may become states. 

Relation of States to One Another.-The forty-eight states in the Union are not equal in size, wealth, or population, but they are legally equal. Each state is represented by two United States Senators. In many important matters a state is supreme within its own boundaries. 

The states are neighbors of each other and must necessarily have various kinds of dealing with one another. The 

Constitution of the United States provides that an act legally done in one state must be accepted as a legal act by the other states. Thus, a marriage lawfully entered into in one state will be recognized as a lawful marriage in all other states. A state must also give a citizen of another state who comes within its borders all the rights and privileges that it gives to its own citizens. For example, a citizen of Georgia can go into Florida and move about and transact business on the same terms as the citizens of the latter state.

When a criminal flees from a state in which he has committed a crime into another state, the governor of the latter state is charged with the duty of returning him to the officials of the state in which the crime has been committed, in order that he may be taken back there for trial. 

This process of returning criminals from one state to another for trial is called extradition. Usually governors readily grant extradition papers when they are asked to do so. But if a governor of any state for any reason, good or bad, should refuse to do this, there is no way to compel him to perform this duty. 

Trade Barriers Between the States.-Under the 

United States Constitution states are forbidden to pass tariff laws. In the past Americans have always been proud of the fact that there are no tariff walls between the states

and that commerce flows freely from one section of this country to another. Free trade among the states has had a great deal to do with the rapid growth of the United States as a prosperous and powerful nation.

During recent years, however, many states have set up trade barriers, using different means of discriminating against out-of-state goods. In some cases they levy taxes which fall heavily on out-of-state producers and shippers. 

In order to aid the dairymen and farmers within their borders, some states have taxes of 10 and 15 cents a pound on all oleomargarine. Some states which produce oranges, lemons, and grapefruit keep out shipments of such fruits from other parts of the country by means of quarantine laws.

The outstanding example of barriers against free trade is found in the state regulation of the trucking industry. 

Many states have set up regulations which make it almost impossible for trucks from other states to carry goods across their borders. Thus, the driver of a five-ton truck from outside of the state, traveling 400 miles, may have to pay $30 in addition to the regular gasoline taxes. Some states insist that out-of-state truckers must buy licenses before they can use their highways. Interstate trucking is also hampered by the wide differences in the requirements of the various states concerning the length, load, and equipment of trucks. In order to enforce the various highway regulations, several states have set up "ports of entry" along their borders, where the drivers of out-ofstate trucks must stop and pay mileage taxes, and have their trucks checked against the safety standards. 

State Constitutions.-Each state has a constitution. 

It is the fundamental law so far as that state is concerned if it does not conflict with the Constitution of the United States, laws passed by Congress in pursuance of the Con-

State governments differ chiefly in the power given to the governor. 

In some states important executive officers are appointed by the governor; in others they are elected by the people.

stitution, and treaties made by the United States. Most state constitutions are made by conventions composed of delegates selected by the people. Their work is, as a rule, submitted to the people for ratification. 

State constitutions of the present day are usually quite long. Some contain 50,000 words or more. The federal 

Constitution can be read in about fifteen minutes, but it would probably take three hours or more to read some of the state constitutions. 

A state constitution contains a bill of rights, which ordinarily is given first. Then come the parts which provide for the framework of the government-the legislature, the executive department, and the judiciary. A great many details about the government of the state are usually found in its constitution. Amendments are generally proposed by the legislature and submitted to the people for ratification. 

B. LAW-MAKING IN THE STATES 

Laws Made by States.-As previously stated, the powers of the states are defined in the tenth amendment of the federal Constitution. State governments have authority over matters which, for the most part, are of local concern. The life of the average person is touched more by the state and local governments than by the national government. The state builds and maintains highways, promotes education, protects the health of its citizens and tries to prevent disease, encourages agriculture, regulates marriage and divorce, makes and enforces most of the laws concerning the conduct of business and industry, and carries on numerous other activities. Most criminal law is state law.

Law-Makers for the States.-Every state has a legislature or law-making body. The members are elected by the people and the terms of office vary from two to four

STATE GOVERNMENT 

111 

Ewing Galloway

The unicameral legislature of Nebraska is shown in session. years. All the states, except Nebraska, have two-house legislatures. In 1937 Nebraska began its plan of a onehouse legislature. If this plan proves successful, some of the other states will probably adopt it. 

The upper house in each state is called the senate. The upper house is always smaller than the lower house. The senate has certain special powers. It confirms most of the appointments by the governor and acts as a court to try impeachments. Usually the lieutenant governor presides over the senate.

The lower house is usually called the house of representatives. It is a much larger body than the senate. A speaker and other officers are selected by the members. Money bills are always started in the lower house. 

In most states the legislature meets every two years, but in a few it meets every year. In Alabama regular sessions are held once in every four years. The salary for both senators and house members is usually the same. 

How State Laws Are Made.-When a member of the legislature wishes to have a certain law enacted, he must
