first write the bill in the proper legal manner. Many states now have legislative reference and drafting bureaus where members of the legislature can get reliable information and expert assistance in writing bills.

The general procedure in law-making in the state legislatures is very similar to that in Congress. The legislature does most of its work through committees. All bills are referred to appropriate committees and are there studied, ignored, or changed. Sometimes they are reported out of committee in their original form. Before they become

STATE GOVERNMENT 

113 laws they must pass each house by three readings, and must be sent to the governor for approval. If he signs the bill, it becomes a law. If he vetoes it, it may be passed "over his head"; this usually takes a two-thirds majority. 

In the state legislatures, as well as in Congress, lobbyists try to influence legislation which they desire passed or defeated. Representatives of teachers' organizations, labor unions, business men, and other groups frequently present their arguments before legislative committees or to individual members of the legislature. Some states require lobbyists to register their names and the names of the organizations they represent, and at the end of the legislative session to file a sworn statement of their expenses. 

Laws Made by the People Directly.-Most state laws are made by the members of the legislature who represent the people, but there are some states where the people may make laws directly. This is done by means of the "initiative" and the "referendum." The initiative gives a certain percentage of the voters the right to sign a petition asking that a certain measure be referred to all the voters for their consideration. If a majority of the voters favor the measure, it becomes a law. Submitting a law or other proposition to all the voters for their approval or disapproval is called the referendum. 

Democracy and State Law-making. The initiative and referendum are supposed to make state law-making more democratic because they place some of the lawmaking power directly into the hands of the people. The results thus far, however, have been somewhat disappointing. A California ballot once carried, in addition to the list of candidates, forty separate propositions which the voters were expected to approve or disapprove. The people can hardly be expected to become directly interested in and informed about so many proposed laws.

Well-informed and alert citizens can influence lawmaking even though direct legislation is not permitted. 

They can choose their representatives carefully, watch their records in the legislature, and keep in constant contact with them through letters and visits. 

C. ENFORCING STATE LAWS 

The Governor. The chief of the executive depart ment in each state is the governor. He is elected by the people and the term varies, in different states, from two to four years.

The governor has a great many powers. He is charged with carrying out state laws. In certain cases he may use the state militia if he thinks it necessary. The governor also appoints many persons to office, but most major appointments have to be ratified by the senate. 

The governor can send messages to the legislature, telling the members what laws he thinks they should pass. 

Many governors exert a powerful influence on law-making. 

The extent of this power depends much upon the personality of the governor. In every state except North 

Carolina the governor has the power of veto. The legislature can usually override a veto by a two-thirds vote. In nearly all states the governor is allowed to veto single items in bills appropriating money.

The governor in most states has the power to release a law-breaker from paying a fine, lessen his punishment, or pardon him outright. This power is much abused in a number of states. Too many people are pardoned or have their punishment lessened. It has been suggested that this power should be placed in the hands of a small group of judges.

Other State Executive Officials.-Most of the states have a lieutenant governor who is elected by the people,

STATE GOVERNMENT 

115 and succeeds to the office of governor if a vacancy occurs there. He usually presides over the senate. 

The secretary of state takes care of important state papers and publishes the laws. The state treasurer receives the money of the state for safe keeping and pays the bills. 

Courtesy Department of Public Safety, Texas 

This Texas ranger is assigned to the Texas-Mexico border patrol.

The attorney general gives legal advice to the governor and other state officials and represents the state in suits at law. The auditor examines the financial accounts of state. officers and must approve bills before they can be paid by the state treasurer. Other state executive officials are concerned with agriculture, welfare, schools, labor, health, highways, public utilities, and conservation.

Improvement in State Executive Departments.— 

The executive officers in the states do not as a rule form a cabinet as in the federal government. Some of them are elected by the people and are not directly responsible to the governor. State governments might be conducted more

Courtesy State Bureau of Publicity and Information, Alabama The state highway building at Montgomery, Alabama, is built of reinforced concrete with interior walls of pressed briquettes. These walls are removable to permit redesigning of the interior of the building if necessary for greater efficiency in use of space. efficiently if the governor were given greater authority and allowed to appoint more of the important state executive officials. Thus, the governor could be held directly responsible for the administration of the state's affairs. These reforms have been undertaken in some states. 

Each state has hundreds and perhaps thousands of employees whose work requires special training and detailed knowledge. At the present time many of these persons get their jobs through political influence. Less than half of the forty-eight states have civil service laws for state employees. In order to have more efficient state government, the merit system should be extended to all state employees, except the leading officials who help to determine the general policies of the government. 

D. STATE COURTS Why We Need Courts.-We need courts to interpret the laws as they apply in particular cases. A criminal case is one in which a person is accused of breaking a law. The

STATE GOVERNMENT 

117 值 ​Another beautiful state building is this new state office building at 

Raleigh, North Carolina. state prosecutes or brings suit against a person who is accused of having committed a crime. When a person is arrested for murder, the courts try to find out whether he is innocent or guilty of the crime. The courts must also find out under what circumstances the crime was committed. In this way the severity of the punishment may be determined.

In civil cases one person or corporation claims to have been wrongly treated by another. There is usually a claim for "money" damages. The person who brings the charge is known as the plaintiff, and the one who is sued is the defendant. The courts must decide which of the two parties is right. If they decide against the defendant, they must determine how much he must pay to the plaintiff as damages.

Kinds of Courts.-The usual state courts, in ascending order of rank, are justices of the peace, county courts, circuit or district courts, and a supreme court. In a great many states there are also municipal courts. 

1. Justice of the Peace. The justice of the peace hears

civil cases when only a small sum of money is involved. He tries persons accused of crimes for which the punishment is a small fine, or imprisonment for a short time. If the crime is of a more serious nature, the justice of the peace may hold "preliminary hearings," but the case must go to some higher court for trial. Justices of the peace are usually elected by the voters. 

2. County and Municipal Courts. The county and municipal courts are next in rank. They try more serious civil and criminal cases. 

3. Circuit or District Courts. In many states circuit or district courts exist above the county courts. A circuit or district generally contains several counties. The judges usually travel or make a circuit over their district instead of holding court only in one place. Most of the business of these courts consists of cases which have been appealed from the lower courts. 

4. The Supreme Court or Court of Appeals. The high est court in the state hears appeals from lower courts and makes final decisions on most cases. Appeals may be taken to the federal courts only in cases where the Constitution of the United States or federal laws are involved. 

Judges. Judges are elected by the people in some states, in others they are chosen by the state legislature, in still other states they are appointed by the governor with the consent of the senate. Their term of office varies from one year to life or during good behavior. The judges of the higher courts usually have longer terms than those of the lower courts. They may be removed for misconduct by impeachment. In a few states they may be removed from office by the use of the recall by the voters. 

Criminal Cases.-1. Arrest. A person who is suspected of having committed a crime is arrested by an officer of the law who acts by authority of a "warrant." If a person

STATE GOVERNMENT 

119 is caught in the act of committing a crime or in suspicious circumstances, he may be arrested without a warrant. 

2. Hearing. The person who has been arrested is brought before a justice of the peace or magistrate or alderman for a "hearing." If the offense is not serious, the case may be settled by this court. If the crime is serious, the accused person is ordinarily held in jail; in most other cases he is released until the grand jury meets, provided he can give bail. This means that a reliable person signs a bond, agreeing to pay a certain sum of money to the court if the accused person does not appear for trial. 

3. The Grand Jury. This body usually consists of twenty-three members whose duty it is to consider the charges against persons accused of crime. They decide whether the evidence is sufficient to hold a person for trial. If the members of the grand jury think the accused person ought to be brought to trial, they bring in a true bill. 

4. The Trial. If the accused person pleads "guilty," and is of sound mind, the judge usually pronounces sentence and the case ends. But if he pleads "not guilty," he is entitled to a trial by jury, which is called a petit jury, made up of twelve people selected for the trial. If the prisoner cannot afford an attorney, the judge appoints a lawyer to defend him. In most cases this attorney is paid a small fee by the state. Witnesses are brought into the court by the prosecuting attorney and by the defense. They tell what they know about the case. Opportunity is given for cross-examination. Before the close of the trial the attorneys for both sides deliver closing speeches to the juries. 

The judge charges the jury telling them about the laws that apply to this case. 

5. Verdict. The jury retires to another room to decide on the verdict. They determine whether the accused person is innocent or guilty. When all twelve members agree,

they return to the court room to announce a verdict of "guilty" or "not guilty." (If they cannot all agree after a reasonable length of time, they report that fact to the judge and there may be a new trial with a different jury.) 

6. Sentence. If the verdict of the jury is "guilty," the judge imposes sentence. In some cases the judge is allowed considerable discretion concerning the amount of the fine or the length of the term in prison. It is the duty of the sheriff to see that the sentence is carried out. (If the verdict is "not guilty," the accused person is discharged and can never again be tried for the same crime.) 

7. Appeal. If a person is convicted of a crime, he may appeal his case to a higher court. This may be done on the grounds that the verdict is not according to the law, or to the evidence, or that some error has been committed in the trial. If the higher court decides against the convicted person, he must serve his sentence.

Civil Cases.-In civil cases the trial is somewhat different. The plaintiff's lawyer first files a statement with the court, giving the reasons why he thinks the defendant has done wrong and should pay damages. The defendant's attorney files a statement in which he either admits the truth of the charges or denies them. (If the defendant admits the charges, judgment against him is entered at once. This happens only in rare cases.) When the civil case is tried in court, witnesses are usually brought in to testify by both the defendant and the plaintiff. The case may be tried before a jury, but in many states a considerable number of civil cases are tried without a jury. If the decision is in favor of the plaintiff, the judge or jury decides how much damages the defendant shall pay. Appeal to a higher court may be taken the same as in criminal cases. 

Improvement in Court Procedure. Our system of court procedure has been set up to make sure that no inno-

STATE GOVERNMENT 

121 ! cent person will suffer. An accused person is always regarded as innocent until he is proved guilty. To safeguard the rights of the accused person there are many detailed laws concerning the selection of juries, the drawing up of legal papers, the admission of testimony, and the making of appeals to higher courts.

Because of these many details, lawyers sometimes find legal loopholes that make it possible for guilty persons to escape the punishment they deserve. Too many technicalities cause long and wearisome delays.

State legislatures should pass laws which would simplify court procedure and make it impossible for guilty persons to escape punishment.

Chapter VII LOCAL GOVERNMENT A. THE NEED AND FORMS OF LOCAL GOVERNMENT 

The Need for Local Government.-Many activities of government which affect the daily lives of the people are not carried on directly by the state and federal governments. The states are usually divided into counties, towns or townships, boroughs or villages, and cities. These units of local government provide for the construction and repair of streets and highways, the protection of the people from crime, the establishment and maintenance of schools, the conduct of elections, the protection of the public health, as well as many other things. Even the enforcement of many state laws must be carried out directly by local officers.

Forms of Local Government.-The state constitu tion and the legislature prescribe the forms and the powers of local government in each state. Units of local government receive all their powers from the state government. 

The forms of local government vary greatly in different parts of our country. The three general types developed during our early history are the county system of the South, the town system of New England, and the countytownship system of the Middle Atlantic States. 

The county system developed in the South because the early settlers there lived on large plantations which became the centers of business and government. These plantations became the basis for the counties. In the South 125

today, and in other parts of the country where this system of local government has spread, most activities of local government are carried out through the county. 

The town system of local government developed in New England because the early settlers lived close together, frequently in small villages. The term "town" came to be used for the village and the land surrounding it. In the 

New England states today, and in a few other states where this system of local government has spread, most activities of local government are carried out through the town. 

In the Middle Atlantic colonies the counties were important; but counties were also subdivided into townships which carried out a number of activities. Thus, these states today have a mixed form of county-township government. 

This system of local government was also adopted by some of the other states, for as the early pioneers went West they took their ideas of local government along with them. 

B. COUNTIES 

Counties. Each state is divided into counties, and in many states, especially in the South and the West, the county is the most important unit of local government. 

County governments have to do with such matters as police, construction and maintenance of roads and bridges, poor relief, schools, settlement of estates, hospitals, and elections.

Courts for the trial of civil and criminal cases are usually held in the counties. Each county has a courthouse where the sessions of the courts are held, and where the various county officials have their offices. The place where the courthouse is located is called the county seat. 

County Officers.-The work of the county is carried on by officers who are usually elected by the people. A board of commissioners or supervisors usually fixes the tax

LOCAL GOVERNMENT 

127 RR.Price

CITY OF SAN AUGUSTINE Four photos by Lee: Courtesy Farm Security Administration 

The offices of treasurer, justice of the peace, clerk, and tax collector are among the important positions in local government.

rate and looks after the business of the county. The sheriff has charge of the maintenance of law and order. In some states he has charge of all county prisoners. The coroner investigates all accidental and violent deaths in which there is some suspicion of crime. The treasurer handles the county money. The district attorney or state's attorney prosecutes persons accused of having committed crime. A clerk of courts keeps the record of the court proceedings. 

Other officials keep records of deeds, wills, and mortgages. 

An auditor or controller supervises the finances of the county. Most counties have a superintendent of schools who supervises the rural schools. 

Reform of County Government.-The affairs of our county governments are not always conducted honestly and efficiently. Unfortunately, the list of county officials elected by the people is usually so long that voters find it difficult to know the qualifications of the candidates who are running for office. Many people believe that only a few of the county officers, a small board of commissioners, and a district attorney should be elected by the people. Other officers should be appointed by the county board to which they would be responsible for the efficiency of their service. 

Civil service examinations should be given and the merit system used in appointing these county employees. 

There are three counties in Virginia and a few in other states that have county-manager government. In such a county there is a board or commission elected by the people. This board selects a county-manager who holds office at the board's pleasure. This official has wide appointive powers and manages the business of the county. 

C. TOWNS, TOWNSHIPS, AND VILLAGES 

Towns.-Towns are subdivisions of counties; they are found in New England. In this part of our country most of

LOCAL GOVERNMENT 

129 the work of local government is carried on by towns or cities. Counties have very little power in these states and are usually not much more than districts for holding courts.

The most unusual feature about the government of towns is the annual town meeting at which voters assemble to discuss and decide problems of the local government. 

Opportunity is given for each voter to speak on any proposed action. Problems like the opening of a new street or the erection of a new school building are openly discussed and finally voted on. The town meeting is a form of direct democracy.

A town is also governed by the selectmen, a small body of men, chosen by the people, who have some of the same duties as the mayor and council of a city. Some of the other important officers are clerk, treasurer, auditor, chief of police, and superintendent of schools. 

Townships.-Townships are also subdivisions of counties; they are found in the Middle Atlantic states and in some parts of the Middle West. In these places some activities of local government are carried on by the townships and some by the counties. On the whole, township government is declining in importance. Such activities as the building of roads, the maintenance of schools, and the care of the poor, are in many cases being taken over by the county or the state.

Usually there are no town meetings in a township. The government is carried on by officers who are elected by the people. Almost everywhere in the townships we find supervisors or trustees, a treasurer, auditors, justices of the peace, and a constable. 

Villages or Boroughs.-It sometimes happens that a number of people live together so as to make a community of considerable size inside a township or county. Thus,

there is a need for public improvements such as sidewalks, street lights, and a water supply. In order to make it possible for such a community to have these public conveniences, the state permits them to form a government separate from that of the township or county. These small centers of population which have their own local governments are called towns (not to be confused with the towns in New England), villages, or boroughs.

The people usually elect a council and a mayor. Other officers are about the same as in a township. 

D. CITIES The Growth of Cities.-In the days when George 

Washington was President of the United States, about four per cent of the American people lived in cities; now more than fifty per cent do. In 180o, there were only five cities in the United States with a population of ten thousand or more; today there are approximately a thousand such cities. New York City alone has a population of over seven million.

This growth in cities has been caused by the increase in factories and other forms of business which attract people who seek work. Higher wages and more opportunities have lured many people from the country to the city. 

The use of machinery in agriculture has also decreased the number of workers needed on the farm. 

The Activities of City Governments.-The activities of city governments are many and varied. For the thousands of persons living in the city there must be a constant supply of pure water. Garbage, sewage, and other waste materials must be disposed of without danger to health. People must be transported to offices, factories, stores, and other places of business. This makes necessary the building of streets, subways, and the regulation of

LOCAL GOVERNMENT 

131 buses and street cars. Traffic must be controlled in the interest of safety. Thousands of lives and millions of dollars worth of property require constant protection from fire. 

In parks and playgrounds public recreation must be provided for young and old. All the people must be protected from crime. Public schools must be provided. The poor and unfortunate have to be cared for.

Cities in the United States spend for all purposes every year about two billion dollars. 

How Cities Are Formed.-Cities, like other units of local government, receive all their powers of government from the state exercised through the legislature. A city is usually governed under a charter which outlines the form of government and the extent of its powers. This charter is really a constitution for the city. Many states classify their cities according to population, and provide one type of government for one size and another for a different size group.

In most states a city is also under the county government just as a town, township, or village is. A few of the larger cities, however, are separate and in some cases, as in Philadelphia, the city and the county cover the same territory.

City Government.-There are three prevailing types of city government: (1) the mayor-council, (2) the commission, (3) the city-manager.

LOCAL GOVERNMENT 

133 

Ewing Galloway

Interested citizens visit the city council in session at Seattle, Washington. 

The mayor-council type is the most common. This form of city government is based on the idea that the legislative, executive, and judicial departments should be separated as they are in the federal government and in the state governments.

The law-making body is usually called the council and is elected by the voters of the city. It fixes the tax rate, appropriates money, and makes other laws called ordinances.

The chief executive officer is the mayor who is elected by the people. It is his general duty to see that the laws of the city are enforced. He signs or vetoes all ordinances passed by the council. He usually appoints the heads of administrative departments with the advice and consent of the council.

Most cities have departments of public works, public safety, health, charities or welfare, and others. Cities

In the mayor-council type of city government the voters usually elect the mayor, the municipal judges, the councilmen, and sometimes other officials and boards. usually have a controller, sometimes called comptroller, and a treasurer who deal with finances, and a city solicitor or attorney who looks after legal affairs. The board of education frequently receives its powers directly from the state legislature and is allowed to manage its affairs independently of the other departments. 

The judicial work of the city government is carried on by police judges or magistrates, municipal or county courts.

In recent years there has been much criticism of the mayor-council form of government because it has so frequently resulted in dishonesty and poor service. Many people think that it is so complicated that it is almost impossible to fix responsibility for inefficiency. A person

In the commission plan of city government the voters elect five commissioners who carry on the executive and the legislative functions of the government. One of the commissioners is the mayor of the city. with a grievance goes to the mayor who sends him to the head of the department. The department head blames the council, and the councilmen blame each other and the mayor. Another criticism is that frequently officials are elected or appointed who are not trained and experienced in the work of governing. 

The commission plan of government is used by about four hundred cities. Under this plan the voters elect five commissioners who carry on the executive as well as the legislative functions of the city government. One of the five commissioners is the mayor of the city. Together these five commissioners form the city council and pass ordinances. Each commissioner is also head of an executive department such as public safety, public property, streets,

In the city-manager plan the voters elect a commission or small council which selects a city manager trained for the work of governing a city. 

This plan has improved the government in a number of cities. and finances. This plan has improved the government of many small- and medium-sized cities because it concentrates responsibility in the hands of a few persons. Its chief weakness lies in the fact that the commissioners frequently have no training for the work of their departments.

The city manager plan of government has been adopted by more than five hundred cities in recent years. Under this plan the voters elect a small council which passes all ordinances and decides on all general policies. But the chief task of the council is the selection of the city manager. Usually they appoint a man who has been trained for the work of governing a city. Sometimes he has had experience as manager of a smaller city.

LOCAL GOVERNMENT 

137 

The Administration building at Birmingham, Alabama, Municipal Air port, represents another activity of the local government in some cities. 

The city manager appoints all important city officials. 

He assumes full responsibility for the efficient and businesslike conduct of the city government. He is, of course, responsible to the members of the city council and may be removed from office if they do not like his work. This plan has improved the government in a number of cities because it centralizes responsibility and unifies the work of the city departments. It selects the expert-not necessarily a local man-for the work of governing the city. Cities which have used the manager plan have been able to bring about considerable reduction in the cost of government. 

E. IMPROVING LOCAL GOVERNMENT Democracy and Local Government.-Jefferson said that local self-government is one of the two nails on which democracy hangs. It is a heritage that has come to us from England and from our early forefathers in this country. Citizens of the American democracy have never been accustomed to a strong centralized government such as

Ewing Galloway

The New York Civil Service Commission is shown in session. The civil service merit system helps to provide efficient government. there is in France; they have always exercised considerable control over the government in their own communities. Local self-government, therefore, is an important part of our democracy which we must keep, but not without some changes when they are needed. 

Improving Local Government.-To keep democracy in America means that we must constantly find ways to improve it.

James Bryce, an Englishman, who studied American democracy very closely, said, "The government of cities is the one conspicuous failure of the United States." Some city governments have been cheated out of millions of dollars by dishonest contractors and the corrupt politicians who ran the city. Graft and corruption in our city governments have been altogether too common. Good citizens in a democracy will do everything in their power to correct these conditions. Government in a democracy exists to serve the people, not to enrich the politicians.

LOCAL GOVERNMENT 

139 Conditions of living in our country have changed greatly since the days of our early history when the forms of local government developed. In the days of mud roads and oxcart transportation a very small unit of local government was useful in many parts of the country, but we are now living in an era of automobiles and paved highways. 

Many students of local government, therefore, suggest that townships should be abolished and that in many cases counties should be consolidated. This would result in the saving of considerable money to the taxpayers because there would be fewer officeholders to pay. 

Democracy means rule by the people, but it does not necessarily mean that the voters should elect all of the officials in the township, county, state, and federal governments. Too many names on the ballot merely confuse the voter. Even the most intelligent and well-informed voter can know only a few of the candidates. The number of elective officers should, therefore, be reduced. 

Only those officials who determine policies should be elected by the people; others should be appointed on the basis of the merit system. The problems of government today are so complex and difficult that the voters must learn to employ experts who are trained to do this type of work efficiently. In this manner, democratic government can be made to give the greatest amount of service to the people.

SUMMARY 

Local government deals with most things which affect our lives directly helps to enforce the state laws receives its power and authority from the state differs greatly in various parts of the country centers in the county in the South and parts of the West

centers in the town in New England centers in both the township and the county in some states Counties are found in all states have governments under officers usually elected by the people 

Towns are subdivisions of counties in New England have direct democracy in the form of the town meeting 

Villages or boroughs are established in centers of population to permit a greater amount of government services 

Cities have grown greatly in size and number have governments with many activities; they are governed under a charter granted by the state have one of three types of government-mayor-council, commission, or manager

Local governments are an important part of our democracy should be freed from dishonesty and inefficiency can be reduced in number should employ people who are trained for their work.

UNIT THREE SUPPORTING THE WORK AND DIRECTING THE POLICIES OF OUR GOVERNMENT 

The citizens of a democracy owe a number of important responsibilities and duties to their government. Unit Three shows how we must support the work of our government financially and how we should aid in directing its policies. 

Chapter Eight explains how the services of government cost money which has to be raised by taxing the people. There are many different kinds of taxes collected by the federal, state, and local governments.

Voting is one of the most important duties of citizens in a democracy. Chapter Nine explains how the people choose public officials in our government. It also describes the work of political parties and shows their part in helping the people to express their wishes. 

In a democracy the people should be intelligent and well-informed about public questions. Chapter Ten tells what public opinion is and how it is formed. It suggests ways of detecting propaganda and getting facts on public questions.

Ewing Galloway

The citizen's vote directs the government's policies. 144

Chapter VIII  A. THE SERVICES AND COSTS OF GOVERNMENT The Services of Government Cost Money. Our na tional, state, and local governments perform many services for us. Naturally these activities cost a great deal. 

While we are attending public school, we are using buildings with rooms, laboratories, and equipment provided at considerable cost by the government. Salaries of teachers have to be paid out of state or local funds. 

Money is necessary for construction of roads and bridges, the building of waterworks, the paving and lighting of streets, the digging of sewer systems, and the building of battleships and airplanes. Policemen and other law enforcement officers who protect the people from crime must be paid.

One of the chief items of expense of the national government is the maintenance of the army and the navy which are needed to protect us from foreign enemies. 

There are thousands of persons who are poor or unfortunate and have to be taken care of by the government. During the times when many people are unemployed the government provides jobs at public expense. 

The payrolls of our governments include postmen, policemen, soldiers, sailors, legislators, clerks, scientists, teachers, and many others.

The Increase in the Cost of Government.-We hear a great deal today about the increase in the cost of government. It is claimed that about one-fifth of our national 145

Government spending has almost doubled since 1923. income goes for taxes. The total yearly cost of our local, state, and national governments in recent years has been over $17,000,000,000. This is, of course, much greater than the total cost of government fifty years ago. The increase in the cost of government, however, has not been as great as some people think. In comparing the cost of government today with what it was fifty years ago one must remember four things. (1) There are more people in the United States today than there were fifty years ago. 

All comparisons should be made in cost per person-per capita costs. (2) Prices are much higher than they were fifty years ago. In 1920, when prices were especially high, a dollar bought only half as much as in 1913. (3) The total income of the country today is greater than it was fifty years ago, and the people can therefore afford to spend more for everything, including government. (4) Today governments are called upon to give a greater amount of service to the people in the form of education, roadbuilding, relief, farm payments, and many other things.

Reasons for the Increase in the Cost of Govern ment. 

1. War. When we look at the expenses of the national government, we are impressed by the great amount of money which goes to pay for wars, past or present. World 

War I left us with a record-breaking debt on which the interest alone was over $800,000,000 every year. In addition large sums of money are spent on the care of veterans and for pensions. World War II, however, has made the cost of government higher than at any other time. 

2. Highways. Another large item in the cost of government today is the construction of highways. Because of the increase in the number of automobiles, there has been a great need for good roads. The state and federal govern-

ments spend about two billion dollars for this purpose each year. 

3. Education. More of our young people attend high schools and colleges. Naturally this has brought about an increase in the total cost of education. About two billion dollars a year is spent on the education of our pupils in public schools and on government aid to colleges and universities. 

4. Relief and Recovery. Since 1930 business conditions have been so bad that millions of persons have been unable to find work. For these people the government has spent billions of dollars for relief and work projects of various kinds. It has also loaned money to business men, and has made direct payments to farmers for crop reduction and soil conservation. 

5. Social Insurance. Since 1936 the government has entered upon a vast program of collecting and paying out insurance for unemployment and old age. This adds more billions to the cost of government from which the people in return will receive benefits.

B. SPENDING AND RECEIVING GOVERNMENT MONEY How Governments Plan Spending.-Appropriations and Budgeting.-Who shall decide how much public money shall be spent for various things? The final decisions are made by Congress, the state legislatures, the county boards, and the city councils. These law-making bodies have the power to pass appropriation laws stating how much money shall be spent for each activity. 

Experience has proved that it is necessary for governments to plan carefully how the money is to be spent. 

The plan for spending the money is called a budget. Our national government has operated under a budget plan since 1921. All the departments of the federal government

make estimates of the amount of money which they will need for various purposes during the following year. These estimates are sent to the Bureau of the Budget in the Treasury Department. The Director of the Budget and his assistants study these estimates and make changes where they think it is necessary. They prepare the budget and submit it to the President. When the total expenses are about the same as the estimated income during the year, the budget is said to be balanced. If the President approves the budget, he sends it to the House of Representatives. Here it may be adopted with or without changes; it is then sent to the Senate where it goes through a similar process. It is finally enacted into a law when the President signs it. He is not permitted to veto separate items in the budget, but must approve or reject it as a whole. 

Most states, cities, counties, and other units of government use the budget system of planning their expenditures. 

In many states the governor is given power to veto separate items in the appropriation bill; thus, if the legislature increases the budget by $100,000 for a new state park, the governor may veto this one item while he signs the rest of the bill and it becomes a law. 

The steps in framing a budget bill are outlined in the chart on page 112.

Centralized Purchasing.-After the budget has been adopted, the next problem is how to spend the money wisely. Taxpayers have the right to expect that the finances of the government shall be managed in a thrifty manner.

When one person or department does all the buying for a city, county, or state government, we call it centralized purchasing. There are a number of advantages for this method of buying. In the first place, the buying can all be done by a few persons who are experts and know how to

get the best values for the money. The head of the Department of Welfare in a state may require a new typewriter for his office. Since he has many other things to do, he probably does not know much about buying typewriters. The purchasing agent, however, has charge of buying all the typewriters for the state government, and he can make a special study of values. Another advantage of centralized purchasing is the fact that many things such as stationery, fuel, ink, automobiles, can be purchased in large quantities at lower prices. 

In order to prevent favoritism and dishonesty in buying, many states and cities require that all purchases over a certain amount shall be made by competitive bidding. 

Under this plan when a city wishes to buy five new trucks, the size, weight, and quality of the trucks are carefully stated in a public announcement. All dealers wishing to sell these trucks to the city then make bids which are kept secret until all of them are opened at a stated time and place. The order for the trucks is then given to the lowest bidder.

How Governments Obtain Money.-Taxes. When people talk about the high cost of government, they are usually thinking of high taxes. More than three-fourths of the income of our governments comes from taxes. Our national government, states, cities, school districts, and the like, all have the power of taxing. Sometimes there is considerable overlapping, as in the case of gasoline, which is taxed by both the national and the state governments. 

Tax rates are fixed by our law-making bodies-Congress, the legislatures, city councils, and county boards. Taxes may be direct or indirect. A direct tax is one on the property itself and cannot easily be passed on to someone else; an example is the income tax. Many of our taxes are indirect. An indirect tax is one on the sale or transfer of

ATTENTION OF TAXPAYERS MPORTANT 

Ewing Galloway

More than three-fourths of the income of our federal, state, and local governments comes from taxes. property and can be shifted from the person who pays it to other persons. An example is the federal tax on tobacco. 

The manufacturer pays the tax to the government when he buys stamps to stick on each package; but finally the customer pays the tax, since the manufacturer adds the cost of the stamp to his selling price.

Service Charges, Interest Payments, Licenses, and 

Special Assessments. Since the federal government has loaned large sums of money for building houses and for the conduct of other forms of business, some income is obtained from the payment of interest and principal. 

People who live in government-owned houses pay rent. 

The government income obtained from charges which are made for service amounts to a considerable sum. Federal government-owned dams generate electricity which is sold

Courtesy USHA: Photo by Sekaer 

Government loans helped to finance the building of these apartments in Cleveland, Ohio. to cities and towns. Tolls on the Panama Canal amount to millions of dollars annually. People who send letters, packages, or periodicals through the mails pay the government. Most of the income from these services, of course, goes to meet the expenses involved. 

In the state and local governments, service charges are an important part of the income. Tolls are often charged for the use of bridges and tunnels. Many city governments secure money from the service given by their own waterworks, markets, street railways, and power plants. 

For granting a license, the government charges a certain fee. Licenses for motor vehicles, insurance companies, and other forms of business account for most of the license money. There are also licenses for hunting and fishing, and for keeping dogs.

When improvements to streets and alleys are made, cities often charge the owners of adjoining property a special assessment. The owners pay a part of the cost because the value of their property is increased by the improvements. .:

Fines and Gifts. A fine is a certain amount of money which is collected by the government as a punishment for law-breaking. The total income from fines is comparatively small.

Occasionally some generous person gives cash to the government, or builds public libraries, museums, art galleries, or schools. When Justice Oliver Wendell Holmes died in 1935, after serving on the Supreme Court for twenty-nine years, he left a will giving $250,000 to the 

National Treasury. 

Borrowing. This method of securing government funds will be discussed in the latter part of this chapter. 

C. FEDERAL GOVERNMENT TAXES 

Income Taxes.-The federal government obtains a large share of its income from two kinds of income taxes: (1) a tax on the net income of all corporations doing business in the United States; (2) a tax on the net income of individuals. Each person who has to pay a federal income tax fills out an income-tax-return blank and files it with the Collector of Internal Revenue at the time designated each year.

Excise Taxes.-Indirect taxes on goods manufactured in this country are known as excise taxes. About one-half billion dollars each year is secured from taxes on tobacco. 

There are also taxes on theater tickets, gasoline, cosmetics, furs, automobiles, liquors, playing cards, electricity, and many other selected articles. Some of these taxes are on luxuries, but others are on things that are considered necessary in many homes. 

Taxes on Imports.-The tax on goods brought into this country from other parts of the world is called a tariff. 

Several thousand articles are taxed when imported or brought into this country. The purpose of the tariff is two-

fold: (1) It produces income for the government. The largest producer of government revenue in this class is the tariff on sugar, about two cents a pound, which amounts to over $100,000,000 each year. (2) The tariff is used to protect American producers from the competition of goods from foreign countries which are often produced at a lower cost. The tariff on some articles has been made very high so as to keep out foreign goods. 

Articles purchased abroad by an American traveler for personal or household use may be brought into this country without paying tariff up to the value of $100. 

Estate Taxes.-All estates of more than $40,000 are taxed by the federal government before the money is divided among the heirs. The rates are progressive, varying from three to 77 per cent, depending upon the size of the estate. If the heirs of an estate pay a state inheritance tax, they are permitted to use their receipts for inheritance taxes paid to the state to cancel up to 80 per cent of the federal tax.

Social Security Taxes.-In 1935 the United States 

Congress passed the Social Security Act which sets up a vast program for unemployment and old-age insurance. 

Employers and employees pay a part of the tax for unemployment insurance to the federal government. 

D. STATE AND LOCAL GOVERNMENT TAXES 

Taxes on Automobiles and Gasoline.-There are three types of taxes on automobiles. (1) Licenses are required for the operation of automobiles, trucks, and buses on the public highways. The amount of the license usually depends on the horsepower, weight, or value of the motor vehicle. Naturally, the licenses of trucks and buses cost more than those of passenger cars. Since there are more than 25,000,000 auto licenses in the United States, a large

Photo by Lange

Gasoline taxes are used chiefly to build and maintain public highways. portion of state income is derived from this source. (2) A considerable portion of state income is derived from taxes on gasoline, which vary from 2 to 7 cents a gallon in the various states. Both the taxes on automobiles and gasoline are used chiefly to build and maintain public highways. In some states, however, part of the tax on gasoline is used for other purposes. (3) A property tax is sometimes levied on automobiles.

Business Taxes.-Many states require licenses or taxes from banks, insurance companies, and other forms of business. Many states tax the income or the capital stock of corporations. In order to provide money for unemployment insurance payments, employers pay a state tax on their payrolls.

State Income Taxes.-The use of state income taxes has become more common in recent years. More than half the states now tax the income of individuals. Some state constitutions, however, forbid this form of taxation. State income taxes are like the federal income tax in that they provide exemptions and progressive rates.

Inheritance Taxes. All the states except Nevada have inheritance taxes. The taxes are based on the share which each heir receives after the estate has been divided. 

Usually the rates are higher for heirs who are not close relatives of the deceased person.

Sales Taxes.-During the depression years a number of states and cities have found it more and more difficult to raise enough money to run the government. About half of the states and a number of cities have passed general sales taxes, taxes of 1, 2, or 3 per cent on most goods sold at retail. The sales tax has helped to raise considerable income for a number of states and cities. 

The General Property Tax.-The general property tax is a source of income for some states and it is the chief support of cities, school districts, counties, townships, and other units of local government. 

1. Assessment. An officer called an assessor, usually elected by the people, places a value on each piece of taxable property in his district. This includes real estate (lots, houses, farms, and buildings of all kinds), as well as stocks, bonds, mortgages, and bank accounts. The assessed valuation of real estate is often much less than the true market value of the property. There appears to be some dissatisfaction with the work of local assessors because they are not always fair in their assessments and are not trained for their work. However, if the owner of a property is dissatisfied with an assessment, there is usually some provision made for appeal to another authority.

2. Fixing the Tax Rate. We can best understand how the tax rate is fixed by an example. When the assessment of a city is completed, it may list the total value of all taxable property at $50,000,000. If the city council decides on a budget which requires $500,000 to be raised by the general property tax, the tax rate is obtained by a simple process of arithmetic. If $500,000 is to be raised from $50,000,000, the amount due from each thousand will be $500,000 divided by 50,000,000 or $10. The tax rate is then said to be $10 per thousand. In reality, of course, city councils may and often do fix the tax rate first. Then the size of the budget is limited to keep within the expected income from various sources. 

On the same piece of property assessed at $5,000 the city council might place a tax of $10 per thousand, the school district $15, and the county $5. So that there would be tax bills of $50 from the city, $75 from the school district, and $25 from the county, or a total of $150. This is sometimes criticized as being unfair, but we must remember that the taxpayer gets different services from each type of government which taxes his property. It is natural and proper, therefore, that each should send him a bill, just as he gets separate bills from his grocer, his milkman, and his telephone company. People who do not own their own homes help to pay the tax on general property when they pay their rent to the landlord.

Criticisms of General Property Tax.-We have already noted that assessments on property are not always fairly made, but there are also other criticisms of the general property tax. When this form of taxation was first used in this country, most of the wealth was in the form of real estate and other forms of personal property which could easily be seen and valued by the assessor. Today wealth is often in other forms, such as stocks and bonds,

or bank accounts, which the assessor cannot see. The result is that the general property tax today is usually only a tax on real estate (lots, farms, houses, and other buildings). It is also claimed that this form of taxation places a heavy burden on those least able to pay. Thus, a poor farmer who is struggling to make a living from his land may pay a higher property tax than a well-to-do person who lives in a neighboring town. It is also true that a high general property tax in a city may prevent the renting of homes at a figure which the poorer people of the community can pay. 

Collecting the Tax. After the taxes are assessed and the lists made, each taxpayer usually receives a statement of the amount which he owes and the date when payment is required. General property taxes are usually paid to local tax collectors in the city, county, or township. If the tax is not paid when due, a penalty is added. Taxes which are overdue are said to be delinquent. If the tax is not paid for several years, the government may take the property and sell it in order to get the amount of the taxes. 

E. GENERAL PROBLEMS OF GOVERNMENT FINANCE Why We Pay Taxes in a Democracy.—Every year the governments of our country-national, state, and local -collect millions of dollars from the people in the form of taxes. This money, as we have tried to show at the beginning of this chapter, is used for the benefit of every citizen; therefore every citizen should help to supply it. 

The good citizen in a democracy pays his taxes willingly as his just share of the cost of government. 

When we buy articles from a store, we do not begrudge the money which we pay for what we get. If we use a little imagination at the time when we pay taxes, the services of our government will seem as real to us as the articles

Courtesy Pierre Du Pont High School, Wilmington, Delaware

Tax money is used for the construction of schools and other public buildings. 

Courtesy Public Roads Administration 

Tax money is used for highway construction. This road has been built to eliminate a grade crossing. which we buy from the store. We pay our tax money—and we are given good roads, well-lighted streets, public parks, public libraries, and schools where rich and poor alike may

study. With our tax money, we are buying services, but we are also helping to build a democracy. It is necessary that the good citizen, having paid his taxes, maintain an interest in his government and see to it that all tax money is spent wisely and honestly. 

What Is a Good Tax?-What kind of taxes should be used by the government to raise money? Many answers have been given to this question, but three principles of taxation have now come to be generally accepted. 

Ability to Pay. The government usually adopts the policy that ability to pay should be the basis for taxation. 

This means that the person who has much property or is receiving a large income ought to pay more than the person with only a little property and a small income. The federal income tax is definitely based on the ability to pay. 

People with small incomes pay no tax at all, those with moderate incomes pay some tax, but those with large incomes pay much more tax because the rates are higher. 

Fairness. Taxes should be fair to all persons. The assessor should place the correct value on all property in his district; scientific methods for doing this have now been worked out. Taxes should not be so high that people find great difficulty in paying them.

Convenience. Taxes should be as easy to collect as possible. They ought to be collected at whatever time and place is most convenient for the taxpayer, and at little cost to the government.

How Governments Borrow Money.-In addition to the money secured from taxation and other sources, all governments occasionally borrow money. Sometimes when the city council finds the treasury empty, it may arrange for a loan from the banks until the next tax money comes in. A more common reason for borrowing money, however, is to make some permanent improvement, such as

highways, school buildings, water and sewage systems. For example, a school district may wish to erect a new building. Since future generations expect to use this building, it is expected that they should help to pay for its construction. The school district, therefore, sells bonds, which are its promises to repay the amount borrowed at a specified time, with interest. Before these bonds fall due, the school district must prepare for their payment by setting aside a certain amount of money each year. This is called a sinking fund.

Sometimes governments borrow money in emergencies such as arise during the time of severe droughts, floods, and business depressions. Some day all the money which has been borrowed will have to be repaid and in the meantime the interest must be borne by the taxpayer. 

Our Public Debt.-Because of lavish spending in days of prosperity and unusual conditions during the depression, many state and local governments have borrowed heavily. In some cities as much as sixty cents out of each tax dollar that can be collected is needed for the payment of interest and principal on bonds. 

World War I in 1917-1918 left us with a national debt of $25,000,000,000. This amount was reduced each year until 1930 when it was only $16,000,000,000. But then came a business depression with widespread unemployment and our government borrowed money to provide jobs and relief, and to help business. At the beginning of 

World War II in 1941 our national debt had reached $42,000,000,000.

World War II made necessary the greatest expenditure of money in American history, and Congress was forced to raise the national debt limit to new heights. Fortunately, the public had more money with which to pay taxes and buy war bonds.

SUMMARY 

The cost of government must be considered on the basis of: (1) the number of people in the country (2) prices (3) services which are rendered and have increased because of greater expenditures for war, highways, education, relief and recovery, and social insurance

Spending government money is in the hands of our law-making bodies is usually based on a budget should be done through centralized purchasing and competitive bidding

Governments obtain money from taxes, service charges, interest payments, licenses, special assessments, fines, gifts, sale of public property, and borrowing State governments levy taxes on automobiles and gasoline, taxes on business, taxes on income, inheritance taxes, sales taxes The federal government levies income taxes, excise taxes, taxes on imports, estate taxes, and taxes for social insurance 

The general property tax is the chief support of local governments is based on the value of property as listed by the assessor is criticized because the burden on real estate is too great Taxes should be paid cheerfully because of the services we receive from our government should be based on ability to pay should be fair and easy to collect 

Governments borrow money for temporary purposes, permanent improvements, and emergencies

by selling bonds which are due at some future date but taxes must eventually be used to repay the principal and the interest 

Our public debt rose during the World War, was reduced by 1930, and has been increasing since that time.

Chapter IX  A. WHAT ARE POLITICAL PARTIES? 

The Activities of Political Parties. During the time immediately before an election many political speeches are made. The daily and weekly newspapers carry news about the political campaigns. Slogans in large letters on posters, banners, and billboards tell us for whom we should vote. 

Pamphlets and even books are written to support one set of candidates or the other. A considerable amount of radio time is taken up with political speeches. Many people are asked personally to vote for certain individuals or for all the candidates of a particular party. Other people receive letters urging them to vote in a certain manner. On the day of the election "last minute" appeals are issued. Most of these activities are directed and carried on by the workers in the political parties.

A political party is a group of persons who hold similar ideas on public questions and who try to have their candidates elected to office. Political parties are usually most active during the days preceding an election. It is at this time that they do all within their power to have their candidates elected.

Political Parties in Our Early History.-Political parties are not mentioned in the United States Constitution, and even so wise a man as George Washington did not feel the need for them. He came out against political parties in his Farewell Address. In spite of what Wash- 166

ington said against parties, however, he actually was a Federalist, or at least he acted with that political group. 

After all, political parties are inevitable in a democracy. 

People always have different ideas about the things which the government should do, and those whose ideas are similar unite in political parties. 

The Federalist party came into existence in Washington's time. Among the noted Federalists were Alexander 

Hamilton, John Adams, and John Marshall. This party believed in a strong national government, a liberal interpretation of the Constitution of the United States, and rule in the interests of those people who were engaged in business. As a party the Federalists won only a single presidential election that of 1796. By 1820 the party had disappeared from the national scene. 

The short-lived National Republican party and the Whig party grew out of the Federalist party. Among the prominent Whigs were Henry Clay, Daniel Webster, and

William Henry Harrison. The tariff became an issue in 

1828; the National Republicans (who became Whigs) fought for a high protective tariff. Later this became a great issue between the Democrats and the Republicans. 

The Democratic-Republican party, forerunner of the Democratic party, was founded by Thomas Jefferson. Its beginning dates from a letter written by Jefferson to Washington in 1793. This party was supported by the farmers and the workers. It stood for strict construction of the Constitution and states' rights. The Democratic creed was set forth by Jefferson in his first inaugural address of March 4, 1801. The Democratic party with few exceptions remained in control of the presidency from 

1800 to 1860. During Andrew Jackson's time more people received the right to vote. In many ways Jackson brought about the rule of the common man.

CIENDS HENRY CLA MEET HERE 

THE FRIENDS 

J.K.POLK 

MEET HERE 

ANSTOM 1844 

This scene outside the voting place at the Whig vs. Democrat presidential election of 1844 was sketched by an artist of that time. 

The Republican party was born at Ripon, Wisconsin, in 1854, in opposition to the spread of slavery. It ran 

General John C. Fremont as a candidate for the presidency in 1856, but he was defeated. The party triumphed in 

1860 with Abraham Lincoln as the standard bearer. There was at this time a split in the Democratic party, one wing being led by Stephen A. Douglas and the other by 

John C. Breckenridge. Douglas believed that territories should decide for themselves when they came into the Union whether they wanted to be slave or free states. On the other hand, Breckenridge believed that slaveholders should be protected by the national government in free territory. Lincoln had said that the country could not continue to be half-slave and half-free. Although he was opposed to slavery, he was not an abolitionist.

Political Parties Since 1860.-Since 1860 the Demo cratic and Republican parties have been the major political groups in our country. During this time the Democratic party has won only seven presidential elections. Each of the two major political parties has been stronger in some parts of the country than in others. The Democratic party has been most powerful in the South and in some of the large cities of the North. Six southern states, South Carolina, Georgia, Alabama, Mississippi, Louisiana, and Arkansas, have voted Democratic in every national election since 1876. During the same time, Virginia, North Carolina, Florida, and Texas, have voted Democratic in every national election except in 1928. The Republicans have been most popular among the business and financial interests. 

Some of the chief issues between the Democratic and Republican parties have been the tariff, the trusts, the currency, and the League of Nations. Quite frequently, however, there have been no outstanding differences between the two major parties. In both parties there have been persons of almost every shade of opinion on public questions. The leaders of both parties have usually been unwilling to take a firm stand on many things because this would drive people from the party. Political parties, therefore, have often tried to mean "all things to all men." 

What Do Party Labels Mean?-During the presi dency of Franklin D. Roosevelt, however, there developed sharp differences in opinion about what the government should do. Because of the severe depression with much unemployment and low farm prices, the government engaged in many new activities. It took strong action in controlling industry and it tried to control the production of some farm crops. The power of the government was increased when Congress passed a "wages and hours" law. 

In some parts of the United States the federal govern-

ment constructed large dams for flood control and for the production of electricity. Billions of dollars were borrowed to provide work projects for the unemployed and relief for the unfortunate. Government aid was given to housing and ownership of farms for the poorer classes of people. 

Insurance for unemployment and old age was established. 

Labor unions were encouraged and given certain rights by the government. 22 Those people who favor this program became known as "New Dealers"; sometimes they were called "liberals."

In general, a liberal is one who seeks reform and change in government. President Franklin D. Roosevelt frequently refers to himself as a “liberal." But even though Mr. 

Roosevelt was four times elected as a Democrat, many members of his own party did not support his New Deal. 

On the other hand, some of the more liberal members of the Republican party were sympathetic to the New Deal. 

Those who opposed the President's New Deal program were usually Republicans and conservative Democrats. In general, a conservative is one who is opposed to change. 

Many conservatives believed that the government should not engage in so many activities, and should not try to impose too many regulations on business. They also believed that the government was going into debt at an alarming pace and that it should have stopped borrowing money. In general, their belief is that in time of peace the government should balance its budget, and should stop interfering with business and industry. Business will then regain its confidence and find work for all the people. In this matter they think prosperity will be restored. 

On the basis of what has happened during the Roosevelt administration, it can be said that the old party labels, Democrat and Republican, do not mean much today. Better labels might be "liberal" and "conservative."

Minor Political Parties.-There are a few minor parties which should be mentioned. Among them are the 

Socialist, the Communist, and the Prohibition parties. 

The Socialist party stands for government ownership and operation of business and industry. The Communist party is more revolutionary. In state government, the Progressive party has played an important role in Wisconsin. The 

Farmer-Labor party is strong in Minnesota. 

Political Parties in a Dictatorship and a Democracy.—Under a dictatorship there is only one political party. Its leaders usually hold the most important positions in the government and direct its policies. In fact, it is sometimes difficult to distinguish between the political party and the government. All forms of opposition to the government and the party are ruthlessly crushed. 

In a democracy, however, there is always more than one political party. The United States has usually had two major political parties. Two or more active political parties are essential for good government in a democracy. With the help of different political parties the people are permitted to hear all sides of public questions fully discussed. 

If there is dishonesty and inefficiency in government, it can be detected and corrected. Let us say, for example, that the Democratic party is in power. Then the Republican party is always ready to see and criticize the mistakes the Democrats are making. This makes the 

Democrats more alert; they have to watch their step and make sure that they are conducting the government in a proper manner.

B. HOW POLITICAL PARTIES ARE ORGANIZED 

Party Committees.-Each of our two major political parties is well organized to carry on campaigns, hold conventions, and raise money to pay its bills.

In the cities there are usually important party leaders or committeemen in each voting district or precinct. There are also leaders for larger districts known as wards. Above the precinct and ward leaders is the city committee. 

Outside of the large cities there are also important county committees. The various committee members are either chosen by the party members in primaries or selected by conventions. 

There is also a central party committee in each state; it arranges party meetings and keeps in touch with local party politics. At the top of the party organization is a national committee, composed of representatives from all the states and territories. There is one committeeman and one committeewoman from each state and territory. At the head of this national committee is the national chairman. 

The chief work of this national committee is to provide for the national convention of the party and to conduct the campaign for the presidency. Both of the major political parties maintain permanent offices in Washington. 

The Political Machine.-The work of a political party in a local area is frequently so highly organized and so completely ruled by a few men that we refer to it as a "machine." We should remember, however, that the political machine is not a part of the regular government of a state or a local district. One of the most important persons in the political machine of a city is the precinct committeeman or leader. He looks after the interests of the machine in his own particular voting district. He knows all the voters in his precinct; he does little favors for people who often repay him by voting for his candidate. His busiest time, of course, is on election day when he tries to get as many voters as possible to come out and cast their ballots for his candidates. 

Another important leader in the political machine is the

man who looks after the control of an entire ward. This district may contain from fourteen to thirty precincts. In nearly every ward the machine maintains a political club which is a social as well as a political organization, providing entertainment and recreation for the people of the neighborhood. Through the ward and precinct leaders food, fuel, and other necessities are often distributed to needy families. The ward leader usually has much power in the selection of candidates. On election day the ward leader rushes from precinct to precinct checking up on the workers in those districts. 

At the head of the political machine in a city or a county one frequently finds a "boss." The entire political machine is under his control and he is often the most influential person in the government of the community. When people want special favors from the city government, they go to him, and people who wish to run for office seek his support. Many officials and employees of the city and county government receive their jobs through his influence. Those people who obtain their jobs through the aid of the boss are expected to vote for the machine candidates for office and they are urged to get other members of their families to vote likewise.

Some of the leaders in the political machine hold state, city, or county offices. Others are working in the hope that they may be appointed to office or receive other favors from the government.

Dishonesty and inefficiency sometimes exist in our local governments because they are controlled by machine politicians. These men are usually in office for what they can get out of it for themselves and their friends. The political machine flourishes and usually elects its candidates to office in those places where the masses of the voters are indifferent to their government. The power of

the machine can be broken when a majority of the voters take a more intelligent interest in their government and cast their ballots for the best candidates. The merit system under which many city, county, and state officials are appointed on the basis of their rating in civil service examinations should be extended. This would help to break the power of the political bosses.

How Political Campaigns Are Financed.-Political campaigns cost a considerable amount of money. One of the biggest items of expenditure today is the purchase of radio time. Newspaper advertisements cost thousands of dollars. Traveling expenses of candidates and other speakers have to be paid, and there are bills for the printing of pamphlets, posters, and stationery. Campaign headquarters and meeting halls have to be rented and the salaries of stenographers and other party workers have to be paid. It is estimated that more than $25,000,000 was spent by all parties in the presidential campaign of 1936. 

Money for a political campaign is obtained in a number of ways. Loyal members who believe in the principles of the party give financial help. The candidates for office provide money for their campaigns. Personal friends of the candidates usually make additional contributions. 

Federal and state laws place certain restrictions and regulations on the receiving and spending of party funds. 

Large campaign contributions by wealthy persons are not considered good for free and democratic government, because the candidate who wins in an election may be influenced to grant special favors to those friends who made large contributions. Therefore, federal and state laws forbid contributions from large corporations, such as railroad companies. Collecting campaign contributions from federal civil service employees is also forbidden by law. A federal law forbids a candidate for representative in Congress to

Ewing Galloway

Thousands of delegates from every state in the Union and from the territories meet every four years in a national convention to nominate the party candidate for the presidency. spend more than $2,500 toward his election and a candidate for senator to spend over $10,000. Bribery, including gifts to influence voters, is everywhere illegal. Usually, party treasurers and individual candidates are required to make public statements showing who gave the campaign money and for what purposes it was spent. 

C. CHOOSING OUR OFFICIALS 

Nominating Candidates. One of the most important activities of political parties is to select the candidates to represent the party on election day. There may be three 

Democrats and four Republicans who would like to run for the same office, and there must be some way of deciding which candidates shall represent the parties in the election. The picking of candidates to be voted on in a regular election is referred to as nomination.

At one time most candidates were nominated by a caucus or a convention. A caucus is a meeting of party leaders from a certain district such as a city or a county, or it may be a meeting of the party members of Congress or a state legislature. A convention is made up of a large number of delegates who have been chosen to represent their party. These delegates meet together for the purpose of nominating candidates and adopting party platforms. 

A platform is a statement of the principles for which a party stands. Each separate topic in the platform is referred to as a plank. Candidates for the Presidency are nominated by a national convention meeting in one of our largest cities and consisting of several thousand delegates who come from every state in the Union and from the territories.

Nominating Candidates in the Primary.-Because political bosses had too much control over caucuses and conventions, most states now provide for the nomination of candidates by the direct primary. The primary is conducted in practically the same way as a regular election. 

Voters go to the polls, receive ballots, and mark them. 

However, only the supporters of the party are expected to vote in the party primary. Only Republicans are expected to join in the selection of Republican candidates, only Democrats in the choosing of Democratic nominees. Each political party has a separate ballot. Most states provide that a person who wishes to vote in a primary must register as a member of some party several months before the primary election. The person who receives the largest number of votes in the primary for a certain office usually becomes the party nominee for that office, but in some states a second primary is necessary if no candidate receives a majority of the votes cast in the first primary. 

Candidates who wish to be nominated at the primaries
