Placards advertising the candidates for office cover the McLennan County Court House, Waco, Texas, just before a primary election. must first have their names placed on the ballot. In most states they can do this by filing a petition with the signatures of a small percentage of the voters; usually from 5 to 10 per cent of those who voted in the last election is the number required. (In some states no petition is necessary. Candidates need only pay an entrance or filing fee.) 

Candidates for local and state offices, members of Congress, delegates to political conventions, and members of party committees are usually chosen at primaries. Some states also have presidential primaries in which voters may express their preference for candidates for the Presidency.

In some parts of the country, as in the Solid South, and in some local districts where political parties are not evenly divided in numbers, the primary is more important than

the regular election, because the candidates chosen by the strongest political party are sure to be elected. 

Who May Vote?-The qualifications of voters in the

United States are, for the most part, regulated by the states. The states determine who may vote in the primaries and elections.

In the early days of our country a person was required to have property in order to vote. Gradually this requirement was dropped. Today the chief qualifications for voting are: (1) The voter must be at least 21 years old. (2) He must be a citizen of the United States. (3) He must be a resident of the state and of the election district where he wishes to vote. 

Since 1920 women have been allowed to vote thoughout the country. There are some states that require the payment of taxes in order to vote, especially a poll tax. Nearly half of the states insist upon some kind of educational test as a requirement for voting. But this is usually very simple, being no more than the ability to read an article of the national or state constitution, or the ability to understand a simple paragraph of English. 

In order to prevent dishonesty in elections, many states require voters to register some days or weeks before an election. At this time of registration their residence, age, and other details are recorded so that only the proper persons may vote.

Election Day.-The Tuesday after the first Monday in November is the day on which general elections are held throughout the country. In some states, however, elections for local officers occur on other days. Primaries are held on different dates in the various states. 

Each county or city is divided into voting districts or precincts. The place where the voting is done in each dis-

trict is referred to as the polling place. On election day the polls are open during certain hours, usually from 6 a.m. to 6 p.m., and sometimes longer. Each polling place is in charge of an election board whose members open and close the polls, check the qualifications of the voters, and hand out the ballots. After the polls close they count the votes and send the results to the proper officials. The officials. The person who receives more votes than anybody else for the same office is said to have a plurality of the votes. If a person receives more than half the total number of votes cast for an office, he has a majority. In most states a plurality of the votes cast is sufficient for nomination or election. A "watcher” from each political party is allowed to challenge any person whom he does not believe to be qualified to vote, and to see that the votes are fairly counted.

Methods of Voting.-At one time people voted orally. 

This made it hard for many people to vote as they really desired because bystanders could hear their choice of candidates. Even the first use of paper ballots was not satisfactory; they were printed and distributed by the candidates and their political managers. Since different political parties had ballots of different colors, it was possible for onlookers to see how a person voted by the color of the ballot he used.

Today all states use, at least in part, the Australian ballot, so called because it originated in Australia. This method of voting has three distinct features: (1) The ballots are provided by the state or local governments, and not by the political parties or the candidates themselves. (2) The names of the candidates of all parties are printed on the same ballot, sometimes in columns under the heading of each political party, and sometimes in alphabetical order under the titles of the office. (3) The voter takes his ballot into a curtained booth or some other

The voting machine is being used instead of paper ballots in a number of places. The machine automatically counts each vote when it is cast. protected place, where he marks it in private; after folding the ballot he returns to the table where the election officials sit and drops it in the ballot box. 

The arrangement of names on the ballot is important. 

When the names of the candidates are arranged under the heading of the political party, the voter is tempted to make one mark at the top of the column for all the candidates of that party. Thus, he is said to vote the straight ticket. When the names of the candidates are placed under the title of each office, the voter is required to vote separately for each office and is more inclined to vote a split or mixed ticket.

A method of voting which is becoming more common every year is the voting machine. This device is arranged in such a way that the front of it looks much like an enlarged paper ballot. By pulling knobs or levers on the

machine the voter records his vote for the candidates or party which he prefers. Since the machine automatically counts each vote when it is cast, the total number of votes for each candidate is known as soon as the machine is opened after the election is over. 

D. MAKING DEMOCRACY MORE DEMOCRATIC The Work of the Voter.-Mr. and Mrs. Average 

Voter are important parts of the democracy under which we live. Many state and local officers, as well as members of Congress and the President, are elected by the people. 

Candidates for office have to be nominated at the primaries. Frequently, changes in the state constitution are referred to the voters. Matters of local concern, such as the borrowing of money by the units of local government, are decided at the polls. In those states and cities which have the initiative and referendum, important new laws are proposed for the consideration of the voters. Usually national and state officials can be removed only by impeachment, but twelve states and several hundred cities give the voters the right to remove certain officers before their term has expired. This is known as the recall. Whenever a number of voters become dissatisfied with the work of a certain officer, they can sign a petition for a new election. In this case the officer whom they wish to remove must again stand for election.

The Need for the Short Ballot.-It is not unusual for the American voter to be called upon to vote a ballot containing from 50 to 100 names, and carrying several proposals for new laws and state constitutional amendments. In an election in New York City several years ago the ballot was fourteen feet long. There are so many candidates, so many offices to be filled, and so many questions to be answered, that in despair the voter frequently

votes the straight party ticket or follows the marked ballot furnished by the party worker in his precinct. In reality, the long ballot frequently places the selection of our officials into the hands of the politicians, for the candidates are too numerous, the issues too confused and complex for the voter to decide things for himself and vote intelligently.

The great need today, therefore, is a short ballot, on which there are fewer elective officers. We will have more democracy, more control by the people if at election time the voters can concentrate on the selection of a smaller number of important officials. These could be held definitely responsible for the conduct of the government and the appointment of those who serve under them. For the selection of many of our government employees we can set up civil service examinations to find out whether applicants have the proper training for particular kinds of work. We also need some evidence as to their character, dependability, and practical experience. 

The Non-Partisan Ballot.-Many people are in favor of a non-partisan ballot for local elections and for the choosing of judges. On such a ballot there is no indication of the political party to which a candidate belongs. 

Whether a man is a Democrat or a Republican should not be taken into consideration in determining fitness for local offices. It makes no difference, as a rule, to what political party the mayor of a city belongs. The ability and honesty of the candidate, as well as his attitude on problems of immediate local importance, should be the basis on which a local official is chosen. Likewise, judges should be selected for their personal ability, honesty, and knowledge of the law, rather than for their politics. 

Preferential Voting.-One fault that has been found with our system of elections is that the winning party often

gets too complete control of the government. For example, in the New York City election of 1931 the Democrats cast only sixty-five per cent of the votes, but they elected sixtyfour of the sixty-five members of the Board of Aldermen. 

The Republicans and Socialists together had thirty-five per cent of the votes, but the Republicans elected only one member of the Board and the Socialists none. 

Various plans are being tried today to give the minority party or parties a fairer share of representation in the government. For this purpose preferential voting is used in some cities. Under this plan the voter marks his ballot for first, second, and maybe his third, fourth, and fifth choices. In the counting of the votes the first, second, and perhaps further choices are taken into consideration. 

Voting Intelligently. The success of our democratic form of government depends on the willingness of the citizens to go to the polls and vote intelligently. The average voter who lives through one of our political campaigns in which the radio blares forth speeches on different sides, and in which newspapers and magazines print conflicting articles and editorials, may become bewildered and confused. The voter, in a democracy, however, has the solemn duty of trying to sift truth from falsehood during an election campaign. He should be open-minded and study both sides of public questions. He should support the political party which he thinks will do the most good for the country as a whole. He should vote for those candidates who are best qualified to carry out the duties of the office for which they are running.

The Duty to Vote.-Among the great dangers to democracy is the indifference on the part of the voters. In some elections fewer than half of the qualified voters go to the polls. Even in the hotly-contested presidential election of 1940, millions of voters failed to go to the polls.

Some of these, however, were kept from voting because of illness or some other good reason.

Under our democracy, the government is good or bad, depending on what the voters make of it. It is the patriotic duty, therefore, of every qualified voter to go to the polls on primary and election days to help maintain good government.

Chapter X PUBLIC OPINION A. WHAT IS PUBLIC OPINION? 

Public Opinion. The nature of public opinion can readily be understood by referring to some phases of our living together in school. Who shall be elected President of the Student Council? Should the Student Council establish a court which will have power to punish pupils who disobey traffic rules in the corridors? Whatever the students think about this or any other problem of student government is said to be public opinion in the school. 

What do the people think about the President of the United States? Do they approve of the laws which he recommends to Congress? Do they agree with what he says in his speeches? Do they think that he is conducting the executive department of our national government in an efficient manner? Whatever the people think on these matters is said to be public opinion about the President. 

Public opinion is what the people think about matters of common interest. Since the majority rules in America, public opinion is actually the opinion of the majority of the people. In a democracy the people should and do think about many matters of common interest, such as balancing the budget of our national government, providing jobs for the unemployed, the size of our army and navy, the need for a new school, or the necessity for greater honesty and efficiency in government. It is at election time that the public really speaks. It is then that we 188

PUBLIC OPINION 

189 learn what the people think about the candidates who run for office and about the issues of the campaign. 

Measuring Public Opinion.-Today, however, we do not always have to wait until election day to find out what the people of the country are thinking. Fortune magazine and the American Institute of 

Public Opinion are continually finding out what the people think about candidates for office and about many public questions. They take samples of public opinion by asking tens of thousands of voters what they think about many problems of our day. As nearly as possible they try to get a cross section of the voting population of the country. A nation-wide poll includes (1) voters from every state; (2) men and women; (3) voters in city and country; (4) voters of all age groups; (5) 

A representative of the Ameri can Institute of Public Opinion interviews men on the Fish 

Wharf, Boston. voters who are rich, of modest means, and poor. All these are represented in the proportion that they hold in the voting population.

The results of the polls of the American Institute of Public Opinion are published in many newspapers of the country; they are usually accurate to within four per cent. 

Such polls should be useful in a democracy because they enable men in office to determine governmental policies in accordance with the expressed wishes of voters whose opinions are considered to be representative of the entire country.

B. HOW PUBLIC OPINION IS FORMED 

Shaping Public Opinion.-An enlightened public opinion is an essential part of the life of a free people under a democratic form of government. In order to understand democracy, therefore, it is necessary to know how public opinion is formed. The minds of the people are shaped and molded by what they see, read, and hear from day to day. A person's ideas on public questions are formed by many influences, such as the home, the school, the church, clubs and other organizations, the radio, books, newspapers, magazines, and movies. 

Too often people make up their minds in advance on what they wish to believe without studying all sides of public questions. Thus we can say that they are prejudiced for or against some particular point of view. Persons who are prejudiced usually see, read, and hear only those things which will agree with the beliefs which they already hold. Thus, a voter whose parents belonged to the Democratic party might hold the same political beliefs; during the election campaign he would read only Democratic newspapers and hear only speakers on the Democratic side.

The well-being of democracy as a form of government requires voters who are not prejudiced, but are openminded and study all sides of public questions. The right kind of public opinion is built on an understanding of the truth. Democracy cannot succeed on a basis of ignorance and prejudice.

The Home. The kind of family into which a person is born has a great deal to do with the attitudes and opinions which he holds on many questions. A boy hears his father talk favorably about a certain political party, and often he will become a member of that same political

PUBLIC OPINION 

191 group in later life. Our political beliefs, our religious ideas, and many other things are influenced by the home in which we are brought up. 

Children who are brought up in prosperous homes and have plenty to eat and good clothes to wear, will hold opinions about industry and business, work and play, that differ greatly from those of underprivileged children who are brought up in the slums of the city or in the sharecroppers' cabins of the South.

City children often hold opinions that are very different from those held by farm children. 

In the home we acquire many of the opinions that we hold on matters of honesty, fairness, and tolerance. Children in the same neighborhood usually play together and thus the influence of one home may be reinforced or changed by another. The neighborhood in which we live helps to shape many of our ideas and opinions. 

The School.-In the school we associate with boys and girls from other homes and neighborhoods. From them new ideas and opinions are often acquired. Most important in the school are the various subjects we study and the teachers who may influence our ideas. The school should teach us how to make up our minds, not on the basis of prejudice, but by studying the facts and the arguments on all sides of public questions. One great modern writer insists that real education in a democracy should not teach us what to think, but how to think. 

The Church.-Often the finest part of our personality has been built up under the influence of the church; such virtues as honesty, truthfulness, and honor may be implanted by the home and further strengthened by the church and the Sunday school. Church leaders sometimes influence public opinion greatly; in the past many of them were leaders in the movement for prohibition of the liquor

Ewing Galloway

Free and intelligent discussion of public problems is essential in a democracy. The government must carry out those policies that are for the good of the whole country. traffic; today many of them are leaders in the effort to establish world peace. Freedom of religion is one of the essentials of democracy, and no church, Protestant, Catholic, or Jewish, should ever teach attitudes of intolerance toward persons of a different religious faith. 

Work and Income.-The kind of work which a person does and the amount of income which he receives from it have much to do with his attitude on public questions. 

The cotton farmers of the South, the corn and wheat farmers of the Middle West, are usually in favor of the United States government giving financial help for soil conservation and the reduction of crops. The working man who belongs to an active labor union in the city is in favor of the National Labor Relations Act which guar antees his right to belong to the union. Teachers acting through their state and national organizations are usually

PUBLIC OPINION 

193 in favor of greater appropriations of money for public education. Business men acting through the Chamber of 

Commerce and other organizations are usually asking for a reduction of government expenditures and a lowering of taxes.

It is well to remember, however, that our democracy cannot survive on a basis of catering to one particular class of people. A democracy is government of the people, by the people, and for all the people; the government must follow the wishes of all the people and carry out those policies which are for the good of the whole country. 

Clubs and Other Organizations.-America has many clubs and organizations with millions of members. There are labor unions, chambers of commerce, lodges, service clubs, women's clubs, veterans' organizations, and many others. These organizations also play an important part in determining our attitude on many matters of public interest. At their meetings the members talk with each other about wages, hours of labor, candidates for public office, and many other problems. Frequently, outside speakers who talk on some topic of public interest are invited to appear at a meeting. At state and national conventions important resolutions are passed, and the officials give out statements for or against taxes, relief, aid to the farmer, national defense, and other subjects in the news of the day.

As a general rule, people join these organizations because they already believe in the principles and ideals for which they stand. With some notable exceptions, therefore, it is difficult to hear both sides of controversial problems in a club meeting. A labor union will hardly ever invite an employer to speak to its members, and an employers' association will not, as a rule, invite a labor leader to express his ideas at one of its meetings.

There are, however, organizations which make a de-liberate effort to educate the voter and give him an opportunity to learn the facts and arguments on all sides of public questions. The League of Women Voters is an organization which insists on hearing speeches from the leaders of all political parties at its meetings. For more than twenty years the Foreign Policy Association has been publishing books and pamphlets which give impartial information on foreign affairs and foreign policy. At its meetings there are usually two or three speakers, each one emphasizing a different point of view about the same problem.

Forums and Institutes. In a democracy public opinion is formed by free and open discussion based on the facts in the case. Public opinion is affected by forums and other meetings where public problems are discussed. The public forum is gaining rapidly in the United States. This is a healthy sign, because democracy can develop more fully in our country if the people are given more opportunities to discuss their problems in open meetings. A wellconducted forum gives people an opportunity to learn the facts about public questions and it also permits them to state their opinions.

A forum meeting of this kind usually has one general topic to be discussed. If the theme is "Post-War Planning," the leader presents the facts which are necessary for understanding the problem. He points out some of the leading issues which are at stake. Then various members of the audience are permitted to speak and state their views on the subject. 

Institutes of citizenship also are playing an important and increasing part in developing intelligent public opinion in public affairs. Many of our universities and colleges have institutes of citizenship and public affairs that are

PUBLIC OPINION 

195 

Courtesy U. S. Office of Education 

This public forum in Colorado Springs, Colorado, has a panel of speakers to whom questions can be referred. Such meetings keep the public informed on current problems and offer opportunity for general 

giving distinguished service. Discussions by able speakers are put on for several days annually. These institutes reach thousands through personal attendance and thousands more through their published proceedings, newspaper accounts, and radio broadcasts. 

Many newspapers in the nation sponsor radio programs and public forums. For example, the New York Herald 

Tribune conducts a forum every year in October, which is attended by more than three thousand persons from all parts of the country. During a period of several days there are usually about sixty speakers who represent every shade of political opinion in the United States. 

Newspapers and Magazines.-One of the largest factors in molding public opinion is the press. There are several thousand daily newspapers and even more weekly papers in our country. It is not uncommon in these days for a single publication to boast a total circulation of sev-

eral million copies every day. Many people read newspapers every day and others read them occasionally. 

In some cases newspaper owners publish only one paper, but there are also chains, consisting of two or more newspapers owned by the same person or corporation. More than 300 papers with almost half of the total newspaper circulation are controlled by chains, such as Scripps-Howard, Hearst, and Gannett. The owner of a newspaper chain usually has much to do with its policies and control. 

The great influence of the newspaper comes from the fact that so many people believe what they see in print to be true. It is probably through the editorial columns that public opinion is most affected. Persons like Walter Lippman and Dorothy Thompson write daily columns of comment on the news which are printed by many papers. 

These columns are widely read throughout the country and have a great deal of influence on public opinion. 

Readers who pay no attention to the editorial columns may be influenced by other parts of the paper. Newspapers influence their readers by the kind of headlines which are used to display the news, by the manner in which the articles are written, and by placing certain news items in a more prominent position in the paper than others. Thus, we may expect a newspaper whose editor favors the Democratic party to use big headlines in the paper when the candidates for that party speak, to give this news item a favorable write-up and to place the article in a prominent position on the front page. The news of the Republican candidate's speech will also be published by this paper, but will probably not receive such a favorable display.

A large number of magazines are published in America, some of which are put out weekly and some monthly. Some of these periodicals have millions of subscribers or pur-

PUBLIC OPINION 

197 Keystone

View Co. 

The Nets Berk Times Wide World Studio 

Newspapers are read by millions of people every day. They are one of the most important influences on public opinion in any country. chasers. A magazine like the Saturday Evening Post reaches millions of people and may exert considerable influence on public opinion. 

To what extent magazines and newspapers are free to print what they please, is an interesting question. The charge is often made that a great many of them are considerably influenced by the advertisers. A newspaper as well as a magazine is dependent upon advertising for the larger part of its income. The price paid by the subscriber

is a small part of the publisher's income as a rule. What advertisers think, then, may be reflected in the editorial and news columns of a newspaper or magazine. 

In order to get more advertising and derive a large income from it, newspapers and magazines must get as many subscribers as they can. The larger the number of subscribers a publication has, the easier it is to get advertisements, for the advertisers reach a much larger number of people through such a paper or magazine. In order to get more subscribers a publication must cater to the people and give them the sort of articles they like. Many thinking people would prefer fewer sensational articles in newspapersfewer stories of crime and scandal-but most publishers insist that this is what the public wants. It must be remembered that a newspaper or magazine must make money in order to keep going. If the material published does not appeal to the buying public, then the publication will soon go into bankruptcy.

Books. Books which deal with problems of public interest are not as widely read as newspapers and magazines. 

Occasionally, however, a book may influence public opinion greatly. In 1906 Upton Sinclair published a novel, The 

Jungle, which described unsanitary conditions in the slaughter houses of Chicago. The wide reading of this book aroused public opinion and helped to bring about the passage of the Pure Food and Drug Act by Congress a short time later.

The Radio. Another great force in the molding of public opinion is the radio. Today there are millions of receiving sets and millions of people are frequent listeners. 

There are several chain broadcasting systems and hundreds of radio stations in America.

Think of the influence that the radio has in political campaigns today. National, state, and local campaigns

PUBLIC OPINION 

199 

Annoillion here 

Courtesy Columbia Broadcasting System

This school audience approves the use of radio in education. have been greatly changed by the radio. Formerly a Presidential candidate had to travel thousands of miles to reach the people. William Jennings Bryan is said to have traveled 18,000 miles in one of his campaigns. Today candidates travel less and use the radio more. It is not uncommon for an important personality like the President of the United States to have millions of persons in the radio audience listening in to his main speeches. 

There is no doubt but that large numbers of people make up their minds on how to vote by listening to radio speeches. Many people have no opportunity to hear a candidate personally and they may not read the newspapers. 

Certainly the radio is a powerful force in influencing public opinion.

During recent years there have been an increasing num-

ber of radio programs with speeches and discussions on all sides of public questions. An outstanding example is "America's Town Meeting of the Air." Once a week an audience of several thousand people gathers in Town Hall, New York City. The meeting is conducted somewhat in the form of a debate; there are usually two or three 

Photo by Charles A. Seawood 

Raymond Gram Swing is wellknown as a radio news commentator. speakers who discuss different sides of some public problem, such as unemployment, free speech, or foreign policy. After the speakers have finished, members of the audience are permitted to ask questions. The entire proceedings-speeches, questions, and answers are broadcast and millions of American people listen in. In this manner public opinion in a democracy is formed through free and open discussion. 

Motion Pictures.- 

Motion pictures also influence our opinions on many questions. They show action and make an impression on our minds through the ear as well as through the eye. 

Every week millions of Americans see at least one "movie"; many see two or more. Most of these pictures are for purposes of entertainment, but often the story or the kind of characters used shape our opinions. A story which shows the horrors of war may bring about a greater desire for peace. A picture which shows a number of Chi-

Pictures such as this showing the destructiveness of war may bring about greater efforts for peace. nese selling dope may give people the false impression that most Chinese are criminals. 

In the news reels the people can see and hear their President and other public officials. Pictures of the horrible destruction of Chinese cities by Japanese air raids have helped to create public opinion against Japan. Important public events at home and abroad are shown in the news reels. The March of Time, a two-reel news film, is unusual in that it gives the background of current happenings. 

C. GETTING THE FACTS ON PUBLIC QUESTIONS Conflicting Statements.-When the average citizen in a democracy is trying to make up his mind intelligently about public questions, many difficulties stand in his way.

Friendly arguments sometimes help to form public opinion. 

One speaker will argue that government health insurance is all wrong; another may seem equally persuasive in favor of it. People who are unemployed may claim that 

Congress should make larger appropriations for work relief; others will argue that the government cannot afford to spend so much money for this purpose. During a time of labor trouble, the employees may insist that they must have higher wages; the employers will argue that they cannot afford to pay more. One newspaper will print many favorable things about the Republican party, another may try to show that the Democratic party is the best. Especially during the time of a political campaign, when there is a flood of opposing and contradictory arguments from all candidates, the voter becomes confused. He begins to wonder which statements are true and which are false. 

Conflicting arguments and reports make it difficult for the citizen to get the real facts. 

Propaganda.-Propaganda is an organized effort to influence public opinion in a given direction; it often

PUBLIC OPINION 

203 makes it difficult for the people to get the real facts. No sooner had World War I begun in 1914 than each group involved the Allies and the Central Powers-hoped to draw the United States into the war on its side. To do this the nations at war spread propaganda among the American people. Both sides spread stories of shocking cruelties committed by the enemy troops. By means of newspapers, magazines, and public lectures both Germany and Great Britain circulated such stories through the United States. After the war was over it was found that most of these stories were false.

Today the citizen in America must realize that there is much propaganda coming from many sources. Some individuals and business concerns employ publicity agents to publish favorable news. Often these agents furnish free material to the newspapers; they use every opportunity to make public speeches. Each political party maintains national headquarters from which propaganda is sent out to the newspapers. Pamphlets and posters are also distributed. Labor unions chambers of commerce, and other groups also circulate propaganda favorable to their interests. 

The word "propaganda" has a bad meaning for many people. It is true that much propaganda may be put out by individuals or groups for selfish reasons, but we should remember that propaganda may also serve a useful purpose. 

The National Child Labor Committee may put on a campaign to abolish child labor. Some organizations print much reading matter and send out numerous speakers to persuade the people that the United States must co-operate with other nations to maintain peace. A community may need a new school building, but perhaps the people do not realize the overcrowded conditions in the old building. In such a case, the superintendent, teachers, and pupils may print pamphlets and speak to inform the people.

Propaganda is a bad influence only when it is used deliberately to mislead and misinform the public in order to promote the selfish interests of a few people. 

How to Detect Propaganda.-In order that the citizen in a democracy may be correctly informed he should know how to detect propaganda when he sees it. The Institute for Propaganda Analysis states that there are seven devices used by propagandists that the citizen should know about. (1) The name calling device is frequently used. Conservatives in politics who do not like their opponents sometimes call them "communists," "reds," or "radicals."

Liberals may refer to their opponents as "economic royalists," "Tories," or "reactionaries." It is necessary for the people to be alert and look beneath these names which political opponents often hurl back and forth at each other. 

(2) The glittering generalities device is used when the propagandist employs such words as "Truth," "Justice,' "Public Service," "Progress," "Peace," and "Prosperity."

He knows that in a general way, at least, we are in favor of the things for which these words stand. We may not always understand fully what such words mean, but when we see or hear them our emotions are aroused. (3) The transfer device is used by the propagandist to carry over the authority and prestige of something we honor and respect-our country, for example-to something that he would have us accept. All of us believe in the extension of civilization; therefore, when Italy conquered 

Ethiopia she claimed that she was trying to civilize that country. (4) The testimonial device is used by propagandists to try to show that some famous person endorses their ideas. 

Advertisers often use testimonials by famous movie stars. (5) The plain folks' device is used during a political

PUBLIC OPINION 

205 da bi Haifa 

Ewing Galloway

This street orator is trying to sway public opinion for his cause. campaign in order to make the candidate appear to be one of the common folk-just "plain folks among the neighbors." Thus a candidate for President, for governor, or for United States Senator, is pictured with his family on his way to church, buying a Red Cross membership, fishing, playing golf, or hunting. (6) The card stacking device is illustrated by the deliberate use of facts and figures to deceive the people; sometimes falsehoods are presented as true statements. 

Only one side of the question is presented. (7) The bandwagon device is well expressed in the slogan "Everybody's doing it." We are urged to follow the crowd and fall in line with what everybody else believes. 

The propagandist may arrange for big meetings, hire auditoriums, fill stadiums, and march men in parades to call attention to his purpose.

In addition to these seven devices it is well to remember that slogans are often used in propaganda. At one time in our history politicians promised a "full dinner pail" to all citizens if their party was returned to power. "Remember the Maine" was the slogan used during the Spanish-Amer- 

Courtesy The Birmingham News 

Careful listening is a part of understanding public questions. ican War, and the World 

War was "to make the world safe for democracy."

Advertisers also use slogans effectively.

How to Get the Facts. 

-On the average citizen in a democracy falls a serious responsibility for getting the facts so that he may decide wisely who shall be elected to public office. Frequently, the voter is asked to decide directly some public question, such as whether or not the state constitution should be amended. Not only during political campaigns, but all through the year citizens should follow the important news events of each day. The large number of newspapers, news magazines, and radio programs leave no excuse for anyone to remain ignorant concerning the issues of our day. The success of our democracy in the future will depend on how well we as citizens can keep ourselves informed about public questions. The following suggestions are offered in the hope that they may help us to learn the facts: 

1. Avoid prejudice. Do not let it stand in the way of

PUBLIC OPINION 

207 your learning the truth about anything. Try to look at problems from the point of view of what is best for the whole community and for the entire country. 

2. Learn to distinguish between opinion and facts. "America is not prepared to defend herself against foreign enemies." Just because a speaker shouts this statement or a newspaper prints it in large headlines does not necessarily make it true. Facts, figures, and arguments on all sides must be used before such a statement can be proved. 

3. Be on the alert for propaganda. Acquire the technique of detecting it whenever it appears on the radio, or in the newspapers, magazines, books, or in other forms. 

As a rule, we should listen to and read propaganda, but we should evaluate it—try to find out how much of it may be true and how much false. 

4. Learn to read newspapers intelligently. Find out what the political bias of the editor is and always remember that his prejudice may show itself in almost any part of the paper. Remember that headlines are often misleading. Usually it is not necessary to read the whole newspaper article; the main facts are usually found in the first few paragraphs. Remember that the most important news items do not always get front page space. Newspapers too frequently are a means for furnishing the people entertainment; they do not give all the facts on public questions to the readers. 

5. Read magazines intelligently. If possible try to find out what the editorial policy of the magazine is. In news magazines like Time and Newsweek, the reader can usually find more unbiased information than in the daily papers. When the author of an article is given, find out what his qualifications are. 

6. Listen to the radio intelligently. It is now possible to use the radio for more than mere entertainment. Nu-

merous programs now feature debates and discussions on public questions. News commentators are on the air every day to keep us informed about the important events in this and other countries. 

7. Visit places of public interest. Civics classes, clubs, and other organizations, as well as individuals, should visit those places where the work of the government is carried on the local city hall, the county seat, the state and national capitols, public housing projects, the TVA. Through a well-organized visit for purposes of study one may obtain more facts than could be acquired through months of reading.

D. THE DIFFERENCE BETWEEN PUBLIC OPINION UNDER A DICTATORSHIP AND IN A DEMOCRACY Public Opinion Under a Dictatorship.-Under a dic tatorship one man or a small group of men decide what they want the people to believe; they determine what shall be printed in the newspapers, magazines, and books; they allow only certain kinds of speeches to be made. 

Even motion pictures, the theater, and the radio are brought under their control. By these methods public opinion is "manufactured" according to the wishes of those who are in power.

In 1937 when England had coronation exercises for the new king, this fact was not mentioned by the Italian newspapers, because Italy and England were not on good terms with each other. During the summer of 1934 when a number of leading Nazi party officials were killed and imprisoned by order of Hitler, the German newspapers were not allowed to print anything about it for several days. In fact, people in other countries soon knew more about this "blood purge," as it was called, than the Germans did. 

In the dictator countries all propaganda is carefully or-

PUBLIC OPINION 

209 ganized to make the people believe that the government is always doing great things for the people, and that the dictator or leader is a wonderful person. Hitler, Mussolini, and Stalin were looked upon almost as if they were gods by many people in their countries. 

Public Opinion in a Democracy.-Because govern ment in a democracy is based on the wishes of the people, what the public thinks is very important. Many people believe that a government official who is dependent on the public for his office should follow public opinion at all times rather than attempt to mold it. But it seems desirable for a real political leader also to mold public opinion. The President of the United States, the Governor of a state, or any other prominent public official if he is well liked by the people can do much to influence the mind of the public when he makes speeches or issues statements to the newspapers. 

Public opinion in a democracy must depend on freedom of speech and press. In order to preserve democracy we must at all times zealously guard these rights of the people. 

By reading newspapers, magazines, and books, by hearing public speeches and listening to the radio, and by talking with their friends, the people learn the facts, opinions, and arguments on all sides of public questions. Thus, by free and open discussion the people can make up their minds on an intelligent basis; they decide for themselves what they want to believe after they have learned the facts. 

SUMMARY 

Public opinion is what the people think about matters of common interest can be measured by asking many voters what they think about the problems of our day should be based on a knowledge of the truth

is formed by many influences, such as the home, the school, the church, clubs and other organizations, the newspapers, magazines, books, the movies, and public forums is also determined by the work and income of the people Getting the facts on public questions is difficult because of conflicting statements and propaganda Propaganda is an organized effort to influence public opinion in a given direction may be circulated for selfish reasons or for useful purposes may be carried on by seven different devices Facts on public questions should be obtained by the citizens of a democracy can be obtained by avoiding prejudice, learning to distinguish between opinions and facts, being on the alert for propaganda, learning to read newspapers, magazines, and books intelligently, listening to the radio intelligently, visiting places of public interest 

Public opinion under a dictatorship is made by a few persons who decide what the people shall see, hear, and read in a democracy is created by free and open discussion among the people, and is based upon freedom of speech and press.

UNIT FOUR AMERICAN DEMOCRACY AND WORLD AFFAIRS 

In our modern world science and invention have caused the different countries to become close neighbors, but the problem of maintaining world peace is as great as it ever was. Unit Four discusses the relation of American democracy to world affairs. 

Chapter Eleven shows how modern civilization is international; it describes how our country exchanges goods with other nations. 

The relations of our government to other nations are discussed. In the past the different countries of the world have gradually learned to cooperate with each other; they have settled many of their disputes peaceably. But the problem of war is still very serious. Modern warfare is so costly and destructive that it threatens our civilization and our democracy. The American people desire peace, but they are not always agreed about the best methods of achieving it.

Courtesy Pan-American Union 

Pan-American Union Building, Washington, D. C.

Chapter XI  A. OURSELVES AND THE WORLD 

Nations as Neighbors.-Modern science and invention have made the sixty or more nations of the world close neighbors. In Washington's time it took about six weeks to cross the Atlantic Ocean to Europe, but today fast ships run the 3,000 miles or more in four or five days. 

Transatlantic plane service has cut the time between New York and London to less than twenty-four hours. We can call western Europe, South America, or parts of Asia and

Africa by telephone, and we can easily tune our radios to receive short wave broadcasts from abroad. During days of tension among the nations of the world we can sit in our homes and hear European statesmen speaking words that make peace or war. It is as though Europe were just one hundred miles away instead of the 3,000 of Washington's day.

In spite of these facts, the nations of the world do not seem to get along with each other any better today than they did in the past. Fear, suspicion, hatred, and misunderstanding are common among the people of many nations. To maintain relations with foreign countries so as to preserve peace and promote cooperation on a friendly basis is an important problem of our government. 

Exchanging Goods with Other Nations.-The na tions of the world do not live independently of each other.

215

Courtesy United States Department of Commerce 

American ships carry cargo between many ports. 

The United States carries on a considerable trade with countries in various parts of the world. 

All of us benefit from commerce with other countries. 

Look at your breakfast table and think of the many things which have come from outside of the United States. The bananas came from Central America, sugar from Cuba, coffee from Brazil, pepper from Borneo, and cocoa from 

Ecuador. Perhaps your table is made out of mahogany wood from Honduras. The raw materials for making your silk stockings have come from Asia. When you ride in an automobile, it is well to remember that many parts of it have come from other countries: cork from Algeria or 

Portugal, tin from Bolivia, chromium from Rhodesia, shellac from India, and rubber from the East Indies. 

Other nations also depend on us for many of the things which they need. To different countries of the world we send wheat, cotton, tobacco, automobiles, machinery, petroleum products, and many other products. Our pros perity in this country depends to a certain extent on our

Modern Civilization Is International.-The great achievements of any one nation in music, art, literature, science, and invention are never confined to that one country. They soon become a part of civilization in every part of the world. The nations of the world today depend upon one another for products of the mind. Our symphony orchestras play the works of great composers from many dif-

ferent countries; all of us may enjoy the paintings of the greatest artists, regardless of their nationality. Great writers like Shakespeare and Goethe no longer belong to any one country; modern science and invention know no national boundaries.

The daily newspapers and the radio bring us important news events from all parts of the world. Speeches by the 

British prime minister or by the rulers of Germany and Italy are often read with eager interest here as well as abroad. Thousands of American tourists travel in other countries every year and learn about conditions there.

Foreign and American universities also exchange some students and professors. A number of organizations, such as the Rotary Club, have members in many different countries.

B. THE RELATION OF OUR GOVERNMENT TO OTHER NATIONS 

Our Representatives Abroad. To other countries of the world the United States sends two types of representatives: (1) diplomatic and (2) consular.

The diplomatic service includes ambassadors and ministers; ambassadors are usually sent to the larger countries and ministers to the smaller ones. The work of both, however, is very much the same. They reside and have their offices in the capital cities of the countries to which they are sent. The official residence of an ambassador is known as an embassy; ministers live in legations. Ambassadors and ministers are official representatives of the United States to the governments of the country where they are sent.

They try to prevent misunderstandings with other nations; they can often do this by maintaining friendly personal relations with the officials of other countries. 

They also study the foreign policies of the countries to which they are sent, and the conditions under which the people live so that the United States may have reliable information about world affairs. At all times our representatives abroad try to protect American citizens and their interests.

Members of the consular service, known as consuls, are located in most of the important cities of the world. Some of the duties of consuls are: to promote American trade and commerce by providing information and establishing commercial contacts for American business men; to protect the interests of the American government by check-

ing on the value of goods leaving foreign ports for the United States; to assist in the enforcement of the immigration laws by examining prospective immigrants and rejecting those who do not qualify; to act as a friend to any American citizens who may need their help in time of distress. During a war or other disturbance abroad members of both the consular and the diplomatic services cooperate in removing American citizens from scenes of danger.

To enter the diplomatic or consular service of the United States, candidates must pass oral and written civil service examinations. Those who receive the highest ratings are appointed to office when vacancies occur. A young man who does good work in either branch of the service may in time be promoted to higher positions. Ambassadors and ministers, however, are not under this merit system. 

Conducting Foreign Affairs.-The conduct of foreign affairs of the United States government under the Constitution is largely in the hands of the President. He appoints ambassadors and ministers who represent the United States in foreign countries. He also appoints the 

Secretary of State who is considered the most important member of the Cabinet. Some Presidents entrust foreign affairs largely to the Secretary of State; others make most of the important decisions on foreign policy themselves. 

The President has the authority to make treaties with foreign countries but before a treaty is binding it must be ratified by two-thirds of the Senate. In the case of an important treaty, the Senate is likely to debate for weeks before taking a vote. It may approve the treaty just as the 

President proposed it, or approve it with certain reservations, or reject it entirely. 

The President determines with what nations the United States shall maintain friendly relations. He does not send

diplomatic representatives to a country whose government we do not recognize. When a country's government is overthrown and a new one is established, the President must decide whether or not we will recognize the new government. The Communist government was established in 

Russia in 1917 but the United States did not send an ambassador, and so recognize this government, until sixteen years later. 

The President cannot declare war; this authority rests with Congress alone. The President is, however, Commander-in-Chief of the Army and the Navy. By sending armed forces into a peaceful country he can practically start a war. In 1927 President Coolidge sent several thousand United States marines to Nicaragua, where they remained for several years.

The Monroe Doctrine.-Because of their geographical nearness the countries of Central and South America have always held a great deal of interest for us. More than a hundred years ago President Monroe laid the foundation for our foreign policies in this hemisphere. The Latin 

American countries had won their freedom from their European rulers during the early part of the nineteenth century, but in 1823 it seemed likely that the European monarchs, who were determined to stamp out democracy at home, would help Spain to re-establish her power in Central and South America. This prospect displeased the 

British who had built up a profitable trade with these newly established republics. It also displeased the United 

States which disliked the possibility of a powerful European-controlled empire so near by.

Knowing that he could depend on British support, President Monroe in his famous message to Congress in 

December, 1823, declared that the United States government would regard "any attempt on their (European gov-

ernments) part to extend their system to any portion of this hemisphere as dangerous to our peace and safety." 

He made it clear that the Americas were no longer open to further colonization and that if the European Powers tried to control the free and independent republics of this hemisphere we would consider it an "unfriendly act." 

The Monroe Doctrine is not a part of our law, nor is it a part of any formal treaty; it is merely a declaration of our policy. But it has been applied a number of times, and for more than a hundred years it has been the cornerstone of our foreign policy in this hemisphere. 

Today the Monroe Doctrine is again considered of great importance. The Fascist powers of Europe and Asia have spread propaganda in Latin America, seeking to increase their trade there, and trying to gain political power and influence in this hemisphere. Under the Monroe Doctrine the United States may be expected to resist any efforts which might result in the loss of the independence of any of the Latin American countries. To counteract the influence of the Fascist powers the United States is bringing about closer ties of friendship among the twenty-one 

American republics.

The Good Neighbor Policy and Pan-Americanism. 

-In the past the United States government has sometimes sent armed forces into Latin American countries to put down revolutions and safeguard American lives and property. This has given rise to fear and suspicion on the part of the people in these countries who felt that we were trying to gain control over them. 

Presidents Hoover and Roosevelt, however, sought to prove that these fears and suspicions were groundless. 

Neither of these Presidents sent any troops or warships to the Latin American countries. In fact, President Roosevelt has stated our foreign policy is that of Good Neighbor.

He declared that the United States would send no more armed forces into these countries. In 1934 he withdrew the marines from Haiti where they had been stationed since 1915.

The Good Neighbor policy emphasizes Pan-Americanism, the promotion of friendship among all the nations of the Americas. Conferences of representatives of the twenty-one American nations are held to plan how peace can be preserved in this hemisphere. Mutual friendship and understanding among the American nations is the aim of the Pan-American Union, whose headquarters are in a beautiful white marble building in Washington, D. C. This organization was started in 1889; it maintains a large 

Pan-American library which is open to the public; it publishes monthly magazines in English, Spanish, and Portuguese, as well as pamphlets and maps. It also has a motion picture library with films available free for showing in schools.

The Open Door in China.-The United States has always been the friend of China. During the nineteenth century the Great Powers of Europe were helping themselves to the best parts and the richest regions of that great but weak empire. Our business men were alarmed at this tendency; they feared that as each nation got control over a part of Chinese territory, American trade and investment there would suffer. So, to protect our trade, Secretary of State John Hay in 1900 persuaded the Great

Powers to accept the principle of the Open Door, by which all nations were to have equal trading privileges in China. 

For many years the Open Door has been the foundation of our policy in the Far East. However, the rising power of Japan in China made it more and more difficult for all nations to trade there on an equal basis. Repeated protests in the form of notes from this country

The President looks on in his office while the Secretary of State and the Canadian Prime Minister sign a reciprocal trade treaty. to Japan did not bring any favorable results. While Japan and the United States were in the midst of negotiations over the problem of peace in the Far East, on December 7, 1941, Japan treacherously attacked our air and naval base at Pearl Harbor in Hawaii, thus bringing the United States into the second World War.

Reciprocal Trade Agreements.-Believing that for eign trade has much to do with our prosperity, Secretary of State Hull in recent years has done much to break down the tariff barriers between the United States and other countries. Reciprocal trade agreements have been negotiated with about twenty nations, including Great Britain. and Canada. These treaties are "give and take” affairs. 

That is why they are called reciprocity treaties. One nation grants a favor by reducing the tariff on imports from another country, and then the other country "reciprocates" by granting similar favors to its partner in the treaty arrangement.

In the treaty with Great Britain the most important concessions given by that country to us relate to agricultural products, such as wheat, lard, fruit juices, rice, apples, pears, and canned fruits. On these products shipped from our country Great Britain has either removed or lowered her tariff. In return for this we have lowered our tariff on certain manufactured goods coming here from Great Britain. Duties are lowered on British textiles, metals, certain kinds of cotton and woolen goods, pottery, glassware, and furniture.

C. PROGRESS IN INTERNATIONAL COOPERATION International Cooperation.-The nations of the world cooperate with each other for many purposes. Today it is possible to send mail to almost any part of the world, and the cost is usually only slightly greater than when we send it to some point in this country. This convenience was made possible by the establishment of the International Postal Union in 1875. Postal rates are set by this international organization and cannot be raised by any member without its consent.

There are also international unions to regulate weights and measures, to deal with trade marks and copyrights, and to preserve peace between the nations. 

Moving picture films are made a uniform width (35 millimeters) so as to fit into projection machines all over the world. Most nations use the same calendar, and in nearly all countries a standard time has been adopted that differs from country to country by even hours. 

Criminals who flee from one country to another can usually be returned to the country from which they fled. 

International Law.-International law regulates the relations between nations. International law has never been enacted or agreed upon in a formal way; it includes

a variety of customs, rules, and agreements that have grown up over a period of time. For example, a nation's control extends three miles off shore in the open sea along its coast; the boundary line of two countries separated by a river is in the middle of the stream. Much international law is unwritten; there is no way of enforcing it, and it does not apply to some of the more important matters in international relations. 

Mediation.-Disputes which cannot be settled between the two nations directly concerned are sometimes referred to a third party for solution. Usually a compromise which is acceptable to both sides is suggested. This method of settling disputes is called mediation. President Theodore 

Roosevelt in 1905 helped to settle the war between Japan and Russia in this manner. 

Arbitration. An international dispute is sometimes referred to a special court in accordance with an agreement between the parties for arbitration. A great increase in the use of arbitration followed the settlement between the United States and Great Britain of the Alabama Claims. During the War between the States, the 

Alabama and other armed ships, built in England, sailed out under the Confederate flag and did much damage to United States commerce. The United States claimed damages from England. In 1871 the matter was referred to an arbitration court of five members, one each from the United States, England, Italy, Brazil, and Switzerland. The United States was awarded damages to the amount of $15,500,000 from Great Britain. 

The League of Nations.-At the close of the World 

War the League of Nations was organized "in order to promote international cooperation, and to achieve international peace and security." President Wilson was the chief founder of the League of Nations, but the United

Ewing Galloway

The League of Nations met in the League Palace in Geneva, Switzerland.

States did not become a member because the Senate refused to ratify the peace treaty that would have included membership. Those who opposed our entering the 

League of Nations were isolationists; they insisted that we should stay clear of all entangling alliances; they claimed that membership might involve us in some European war that is of no direct concern to us. Supporters of the League argued that American peace and prosperity is inseparable from that of the rest of the world. They said that as a world power we could not avoid taking an active part in world affairs, that we should cooperate with other countries in preventing and settling disputes that might lead to war.

Before the activities of the League of Nations were halted by World War II in 1939, about fifty countries belonged to the League. Its law-making body was the Assembly which was made up of one delegate from each

country that was a member. The activities of the League were carried out by a smaller body known as the Council, which consisted of the representatives of twelve to fourteen nations. The business affairs of the League were conducted by a Secretariat. The headquarters of all these activities was Geneva, Switzerland. While the United

States was never a member of the League, it has participated in much of the League work. 

Among the activities of the League in promoting international cooperation were: maintaining a vast organization to suppress epidemics and promote public health, suppressing the illegal sale of drugs, abolishing slavery, arranging for the care of refugees, and gathering information on a great many phases of international relations. 

One of the chief purposes of the League was to prevent war. If a member nation went to war against the wishes of the League, the other members might punish such a nation by means of an economic blockade or a boycott. Over thirty disputes between nations were dealt with by the League; sometimes it was successful in preventing war, but in recent years it lost much prestige because it was powerless to prevent Japan from invading China, and Italy from going into Ethiopia. Neither did it exert any effort to stop the march of Germany into Austria, Czechoslovakia, and Poland. Three large nations, Germany, Italy, and Japan, withdrew from the League. After World War 

II the United Nations was established and the League of 

Nations was dissolved.

The World Court.-The Permanent Court of Inter national Justice, usually called the World Court, was established by the League of Nations to hear and decide upon any questions of international law submitted to it by the nations concerned in the matter. The headquarters of the Court were in the Peace Palace, located in The

Hague, Holland. There were fifteen judges elected for a term of nine years by the Council and the Assembly of the League. Even though the United States was not a member of the Court, one of the fifteen judges was from this country.

The Reduction of Armaments.-Most of the countries of the world today are spending a large part of their national incomes for enlarging and improving their armies, navies, and air forces. It would be to the advantage of these nations if they could get together, talk over their problems, and agree to reduce the size of their armaments. 

This would save a great deal of expense and it might help to prevent war. The United States and Canada have over three thousand miles of common frontier without any military or naval protection on either side. The League of 

Nations has studied disarmament among the countries of Europe but has not obtained any real results. From 1922 to 1937 the navies of the large countries, the British Empire, the United States, Japan, France, and Italy, were limited in size by agreements made between these nations, but all efforts to renew these treaties have failed. Today more is being spent on armaments than ever before. 

The Kellogg-Briand Peace Pact.-A great advance in international ideals was made in 1928 when French 

Foreign Secretary Briand and Secretary of State Kellogg drew up the famous Pact of Paris. It has two main points: (1) the nations condemn war as an instrument of national policy, (2) they agree to settle all disputes and conflicts by peaceful methods. This treaty has been signed by the United States and has been ratified by the Senate. 

Some fifty other nations, including the governments of all the larger countries, have likewise accepted this agreement. At the time when the Peace Pact was made it was acclaimed as a great step towards international peace, be-

cause most nations of the world definitely pledged themselves to outlaw war. Later events, however, have shown that many countries make war despite this treaty. 

D. THE PROBLEM OF WAR Dictators Threaten Civilization By Making War.— 

Modern warfare is on a larger scale than anything the world has ever seen before. With airplanes, bombs, machine guns, and poison gas, modern armies bring destruction and suffering upon men, women and children. Whole cities and their inhabitants may be ruthlessly wiped out by these machines of destruction. Unless something can be done to stop the menace of war, many people fear that modern civilization may be destroyed. 

Many Americans believed that the first World War which ended in 1918 was fought "to end all wars." The 

League of Nations, the World Court, and the Kellogg- Briand Peace Pact, were hailed as steps in the effort to bring about perpetual peace among the nations of the world. Dictator nations, however, refused to co-operate with any efforts to maintain peace. Germany started another great World War; she was joined later by Italy and then by Japan. Although the United States endeavored to remain neutral the attack by Japan on Pearl Harbor forced us to enter the conflict. American democracy then found itself fighting for survival.

The Results of the First World War.-The costs of war are tremendous. The cost of the first World War, including the cost of ammunition, war supplies, and direct military expenses was 186 billion dollars. The indirect costs, including property losses and the value of the lives lost, have been estimated at 152 billion dollars. Here we are dealing with figures whose size surpasses our ability to comprehend. The total cost of World War I was equal

The total cost of World War I to the United States equals all these social expenses. Each figure represents $500,000,000. to $20,000 for every hour since Christ was born. The average daily cost of the war was more than 215 million dollars, or 9 million per hour. 

In World War I ten million soldiers were killed; twenty million others were wounded, three million of whom

died from wounds. Thus, a total of thirteen million of the most vigorous young men, most of them with splendid minds and bodies, were selected for death. It is hardly possible for us to understand the meaning of thirteen million men killed in the war. A parade of thirteen million soldiers, marching from daylight to dark, ten abreast, would require 50 days to pass a given point. We should remember that many who were not soldiers also died from famine, disease, and massacres. Many of the soldiers who returned from the war were no longer strong and healthy; thousands of them are still in veterans' hospitals. 

The Effect of World War I and World War II 

Upon the United States. Since we were in these wars for a shorter period of time, our country did not suffer as much as the others; in World War I 126,000 soldiers, sailors and marines were killed; in World War II the casualties have been estimated at 250,000. The direct money cost of World War I to the United States has been estimated at 88 billion dollars, while the cost of World War II will be greater than 300 billion dollars. Much of our federal income for a long time in the future will be used for paying the costs of World War I and World War II. 

Wars Come Frequently.-Wars in the modern world frequently plant the seeds of future conflicts. In 1870 the 

Germans defeated the French and took the territory of Alsace-Lorraine from that country. The French determined some day to get it back; they did during World 

War I. 

The Treaty of Versailles after World War I created many grievances in Europe. That war was fought to "save the world for democracy," but afterwards dictatorships were established in a number of countries. This led to the outbreak of another terrible war in 1939.

Modern dictators do not stop to figure the cost of war;

their own power and glory is their chief concern. For this reason, the people of a democracy have to fight when attacked. They have to help to build a world in which, as 

President Roosevelt has said, "This nation, and all that this nation represents, will be safe for our children.”

The Causes of War.-Wars are started by specific events, such as the German invasion of Poland, or the 

Japanese attack on Pearl Harbor. But the fundamental causes of wars are found in underlying conditions and trends. One of the chief causes of war is commercial rivalry among the nations of the world. Industrial nations desire more territory to secure raw materials for their factories and create markets for the manufactured goods. After the close of World War I President Wilson stated, "The real reason that the war we have just finished took place was that Germany was afraid her commercial rivals were getting the better of her, and the reason why some nations. went into the war against Germany was that they thought Germany would get the commercial advantage of them." 

Nationalism, an exaggerated loyalty and devotion to one's country, is another cause of war. In the dictator countries young people, especially, are taught by clever propaganda that they must sacrifice everything for their country. Dictators today claim to be ready to lead their people to war for the honor and glory of their nation. 

Every dictator knows, of course, that his own power and prestige among his people will be increased through successful warfare.

Fear is another cause of war. Preparation for war on the part of each country causes fear on the part of everybody. Military leaders in other countries will immediately demand larger and larger appropriations for the maintenance of their own armies, navies, and air forces.

E. THE UNITED NATIONS 

The United Nations. This organization was established at a conference of delegates from fifty nations at 

San Francisco in 1945. The United Nations Charter was promptly ratified by the "Big Five" and most of the other nations.

The chief purpose of the United Nations is to maintain and protect the peace of the world. It sets up machinery for settling disputes and for taking collective action against nations which make war. It also hopes to remove the basic causes of war through a number of agencies which aim to develop co-operation.

The General Assembly consists of representatives from all the member nations. It has annual meetings; its chief work is to study and discuss anything which affects international relations, and it can make recommendations on these matters to individual members and to the Security Council. It also controls the budget of the U. N. and determines what the dues of each member nation shall be. 

The Security Council has eleven seats. Five of them will be occupied permanently by representatives of the United States, Great Britain, Russia, China, and France. The six remaining seats will be occupied by representatives of the smaller nations who will be elected by the Assembly for a period of two years. On important actions the Security 

Council must have the unanimous consent of the Big Five. 

The Security Council is the strong arm of the U. N. 

It is provided with powerful machinery to promote the peaceful settlement of disputes and to prevent or stop wars. It can enforce its decisions by calling upon all U. N. members to break off physical relations and communication with aggressor nations. If this is not effective the final step would be to take action by armed forces.

The Military Staff Committee consists of the chiefs of staff of the Big Five. It advises the Security Council in the regulation of armaments and the use of military force. 

The Economic and Social Council is one of the most important agencies of the U. N. because it is concerned with removing the causes of war. It consists of 18 members, elected for three-year terms by the Assembly. It will study and make reports on many problems, such as, trade, tariffs, housing, health, education, and conditions of labor. 

The Trusteeship Council consists of members divided equally between nations which hold trusteeships and those which do not. The latter group will be elected by the Assembly for three-year terms. This Council is designed to oversee the administration of some of the backward areas of the world which do not have self government. 

The International Court of Justice consists of 15 members elected by the General Assembly and the Security Council. No nation is forced to submit a dispute to the 

Court, but once the Court does take over a case, its decisions must be accepted by all parties concerned. 

The Secretariat consists of a Secretary-General (appointed by the General Assembly on the recommendation of the Security Council) and a staff which will do the yearround office work of the U. N. It will arrange meetings of the U. N. and provide the necessary clerical force. 

The nations of the world

The American people must co-operate with other nations to remove the causes of war and preserve peace.
