UNIT FIVE BUILDING AND MAINTAINING DEMOCRACY 

To build and maintain democracy in America it is necessary for our citizens to live in good surroundings in the home, the church, the school, and the community. 

The training for good citizenship begins in the home. Chapter 

Twelve describes the changes which have come about in our homes; it also discusses some dangers to the home. 

In a democracy education is necessary so that the people can learn how to govern themselves wisely. Free public schools help to give everybody equality of opportunity. Chapter Thirteen describes the purpose of our schools, the development of education in the United States, and the control and support of our schools. 

The church provides a place for worship and encourages right living. Chapter Fourteen discusses the meaning of religion, the work of the church, and shows the relation between democracy and religion.

The success of our democracy depends on cooperation and teamwork in our community life. Chapter Fifteen describes the different kinds of communities and shows the relation between democracy and community life.

Upper:  Lower: Courtesy USHA: Photo by Sekaer 

In building and maintaining a democracy, one of the first things needed is to replace slum areas with well-built houses and to provide healthful surroundings for all.

Chapter XII THE HOME A. THE IMPORTANCE OF THE HOME What Is a Home?-When the word home is men tioned, you immediately think of mother, father, sister, brother, and other relatives with whom you live. You also think about pets, furniture, a house, yard, garden, trees, flowers, and other things which invite you daily to a place where you can live in freedom and have some voice about those things you desire to do. The quality of the home is not measured by the size of the house and the number of luxuries which the family may enjoy. It is the spirit of the home which makes your life as well as the lives of the other members of your family beautiful.

Home is the place where the members of the family live together. It is more than merely a house. When we think of home, we refer to the affection of parents for their children, and the love of children for their parents. Home is a place where brothers and sisters see in one another the best of playmates and the closest of friends. The members of a family are bound together by ties of affection and companionship, and look upon their home as the best place in the world.

What the Home Does for Its Members.-You are likely to take for granted the many things which the home does for you. Your home cares for you through infancy, watches over you when you are sick, and provides you with food, clothing, and shelter. Good home life builds 241

Courtesy RCA Victor

Home should be a place where the members of a family enjoy doing things together. good citizens. Your opinions and tastes are first formed in the home. If your parents are interested in books, magazines, music, or art, you are apt to become fond of these things.

Obedience to law and order is taught by parents to their children. The boy or girl who learns to obey his parents at home rarely has trouble in other places. Ideals of right and wrong are acquired in the home. The way in which children are taught at home determines whether or not they become courteous, unselfish, and honest in their dealings with other people. Our deepest and most vital impressions of religion are associated with the home. The influence of religious instruction received in childhood will continue throughout life.

THE HOME 

243 

B. THE CHANGING FAMILY 

Family Life as It Used to Be.-During the early days of our nation's history, family life was quite different than it is now. Most of the food and clothing was then made in the home; each family created its own entertainment and recreation. The majority of families lived on farms. Then men and older boys were kept busy with plowing, planting, cultivating, and harvesting the crops. The food was stored in barns and storerooms. The animals on the farm had to be cared for several times each day. Firewood had to be cut and brought into the house. The livestock raised for food had to be slaughtered and the meat preserved.

In this kind of home the women and girls were kept busy getting meals, keeping the house clean, preserving and drying fruits and vegetables, carrying water from wells or springs, or churning butter. During harvest time they sometimes had to help with the work in the fields. 

These early families made most of their own tools and implements. Wool sheared from the sheep and flax grown on the farm were made into thread and woven into cloth. 

During the long winter evenings the women and girls were busy spinning, weaving, and making clothes. Recreation, including many forms of entertainment such as husking bees and quilting parties, was also centered in the home. 

Some Changes that Have Come About.—Today the conditions under which most of our families live have changed. The majority of people now live in towns and cities, and even those who live on farms find life greatly changed because of the industrial revolution. 

Modern science and invention have brought about many changes, especially in the home. Much of our food is now prepared outside. Bread, pies, cake, and pastry can be

purchased from the neighborhood bakery about as cheaply as they can be made. The grocery store supplies canned soups, fish, vegetables, and fruits practically ready for serving. Some of the housework, such as laundering, pressing, and repairing of clothes, may be done away from home. Whatever tasks must be done in the home, such as washing dishes and cleaning house, are very much simplified because of the invention of labor-saving machinery. 

Such improvements as running water, gas, electricity, the vacuum cleaner, and the electric refrigerator have lightened the burden of housework. At one time long hours were spent by mothers and daughters in making clothing for the family. Most of our clothing is now made in factories and purchased ready-made in stores.

How These Changes Have Affected the Family.— 

The development of science and invention has caused many changes in the way the family lives together. In rural areas these changes are not so great as in our towns and cities, for the members of the rural family still live together a considerable part of the time. In our towns and cities, however, the father, and often the older children and the mother, work in factories, stores, or offices. Numerous clubs, movies, and other outside recreations attract members of the family away from the home during the evenings. Often members of city families are interested in so many outside activities that they see one another only during hurried meals. 

Less than a hundred years ago the only career for the average woman was marriage. A woman usually had to depend on her father or brothers for support until she married. After marriage she depended on her husband. Today this has changed. Unmarried women no longer need to depend upon someone else for support. 

Women have entered numerous occupations and profes-

The old-fashioned kitchen pump, which brought water into the house, was once thought a great step forward. About one-third of these women workers are married; they are usually working to increase the total income of the family. The greater opportunities for women outside the home have given them greater freedom and independence.

C. DANGERS TO THE HOME 

Since the home is so important in shaping the lives of the people in a democracy, it is necessary that we become aware of any dangers that may threaten its well-being. 

The homes of our country are like the foundations of a building which must always be kept strong and secure. 

Sickness and Death.-Among the ever-present dangers to the home are sickness and death. It is estimated that every day three million people in our country are ill and

that at least half of this sickness could be prevented by following well-known laws of health and hygiene. Sickness in the home brings added expense; often extra burdens are placed upon all members of the family. When the father is ill and cannot work, the family income is decreased or may be lost entirely. When the mother is ill, often there is nobody to care for the home in the proper manner. Wise fathers and mothers carry insurance which pays sick benefits so that there may be some income during times of illness.

If sickness of a member of the family results in death, the loss is even more severe. The death of either one of the parents often results in the breaking up of the family. The children may be adopted by relatives or neighbors, or may be reared in orphans' homes. A father who is able to do so should carry enough life insurance so that if he should die, his family may still have some income. Most states now have workmen's compensation laws which provide for insurance payments to the worker or his family if he is injured or killed accidentally while he is employed. 

In some states there are mothers' pension laws which provide financial aid to needy widows with children. 

Poverty. Lack of sufficient income is another danger to the home. Parents whose wages are very low cannot provide a decent and healthful standard of living for the members of the family. The members of the family need enough of the right kind of food, proper clothing, and a comfortable house in which to live. These things cannot be provided in a home unless there is sufficient income. 

Because of the low wages of the father, sometimes the mother is forced to work outside the home in order to increase the family income. When this happens and the home is not properly cared for, the children often spend too much time on the streets. When parents are too busy at

THE HOME 

247 

Left: Courtesy Farm Security Administration: Photo by Post Upper and right: Courtesy USHA: 

Photos by Sekaer 

To stimulate the improvement of housing conditions such as these pictures show, the Federal Housing Administration has been established.

work, they cannot find time to make the home what it ought to be.

Today a large number of families rent the houses or apartments in which they live. More money for the wage earners would make it possible for more people to own their own homes. When the house is owned by those who live in it, the ties which hold the family together are strengthened and there is a greater sense of security. 

Bad Housing.-Next to its income nothing so influences the life of a family as its house. Thousands of 

American families live in houses which are inadequate from the standpoint of health, comfort, and decency. In the cities there are slum areas where many families are crowded together into small spaces. Under these conditions there is a lack of fresh air, sunlight, and proper ventilation. Sometimes whole families are crowded together in one room and there is no privacy. Not all bad housing is in the cities. In some rural areas there are families living in ramshackle, leaky houses, unprotected from the cold of winter and the heat of summer. 

There is a close connection between health and housing. 

Among families that live under bad housing conditions sickness is common, especially among the children, and the death rate is high. Lack of food, air, sunlight, and proper heating may cause such diseases as rickets, colds, tuberculosis, diphtheria, and pneumonia. In slum areas where there is overcrowding and a lack of proper sanitation, disease spreads rapidly from person to person. 

Bad housing also breeds crime. Within the crowded homes of the slums there is no place for the children to play and they soon find their way into the street. In these neighborhoods playgrounds and play spaces are usually lacking. At an early age the child may already belong to a gang led by the bad boys of the neighborhood.

THE HOME 

249 Realizing the evils of bad housing, our government is now taking steps to improve the living conditions of the people. The first attempt by the national government to eliminate bad housing was made through the Public Works in den 976 299607 

Courtesy USHA:

Courtesy Farm Security Administration: Photo by 

Rothstein

The house at Cumberland Homesteads, Tennessee (upper photo), and the new government-financed apartments at Milwaukee, Wisconsin (lower photo), are typical of the work that is being done throughout the country to improve housing conditions. 

Administration whose housing division has now completed over fifty housing projects in thirty-five cities. These new fireproof buildings provide modern conveniences, fresh air and sunshine, as well as means for recreation. 

To stimulate the building of homes the Federal Housing Administration has been established. This government agency insures loans for building, repairing, and improving houses. As a demonstration of model housing the fed-

eral government itself has built three large suburban "green belt" towns near Washington, D. C., Cincinnati,

Ohio, and Milwaukee, Wis. The chief need in solving the housing problem is to build good houses that can be sold or rented at a sufficiently low figure for families with low incomes. Thousands of poor families now living under bad housing conditions cannot afford to pay the rents which must be charged for decent housing. For this reason the Federal Public Housing Authority has been established to make loans and contributions to housing authorities set up by local communities. This agency loans up to 

90 per cent of the total cost of a housing project at low interest rates. In addition it will pay a fixed contribution each year so that the rents may be kept within the means of those families that need better housing and improved living facilities.

Failure of Some Marriages.-Marriages are not always successful. When two married persons cannot get along with each other, the state makes it possible for them to become legally separated or divorced. Divorces are granted for certain reasons, such as cruelty and non-support. The divorce laws vary in different states. Unhappily the United States leads the world in the number of divorces granted. Divorce is increasing more rapidly than population. Children need the care and attention of both parents. Consequently, when families are broken up, the children suffer most. From broken homes come a majority of the boys and girls who appear before juvenile courts and who are later sent to reform schools. 

There are a number of reasons for the many divorces in our country. Women have more opportunities for entering occupations and earning money for themselves. Therefore, they no longer feel that they have to remain married if their lives are unhappy. There is also a changed attitude

THE HOME 

251 toward marriage. At one time most people looked upon a wedding as a sacred religious ceremony, and persons who married expected to go through life together. Unfortunately, today some people look upon marriage as a mere legal contract and feel that such a contract may be broken for the slightest reasons.

Hasty marriages may be another cause of divorce. 

Young people sometimes get married before they have had any opportunity of knowing each other. These hasty marriages are possible because in many states the laws do not prevent them. One state may make a law which provides for a waiting period between the time of getting the license and the performance of the marriage ceremony, but people who do not like this law may go to another state where the law is different and get married. 

Another cause for divorce is that many people enter marriage with wrong ideas about its obligations and duties. 

A successful and happy marriage must be based on mutual love, unselfishness, and willingness to sacrifice for each other.

Both marriage and divorce laws should be made more uniform among the forty-eight states. 

D. BUILDING FOR DEMOCRACY IN THE HOME 

Laws that Help the Home. Our state governments have made laws that are the basis on which our homes are established and maintained. If your parents own the house in which you live, a record of this ownership is kept in a legal document known as a deed. In a county office provided for that purpose deeds are recorded and the ownership of property is thus legally established. This is done to protect the owner against fraud. Practically every state also keeps a very careful record of all births, deaths, and marriages.

When two persons wish to get married, they must secure a license from the proper authority, usually the county clerk. The laws of the state prescribe who shall be able to secure a license. A minimum age for marriage is required; in many states persons under twenty-one must have the consent of their parents. In most states persons who are epileptic, or feebleminded, or insane, are not permitted to marry. In some states a physician's certificate stating that they are free from social diseases is necessary for those who wish to marry.

The marriage ceremony may be performed by a minister or priest of a recognized religion, a justice of the peace, or certain other officers. After the ceremony a marriage certificate is made out and signed. 

Democracy in the Home.-In the ideal home the spirit of democracy prevails. One member of the family does not try to dominate the others. The children are encouraged to think for themselves and are given some share in the making of family decisions. As they grow older they are welcomed into discussions or family councils over the budget, family recreations, and other matters that concern them. If the wife is employed, or if she has much work to do in looking after the children, the husband helps with the housework. The children, as soon as they are able, are given some regular household duties. Boys may care for the furnace, clear the walks of snow, or mow the grass in the yard. Girls may help with the meals, or do some of the cleaning in the house. Recreations in which the whole family may share are arranged. Family picnics and vacation trips are planned. The regular observance of family anniversaries and birthdays adds variety to the pleasures shared in the home.

Good Citizenship in the Home. We are told that the average American child who is fourteen years of age

THE HOME 

253 has already cost his parents five thousand dollars. Of course, no home regrets the money and care expended upon its children; neither is it expected that children shall repay in money what they have cost their parents. Every worth-while boy and girl, however, will want to show his appreciation. Children can do this by being worthy members and good citizens in the home in which they live. 

America today needs citizens who are able and willing to do their share to preserve and improve our democracy; a nation can be no better than the citizens who are its members. It is in the home that we acquire the habits of good citizenship. Each member of the family must do his part to make the home a comfortable and pleasant place. 

Each one should gladly do his share of the work. To be neat and orderly is an important part of citizenship in the home. If a son or daughter in the family is careless and leaves books, clothing, or other possessions scattered around the house, someone must come along and put things in order. Obedience to law and order, so essential to good citizenship, begins in the home when children are taught to be respectful and courteous to everyone.

Chapter XIII THE SCHOOLS A. WHY WE HAVE SCHOOLS The Importance of Education in a Democracy.— 

Democracy is a form of government under which the people rule. It is extremely important, therefore, that the schools should educate young citizens in the principles on which our government is founded and in the way its activities are carried on. In order to rule wisely and well, the future voters must be trained to choose their officials intelligently. They must also be made aware of the dangers which threaten our democracy today; they must be given information on how to solve the problems of our country.

Democracy can succeed only when its citizens are intelligent and well-educated so that they may decide how to vote for the best persons and how to choose the wisest policies for our government to follow. This type of education which keeps the citizens of our country well-informed about our government and its policies should continue after young people finish school. Thus, adult education becomes an important part of democracy. 

Public schools are important because they make equality of opportunity possible. Children from all walks of life benefit from public school training. Our free public education through the high school, and in some cases through the college and university, gives all young people the same opportunities to develop their abilities and find the type of work for which they are best suited. 257

Preparing to Earn a Living.-No matter what kind of work we expect to do in the future, we shall find that education is necessary. All of us need to know how to read, write, and use numbers. These "tool subjects," as they are often called, open the way so that we may acquire more training in other subjects. Directly or indirectly the school 

Courtesy National Youth Administration Courtesy Public Works Administration 

These boys and girls are learning skills that will prepare them for after-school occupations. is preparing us for the occupation which we expect to follow: for farming, the trades, clerical work, the professions, homemaking, or for any one of the hundreds of jobs. The way to advancement and promotion is usually on the basis of knowledge and training as well as experience. In some cases, high schools train specifically for some occupation, but usually the school through its courses in English, mathematics, and the sciences gives the student a back-

THE SCHOOLS 

259 ground of knowledge which will be useful in any occupation. For those who wish to continue their schooling after they graduate, the high school furnishes preparatory training.

Learning to Cooperate with Others.-The procedure of every class in school should be a valuable lesson in cooperation. In order to receive the most benefit from the recitation period, pupils in the group learn to follow the directions of the teacher. Some classes may be organized on a democratic basis with a student leader and a student secretary. School clubs and sports when they are properly conducted give valuable training in cooperation. 

If the camera club wishes to take a hike and have a wiener roast after school, plans must be made and committees appointed to look after every detail. In this manner the members learn to work together as a group. Playing on an athletic team should teach cooperation as well as the rules of good sportsmanship and fair play. Whether the team which represents the school wins or loses should not make any difference in our support. Loyalty to our school is an important part of our education. Members of the student council get valuable experience in leadership, and every boy and girl in the school should learn how to cooperate with these officials who have been democratically chosen.

Enriching Our Lives.-Our happiness will depend in part, at least, on the extent to which we enjoy good health. 

For this reason the school gives training in those habits which help us to build strong and healthy bodies. Courses in hygiene, physical education, biology, and general science usually include the rules for healthy living. 

The school teaches us how to enjoy our leisure time. 

Our lives become enriched by contact with the world's greatest art, music, and literature. Classes in English help

Courtesy Florida State College for Women 

These high school students are working on a school mural. In this art project group cooperation is essential. us to enjoy what is best in reading; music appreciation hours make real to us the works of the great composers; classes in art teach us to make beautiful things and help us to know what the great masters in painting have done. 

We learn about what man has done in the past. Hobbies, which are such an important part of leisure time activities, may be developed in the school shops and in the school clubs.

Education broadens our interests in a great many things; it gives us more things to talk about; it widens our sympathies. The whole world with its great variety of countries and peoples is placed before us through the courses we study in school. 

Education and Success.-Boys and girls sometimes leave school at an early age and begin work selling news-

THE SCHOOLS 

261 papers, delivering packages, or clerking in five-and-ten cent stores. Positions of this kind are sometimes referred to as "blind-alley jobs" because there is little or no chance for advancement. Boys and girls who leave school at an early age can usually secure only jobs that require little training. After ten or fifteen years a worker without training is generally no farther ahead than he was at the beginning. An educated person, however, usually has a better chance to be promoted and increase his earning power as time passes.

Does education help a person to earn more money? 

Studies have been made that show that the high school graduate earns more money than the one who does not go through high school. Other studies show that college graduates earn very much more than those who did not go to college. Among the famous men and women whose names appear in Who's Who in America the percentage of high school and college graduates is very large. 

B. THE DEVELOPMENT OF EDUCATION 

Early American Education.-At the time when the first American settlers came to this country the masses of the people in Europe could neither read nor write. There were no free public schools for all the children of all the people as we have today. But among the early settlers in 

New England were a few men who had been trained in the universities of the mother country. These men became the leaders in the movement for schools in America. In 

1635, five years after the founding of the Massachusetts Bay colony, the Boston Latin Grammar School was established. In 1636 Harvard College was started. 

In 1647 the legislature of the Massachusetts colony passed an important law establishing public schools. It required that each town of fifty families maintain an ele-

mentary school and each town of one hundred families a secondary school. These schools were to be supported in part at least by public taxation. Most of the other New 

England colonies followed the example of Massachusetts, but progress in other parts of the country was slower. 

The chief purpose of these early schools was religious, to teach people to read and understand the Bible; secondary schools and colleges had as their chief objective the training of young men for the ministry. 

Schools for Everybody. When the United States became an independent nation in 1783, the number of people who could neither read nor write was still very large. Throughout the country there were schools and teachers for the children of those who could afford the tuition charges, but in many regions there were no free, tax-supported schools. The idea of education as essential to citizenship in a democracy had not yet made a strong appeal.

It was not until well into the nineteenth century that public elementary schools began to be established. 

The great leader in this movement was Horace Mann, a young lawyer who became secretary of the board of educacation in Massachusetts. He believed that both boys and girls, rich and poor, should have equal educational opportunities, that the public schools should be so good that even wealthy parents would prefer them to private schools. He insisted that school buildings should be well built and well equipped and that teachers should be carefully trained in their profession. Largely as a result of the efforts of Horace Mann, the state of Massachusetts passed a compulsory school attendance law in 1842. This was gradually followed by similar laws in other states. Today all forty-eight states have compulsory school attendance laws.

THE SCHOOLS 

263 Most states require children to go to school until they are 16 years old; a few require attendance until the age of 18. Largely because of compulsory school attendance, illiteracy, which the United States Census Bureau 1890 1910 1930 ΛΛ ^ ^ ^ ^ ^ ^ ^ ^ 1934 ^ ^ ^ ^ ^ ^ ^^^^

The Growth of the Public High School.-Before the War between the States most education beyond the elementary schools was in private schools. In 1860 there

were only about three hundred public high schools in the entire country. In 1890 only six per cent of American children of high school age were in high school. It was thought that high school training was only for those preparing to enter the professions. The course of study was designed solely for those who were to go to college or professional school.

During the past forty years, however, the course of study has been expanded to include many “practical" courses, such as stenography, bookkeeping, woodwork and machine shop practice, the household arts, and agriculture. 

These changes have brought about a great increase in high school enrollment. The number of high school students for the country as a whole increased from 200,000 in 1890 to more than 1,000,000 in 1910. In 1938 the enrollment was more than 7,000,000, which included half the persons of high school age. The modern American high school, with its varied opportunities for training and development, is an important part of our democracy. 

Colleges and Universities.—The colleges established in colonial times were chiefly for the training of ministers. 

Among these first institutions of higher learning were 

Harvard (1636), William and Mary (1693), Yale (1701), Princeton (1746), Pennsylvania (1749), and Columbia (1754). The privileges of higher education were at first reserved entirely for men.

During the past fifty years colleges have greatly expanded their work and increased their enrollment. Some universities today have from ten to twenty thousand students. In 1890 there were about 110,000 students in our colleges and universities; today the number has increased to over a million. No other country has so many colleges and universities as the United States; there are more than a thousand institutions of higher learning, of which forty

More than one million students are enrolled in universities and colleges in the United States. Junior colleges, planned to give two years of college work to high school graduates, are steadily growing in numbers. These institutions make it possible for great numbers of young people to get some college training while they live at home. 

To enter a junior college, college, or university, graduation from a four-year high school or its equivalent is required of all applicants. Today it is possible for nearly every high school graduate to receive a college education if he has the necessary ability and energy. Thus, the spirit of democracy, the idea of equal opportunity to all, is being realized as every citizen has the privilege of being educated according to his abilities. 

Private and Parochial Schools.-Some parents do not wish to send their children to public schools; instead they enroll them in private schools, which often give training

Courtesy Department of Publicity, University of Florida 

Libraries, such as this one at the University of Florida, are the true centers of learning. from the lower grades through the high school. These institutions are conducted by individuals or organizations and are partially supported by tuition fees. Private schools and colleges often have endowment funds from which they obtain incomes to help carry on their work. 

To some churches the matter of religious training for young people is so important that they have provided, at their own expense, what is known as parochial schools. 

These institutions teach the regular school subjects, but they also give special training in religion. They often provide education from the first grade through the high school. Most private and parochial schools meet the educational standards set by the state. 

Democracy in the Classroom.-Not only has the course of study in our schools and colleges changed a great deal, but methods of teaching have improved. Too often in the past the work of the pupils in the classroom consisted only of mastering memory and drill exercises. There was little chance for discussion or pupil activities. Discipline was rigid and punishment was often severe. 

Today in many schools the classroom has the spirit of a

THE SCHOOLS 

267 

Courtesy Board of Education of the City of New York 

In a schoolroom with limited equipment, the teacher of this class is still able to carry out planned group projects. happy home. Children work together with enthusiasm under the guidance of the teacher. There is much pupil activity and freedom of movement. Children may make suggestions and engage in discussions. Pupils and teachers no longer look upon each other as enemies, but as partners who are working together for a common purpose. Boys and girls today are being trained to live in a democracy. 

Adult Education.-In a rapidly changing world people find it necessary to continue their education after they graduate from high school and college. Millions of men and women take courses in evening schools, or attend special day schools. Others attend lectures, demonstrations, and take extension courses given by colleges and universities. There are classes for adults who are college graduates, and for those who are just learning to read and write. During a recent ten-year period the number of pupils enrolled in adult education classes increased fifty per cent.

Public forums which are now in existence in several hundred American communities are an important part of adult education. Democracy can flourish only by the spread of information, and on the basis of free and open discussion of all sides of public questions. In this type of adult education, forums whose proceedings are broadcast over the radio are becoming increasingly important. 

Public Libraries.-There are intelligent, cultured persons who have never been able to attend school very long, but who have used other opportunities for self-improvement. The use of libraries, museums, and art galleries broadens one's knowledge and outlook. The public library is an important means of education. The library is generally supported by public taxation, or by private gifts, or both. Rural communities and smaller towns are sometimes served by traveling libraries in trucks or buses. It is estimated, however, that at least thirty-five per cent of the American people, the vast majority of them in rural areas, are without library service.

The greatest library in the country is the Library of Congress, housed in one of the most beautiful buildings of the nation's capital, and maintained by the federal government. It contains copies of all books copyrighted in the United States, as well as many important documents, charts, maps, and photographs. Its catalog contains more than ten million entries. People come from all parts of the world to consult the books and other materials housed in it.

C. THE CONTROL AND SUPPORT OF OUR SCHOOLS 

Our States and the Schools. In our country the control of education is almost entirely in the hands of the states. The United States Constitution does not say anything about schools or education.

THE SCHOOLS 

269 

NEBRASKA PUBLIC LIBRARY COMMISSION Courtesy Nebraska Public Library Commission 

This library on wheels serves the people in the smaller towns and rural communities in Nebraska.

Each state has laws which provide for the support and control of its schools. The minimum length of the school term, the age for compulsory attendance, the qualifications of the teachers, and a general outline of the course of study, are usually fixed by state laws and regulations. 

Each state has an officer, known as the superintendent of public instruction or commissioner of education, who is elected by the voters or appointed by the governor. This officer exercises considerable control over the schools of the state, advising local superintendents and seeking ways to improve the educational system of the state. Since each state may decide for itself how it shall administer its schools, there is no uniformity in our state educational systems.

A considerable amount of educational control is left to local communities. In both rural and city districts the direct control of the schools is usually in the hands of school boards, or boards of education, which are either elected by the people or appointed. They usually fix the

tax rate for schools, provide for the buildings, select the school officials, fix the salaries of school employees, and direct the purchase of supplies. In some states a county superintendent has general supervision of all the schools within a county; in others he supervises the rural schools only. In the interests of greater efficiency the small school districts in some rural areas have been merged and the children are transported to consolidated schools. In the larger towns and cities the schools are under the supervision of a superintendent and principals appointed by the school board of the community. 

The National Government and Education.-While the national government does not control education, it does lend assistance for the support and improvement of the school. The U. S. Office of Education, formerly in the Department of the Interior and now under the Federal Security Agency, collects and distributes information about education throughout the country. In many states the federal government has set aside certain tracts of land to help support the schools and colleges. Local schools that maintain vocational and home economics courses may receive financial help from the federal government. Some people claim that there should be a new member of the President's Cabinet who would have charge of federal encouragement and support of education. 

Paying for Our Schools.-Education is a big business. 

Each year our schools cost the country more than two billion dollars. This is a large sum, although we spend practically as much every year for tobacco, and twice as much for candy, chewing gum, jewelry, and similar luxuries.

Most money spent for public schools comes from taxes. 

In many states the greater amount of school money is obtained from taxes levied by the school districts. In others

THE SCHOOLS 

271 

Courtesy Public Works Administration 

:bise wit smos bonicido e

The one-room schools in rural areas are being replaced by consolidated schools, such as the one above, which serve larger school districts. 

JOOHO BUT 

 the state government contributes money from its treasury for the support of the schools in the local districts. Usually the state contributes school funds more generously to the poorer districts. Unfortunately many communities still have schools where classrooms are overcrowded, equipment is out of date, and teachers are untrained, underpaid, and overworked. Some people think that the national government should help to pay for the schools in the poorer communities. Schools have received some appropriations from the federal government for the salaries of vocational and home economics teachers since 1917.

Why Our Schools Are Free. Many of our early statesmen, such as Washington, Jefferson, and Monroe,

advocated free public schools for everybody, but the struggle to establish free schools was a fierce and long drawn out battle arousing much bitterness. Today elementary school training, high school, and to a certain extent, college and university education are free to all children. This is a necessity in our country where the people rule. Schools are free so that all citizens may understand their responsibilities and be trained to discharge their duties intelligently. Free education is necessary to preserve and improve our democratic form of government. 

Free public education imposes an obligation upon those who receive it. As President Roosevelt has truly said: "Each one of us who has an education has obtained something from the community at large for which he has not paid. No self-respecting man or woman is content to rest under such an obligation unless he returns it in full in the shape of better citizenship."

D. GOOD CITIZENSHIP IN THE SCHOOL 

How to Study.-The taxpayers of your state and local community are contributing millions of dollars to make your education possible. Your responsibility, as a student, is to make good use of your opportunities while you are in school. To get the most out of school you should learn how to study to the best advantage. To help its pupils in this respect, one school furnishes each pupil with a copy of the following:

HOW TO STUDY 

1. Understand clearly what you are expected to do before leaving the classroom. Pay strict attention to the teacher's assignment. 

The teacher often makes very helpful suggestions. Always ask questions when you do not understand. 

2. Plan your day. Be sure to get a certain amount of recreation and exercise every day-in the open air if possible. Get eight

THE SCHOOLS 

273 or nine hours' sleep. Give each lesson on your program some study each day. Plan a definite time for study. Use your study periods during the school day. The best out-of-school study hours are from seven to nine in the evening, which allows the afternoon for recreation. Plan to spend the greater part of your school evenings at home. 

3. I possible, have a quiet place to study in the best light you can get, and at a desk or table away from the rest of the family. 

Have the needed materials-books, pencil, notebooks, ruler-at hand. It wastes time to go after them after you have started to study. 

4. Try to exclude all else from your mind except the one thing that you are doing. If you are able to concentrate on one thing at a time, you have mastered the greatest essential to success. Mastery comes not so much from the amount of time spent on your work as from complete concentration. Be sure you know the main topic of the lesson you are studying and the main points included under it. Use pencil and paper whenever possible. It helps greatly in fixing the facts in mind. Whenever possible, make an outline of the points in the lesson. You will find that this extra work pays greatly in the end. 

5. When you have gone over your work carefully, close your book and try to recall what you have studied. Make notes of points in which you are weak and study these again. 

6. Do more work than the minimum requirement. This is essential for real mastery. Often it is the extra practice, the bit of volunteer work, that speeds up learning. This habit helps to make for success in life. 

7. Use the dictionary for words that you are uncertain about. 

Public speakers frequently read the dictionary to add to their ease and power of speech.

School Activities. To get the most value from school take part in some of the many activities outside the regular classroom. For the fun of it, go into athletics wholeheartedly and be good sports, win or lose. Debating, public speaking, dramatics, and music are some of the many interests which school clubs provide. It is fun to work with other boys and girls in school clubs. Friendships that last a lifetime are often formed in these groups. There is

CBS 

Courtesy Columbia Broadcasting System

A broadcast of the American School of the Air is being made from Walton Girls High School in New York. a definite limit, however, to the number of school activities that a pupil can carry without interfering with his studies. 

No sensible pupil will engage in too many activities. 

Pride in the School.-Every pupil should have school spirit and be loyal to the ideals and purposes of his school. 

Cheer your athletic teams on to victory and back them in times of defeat. Show good school spirit and take pride in the appearance of your school and take care of its property. Keep desks and other school furniture in good condition, and do not waste or destroy paper, pencils, textbooks, and other materials. True school spirit means

THE SCHOOLS 

275 

Courtesy Public Schools, Coatesville, Pennsylvania

This student council is proud of its work in helping to solve school problems through responsible student self-government. enthusiastic support of all school activities, and willingness at all times to sacrifice time and pleasure for the welfare of the school. In school, as in life, the highest satisfaction is in doing things for others.

Democracy in the School.-"We learn to do by doing." No amount of reading and class discussion about democracy can be nearly as effective as the actual practice of democracy in the conduct of the school. For this reason many high schools today are using some form of student government. The exact form which this plan takes is not important; what does matter, however, is that boys and girls are given actual experience in self-government. In the elections for student officers the pupils receive valuable experience in voting wisely. No school is without such problems as the care for school property, school cleanliness, traffic in the corridors, conduct in assemblies and cafeterias, and study hall regulations. As pupils in the

home rooms and members of the student council work together to solve these problems, they gain the type of experience which will fit them best for intelligent citizenship.

We have schools to train boys and girls for citizenship provide equality of opportunity prepare pupils to earn a living teach us how to cooperate with others enrich our lives 

American education first developed in the New England colonies became free to all the children of all the people during the nineteenth century developed through the growth of high schools in more recent years developed through the growth of colleges and universities is also carried on in private and parochial schools has a greater spirit of democracy in its methods today is carried on for adults as well as for children and young people is carried on through museums and libraries The control of education is entirely under state laws and regulations. is usually in the hands of local boards of education is carried out through state, county, and local superintendents and principals

The support of schools comes largely from public taxes in the local districts and from the state governments which teach vocational and home economics courses comes partly from the federal government 

Good citizenship in the school means taking advantage of opportunities by learning how to study means taking part in school activities means taking pride in your school means learning democracy through experience in student government.

Chapter XIV THE CHURCH A. RELIGION AND LIFE 

The Meaning of Religion.-Whether we live in town, city, or country, we find churches whose steeples and spires remind us that people express their religious feelings and convictions by joining together in organizations. Religion is a matter of faith and belief. It is our worship of and devotion to a higher power. It is our interpretation of life. 

Morality refers to the way we live. Although religion usually influences morality, it is not the same thing. 

There is no record of any group of people in history who have ever lived without some form of religion. Primitive people wondered about the causes of wind, of rain, and of sunshine. They believed these forces of nature to be spirits and worshiped them after their crude fashion. 

They offered sacrifices, sometimes even human lives, to obtain the good will of these spirits. Thus, religion in its early stages was barbaric and cruel, and was filled with fear and superstition.

The ancient Hebrews were the first people to believe in one God. Both Jews and Christians worship God as a father. In the Bible we read the greatest religious and moral teachings the world has ever known. There is nothing better than the Ten Commandments and the Sermon on the Mount. Both Jews and Christians have given us our highest standards of conduct. The Hebrew prophet 

Micah asked, "What doth God require of thee except to 280

THE CHURCH 

281 do justice and love mercy and walk humbly with thy 

God?" Jesus, the founder of the Christian religion, said that all the law and the prophets could be expressed thus: "Love thy God," and "Love thy neighbor as thyself." 

Religious Differences.-Unfortunately, in the past, people have often disagreed about religion, and there have been many wars and much strife about religious beliefs. 

Almost 2,000 years ago when Christianity first began, it was looked upon as a strange religion and its followers were persecuted by the Roman government. Many 

Christians lost their lives because they were true to their religion. Finally Christianity was more universally accepted and it became a religion of the Roman emperors. 

Later on in Europe Christianity itself split into many different sects and denominations whose members were continually fighting with one another because of differences in belief. The heads of governments often forced their people to belong to a particular denomination and inflicted cruel punishment upon those who dared disobey. 

For many years the leading nations of Europe were engaged in religious wars and thousands of people lost their lives in a futile attempt to settle their differences. 

Not only have Christians differed in religious beliefs among themselves, but there have also been wars between 

Mohammedans and Christians. Unfortunately, Jewish people have suffered cruel persecutions many times during the last seven hundred years.

Freedom of Worship.-For centuries before the founding of democracy in America the majority of people could not worship God as they pleased. Many persons fled from religious persecution in Europe and came to America to gain greater freedom. Unfortunately, religious persecution did not always end in America; in some of the early colonies people were cruelly treated because of their re-

ligious beliefs. Three colonies, however, Rhode Island under Roger Williams, Maryland under Lord Baltimore, and Pennsylvania under William Penn, were founded on the idea of religious freedom. People who came to these colonies were allowed to worship God as they pleased. Finally, in the United States Constitution our forefathers set an example of religious freedom to the whole world. The first amendment declares, "Congress shall make no law respecting an establishment of religion, or prohibiting the free exercise thereof." Article VI of the Constitution states that "-no religious test shall ever be required as a qualification to any office or public trust under the United States." 

Our state constitutions have provisions guaranteeing religious freedom. Thus, both the state and national governments allow every person to worship as he chooses. 

One of the most glorious things about democracy in America has been the religious freedom which its people have enjoyed. Today, as in times past, oppressed and persecuted people are again coming to our shores to obtain the right to worship God without any interference from the government.

Religious Denominations.-It is not surprising that people differ in matters of religion as they do in other things. In America the followers of the Christian faith are divided into many different denominations. Some people belong to a certain church because of real conviction about its beliefs and practices; with others church membership is a matter of custom. The largest religious groups in the 

United States are the Protestant denominations, the Roman Catholic Church, and Judaism. There are also some members of the Greek Orthodox Church and the Russian Orthodox Church. There are more than two hundred denominations with over two hundred thousand church buildings, and a membership of fifty-five million. About

Membership in the principal religious groups in the United States is shown in this chart. one-half of all persons over thirteen years of age are members of some church. The figures on membership show that the church is growing in strength year by year, although many members are not active in church work and attendance. Protestant churches have the largest membership, but the Roman Catholic Church is the largest single body, since the Protestants are divided into many sects. 

The great variety of Protestant denominations arises largely because of different ways of interpreting the Bible. 

In many cases, however, there is not much difference among the religious groups. Each person should be loyal to the church of his own choosing, but loyalty to one's own faith should never make one intolerant of others.

Many small towns and villages have too great a number of small churches. When there are so many different

churches in a community, the membership of each one is likely to be small. Each small church, therefore, has to struggle for its existence and the people of the community are not united as they should be. In some towns and 

Courtesy Office of the Dean, Washington Cathedral 

Great churches, such as the Washington Cathedral at Washington, D. C., provide inspiration and a place and service for worship for many people. villages small churches have become united. In this way one or two strong churches may replace a number of struggling ones. Larger churches can afford better trained ministers and provide a more extensive program of activities for their members and the whole community. 

The Government and and the Church.-In some countries of Europe the church is directly supported from public funds which are raised by taxation, and ministers are employees of the government. In the United States, however, since the establishment of our national government, we have followed the principle that the church and the state should be separate. The first amendment of the 

Constitution, as we have learned, definitely forbids Congress from establishing any religion by law. Because of this

THE CHURCH 

285 principle of separation of church and state, the public schools are not permitted to give any formal instruction in religion. Most states do not appropriate any public money for a school or a hospital controlled by any one religious. denomination. The government, however, does favor the churches in the fact that all church property used directly for religious purposes is free from taxation. 

Sessions of the United States Congress and of the state legislatures are opened with prayer. The government main tains, at public expense, chaplains in the army and navy and in prisons and reform schools. An oath taken in any court ends with these words "So help me God." Many of our pieces of money have on them, "In God we trust." 

B. THE WORK OF THE CHURCH 

The Importance of Worship.-The most important function of the church is to provide a place and a service for worship. Through worship people are brought closer to God. Church music, readings, prayers, and architecture are aids to worship. A good sermon well expressed will direct the thoughts and emotions of the congregation toward the better things of life. For many centuries people have found the church helpful in worship. 

The Encouragement of Right Living.—The church inspires people to do right and avoid wrong. It constantly urges them to live according to the highest ideals of conduct. To develop in every person a righteous character is one of the most important aims of the church. It emphasizes the higher, nobler, and purer things of life. Someone has said that we are like clocks in that occasionally we need to be wound up to keep us true to the better things of life. At such times church attendance helps us to renew and strengthen our ideals. In time of trouble the teachings of the church give us faith and courage to carry

on in spite of our difficulties. Even though all people are not members of the church and do not attend its services, they are nevertheless influenced indirectly by the church. 

Its constant emphasis on ideals of honesty, fair play, kind- 

Courtesy Farm Security Administration: 

Photo by Evans

This Georgia church plays an important part in the life of the surrounding community. ness, helpfulness, and justice is sure to have an effect on the life of the entire community and on the nation. 

Most churches have a deep interest in the social and economic problems of the day. They are concerned with such matters as a shorter working day, a living wage, unemployment, and problems of the family. Churches have also been cooperating in an effort to bring about world

THE CHURCH 

287 peace. The movement to curb the evils of the liquor traffic is also strongly supported by many of the churches. 

The Church and the Unfortunate.-Jesus gave comfort and aid to the weak, the outcasts, the poor, and the sick, treating all as the children of God. Christian churches have always urged their followers to care for the sick and help the poor. Since ancient times the Hebrews have also regarded charity as a fundamental duty, and members of that faith today have a splendid record in giving to worthy causes.

Many churches today build and support hospitals with free care for those who are unable to pay. In some cases churches provide food and clothing for the needy, establish and maintain homes for orphans and for the aged. In large cities churches have established settlement houses, where the poor can receive help and enjoy a pleasant social life. A large number of social service organizations, hospitals, and similar institutions have been established either by the churches directly or by men and women of religious zeal. 

The Church as a Social Center.-Most of us are not happy unless for part of the time, at least, we are in the company of others. Dr. Henry C. Link, a great psychologist, has written a book, The Return to Religion, in which he emphasizes the need for people to associate with each other and work together in groups. The church provides social life for the people of the community. In some cases it conducts non-commercial recreations and sets up neighborhood activities. Many churches have kitchens, bowling alleys, swimming pools, and recreation rooms. Some have places for parties and rooms for the meeting of clubs. 

Others maintain libraries, show motion pictures, and sponsor summer camps, as well as Boy and Girl Scout troops.

Some of the most influential religious groups came into being to help the work of the church by providing more social activities. Among these are the Young Men's 

Christian Association, Young Women's Christian Associa tion, Knights of Columbus, Young Men's Hebrew Association, and the Young Women's Hebrew Association. 

The Church and Education.-Since religion plays an important part in the life of the individual and the com- Photo by Knell 

Courtesy Duke University: 

This beautiful chapel on the campus of Duke University provides a place for students to worship. munity, many people believe that it ought to form a part of our education. 

This is why some churches have parochial schools. 

But it would be unfair to teach any particular religious creed in our public schools where the children come from homes of many different faiths. In some schools a special period of the week is set aside when pastors come in to teach the pupils of their faith. In other schools pupils are excused for one period a week to go to the churches of their own choosing to receive religious instruc- tion.

In most communities there are Sunday Schools that offer religious instruction, but it is estimated that less than one-third of all young people under twenty-five years of age attend these schools. During the summer months many churches now provide Vacation Church

THE CHURCH 

289 Schools where boys and girls may come to worship, sing hymns, and study the Bible. Instruction in religion is usually combined with a program of social activities. 

In order to train young men for the ministry and priesthood the churches have always had a great interest in higher education. A large number of the colleges and universities in America were founded by some religious denomination. Nearly half of these are still controlled and partially supported by churches. 

C. RELIGION AND DEMOCRACY Religion Under Dictatorships.-Freedom of reli gious worship and preaching were not permitted in the dictator countries of Europe. The rulers of these countries insisted on loyalty to themselves and their governments above everything else. They were jealous of other organizations, such as the church, and did not permit them any freedom of expression. Loyalty to the dictator, or leader as he was called, became so great that to some extent it took the place of religion. This is why dictators frequently quarreled with the church and its leaders. Religion under dictatorship was the slave of the state. 

In Russia the people lived under a government which condemned religion and destroyed many of the churches. 

Religious practices and ceremonies were ridiculed. The 

Communist government prohibited evangelistic preaching and Sunday School teaching. However, freedom of religion was restored in Russia during World War II. 

In Germany some of the leaders of the Nazi party wrote books and issued statements against the Christian faith. 

Prominent newspapers often printed insulting propaganda against the leaders of the Christian and the Jewish religions. Pastors and priests were no longer free to preach what they wished.

Most shocking of all was the persecution of the Jews by the German government. These people lost all rights of citizenship; their children were no longer permitted to attend the public schools; and most forms of work and business activities were closed to them. Soon after the coming to power of Hitler in 1933 the Jews in Germany suffered many insults and terrible hardships.

Religion and Democracy. Only in a democracy can all forms of religion grow and flourish. In a democracy people are free to worship as they choose. Religion is a personal matter, a relationship between the individual and his God. It is not something that can be imposed from without. The finer and nobler things of life cannot be decided by a dictatorial government. If we wish to keep the highest and best in our religious life, we must maintain our democracy.

Democracy, as we have seen, also stands for the equality of all men. It strives to create equality of opportunity so that all may work and enjoy life on the same basis. Our federal and state constitutions guarantee the same rights to all citizens. Government in a democracy, like religion, is concerned with the welfare of each and every person.

Religious Tolerance.-Among the original settlers were many who came here because of religious persecution. 

The Pilgrims and Puritans came to New England, the 

Roman Catholics to Maryland, and the Quakers to Pennsylvania-all of them hoping to find in this country a place where they might worship in their own way. From the very beginnings of our national history there have been people of a great many different religious faiths in our country. During colonial times those who came here from 

Europe were not always tolerant of each other, but out of the struggles and conflicts between the religious groups

THE CHURCH 

291 there developed the idea that all persons should have freedom of worship.

This idea of religious freedom and tolerance, as we have seen, was made a part of our Bill of Rights in the Constitution. It is a precious heritage which has been passed on to us by our forefathers. Religious freedom is one of the cornerstones of our democracy. There is room for all religious groups-each making its own contribution to the upbuilding of American democracy. Religion

Religion under a dictatorship is not free; it may become the slave of the state can develop and flourish best under a democracy.
