Chapter XV THE COMMUNITY A. LIVING IN GROUPS Why People Live in Groups.-Human beings live together in groups. Most people do not like to live alone. 

Men have learned through thousands of years the lesson that they must work together for the good of each other. 

Back in the days of earliest man there were some things which an individual could not do by himself. He needed help to build his hut, to kill wild beasts for food, and to protect himself from all kinds of danger. The habit of living and working together was started so long ago and has become so firmly fixed that today we are unhappy when we are separated from our friends and neighbors. 

The first and most important group with which the child becomes acquainted is the family; soon he learns to play with other children in the same neighborhood. At about the age of six he begins to associate with another group of children in school. As time goes on the individual becomes a part of more and more groups. He may join many clubs, societies, and other organizations. 

Membership in many of these groups is voluntary; it is up to the individual whether or not he wishes to join. But everyone is a member of the group controlled by his government the city, county, state, and nation. The activities of people and the way in which they get along with each other, living in groups, is the subject of the social studies. 294

THE COMMUNITY 

295 Our Dependence on Others.-No person can live in our modern world without depending on other people. 

There is hardly an hour of the day when we do not benefit by the work of someone else. At the breakfast table we may have oranges, cereal, buttered toast, and coffee.

Perhaps the bread is made from Minnesota wheat, the butter may come from a Wisconsin dairy, the oranges from Florida or California, and the coffee from Brazil.

Thus, an army of workers, the farmer, the miller, the grocer, railroad men, sailors, and many others, have contributed to make our breakfast possible. In a similar manner all our food, clothing, houses, and everything else that we use and enjoy have been made possible by the cooperation and work of many different people in various parts of the world.

Importance of Group Life.-By uniting our efforts, we secure the best living conditions. Because everybody pays taxes, all the members of a community enjoy the benefits that come from free public schools. In order to have a plentiful supply of good water, the people build reservoirs and pipe lines for the entire city, each family paying a small amount for the water it uses. 

There are many other kinds of community cooperation. 

Police and firemen are paid by the people to protect the lives and safety of the community. Other officials look after the health of the people. Many cities provide parks and playgrounds for the free use of the public. In street lighting, in garbage collection, in the construction and maintenance of streets, and in many other ways, the people in our communities cooperate for the common good. We could not have any of these things if each person lived by himself.

Cooperation and Teamwork Necessary. In order to secure the greatest benefits from our living together,

Courtesy Radio Corporation of America 

Cooperation is as necessary in community living as in group athletics. teamwork and cooperation are essential. Everyone knows how important teamwork and cooperation are in athletics. 

By good teamwork five ordinary basketball players can defeat a group of individual stars. Without the assistance of the others, one player alone cannot bring the ball near enough to the basket to make a goal.

In order to have satisfactory group living each citizen in a community must cooperate-pay his taxes, obey the laws, vote intelligently, keep his own house and yard neat and attractive. When necessary he should be willing to sacrifice his own time and money for the good of the community. In industry the people who raise food, make clothing, build houses, and sell goods are all members of a vast team who cooperate to supply the needs of the world. Workers in industry, farmers, business men, teachers, pupils, writers, ministers, and artists, all must cooperate to preserve and improve American democracy.

THE COMMUNITY 

297 

Laws and Rules Are Necessary.-Everyone knows how important rules and regulations are in athletics. The rules of the game tell what each player may do or not do. 

In all our living together rules and regulations are necessary. Thus, in a democracy, the people through their representatives make laws which are for the good of everybody. The law which requires the driver of an automobile to keep to the right prevents collisions. The regulation which requires us to remain at home for a certain number of days when we have a contagious disease prevents epidemics in the community. Laws are not made to prevent us, as individuals, from doing something; their purpose is to make a better life for all. In a democracy a law should represent the will of the whole community. Laws are usually passed only after considerable deliberation, discussion, and compromise. 

Custom Helps to Control Our Actions.-Most of our actions are controlled by custom and public opinion rather than by law. Custom is the habit of the group. We do certain things and refrain from doing other things. We eat and dress as we do largely because of custom. Custom explains many strange things in different parts of the world. It explains why the people in China use chopsticks and why we use knives and forks. Many things which were customary at one time have become laws. It was customary to drive on the right side of the road in our country long before the law required it. 

Public Opinion Helps to Control Our Actions.- 

Many of our actions are controlled by what other people may think about them. Public opinion is what people think about a particular thing, whether it is good or bad, desirable or undesirable. We usually tend to agree with the people of the community and we do not wish to do things which will meet with their disapproval. Public

opinion and custom are closely related. When we consider what kind of clothing we shall wear, or what we shall do on a Sunday afternoon, we are influenced by custom as well as by what people may think of our actions. 

Public opinion in a democracy influences elections and law-making; it may force nations to go to war or compel them to remain at peace. An intelligent public opinion is a necessity in a democracy. 

Control of Group Life in a Democracy.-For the good of the group all of us must cooperate, obey the laws of our community, observe its customs, and pay attention to public opinion. Customs, laws, and public opinion, however, can be changed by the people. In a democracy the people have freedom to discuss ways of change. On many things we do not have to agree with the group. Under a dictatorship everybody is forced to conform rigidly to all customs and opinions of the group that is in control. All changes are supposed to come by order of the dictator. 

Governments under a dictatorship have control over the newspapers, magazines, the radio, moving pictures, and other means of spreading information. Thus, by propaganda they can mold the minds of the people and control their actions.

In a democracy, however, the individual has greater opportunity to be different from the group. Because of freedom of speech and press, he is always given an opportunity to win the group over to his way of thinking. Under a democracy, when laws no longer meet with the approval of the group, they can be changed by the people through their representatives.

B. WHAT IS A COMMUNITY? 

What Is a Community?-A community is a group of people living within a fairly definite area and bound to-

THE COMMUNITY 

299 gether by common interests, customs, and laws. People who live in a town or small city usually make up one community, but in a large city there may be several different communities. In rural areas a community may consist of a township or perhaps an entire county. 

The people of the same community have common interests since they live under the same local government, they belong to the same societies, clubs, and organizations, they attend the same churches, and their children are educated in the same public schools. Each community has its own. stores, hospitals, public parks, playgrounds, and the like. 

It is in our thousands of local communities all over this country that we can best see our dependence on each other, and our need for teamwork and cooperation. 

A community and a neighborhood are not the same thing. A neighborhood consists of a number of families living near one another. A community consists of a number of neighborhoods. The people of a neighborhood often cooperate with each other in a great many ways. They see each other frequently and share each other's joys and misfortunes. Neighborhood activities, especially in the larger cities, are not as important as they once were. There is more neighborly spirit in the smaller towns and in the open country.

Types of Communities.-There are many different kinds and sizes of communities. The United States Bureau of the Census classifies as urban communities all incorporated places with a population of 2,500 or more and townships which have at least 1,000 persons per square mile with a total population of 10,000 or more. Smaller or less thickly settled areas are classified as rural communities. 

Generally, we think of a rural community as one which consists of a collection of farms, perhaps including several small villages which serve as trading centers. Throughout

our history there has been a steady growth of the number of pople living in urban communities. In the census of 

1790 it was found that only 3.4 per cent of our people lived in urban communities; in 1940 the percentage was 56.5.

Some urban communities where there are few large stores and no factories are called residential. Communities of this type near large cities are usually referred to as suburban.

A town or city which is the center of some great industry is referred to as industrial. In addition, there are mining towns, trading centers, transportation centers, pleasure resorts, health resorts, and college towns. Thus, communities often become best known for specialized characteristics.

C. THE RURAL COMMUNITY City and Country Need Each Other.-Rural and urban communities are dependent on each other. The people of the city obtain food, materials for clothing, and many other supplies from the country. The people of the country, in turn, secure manufactured products, such as clothing, furniture, and farm machinery, from the city. 

People from rural areas sometimes come to the city to make purchases in the stores, and to enjoy recreation. In turn, the people from urban areas seek rest and relaxation in the more peaceful and quiet atmosphere of the open country.

Farmers in Europe and America.-In the European countries farmers live in villages. Early in the morning they go out to their small fields to work, and in the evening they return to their homes in the villages. Their community life centers in the village with its church, school, stores, inn, and neighborhood gatherings.

THE COMMUNITY 

301 In America, as a rule, farmers do not live in villages; they live on their farms sometimes separated from their neighbors by considerable distances. Because they meet each other only occasionally, community life is more difficult to maintain. Automobiles, good roads, and the telephone, however, have helped to break down the isolation of the farmer and have brought the members of the farm community closer together. 

Improving the Rural Community. The comforts and conveniences that come from a supply of running water in the home, the telephone, and electricity, are gradually being extended to the people of rural communities. By means of loans for the building of electric power lines and for the purchase of wiring and of electric appliances, the federal government is encouraging a more extensive use of electricity by farmers. Some farmers have their own electric light and water systems. 

Community organizations, such as the Parent-Teacher Association and the Grange, are important in the life of a rural community. Their meetings and social affairs are usually important events. 

The county agricultural agent is an official who also helps to interest people in community improvement. 

Through 4-H Club work boys and girls carry on farm and homemaking projects. They learn by doing and have a lot of fun and recreation at the same time. Nearly a million boys and girls are members of 4-H Clubs. 

Social Centers in Rural Communities.-One of the problems of the rural community is to provide an adequate amount of wholesome recreation and social life for the people. For this purpose rural communities should have a consolidated school house or a community building with an auditorium, kitchen, dining room, library, and committee rooms. Such a building can be used for meetings of all

This attractive community house in Winona, Mississippi, serves as a social center for the entire community. kinds, for lectures, social affairs, plays, boys' and girls' club meetings, athletic sports and contests. It can become a social center for the entire community. 

An important need for rural community life is adequate leadership. Too often, in the past, the best leaders have left their homes in the country and have gone away to live in towns and cities. Teachers in the public schools and ministers in the churches have sometimes looked upon their work in rural communities merely as a stepping stone to something better in the city. To make life attractive and interesting for its members there must be trained leaders who are willing and able to help the rural community to go forward in its activities. 

Beautifying Rural Communities. To make one's own house and lawn beautiful helps to improve the com munity; it is a mark of good citizenship and an inspiration for better living. Country homes, as well as those in the cities, can be made neat, attractive, and beautiful,

THE COMMUNITY 

303 The use of paint on the house and farm buildings, the planting of trees and shrubbery, all help to increase the beauty of our rural communities. 

Billboards along the highway in the open country may do much for advertising but very little for beauty. One may drive along a fine highway admiring the grandeur of the scenery, when it is suddenly shut off by some large billboard. Some people think that laws which restrict billboard advertising should be passed by our state legislatures.

Highway planting is another way of increasing the beauty of our rural communities. The highway departments in some states make the public roads more beautiful by planting trees and shrubbery. 

D. THE URBAN COMMUNITY 

How Cities Grow. It is interesting to study the reasons why towns and cities grow and develop in certain places and not in others. In frontier days a man might put up a trading post at a crossroads. Thus the intersection of two roads or perhaps two rivers may have been the reason for the beginning of a town. Many of the early towns have disappeared entirely or have not grown in size since their beginnings. To grow and develop in size a town has to have a number of advantages of location. Many of our large cities have grown up at places where land and water routes of transportation come together. If the region around the city has fertile farm land, it becomes an outlet for agricultural products, and a means for supplying manufactured goods to the farmers. 

The development of factories near the supply of raw materials may also cause cities to grow. Pittsburgh in Pennsylvania and Birmingham in Alabama have become great steel centers because coal and iron can be obtained in

abundance. Philadelphia, New York, and Baltimore in the 

East, St. Louis and Chicago in the Middle West, San 

Francisco and Seattle in the West, New Orleans and Houston in the South, have grown to be large and important cities because of their location along land and water routes of transportation, and because they are convenient centers for trade and manufacturing. 

Problems of Cities.-After cities became large and congested, people were faced with a number of new and difficult problems, such as health, fire and police protection, recreation, and transportation. 

When people live close together as in our modern cities, disease may spread rapidly from one person to another, and so quarantine laws and sanitation measures become necessary. Food has to be brought in from outside the city and must be kept free from impurities; an adequate supply of pure water has to be made available for the people of the city; and waste materials, such as sewage and rubbish, must be disposed of in a sanitary manner. 

Fire regulations and fire-fighting apparatus are necessary to prevent fires which might destroy the entire city. 

The people of the city must be protected from crime. 

For this purpose an adequate police force must be provided and efforts must be made to remove the causes of crime.

Recreation is another problem. The city must provide safeguards against amusements that are harmful to the people; it should help to provide wholesome recreation for everybody.

Traffic needs bring about another problem. It is necessary to provide cheap and rapid means of transportation by trolleys, buses, subways, and the like, so that people and goods may be moved quickly and safely. The control of traffic in the interest of safety is also important.

THE COMMUNITY 

305 

Courtesy Public Works Administration Courtesy USHA 

The "planned slums" at the right are next to a factory building; there is no place for the children to play except the streets. Contrast this with the protected playground in the housing project at the left. 

Need for City Planning.-With a few exceptions, such as Savannah, Washington, Buffalo, and Detroit,

American cities have not been planned, but have been allowed to grow up in haphazard fashion. As a result, when towns became great cities a number of serious problems developed. Large sums of money had to be spent to widen streets and make other changes in the interests of safety, convenience, and beauty. Much of this expense might have been avoided if our cities had been developed in accordance with definite plans. 

Other problems in cities arise because business, residential, and industrial districts are not kept separate from each other. Residential sections become undesirable for homes when factories near by create noise and dirt.

Garages, filling stations, and stores, if allowed in residential districts, may destroy the beauty of these areas. 

Because of lack of planning, many cities today have an inadequate number of playgrounds and public parks located in the proper places. 

The Plan of Washington, D. C.-An outstanding example of a well-planned, beautiful American city is Washington, the capital of the United States. The original plans for the city were drawn up by the famous French engineer, Major Pierre Charles L'Enfant, under the supervision of Washington and Jefferson. According to the original plan, the Capitol building, located on a commanding hill, is the center of the system of streets. It is connected with the White House by Pennsylvania Avenue, a beautiful and spacious thoroughfare which has become the great parade ground of the city. One of the most beautiful parkways in the world is the Mall, extending from the Capitol to the Lincoln Memorial. In the future more government buildings will be located along this parkway. 

The city is divided into four sections usually abbreviated to read N. E., S. E., N. W., and S. W. The plan for the streets is unique. Streets running north and south are designated by numbers and intersect at right angles with those running east and west which are named by letters. 

Besides the numbered and lettered streets, there are broad diagonal avenues named for the various states in the Union, which intersect this checkerboard system and make it easy to go from one part of the city to the other. At the intersection of these diagonal avenues and rectangular streets, small parks are formed.

City Planning. The purpose of city planning is to provide for the growth and development of the city in an orderly manner that will make for safety, convenience, and beauty. Many large cities now have city-planning

THE COMMUNITY 

307 commissions which have supervision over new developments and can prevent a repetition of the mistakes of the past. Months or even years may be needed to make a complete and satisfactory plan. Some interesting new communities in America that have been built in accordance with definite plans are Radburn, N. J., near New York 

City; Mariemont, Ohio, on the border of Cincinnati; 

Chatham Village in Pittsburgh; and the three "“greenbelt" towns built by the federal government, Greenbelt, Md., Greenhills, Ohio, and Greendale, Wis. These model communities have attracted much interest. 

Some of the purposes of city planning are discussed below. 

1. To improve the means of transportation. The streets of a city should be sufficiently wide and systematically laid out to permit free flow of traffic. The checkerboard system of laying out streets is the simplest method and makes it easy to locate any particular place. In order to have "through" streets which make it easy to get from one section to another quickly, the checkerboard system may be modified by the use of diagonal avenues and boulevards. Detroit partly follows the spider web as a model for laying out its streets. In the residential areas of a city curved and broken streets may be desirable because they discourage through traffic and afford greater privacy and safety. 

2. To improve the location of industries and businesses. 

In the well-planned city suitable and well-located areas are set aside for different types of factories and mills, for stores, offices, and other forms of business. 

3. To insure adequate space for public parks and playgrounds. Not less than ten per cent of the area of a community should be reserved for public parks and playgrounds. In every neighborhood there should be children's

Courtesy Farm Security Administration: Photo by Mydans 

These children live at Decatur Homesteads, Decatur, Indiana. Members of their families are employed in industrial plants in the city. This project provides an example of excellent residential zoning. playgrounds and small parks where the people can have easy access to trees, grass, and open space. Large parks close to the city are also desirable. 

4. To plan for the location of public buildings. There should be a civic center in each city where its public buildings, courthouse, city hall, post office, and library, are located. This should be in some central area and one of the chief beauty spots of the city.

Zoning. A zoning law is a necessary part of city planning; it divides the community into several different kinds of districts. Some of the zones that may be planned for a city are: (1) single family houses, (2) apartment houses, (3) business, (4) industrial. Within each zone or district, the new buildings must all be for the same use and must not be over a certain height and size.

THE COMMUNITY 

309 In a single-family zone only single-family dwelling houses may be built, while in a zone for industries no houses may be built. Houses in a residential district must be placed at the same distance from the front of the lot in order to make the street beautiful. 

A good zoning law not only increases the beauty of a city but benefits everybody. It helps to maintain the value of properties in each district. If there is no zoning law in a city, a citizen may find that he has put his savings of a lifetime into a home only to find that its value is greatly decreased by the establishment of a factory or a garage next door. The beauty and attractiveness of many of our towns could be greatly improved if stores and shops were restricted to a small area instead of being scattered among the dwelling houses. 

E. DEMOCRACY IN THE COMMUNITY 

Community Spirit.-Just as loyalty to your school and willingness to sacrifice for it is known as school spirit, so loyalty to your community and willingness to sacrifice for it can be called community spirit. In order to have a beautiful and attractive community it is necessary for the people to work together for the common good. Everybody should develop a sense of pride in his community. Homecoming days and historical pageants based on local history can be made a part of community celebrations. 

The good citizen tries to make his own home beautiful and attractive, and gives freely of his time and energy for the betterment of his community. He contributes to the church, the community chest, and other organizations that promote community welfare. While we expect every citizen to pay his taxes and to vote intelligently, there are many other civic duties to be performed. In nearly every community there are organizations, such as the Boy

Scouts, Girl Scouts, Y. M. C. A., and Y. W. C. A., which deserve the support of the people because they aim to improve the quality of community life. While in a democracy people may differ about what should be done for the community, no community enterprise can succeed without cooperation and teamwork. 

Community Institutions Serve All the People.- 

Health regulation, fire protection, crime control, transportation, recreation, and planning are problems that every community must face. To solve these problems we must have government, and in a democracy the government is an agency which serves all the people on a basis of equality. Schools afford "all the children of all the people" an equal opportunity for an education. 

In a democracy the people are free to join a great variety of clubs and organizations. There are political clubs, churches, labor unions, fraternal groups, and many others. 

Americans are known as great "joiners." In these groups a person may find fellowship and through them he may work for the causes in which he believes. Sometimes these organizations tend to divide people into cliques and groups that are antagonistic toward one another. These groups, however, should work together for the good of the community.

Being a Good Neighbor.-One of the best ways of improving community life is to be a good neighbor. Our neighbors expect courtesy, kindness, and friendliness. A good neighbor will know the people who live near him. 

When our neighbor is in trouble, is sick, or has some sorrow in his home, little acts of kindness on our part may help to lighten his burden. Sometimes an exchange of gifts or pleasant conversation will do much to promote a feeling of good will.

UNIT SIX MAKING BETTER CITIZENS IN A DEMOCRACY 

Democracy exists only for the good of the people. How we can become better citizens in our democracy is the theme of Unit Six. 

The value of good health is appreciated today as never before. 

Chapter Sixteen shows how the health of the people is being safeguarded through community cooperation. It discusses the problem of medical care for the poor. 

Modern science and invention have made it possible for most of us to have a considerable amount of leisure time. Chapter Seventeen discusses ways in which we may spend our leisure time; it describes community cooperation for recreation. 

Chapter Eighteen discusses the problem of safety in the home, on the farm, and on our streets and highways. It also presents the problem of safety from fires.

The maintenance of law and order is one of the most important tasks of our government. Chapter Nineteen describes our system of law enforcement, the causes, punishment, and prevention of crime.

Ewing Galloway

The future of democracy depends on the boys and girls who are now being trained for citizenship.

Chapter XVI HEALTH A. THE IMPORTANCE OF HEALTH 

Health and Our Happiness. Did you ever try to study your lesson when you had a toothache or attempt to play a game when you had a headache? If so, you realize that good health is necessary for efficient work and joyous play. Our happiness depends to a large extent upon our health. When a person is not feeling well, he can neither do his best work nor enjoy life. Of course, there have been notable exceptions. Robert Louis Stevenson continued to write beautiful and entertaining stories and poems, even though his body was frail and weak. General 

Grant, who was afflicted with a painful and incurable disease, worked on his Memoirs day after day until his death. But most of us would have great difficulty in overcoming these handicaps. We depend on strong, healthy bodies for doing our work and enjoying our play. The value of health is appreciated today as never before. 

Our Attitude Toward Disease.—In early times people had the idea that pain and sickness were the work of evil spirits. They thought that these spirits were somewhat similar to human beings and could enter the body. They believed that through great disturbances these evil spirits could be driven from the patient. Therefore, the medicine man's main duty was to shout, pound implements, and raise such a noise that these horrible, invisible spirits would 317

take flight for safety. Today this all seems like nonsense, but such was the custom of ignorant persons some three hundred years ago. Even as late as Washington's time, doctors treated certain diseases in a manner which often hindered rather than helped the patient's progress. 

Man's knowledge about the causes, cure, and prevention of disease is comparatively recent. Only a hundred years ago many people thought that sickness was a punishment for wrongdoing; today we know that sickness is usually the result of not observing the rules of good health. 

Discovering the Causes of Sickness.-Zacharias 

Jenssen, a native of Holland, invented the microscope in 1590. Through a combination of lenses this instrument will enlarge an object two thousand times its natural size. Less than a hundred years ago, Louis Pasteur, a French scientist, discovered that disease was caused by germs. This great discovery laid the basis for our present-day programs of disease prevention. Koch, a German scientist, discovered the germ that caused tuberculosis. In the early years of our own country, many people were marked with the ugly scars of smallpox. Edward Jenner, an English physician who lived during the early part of the nineteenth century, found that smallpox could be prevented by vaccination. Today the ravages of this terrible disease have been almost completely stamped out. Dr. Walter Reed and a group of brave co-workers found that yellow fever was caused by the bites of certain mosquitoes which carried the germ from a victim of the disease to a well person. 

While we know a great deal about the causes of illness today, there are still some diseases, such as cancer, measles, common colds, and infantile paralysis, that scientists do not entirely understand. Through medical and scientific study, however, progress is being made in these fields each year.

HEALTH 

319 

The Conquest of Disease. Because of these discov eries in modern medicine and the development of the public health movement, one disease after another has been brought under control. Superstition and ignorance about the causes of disease are gradually giving way to evidences 1789

Average life expectancy in Massachusetts in 1789 was thirty-five years. Figures for the United States now place average life expectancy at more than sixty years. established by science. Epidemics of typhoid fever, yellow fever, scarlet fever, and diphtheria have been checked. 

The death rate from tuberculosis has been reduced by more than fifty per cent.

One of the chief results of our greater knowledge and better observance of the laws of health has been the lengthening of human life and the decrease of the death rate. In 1800 the death rate was thirty-one persons per thousand of the population each year. Today this has been reduced to eleven. Since 1900 the average length of

human life expectancy in the United States has been increased from forty-nine to more than sixty years. 

The Amount of Sickness Today.-Even though much progress has been made, the burden of sickness in our country is still great. The death rate in the United States is higher than in several other countries, indicating the need for more work to protect us against disease. 

During the fall and winter of 1935-36, the United States Public Health Service made a survey to find out the amount of illness among the American people. A total of eight hundred thousand families in all walks of life in eighty-four cities and twenty-three rural counties were visited by trained canvassers. Each family was asked about the cases of illness lasting a week or longer that they had had in the past year. On the basis of the results of this survey certain startling conclusions were drawn for our entire population. It is estimated that on an average day during the winter months, six million persons in the United States are unable to work, attend school, or carry on other usual activities on account of illness or injury from accidents. On an average day for the entire year, four million are disabled by sickness or accidents. Each person loses, on the average, ten days a year from work on account of sickness lasting a week or longer.

Much of this sickness and many of the accidents can be prevented by methods now known to science. If this sickness and these accidents were prevented, nearly two billion dollars a year of lost wages and an equal amount for medical care would be saved; more people would be free from pain and discomfort, and everybody would be happier.

B. HEALTH THROUGH COMMUNITY COOPERATION The Need for Community Cooperation.-Our health is determined to a large extent by the surroundings

HEALTH 

321 in which we live. Disease is caused by germs; their spread must be prevented by community action. Individual effort alone cannot help a great deal. Fresh air, sunlight, pure food and pure water, are all essential in maintaining health and often these are not obtainable except through community action.

Typhoid fever may be caused by germs carried in a community's water supply. Yellow fever may be carried by mosquitoes which breed in near-by swamps and stagnant pools. Babies may be poisoned from milk which comes from unclean cow barns or dirty milk cans. Some diseases, such as scarlet fever, measles, whooping cough, and diphtheria, are contagious. Thus, community action becomes necessary in order to quarantine the victims of such diseases so that they cannot spread them to other persons. 

Finally, it should be said that the prosperity of a community and a nation depend to a large extent upon the health and well-being of its people. Community cooperation for health will bring returns in dollars and cents. 

Government and Health.-The most active agency for promoting health is the government. In our towns and cities the government provides a supply of pure water, inspects milk and other foods, enforces quarantine laws, and provides for the disposal of sewage and garbage. 

Our national government has a Public Health Service, recently placed under the Federal Security Agency, which issues bulletins on health and sanitation. It keeps records of deaths and diseases and cooperates with state and local officials. Agents of the national government examine immigrants in order to prevent those with contagious diseases from entering this country. Every ship entering our harbors from foreign ports must stop for the inspection of its passengers and crew by physicians of the Public Health 

Service.

189 +

The Georgia Tuberculosis Sanatorium, like similar institutions in other states, is administered by the State Department of Health. The Georgia 

State Health Department, in cooperation with the local public health units, also operates a state-wide tuberculosis X-ray clinic service. 

Most of our laws for the promotion of health are made and enforced by the states. Every state has a health department, or board of health, with authority to close theaters, schools, and other public places when this becomes. necessary to stop an epidemic. State boards of health usually keep vital statistics, records of births, deaths, and marriages. Our state governments sometimes maintain clinics to discover and treat certain diseases, and to immunize against preventable diseases such as smallpox, diphtheria, and typhoid.

In our large communities, the local board of health is the most important public agency for the promotion of health. It helps to enforce the state health laws as they apply to the local community. State, county, and city governments help to maintain public hospitals, especially for those persons who are unable to pay for hospital care. 

Perhaps the most important function of the department of health is the education of the people in ways of prevention and control of diseases. Educational material is pre-

HEALTH 

323 

This youngster is being given a general examination at the East Side 

Neighborhood Clinic in New York City. 

7. pared and distributed for use in public schools, colleges, and by the public in general. The radio, the press, motion picture programs, and various other methods are used for the dissemination of scientific health facts. 

Community Activities for Health Protection.- 1. Protection from Contagious Diseases. The laws of all states require that a person with a contagious disease shall be quarantined for a certain length of time. The local health officer places a quarantine card on the home warning persons to keep away; at the same time members of the family who have been exposed to the disease are not permitted to mingle with other persons. Our quarantine laws have done much to prevent epidemics of contagious

diseases. All persons should cooperate with local health officers in the enforcement of these regulations. 

2. Securing Pure Air and Sunshine. Good air is most important to health. The city is not the best place to find pure air as city air is often polluted with carbon monoxide gas from cars, smoke from fires, and dust that is being constantly stirred up in the streets. Dust can be reduced to a minimum by the use of large water tanks which flush the busiest streets. The street sweeper also sprinkles as it sweeps, thus a minimum amount of dust is stirred up. 

The smoke nuisance is regulated in many of our large cities by the use of smokeless fuels, smoke condensers, and smokeless heating apparatus.

Sunlight is another important health agency; it is the deadly enemy of the germs which cause many diseases. 

Building regulations in our large cities control the erection of homes and factories to allow the greatest amount of sunlight possible. 

3. Securing Pure Water. Pure water is another necessity for good health. Impure water may be the carrier of disease germs. Many communities have practically wiped out all cases of typhoid fever by the purification of the water supply. Cities have gone to great costs to have pure water. 

Some drill to great depths to secure it from wells, others get their water supply from rivers, lakes, or mountain streams. Usually the water is purified by filtration through sand and gravel and by treatment with chemicals. New 

York City gets its water through large aqueducts from the Catskill Mountains, which are more than one hundred miles away. Los Angeles brings its water in large pipes some two hundred miles over mountains and through deserts. 

4. Obtaining Pure Food. The national government helps local communities to obtain pure food, but it can make

HEALTH 

325 laws only to control the purity of food products which are interstate commerce, that is, that are shipped from one state to another. The federal government enforces the 

Pure Food and Drug Act which protects people from impure food and drugs, and prohibits misleading labels and the selling of articles containing certain poisons. The United States Department of Agriculture has charge of the inspection of meat sold by the packing houses engaged in interstate commerce. A blue label stamped on the meat indicates that it has been passed by a federal inspector. 

Courtesy Armour and Company 

State and local boards of health usually have some responsibility in the matter of pure food. Milk and meat produced and sold within the state are usually inspected to make sure that they are free from impurities. There is no article of food which carries disease germs so easily as milk. For this reason, farmers who sell milk to the people of our cities must keep their barns clean and must not keep cows that are infected with tuberculosis. 

Most milk that is sold in cities is pasteurized, which means A United States government inspector is examining a beef carcass in one of the large meat packing plants.

that it has been subjected to a temperature of about 145 degrees for a half hour to kill dangerous bacteria. 

Handlers of foods are frequently carriers of diseases, too. Health authorities should, therefore, see that all such persons are well and keep clean. 

5. Disposing of Wastes. 

Proper disposal of wastes is an important health measure. Garbage and rubbish may soon become the source of unpleasant odors and the breeding place of flies and rats. Therefore, communities provide for regular collection and removal of waste materials to some place where they can be burned or changed in form. The by-products from garbage reduction may be put to a variety of uses, one of which is fertilizer. In the country and in many small towns each family is responsible for the disposal of its own wastes. 

They must make sure that garbage does not accumulate. 

These men are building one of the largest sewers in Ohio, at Portsmouth.

It is also necessary that liquid waste, or sewage, be disposed of in a way that will not endanger health. In cities, dwelling houses, apartments, and other buildings are connected with sewage pipes which carry away the waste from toilets and kitchen sinks. A number of towns and cities have sewage disposal plants where sewage is treated

HEALTH 

327 with chemicals and exposed to the air until it becomes harmless. Unfortunately, some communities still insist on dumping sewage into creeks, rivers, and lakes where it becomes a danger to health. In small towns and in the open country, septic tanks and pits are often used for the disposal of sewage.

Schools and

Health. Our schools are active in preventing disease and promoting health. They require the pupils to be vaccinated against smallpox. In many communities, inoculation against diphtheria is recommended by the school authorities. Regular medical inspection of school children helps to discover physical defects, such as decayed teeth, diseased tonsils, adenoids, and poor eyesight. 

It is estimated that three out of four chil 

Courtesy U. S. Public Health Service: 

Photo by Rothstein 

Regular examination of school children helps to discover cases of poor eyesight. dren in the American schools have physical defects which endanger health. It is important that these defects should be found and corrected early in life. It has also been found that a pupil's progress in school may depend a great deal upon his health.

The schools promote health by giving instruction in hygiene. They also require physical education to build strong bodies. 

Restraining Bad Habits.-For their own good, people have to be protected against evil habits that undermine their health and happiness. The use of narcotic drugs not only undermines health but brings much suffering to the victims and their families. The person who becomes subject to such a habit finds it almost impossible to break himself of it. For this reason our government has made laws regulating the sale of these products. A federal law prohibits the sale of all narcotic drugs, except for medicinal purposes. This law is being enforced by the federal 

Bureau of Narcotics which is in the Treasury Department. 

Federal officials are constantly working to protect the people by arresting persons who sell "dope." 

Habitual drunkenness is a great evil, often resulting in ill-health and poverty. The drunken driver today is a threat to the safety of other persons on the streets and highways. Because of these bad effects of the immoderate use of alcoholic beverages, the states and communities regulate their sale. Maine had a prohibition law as early as 1846, and by 1920 thirty-two states had adopted statewide prohibition of the manufacture and sale of alcoholic beverages.

In 1920 prohibition was made nation-wide by the eighteenth amendment to the Constitution. However, serious difficulties in the enforcement of nation-wide prohibition developed and large quantities of liquor were sold by bootleggers. Prohibition accomplished much good for many people, but opposition to the eighteenth amendment, largely because of the difficulty of enforcing it, became so great that in 1933 the twenty-first amendment, repealing the eighteenth, was adopted.

HEALTH 

329 Thus, the control of the manufacture and the sale of alcoholic beverages again became the responsibility of states and local communities. Various types of liquor laws are found in the different states today. Local dealers in liquor are licensed and the conditions under which their business may be conducted is carefully prescribed by law. 

Several states make liquor selling a state monopoly; they own and operate stores where the people may buy bottled liquor. Some states have local option, permitting the people of towns, cities, or counties to decide whether or not they want prohibition.

There is much evidence that many of the evils of the liquor business have returned. A few states and a number of local communities are again considering prohibition. 

No doubt a wise and sane program of temperance education is needed.

C. CARING FOR THE PHYSICALLY AND MENTALLY HANDICAPPED 

The Handicapped. We may consider as handicapped anyone who, because of some physical or mental defect, is unable to get along in life without some special help. This includes the crippled, blind, deaf, feebleminded, and insane. It is estimated that there are about four million defectives in our country. For these people, the national government, the states, and the local communities must provide special care, frequently in hospitals or other institutions. The annual cost of caring for these handicapped persons is estimated at eight hundred million dollars. 

Caring for the Physically Handicapped.-About three hundred thousand children in the United States are crippled as the result of accidents and diseases. Some schools provide special teachers and equipment especially adapted for the use of these children.

It is estimated that there are about eighty-eight thousand blind persons in the United States. Some of these have become blind as a result of accidents and others from 

Both photos: Courtesy

Work Projects Admin istration qo be aw The hands of a blind person are shown reading from a book of Braille. 

The group of blind people are listening to a talking book. disease. Special schools for the blind use the Braille system of reading, which consists of raised lines of dots. 

Deaf persons can be taught to understand speaking by lip reading. All states have special schools for deaf children. 

Caring for the Mentally Handicapped.-Mentally handicapped persons are of two kinds, the feebleminded and the insane. A feebleminded person is one whose mind

HEALTH 

331 

CONSONANT CHART 

Ir 8 ng Se .

The teacher is instructing deaf pupils in lip reading. is not fully developed. An insane person is one whose mind was normal but who is suffering from some mental illness which makes it unsafe for him to be allowed his freedom. There are almost a million people in our country who are mentally handicapped.

The causes of feeblemindedness are heredity, accidents, and certain diseases. There are different levels of intelligence among the feebleminded. The adult moron may not have as much intelligence as a twelve-year old child. The imbecile has a mental age of not more than eight years. 

An idiot is practically helpless and does not pass the mental age of two or three years. Feebleminded persons should not be permitted to marry, because children of such parents usually are feebleminded. Feeblemindedness cannot be cured. States provide special institutions for these unfortunate people where they are given training in handwork, such as weaving, basket making, cooking, and sewing. A large proportion of the feebleminded are not in

institutions; they are at large in society and are frequently dependent on charity. Some of them become criminals. 

Insanity may be caused by worry, certain kinds of illness, alcoholism, and heredity. Today the insane are regarded as unfortunates who need medical care in institutions provided for that purpose. About one-third of the insane may hope to be cured. In many hospitals for the insane, the inmates do useful work and are provided with some entertainment. The federal government has provided a number of hospitals which care for veterans of the World War whose minds have become impaired. 

D. PAYING FOR THE COSTS OF MEDICAL CARE IN A DEMOCRACY In a Democracy, All People Should Have Proper 

Medical Care.-There can be no real equality of opportunity in our democracy unless all people have the same chance to be healthy and receive proper medical care when they are sick. Community cooperation for health is for the benefit of all the people. One of the serious problems in our country is to provide medical care on a basis of equality for all people who need it. Hospitals, medical service, and dental care should be made available on the basis of need, not on the basis of the ability to pay. 

At the present time, free clinics and free care in hospitals and by physicians, for those who are unable to pay, help the poorer classes of our population, but these are by no means adequate. 

Adequate Medical Care for All Is a Serious Problem. Studies and surveys which have been made prove that at the present time, the best medical, dental, and hospital care is given to families with good incomes because they can afford to pay for it. While physicians take care of a number of "charity" patients, it is also true that

HEALTH 

333 

Courtesy University of Illinois

Periodic dental examinations are a needed part of the health program. 

These children are having their teeth examined in the University of 

Illinois Dental Clinic, Chicago, Illinois. many families hesitate to call a doctor or go to the dentist because they cannot afford to pay. The result of this situation is that the poorer class of people do not receive an adequate amount of medical care. 

According to the United States Public Health Service, more than half the sick in the slums of our cities receive no medical care whatever. It is estimated that only three decaying teeth out of ten now receive dental care; the rest just keep on decaying.

There are facts that show that the poor families need even more medical care than the families of the well-to-do. 

People on relief and others with low incomes have considerably more sickness than those with higher incomes. 

Not only are the families among the poor more frequently sick but their illnesses, on the average, last longer. It is a known fact that the death rate for the ten major dis-

eases is twice as great among families with incomes of less than $1,000 a year than among those with incomes above that figure. There is still a great need for more hospitals, especially in the rural sections of our country. 

How Can This Problem Be Solved?-In order to help solve the problem of more adequate medical care for all classes of our population, the federal and state governments are making greater appropriations than ever before for public health work to fight such diseases as tuberculosis, malaria, and cancer. In some cases the government has also provided funds to give free medical care for those who are unable to pay for such services. 

Some people insist that health insurance is necessary in order to provide more adequate medical care for everybody. Under this plan a group of people join together and hire doctors who will give them medical care when they need it. An individual or family pays so much a month to the insurance organization. From this fund the doctors are paid regular salaries and treat the members of the group whenever called upon. Each person or family that takes part in the plan pays the monthly fee whether sick or well. Group health insurance plans usually provide for hospital bills as well as doctor bills. 

Health insurance may be compulsory or voluntary. In many European countries health insurance is compulsory for certain classes of the population. In England every person who receives less than $1,250 a year is included in the plan. Each insured person pays about nine cents a week out of his wages. A like amount is added by the employer. The government makes an appropriation to make up whatever deficits may occur.

In the United States health insurance is voluntary; there is no government aid, and the costs are paid entirely by the membership in the insurance systems. Various or-

HEALTH 

335 ganizations of this type are now in operation in more than sixty United States cities. Some of these health associations offer hospital service only; others offer complete medical, hospital, and dental service. An outstanding example in the United States of a voluntary health insurance plan is the Group Health Association of Washington, D. C. 

It was started by government employees and now provides service for more than 9,000 members. The dues are $2.00 per month for an adult, and $1.00 per month for children up to 21 years of age, with no additional dues for children in excess of three in the same family. Members get the services of a staff of salaried doctors and nurses, hospital care, X-ray equipment, and clinics.

There are many arguments against health insurance or socialized medicine, especially if it is to be controlled and operated by the government. Such a system might be dominated by politics, and physicians skilled in the arts of politics would get the best positions. Payment in advance for medical service might result in unnecessary visits to doctors and clinics. The most serious argument against group medical care is that, under some plans, it would destroy the personal and confidential relationship which now exists between physician and patient.

Chapter XVII  A. THE IMPORTANCE OF LEISURE TIME ACTIVITIES I OUT OF 3 WORKING HOURS 

10 

Increase in Leisure.-Leisure or spare time is the time we have left after we deduct the hours given to work, sleep, and care of the body. Only recently has it become possible for people who are not wealthy to have leisure. 

The modern worker no longer has to stay at his job from sunup to sundown. The use of more machines makes it possible for factories and mills to produce more goods in a shorter period of time than before. Thus, the working day has been shortened for millions of people, at first to ten hours, and now in most cases to eight or fewer. 9 فعل 5 3 

HAS BECOME LEISURE TIME SINCE 1890 Courtesy National Association of Manufacturers 

More inventions and improved methods of production will in time probably further decrease the length of the working day. At the present time the work schedule in many industries and business establishments provides for five eight-hour days in each week. This makes it possible for the workers to have long week ends, usually from Fri- 339

With the use of vacuum cleaners, electric washers, and labor-saving devices in the kitchen, housework may be done in a few hours. Many of the articles of food and clothing which were formerly made in the home are now purchased ready-made in stores. This gives the homemaker more opportunity for leisure time activities.
