The Importance of Leisure. The nature of much of our work today is such that we need leisure for rest and recreation. A great many people are working at high speed and under intense pressure. It is also true that much of the work in our modern factories is of a monotonous nature, involving a few operations repeated again and again. 

The worker in the assembly line of an automobile factory who does nothing but fasten bolts for eight hours, becomes weary in mind and body. At the end of the day and at the end of the week he needs a change from his work. Business and professional men also need recreation, something to relieve the stress and strain of their work. 

Many people, however, do not know how to spend their leisure time wisely. For some persons leisure time merely means idleness, and idleness spells danger. It makes a great deal of difference whether people spend their leisure hours just drifting into any form of recreation, or whether they

select wholesome activities which benefit them. The misuse of leisure time may result in harm to both the individual and to the community. 

The Meaning of Recreation.-During their leisure time people engage in recreational activities. Any activity which gives real joy to the person who performs it is recreation. Sometimes there may not be much difference between work and play. Some people enjoy their work so much that it may become like play to them. Usually, however, work has to be carried on so continuously for such long periods of time that it is no longer pleasant. It is fun to play baseball, but if one earns his living that way, even playing baseball may become hard work.

The purpose of recreation is to re-create the tired mind and body; it means making over again, or a renewal of strength. Recreation should leave the body and mind rested, renewed, and refreshed, more eager and ready for work. It is important that a leisure time activity should be real recreation and not something which leaves one more tired, more exhausted than before. 

What Is Good Recreation?-In order to get the most fun and greatest good from leisure time activities, they should be selected intelligently. A person should learn to enjoy not just one, but a number of different recreations. 

In this way he can choose those activities which are most suitable to his needs and interests. A good recreation has the following characteristics: 

1. It should be adapted to individual needs. What appeals to one person may not to another. One person may like to collect stamps, another may enjoy playing a game of tennis. A person's recreation should be something quite different from his work. The office worker should seek after exercise in the fresh air; the tired laborer may wish to put on his best clothes and be free from physical

exertion. A good recreation provides for new experiences and thrills, for additional interests and skills, which the individual does not have in his work. Thus it affords va- mi amis 

Courtesy Pennsylvania 

Department of Commerce

Outdoor activities are an ever-popular form of recreation. The Leonard 

Harrison State Park, Pennsylvania, shown in the upper picture, is one of the many new parks being provided for public use. riety in one's living, and provides release from the monotony and the routine of work. 

2. It should afford an opportunity for growth and development. The ideal recreation is an activity which is not easily and quickly mastered, in which there is always something more to learn. Thus, golf may be a better game to play than ping pong, and the violin may be a better musical instrument than the harmonica. No matter whether it is playing a musical instrument, singing in a choir, or the

Three photos:  

Creative hobbies are an absorbing leisure time interest. building of ship models, the individual should always seek to improve his technique, and aim to raise his achievement to a higher level. The possibilities for growth and development in the pursuit of hobbies are almost unlimited. 

The writer once visited the home of a carpenter who had an unusually large collection of Indian arrowheads. It was soon found out that this man had done more than collect arrowheads. His interest along this line had prompted him to study a great deal of Indian lore and history, not only of his own community, but of the entire country. 

3. For many people outdoor recreation is desirable. 

Except for those whose work is outdoors, most people need recreation that requires them to be in the fresh air and sunshine. For the many workers who are kept indoors, the

ideal recreation is an activity which is carried on outdoors. For many people fresh air and sunshine are the best health tonics. For this reason, cities, states, and the federal government are opening more and more parks and public playgrounds, where swimming, boating, hiking, picnicking, and other outdoor activities can be carried on. 

4. Recreation should give people an opportunity to get acquainted with each other. Games of all kinds, dancing, and club activities provide opportunities whereby the individual may develop friendships and learn how to get along with people. The people who get the most fun out of life are usually those who have numerous contacts and associations with others of their own age and interests. 

Playing games, especially, promotes democracy. It has been said that the playground has been the true “melting pot" for the children of many peoples and nationalities who have come to America. 

5. Recreation should provide for participation rather than entertainment. The ideal recreation is one in which a person is actively doing something instead of merely watching or being entertained. Most of us like to be making or doing something, building a model airplane, tending a flower garden, collecting stamps, or playing ball. It would be better if schools tried to get everybody to take part in some form of athletics rather than train a few students to become highly skillful. The system by which a small number of students get all the training while the others merely watch the games is not developing participation. On the contrary, it causes "spectatoritis," a common affliction among many people who always wish to be amused and entertained. Entertainment in itself is not a bad thing, but people who have no other forms of recreation are indeed unfortunate. They have not learned how to get real joy and pleasure out of their leisure time.

The Importance of Hobbies.-Everyone should have a hobby, some interest which he likes especially to follow during his leisure time. A hobby should be clearly distinguished from a fad, which is something that we do because we are governed by public opinion. Hobbies may be divided into four classes: First, designing and creating objects which are made with the hands; second, competitive games and other types of athletics which develop physical growth; third, collecting rocks, stamps, and the like; fourth, seeking and acquiring information from books. 

Some hobbies can be engaged in for twelve months of the year while others are seasonal. Some are expensive and others cost nothing. Some appeal to certain ages while others are of interest to all ages. Collecting stamps is a universal hobby and one that has great educational value. 

The history of America can be learned from stamps alone if properly studied. 

The hobbies of many men have developed into successful businesses. Luther Burbank became intensely interested in plant breeding and gave his entire life to the study of plant life. As a result of his experiments we have many new types of shrubs, fruits, and vegetables. 

Some of the hobbies of our prominent people are most interesting. Franklin D. Roosevelt is interested in stamps, boats, and fishing. Edward, Duke of Windsor, has an unusual hobby for a man. He crochets and knits scarfs and mufflers for pastime. Henry Ford accumulates antiques of all types and spares no expense in seeing that they are properly housed for observation by the public. Bobby 

Jones acquired his interest in golf at the age of six. 

B. SPENDING OUR LEISURE TIME How We Spend Our Leisure Time in America.- 

How do most of the American people spend their leisure

time? According to a survey made by Fortune magazine, the American people prefer these recreations in the following order: 

1. Listening to the radio. 

2. Going to the movies. 

3. Reading magazines and books. 

4. Hunting or fishing. 

5. Watching sporting events. 

6. Reading newspapers. 

7. Playing outdoor games. 

8. Playing cards and indoor games. 

9. Going to legitimate theaters.

One writer claims that six kinds of recreation lead all others in the United States. They are: auto-riding, attending the movies, listening to the radio, dancing, watching athletic contests, and playing games.

Travel.-In no other field has modern invention so completely changed the leisure activities of the American people as in travel. Our country has approximately thirty million automobiles, enough to take the entire population for a ride at one time. A large proportion of the families of America now have a car and auto-riding for pleasure has become one of the chief forms of recreation. More and more people are taking vacations every summer, and the automobile has made travel as a means of spending vacations a common practice. The seashore, the mountains, world's fairs, and places of historical interest attract millions of tourists. Our federal government has a system of national parks which are visited by more than six million people annually. There are also many state parks. 

Many Americans visit Canada and Mexico. 

Even though much interest has been placed on "seeing America first," every spring and summer many thousands of Americans get their passports and buy steamship tickets

for Europe. Famous art galleries, museums, and other places of interest in the leading countries of Europe are visited by these tourists. No other form of recreation is so broadening in experience and outlook as intelligent and well-planned travel.

Movies.-Making and showing motion pictures is one of the largest industries in our country, representing a total investment of approximately two billion dollars. 

There are about one hundred million paid admissions to our movie theaters every week. How many different persons attend every week is not known, but the number is close to ninety million. Probably more than half of the young people and adults of the nation attend the movies each week.

The moving picture has brought about a great change in our leisure time activities. In the past people often furnished their own amusement and entertainment. Today many millions of Americans pay to be entertained in our movie houses; they listen and observe rather than participate. Most movies are made to entertain people, not to instruct them. As a rule producers avoid "problem" pictures and try not to show anything that might arouse controversy.

In the dictator countries, movies are a part of the huge propaganda machine operated to glorify the government and arouse the people in favor of what it is doing. In our country movies do not take sides in any of the great controversies over political and social issues. During a presidential campaign the news reels aim to be fair to all the leading candidates by giving each of them an equal amount of space and time. This is as it should be in a democracy. 

Most of our motion pictures have been made for adults, yet a large number of the regular movie goers are children. 

Investigations of the effect of movies on children have

SUN MON CARLOT OF BAR 2 CASET OF BAR 20 Courtesy Farm Security Administration: Photo by Lee 

Children should be guided in their choice of movies. shown that some pictures are too exciting and frighten children and may disturb their sleep for several nights. 

There has also been some evidence to show that children have been influenced to commit crime because of what they saw in the movies. 

One of the problems facing America today is how to get better motion pictures. Cities and states have established boards of censors which eliminate scenes they consider objectionable. The movies and the radio are notable in that both are free from the use of profanity. Certain organizations have attempted to arouse public opinion to demand better pictures. In 1934 one of the large churches started a campaign for cleaner pictures and informed its members about pictures that were undesirable. The National Congress of Parents and Teachers influences parents to choose pictures wisely for their children.

Our motion pictures can be improved if we can educate the people so that they will demand the best. Movies are made for profit and the success of a picture is determined by its popularity as indicated by the number of paid ad- 

Lone With A GONE WIT THE WIND Courtesy Metro Goldwyn Picture Corporation 

Going to the movies is one of the American public's favorite leisure time activities. missions. Historical pictures, stories about the lives of great literary figures and prominent scientists, have been among the better pictures recently produced. 

A highly educational and entertaining series in the movies is the monthly March of Time feature. Motion pictures can be a great aid in learning. The development of the 16mm. projector has made possible the wide use of motion pictures in teaching. Many schools now use educational films as a part of their regular classwork. 

The Radio.-The radio, like the movies has become one of our big industries. In 1942 it was estimated that in the United States there were more than 59 million receiving sets distributed among 87 per cent of the American

homes. There are more than eight hundred broadcasting stations and four or five national chains through which the people of the entire nation can listen to a program at the same time. We pay for radio programs when we buy any of the products which are so widely advertised over the radio. 

Courtesy Baylor University, Waco, Texas

Radio is not only a "listening" activity. Members of this radio class are rehearsing a production to be given in 

"The Baylor Little Theatre of the Air." 

America is one of the few countries in the world where the radio stations are not owned and operated by the government. Control on the part of our government is limited to regulation by the Federal Communications Commission which grants licenses to broadcasting stations and determines the transmission power and the wave length for each. In England every owner of a radio set pays a tax of about $2.40 a year. The money from this tax on receiving sets is used to provide programs for broadcasting. This system has an advantage in that radio programs do not have to be interrupted by advertising. A compara-

tive study of English and American radio broadcasting, however, shows that American programs are far superior in quality and in variety. There is a real danger in government ownership of radio in a democracy. In times of an emergency, it becomes easier for selfish political leaders to use the radio for propaganda, as is now being done in the dictator countries. 

The radio has profoundly influenced American life. 

Large numbers of people listening to the same programs, help to make the country more united. The speeches of political leaders and the programs of important national events can be heard by almost everybody, just as if the entire country were one small town. Fine music, operas, and plays can be enjoyed by millions in their homes. 

Both the movies and the radio are sometimes criticized because they provide no opportunity for active participation. But the radio and the movies have brought about a great increase in the interest in music. A writer in the 

New York Times recently stated, "A quarter of a century ago musicians in this country might be reckoned in the hundreds; today they must be estimated in the thousands. Audiences were calculated in the thousands; today they are counted in the millions. . . . 

Hundreds of sym phony orchestras are scattered all over the country." 

Athletics. The importance of athletics among the leisure time activities is reflected in the huge attendance at athletic games and contests. Every fall millions of 

Americans shout themselves hoarse at football games, and every spring hundreds of thousands cheer or jeer at professional baseball games. Receipts at football games have more than tripled since 1920. The paid admissions to the major league baseball games reaches from twelve to fifteen millions annually. 

When we think of the millions of people who spend

Crowds such as this have given the American people the reputation of having sport "spectatoritis." money to sit and watch athletic contests, we can well understand why the American people have been accused of "spectatoritis." Today there is a new interest in active participation in athletics. The number of golf courses has increased eightfold since 1916. Increasing numbers of people are actively participating in golf, tennis, swimming, hunting, fishing, boating, music, dramatics, the arts and the crafts. Thousands of men play in twilight and industrial leagues. High schools and colleges are offering a greater variety of sports and more attention is being given to games played between teams within the school so that more students may take part. 

There is a renewed interest in athletics among the people in many countries of the world, as is indicated by the keen competition in the Olympic games which are held every four years. This great sporting event is international in character and brings together many hundreds of athletes from all parts of the world.

C. COMMUNITY COOPERATION FOR RECREATION 

Education for Leisure.-There is no doubt that more training for leisure time activities is needed. There are many persons who do not get real happiness and satisfaction from their recreation; there are others who engage in leisure time activities which are harmful to them and to their communities.

The best time to train people in the wise use of leisure is during youth. Children should be taught to enjoy the world of nature, to learn water sports, and to play games of various kinds. Young people should be given an opportunity to learn to play an instrument, to paint, to model, and to develop a liking for such activities as hiking and picnicking. A number of organizations, such as the Boy Scouts and the Campfire Girls with their summer camps, perform a valuable service in providing leisure time activities for young people. If an interest in a variety of sports and activities is well begun in youth, it will continue through life. If it is not begun in youth, it may not develop at all, for grownups do not like to learn as beginners.

Schools train boys and girls in leisure time activities through courses in music, art, shopwork, and through numerous clubs which seek to encourage the development of hobbies. In school, pupils should learn to enjoy good books, painting, sculpture, and music. The appreciation of good authors is one of the greatest assets that any person can have. The enjoyment of good books never costs a great deal of money and can be a source of lasting satisfaction.

Community Aids to Recreation. Because they realize the importance of wholesome recreation for the people, our cities, states, and the federal government are spend-

Outdoor swimming pools provide an opportunity for healthful outdoor water sports for many people. ing a considerable amount of money for playgrounds, parks, libraries, art galleries, and museums. Most cities have commissions or departments of recreation to plan and supervise such activities. Sometimes a wealthy, publicspirited citizen will help a community in which he was born or where he made his money, by giving a park, a library, or an art gallery for public use. 

In recent years there has been a great increase in the number of playgrounds in American cities. Equipment usually includes apparatus such as swings, slides, and sand piles. A director who looks after the equipment and teaches children how to get the most out of play is in charge. Playgrounds should be located within easy walking distance of the homes of the children who are going to use them. 

Because many cities were laid out before the importance of playgrounds was recognized, it is often difficult today to secure space for this need in each neighborhood.

Courtesy St. Louis Chamber of Commerce

The St. Louis Art Museum is located almost in the center of the 1,400 

acres of Forest Park. 

Almost every large city now has one or more settlement houses, each located in a crowded area, which help to make life more pleasant for the poorer people in the community. These institutions are modeled after the famous 

Hull House in Chicago, founded by Jane Addams in 1889. 

A settlement house usually has reading rooms, classrooms, a workshop, a gymnasium, and perhaps a theater. 

Most cities have a number of public parks. Some are quite small and have flower beds, grass plots, and trees, which provide rest and relief for those who are weary from tramping the city streets. There are other parks which have baseball diamonds, tennis courts, swimming pools, and provisions for picnicking.

Libraries are usually wholly or partly supported by public taxation. The library in a large city has numerous branches located in different neighborhoods for the convenience of the people. There are a few county libraries which serve the people in rural communities. The bookmobile is an automobile or truck filled with library books which makes regular calls at farm homes.

Art galleries and museums are provided at public expense in our larger cities. Among the great art galleries in the country are the Metropolitan Museum of Art in New York, the Corcoran Gallery and the National Gallery in Washington, the City Art Museum in St. Louis, the Museum of Fine Arts, Boston, and the Academy of 

Fine Arts in Philadelphia. The most outstanding museums are the American Museum of Natural History in New 

York, the Field Museum in Chicago, the Commercial Museum and the Franklin Institute in Philadelphia, the Agassiz Museum at Harvard University, and the Smithsonian Institution in Washington.

State and National Parks.-In order to provide recreation areas outside of our cities, the states and the federal government have established a number of parks. 

These areas are usually selected for their natural wonders and beauty. They consist of many acres of woodland, mountains, lakes, geysers, waterfalls, and other scenic marvels. Special areas of these parks are developed for camp sites, playgrounds, and picnic grounds. 

At the present time there are twenty-seven national parks covering an area greater than that of the state of Maryland. They are under the direction and control of the 

National Park Service of the United States Department of the Interior. One outstanding feature is the guide and lecture service offered to tourists who visit the parks. All the national parks are vast refuges where native wild life is protected and allowed to multiply undisturbed. The national parks have become "the great summer playgrounds" of our country. Millions of people visit them every summer and return home rested and refreshed. 

Our first great national parks were located in the Rocky Mountain and Pacific Coast states. They include the 

Grand Canyon National Park with the greatest example

Courtesy U. S. Department of the Interior 

These horseback riders are enjoying the beauty of Yellowstone 

Falls, Yellowstone National Park. 

of erosion in the world; General Grant Park and Sequoia 

National Park in California with their giant trees; Yosemite, Yellowstone, and Mt. Rainier with their scenic beauty. During recent years the federal government has developed three parks in the East: Shenandoah National 

Park, Virginia; the Great Smoky Mountains National

Park, North Carolina and Tennessee; and Acadia off Maine.

Leisure Time Activities in a Democracy.-Under a democracy the government exists for the welfare of all the people and aims to preserve equality of opportunity. 

It gives every citizen the chance to make the most of himself, so that he may live the happiest and most useful life of which he is capable. We have studied some of the provisions for public recreation which are made by our government. Public provisions for recreation are truly democratic in that they provide the same opportunities

Courtesy U. S. Department of the Interior 

This party is vacationing in Acadia National Park, Maine. for all. The rich and poor alike visit our public playgrounds, libraries, museums, and art galleries. The automobile has made it possible for millions of people to visit our national parks and other places of interest. 

We have also seen how the radio and the motion pictures have done a great deal to bring more opportunities for recreation within the reach of all the people. However, the great job of providing more worth-while leisure time. activities for all is not yet completed. Commercial recreations, movies, dance halls, and the like, which are operated for the profit of the owners, must be supervised so that they will bring no harm to the people who attend them. In the future the government must continue to work hand in hand with such voluntary organizations as the 

Boy Scouts, Girl Scouts, 4-H Clubs, Y. M. C. A., Y. W.

C. A., and other civic and church groups, to provide a fuller and richer life for the people. Nothing will pay greater dividends in terms of good citizenship. More worth-while leisure time activities for everybody will mean a decrease in crime, divorce, and other social evils.

SUMMARY 

Leisure time activities are increasingly important today because working days are shorter are important because people need rest and relaxation from their work 

Recreation should recreate the mind and body be adapted to individual needs afford the individual an opportunity to grow and develop in many cases provide for outdoor activities give people an opportunity to get acquainted with each other provide participation rather than entertainment consist of hobbies 

Our leisure time is spent in many different kinds of activities, including travel, seeing movies, listening to the radio, watching and playing athletic games Communities provide for recreation through education and training of young people in the wise use of leisure by providing playgrounds, parks, settlement houses, libraries, art galleries, and museums In a democracy the government and voluntary organizations provide equality of opportunity for all by making recreational facilities available for everybody.

Chapter XVIII  A. SAFETY FROM ACCIDENTS The Accident Problem.-Every hour during 1938, eleven persons in the United States met death by accident and about one thousand others were injured. In addition to the sorrow which resulted from these deaths and injuries there was an actual money loss of three hundred eighty thousand dollars. The daily losses added up to two hundred sixty deaths, twenty-four thousand five hundred injuries, and a cost of nine million dollars. The number of American soldiers who met death in World War I is only about one third greater than the number of persons killed by accidents in one year. Only two diseases, heart disease and cancer, cause more deaths than accidents. 

The outlook for reducing the number of accidents is hopeful. Because of the united effort of schools, automobile clubs, insurance companies, and the government, the number of accidents is now slowly decreasing each year. 

The practice of safety rules is important for everybody. 

It is no fun to be hurt and there is nothing heroic about being laid up with a broken leg from an accident which might have been avoided. Accidents cause loss of life, time, and money; they bring pain and suffering. Sometimes the victim of an accident may become crippled for life. When the head of a family is seriously injured or killed, the community may have to provide for the family. Safety rules for the individual are important, but safety, like health and recreation, is a community problem. 363

The National Safety Council calls our attention to the importance of accident prevention in the following statement: "Roaring, raging floods swept down on the Ohio River Valley and through the New England states during March, 1936. The eyes of the nation were focussed on a terrific demonstration of nature on a rampage. The newspaper, the radio, and the movie carried the drama of flood waters to a waiting people in every part of the country. Lasting about a week, the floods accounted for a death toll of approximately two hundred. "During that same week, however, seven times as many lives were snuffed out by ordinary accidents. Nothing spectacular about most of them; no mighty exhibition of well-nigh ungovernable forces. For the most part they were just the result of a moment's thoughtlessness, a bit of undue haste, a useless chance taking. Engineers will be called upon-and rightly so-to use their best talents in trying to avoid a recurrence of the 1936 flood disaster. With even greater justification, the National Safety 

Council calls upon every American citizen to use his skill, his common sense, and his caution, to help lower the flood of accidents which is now killing us off at the rate of 

100,000 a year!" 

Where Do Accidents Occur?-The number of automobile accidents every year is large, but there are also many accidents in the home, on the farm, and in industry. 

People are sometimes injured or killed while they are at play. It is surprising to note from the figures given on page 

365 that the number of fatal accidents in the home is slightly greater than the number of persons killed by automobiles.

The four principal classifications of accidental deaths for 1942 are as follows:

Home

Motor Vehicle * 

Occupational Public

30,000 28,200 18,500 14,000 90,700 *There is a duplication of approximately 3,000 in the motor vehicle and Occupational figures. 

Safety in the Home.-According to the accident records, the home is the most dangerous place of all. Upon more careful study, however, the record does not seem to be quite as bad as the figures indicate. We must remember that there are more people in the home and that they spend more time there than in other places. The home must care for all persons, including the very young, the very old, and the invalids, who are likely to have more accidents.

Three little children saw a group of excited people on their lawn, and ran to see what was the matter. They found their mother lying unconscious, with neighbors working over her. Her clothing had been on fire, and she evidently was badly burned. Inside the kitchen there was further evidence of the fire which had been extinguished by neighbors. This is what the three children learned. 

Their mother had used gasoline to start a fire. There was an explosion and the mother was badly burned and disfigured for life.

Fires should never be started by using gasoline or kerosene. When gas is used for cooking or heating, care must be exercised. Electrical appliance cords should always be repaired when the insulation wears off. More accidents happen in the kitchen than in any other part of the home since more activities involving risks are carried on here. 

Falls from ladders, in bathtubs, and on stairways are

everyday accidents in the home. Steps and stairs should be well-lighted and kept free from all objects. Smooth, hard, well-polished floors are accident hazards. Small rugs which slip easily should be "anchored" with proper pads. Curled edges of large rugs may cause a fall. 

More falls in the home take place in bedrooms than anywhere else. They happen to people who get out of bed and try to find their way about in the dark. A flashlight, a bed light, or an electric switch should be within easy reach of the bed. 

Safety on the Farm.-Farming is an occupation of many risks. Some of the accidents on farms come from dealing with animals. Because of construction and repair work, there is always the need to guard against falls. 

Sharp and pointed tools, such as saws, axes, and pitchforks, must be handled with care. Dangling pieces of clothing around machinery in motion are accident hazards. 

Heavy tractors have been the cause of a number of deaths and many more injuries. All power machinery on the farm should be equipped with guards and the operators should be skilled in their work. 

Safety While We Play.-Playground swings, slides, seesaws, and giant strides should be well constructed. On properly equipped and well-supervised playgrounds there are comparatively few accidents. It is estimated that there are one hundred seventy thousand accidents in high school athletics each year. These may be due to improper leadership and to faulty equipment. 

Recent years have seen a great increase in a joyous and health-giving sport, bicycling. However, the increased number of bicycles on our public streets and highways constitutes an accident hazard. The bicycle rider must realize that, like the drivers of other vehicles, he must obey all traffic signals and regulations. The safest place for the

A knowledge of lifesaving prevents many deaths from drowning. bicycle rider is close to the right-hand curb. Bicycles should be equipped with a suitable bell or horn and, for night riding, should carry a reflector or red light in the rear. The steering mechanism and the brakes should be kept in good order at all times. 

All forms of water sports may result in serious accidents unless those who participate can swim. Fortunately deaths by drowning are declining, probably because more people are learning how to swim. Small boats and canoes should be used only by expert swimmers. It is wise to swim only where there are lifeguards; no one should swim alone. 

Every year during the Fourth of July season some persons are needlessly killed or injured by the careless use of fireworks. Burns, lacerations, bad tears in the flesh, and blindness are sometimes caused by these holiday accidents. 

Fortunately, many states and communities now prohibit

the sale of dangerous fireworks. Public opinion is beginning to insist on "safe and sane" Fourth of July celebrations. A good substitute for the careless and dangerous use of fireworks by individuals is a community display which is strictly supervised by competent persons. 

Automobile Accidents.-Headlines such as these may be read almost every day in our newspapers:

JOHN BEEBER KILLED IN AUTO MISHAP 

Engineer at Lukenweld

Plant Loses His Life FIVE INJURED 

IN 

SERIES OF CAR CRASHES 

Two Drivers Fined After

Autos Are Wrecked MOTOR WRECKED 

Police Believe Driver Fell 

Asleep at Wheel

Behind each one of these headlines is a tragic story of needless suffering and grief.

The automobile with its increasing daily use, its highpowered engine and speed, has become the center of discussion in our safety program. Automobile accidents account for about one-third of all the accidental deaths. It is estimated that motor vehicle accidents in the United States cost two billion dollars annually. Every driver pays a portion of this expense through increased insurance rates. With a constant increase in the number of cars on the road each year, the accident hazards grow greater and greater. It is claimed that at the present rate one out of twenty persons will be killed or injured in an automobile accident during the next five years.

"Our nation has engaged in six major wars excepting World War II since its birth in 1776. In total these wars extended over a period of fifteen years. The number of 

American soldiers killed in action or who died of wounds during these fifteen years of war was 244,357 as against an estimated total of 501,228 traffic deaths, 1928-1942, on American highways in fifteen years of peace." (Travelers 

Insurance Company)

It is encouraging to note, however, that recent years have seen a considerable reduction in automobile fatalities. The high point in the number of deaths on our streets and highways was reached in 1941 when the figure was 40,000. But the year 1942 saw a great improvement when the number of traffic deaths was 28,200. 

As one might expect, because of the greater amount of traffic, the number of accidents is greatest on Saturdays, Sundays, and holidays. Death lurks on the highway twenty-four hours of the day, but the greatest number of fatalities occur during the late afternoon and early evening. Contrary to our general impression, most serious automobile accidents do not happen on the crowded city streets, but on the open highway in rural territory. Drivers of all age groups are involved in accidents, but the records show that young people in their teens have the greatest percentage of accidents. The record for those between the ages of 20 and 30 is also discouraging. Real driving wisdom seems to begin at 30. 

Children between the ages of 5 and 14 are less frequently involved in traffic accidents than any other age group. The school child rate is not only the lowest now, but it has actually decreased 15 per cent since 1922. Most people attribute this splendid record to the safety education which has been carried on in our schools.

Prepared for Traveler's Insurance Company accidents do not just happen; they could have been prevented. According to the National Safety Council the causes of traffic accidents are as follows: 

1. Highway deficiencies. There are still many narrow places, sharp curves, blind corners, unexpected railroad

crossings, bad bridges, and unstandardized signs on our streets and highways. Conditions like these are responsible for many accidents. The careful, skillful driver rarely has an accident even on a defective highway. 

2. Vehicle defects. Car defects are responsible for about 

15 per cent of the traffic accidents. Defective brakes, poor headlights, glaring headlights, defective tires, lack of chains on slippery roads, all make driving more dangerous. 

The careful, skillful driver can generally avoid accidents even if his vehicle is somewhat defective. 

3. Shortcomings of drivers. The shortcomings of the driver are the most important cause of automobile accidents. According to some estimates, the driver is responsible for 90 per cent of all the deaths on our streets and highways. One of our greatest enemies of safety today is the drunken driver. A New York traffic court judge states his studied opinion that 40 per cent of all traffic accidents involve liquor and a Chicago traffic court judge places the figure at 60 per cent. Official figures given out by the states, however, are not nearly as high. Other defects of drivers which cause accidents are: poor eyesight, lack of bodily control, and fatigue. Other drivers have accidents because they do not know how to drive. They do not know the proper methods of making turns, backing, signaling, and they do not observe the right-of-way rules, stop signs, or traffic signals.

The most serious mistake of drivers is too much speed. 

In one sense every motor vehicle accident occurs because somebody was traveling "too fast for conditions." A driver should always keep in mind the distance that it will take him to stop at different speeds. All accident statistics show that "the higher the speed, the worse the accident." 

The greatest number of drivers have accidents because they are not sufficiently safety conscious. They take

chances by going too fast; they pass other cars on hills and curves; they cross railroad tracks without looking; they do not slow down at intersections; they fail to signal when they wish to turn or stop; they fail to stop at traffic lights and stop signs. 25 23 21 19 17 15 10 20 30 40 50 60 

MILES PER GALLON MILES PER HOUR 

Courtesy The Atlantic Refining Company

Safe driving saves money. As shown in the graph, the average car gives its best mileage per gallon of gasoline at safe driving speeds and the mileage decreases as speed is increased. 

4. Mistakes of Pedestrians. Two out of every five traffic deaths involve pedestrians, and many of these are the result of carelessness on the part of the pedestrians themselves. Children sometimes play in the street either because they have no better place to play or because they do not know any better. Adults often take foolish chances by crossing a street in mid-block, or against the signal, or when intoxicated-with disastrous results. Others will walk diagonally in street intersections. Some pedestrians have the "I dare you to hit me" attitude toward the driver.

"Clover leaf" and "traffic circles," such as shown in the above picture of a section of the West Side Express Highway in New York, are designed to eliminate dangerous intersections.

How Can Automobile Accidents Be Prevented?— 

That many of our traffic accidents can be prevented is now a well-established fact. During the year 1937 fortyfive states brought the number of deaths from traffic accidents below the record for the previous year. In twenty states the reduction was 20 per cent or more. Some cities have had notable success in reducing the number of traffic

accidents. Evanston, Illinois, is a good example of what can be done. This city reduced its motor vehicle death rate from 8 per thousand in 1934 to 2.9 per thousand in 1935. 

There is no one easy way to solve the problem of automobile accidents. Progress is dependent on the wholehearted interest and cooperation of everybody. The mayor of Evanston, Illinois, gives the remedy for the traffic accident problem in the form of three E's: Engineering. Education. Enforcement. 

1. Engineering. Engineers are constantly studying ways of making our streets and highways safer. Both cars and highways are being constructed to meet definite standards. "Clover leaf" and "traffic circles" are designed to eliminate dangerous intersections. Sharp curves are being eliminated.

The newer roads are being built upon a dual highway plan, with lanes for traffic moving in opposite directions, separated by a plot of ground ten or more feet wide. In some rural areas, where there are many pedestrians, sidewalks along the highways are being provided. 

2. Education. Safety education is a means toward the solution of the problem of traffic accidents. This means education of children in school, and of adults in all walks of life. Some high schools offer special courses for students who wish to get their drivers' licenses. This course includes a study of the motor vehicle itself, and the rules and principles of safe driving. In some schools it has been possible to give boys and girls actual experience in driving an automobile under the guidance of a trained instructor. 

Safety education for adults must be a united effort which makes use of newspapers, magazines, radio, and posters. Insurance companies, automobile clubs, and the 

National Safety Council are leaders in this movement.

Some states require all drivers of automobiles to pass a driving test before being licensed. This helps to keep incompetent drivers off the road.

Junior safety patrols, sponsored by local automobile clubs, have been organized in many schools. Members of these organizations wear badges or other insignia and assist local traffic policemen in helping children to cross streets at intersections and to walk safely on the highways. 

3. Enforcement. Obedience to law is necessary to promote safety on our highways.

All except four states require drivers of automobiles to be licensed. In some states prospective drivers are given a test in practical driving and on their knowledge of the rules of the highway. Serious infractions of the law on the part of the driver may result in either the temporary or permanent loss of his license. By these methods incompetent drivers are kept off the road. Because a defective automobile may be as dangerous as a defective driver,

Cynumber of accidents where such crossings must be made. some states and cities require all motorists to bring in their cars for inspection at regular intervals. 

Traffic rules and regulations concerning speed, manner of passing other cars, turning at intersections, and the like, are usually enforced by the state police on the highways and by the local police in the cities. Police also direct traffic and help to prevent jams at crowded intersections. Whenever possible, police officials try to reach the scene of an accident immediately. It is important to remember that the purpose of the traffic officer is to enforce traffic laws which have been made for your safety. A number of states

are now finding that an increase in the number of enforcement officials on the highways has resulted in a marked decrease in the number of accidents. 

One of the worst evils in the enforcement of traffic laws. has been "fixing tickets." A person who receives a summons for a motor vehicle violation, and then proceeds to have the "ticket fixed," is interfering definitely with the reduction of the highway accident toll. Public indignation is rising against the man, who, to save a few dollars, will stoop to underhanded conniving of this sort. The chronic violator of the law who boasts, "I can get it fixed," does not inspire admiration. Instead he arouses visions of serious accidents which may result from his lawlessness. 

The pendulum is swinging. Informed and sensible motorists are beginning to frown upon "ticket-fixing." The feeling grows that "It's Smart to Obey the Law." 

Pedestrian Safety.-Some of us may be drivers of automobiles, but all of us are pedestrians some of the time. 

It is important, therefore, that we learn to obey those rules which are for our own safety. The following rules for the pedestrian are taken from Highway Safety Text book for High Schools: 

I. Cross at the corner whenever possible. 

2. If there is a traffic signal in operation, cross only when it indicates GO. 

3. Look both ways before you cross. 

4. Never enter the street between parked cars. 

5. Play games away from traffic. 

6. Be doubly alert during rain and snow. 

7. Beware of vehicles backing up. 

8. Use care in boarding or alighting from trolley cars and buses. 

9. On rural roads always walk on the left side facing traffic.

Bicycle Safety. Since 1932 the use of bicycles has almost tripled. Bicycle riders should know and obey the traffic regulations in their communities. In most cities they apply to bicycles as well as to cars. Some important rules for bicycle safety are: Stop for all stop signs and traffic lights. Ride only on the right side of the street in a straight line. Use hand signals to let other drivers know what you are going to do. Keep your bicycle in good conditionbrakes, steering, tires, handlebars.

Safeguarding the Traveler. Our state and national governments protect again accidents those who travel in public conveyances by land, sea, and air. 

The Interstate Commerce Commission has provided for strict enforcement of national regulations concerning mechanical defects on our railroads. Boilers, brakes, and tracks must undergo rigid inspection. The traveler by water is also protected by the national government. The 

Bureau of Marine Inspection and Navigation of the De partment of Commerce inspects engines and boilers of ships. Passenger ships are required to have an adequate number of life preservers and lifeboats to take care of all the passengers. Buoys, lighthouses, and lightships are provided to guide ships in dangerous channels. The United 

States Coast Guard patrols our coast and sends boats and planes to the rescue of ships that are in distress. It operates about 200 lifeboat stations and its personnel is trained for duty in any emergency.

New governmental activities encourage air travel and increase its safety. The United States Civil Aeronautics 

Administration licenses pilots and mechanics, and operates numerous airport traffic-control towers. It maintains beacon lights which guide the pilots and has a complete weather-reporting service which warns pilots in advance of unfavorable flying conditions.

Inspection of Buildings for Safety. A building which is not well constructed or which is made of faulty materials may collapse and cause serious injury to a number of people. For this reason cities and states make regulations which set up standards for building construction. Persons who wish to erect new buildings must submit plans and specifications, and secure permits before they can begin construction. A building inspector, appointed by the city, sees to it that the building is constructed in accordance with the regulations of the city. In the case of public buildings, such as theaters and schools, adequate fire exits must be provided. From time to time inspectors also check on the condition of buildings and condemn those that are unsafe. Elevators in public buildings are checked by inspectors who make sure that they are safe for public use.

B. SAFETY FROM FIRES 

Facts About Fires.-The total money loss from fires in a single year is approximately three hundred million dollars. This is close to one million dollars a day. In addition to the money loss every year, thousands of people lose their lives and others are injured on account of fires. Every minute of the day and night there is some serious fire. 

The buildings destroyed by fire in the United States in a single year, if placed next to each other, would line both sides of a street leading from New York to Chicago. 

The United States has the worst fire record of any country in the world. Our fire loss in 1936 was $2.05 for each man, woman, and child. In the same year the fire loss in England was $1.03, in Germany $.32, and in France $.45. The greater loss from fires in the United States may be partially explained by the fact that more of our buildings are made of wood and other inflammable materials,

while most European buildings are of brick, stone, or similar materials. The chief reason for our great fire loss, however, is our carelessness. The observance of simple rules of fire prevention would prevent many fires. 

Who Pays for Fire Losses?-When a building is destroyed as a result of fire, the owner is paid for his loss if the property is insured. But all of us have to pay for the large fire loss because of the increased insurance rates which are a result of it. The greater the loss from fires in a community, the higher the insurance rates, for the cost of insurance is determined by the amount of the risk involved. Thus, there is a direct financial gain for all the people of a community if they succeed in reducing the losses from fires, because the insurance companies will lower the rates for buildings in that area. 

A high fire loss in a community also brings about the need for much costly fire-fighting apparatus which has to be paid for out of public funds. Thus, everybody will have to pay higher taxes. Fire prevention programs will pay dividends in dollars and cents for everybody.

Preventing Fires.-In order to know how to prevent fires we must know something about the conditions which cause them. A fire may be the result of any one of a number of causes. Stoves should not be set too close to the wall, or if this cannot be avoided, the wall should be protected with some fireproof material. Stovepipes which pass through wooden walls or floors should be carefully insulated. Rubbish, wooden boxes, kindling, excelsior, and old newspapers are the most frequent causes of destructive fires. A stray cigarette, a cigar stub, or a spark may readily set such materials on fire. Rubbish should not be allowed to accumulate in the home. Since many fires start from the careless use of kerosene, gasoline, or various cleaning fluids, such liquids should be used only outdoors. Ashes

from the furnace in the basement or the stove in the kitchen should never be placed in wooden boxes. Electric irons and other electric equipment should be turned off when not in use. Electric cords should be kept in good repair.

Buildings should be constructed in such a manner as to reduce fire hazards. 

Whenever possible, fireproof materials, such as stone, brick, or concrete, should be used. Defective chimneys and flues cost the

American people millions of dollars of fire loss every year. Sparks on the roof often cause fires. The use of metal, tile, or slate shingles prevents fires from this source. An open stairway or elevator shaft may

 cause a fire to spread This fireman is checking over the quickly. These should be enclosed with masonry and board of a fire alarm system. have metal doors at each floor. Automatic sprinkler systems, installed in the ceilings of buildings, are an effective safeguard. When a fire starts, these pipes open automatically and sprinkle water over the floor. 

One of the most important duties of the state and local governments is to make regulations concerning fire hazards in public buildings. In theaters the curtains must be made of asbestos to comply with fire rules, and all exits must be plainly marked and lighted. Exit doors in schools,

theaters, factories, and other public places must open outward. Motion picture theaters are required to project pictures from fireproof booths. Public schools are required to have regular fire drills so that pupils are trained how to leave the building in an orderly manner. 

What to Do in Case of a Fire.-When a fire occurs in your home, it is important to remember a few simple rules which may help you save lives and property: 

1. Keep calm. It is, of course, necessary to think fast and act quickly, but many persons become so excited that they fail to do the simple things that should be done. 

2. Turn in the alarm. In many cases this may be done by telephone. All cities, however, have a fire-alarm system. In each neighborhood there are small boxes on posts from which the alarm can be sent in. You should know in advance how the fire-alarm boxes in your neighborhood are operated. 

3. Use the fire extinguisher, if you have one. Remember that most extinguishers must be inverted before they will work. Direct the stream of chemicals from the extinguisher at the base of the flame. If no extinguisher is available, use water. But do not use water on an oil fire. 

4. If it is a small fire, it can be smothered by a rug, coat, or carpet. 

5. If your clothing is on fire, do not run, because this will increase the flames. Get a rug or a heavy cloth and wrap yourself in it to smother the flames. 

What the Community Does to Fight Fires.-What happens when a fire breaks out? In former times the neighbors tried to help put it out. Placing themselves in a line reaching from the water to the fire, they formed a bucket brigade. Buckets of water were then passed along the line as speedily as possible and poured on the flames. 

This procedure was usually not very effective and not

much could be done to prevent a large fire from spreading. 

In some instances whole cities burned. 

The more efficient fighting of fires began after the invention of pumping engines, hose carts, and other equipment. The development of a city water system was also a great aid in fighting fires. At first this equipment was used by volunteer fire companies. Day or night, when an alarm sounded, men from these companies rushed to the fire and tried to put it out. Membership in these companies was considered a social and a civic honor. Today many of our smaller communities are still protected from fires by volunteer companies. Sometimes the local government employs a few full-time firemen and purchases the equipment.

Our large cities employ professional fire fighters who are on duty at all times. They are organized like an army with a chief or marshal at the head. Rigid discipline and organization are necessary to insure efficient and prompt action when a fire starts. City fire departments today have equipment of every kind to fight fires. Powerful engines are used to furnish a strong and steady stream of water under high pressure. The water tower, which consists of an iron pipe arranged like a telescope so that it can be extended or shortened, is used to fight fires in high buildings, and extension ladders enable firemen to reach the upper windows. Chemicals which drive oxygen away from the blaze are used to fight small fires. Gas masks are used by firemen when they enter burning buildings. 

Forest Fires. Every year thousands of acres of forest lands are devastated by raging fires. This is a great loss not only because of the destruction of valuable timber and buildings, but because forests are so important to soil and water conservation. When large areas of forest land are burned over, the water from heavy rains will flow down

Courtesy U. S. Forest Service 

This patrolman is using the Forest Service field radio set. The loss from forest fires is greatly reduced by the use of lookout and patrol systems for reporting fires. quickly over steep hillsides and carry with it much of the fertile top soil. The destruction of so many of our forests has increased the hazards from floods. 

Forest fires may be caused by lightning, but careless smokers who throw away burning cigarettes or lighted matches are responsible for many fires. Other fires are traceable to sparks from locomotives, or to campfires left by careless campers. Everybody who builds a fire outdoors should make sure that it is built in a safe place and where it cannot spread. Be sure that a fire is out before you leave it.

Small brush or forest fires can often be extinguished by promptly beating out or covering up the flames. Large forest fires cannot be put out directly but their advance can be checked by creating a firebreak. This is a strip of land in the path of the fire which is plowed or cleared wide enough so that the flames cannot cross it. Another way

of checking the advance of a forest fire is by starting a backfire which burns off a strip of land surrounding the original fire.

The loss from forest fires is greatly reduced by the use of lookout and patrol systems. Forest rangers patrol the forests and man the fire towers, keeping a watchful eye for any curling trail of smoke. Telephones and portable radio sending sets are used by our forest rangers and fire wardens. The Federal Forest Service also makes extensive use of airplanes to patrol timberland. The federal and state governments cooperate in the work of preventing and controlling forest fires. Accidents

Chapter XIX  A. LAW ENFORCEMENT Our System of Law Enforcement.-Our govern ment, we have learned, takes measures to provide for health and safety, and in a similar manner it gives us protection against people who are lawbreakers. The officials who are most concerned with the protection of life and property from crime are the police. Cities and towns usually have well-organized police systems. In rural areas the sheriff of the county and the constables in the smaller communities are the chief law enforcement officials. Many of our states also have a separate system of state police for patrolling the highways and controlling crime. The federal government has its own law enforcement officials who deal with crimes against federal laws. 

Each state has a National Guard or militia which may be used by the governor when rioting or serious disorder occurs that cannot be controlled by local authorities. The federal government maintains the Army, the Navy, and the Marine Corps to protect its citizens from foreign enemies. In case of serious public disorder, federal troops may be sent to a state to preserve order. This is usually done only when the governor or the legislature of a state requests it.

The Work of the Police. The law enforcement of ficials whom we see most frequently are our police. Their chief duty is to prevent crime and to arrest people who 390

are believed to be guilty of it, but they perform many other valuable services. We often see them directing traffic at street corners and enforcing parking regulations. They prevent speeding and reckless driving on the part of our motorists. They give information to strangers; they help old people and those who are very young to cross at dangerous intersections. Policemen who are "on the beat" see that the sidewalks are kept clear; they report broken street lights and leaks in gas or water pipes; they guard our homes and places of business from burglars. They give valuable aid during the time of a fire and they preserve order at all places where crowds of people gather. In case of a serious accident the police are often prepared to give first aid treatment. There are numerous cases on record where police have risked their lives to save people from fires or drowning.

Police are valuable public servants who perform many worth-while services; they deserve the respect and cooperation of the public.

Local Police Forces.--In our larger towns and cities the police forces are organized in somewhat the same manner as an army, with strict discipline and with different officers of varying degrees of rank. At the head of this organization is the chief of police. The patrolmen rank as privates. Then there are sergeants, lieutenants, and captains who are officers in the organization. In order to protect the community night and day, members of the police force are usually divided into three shifts so that some of them will always be on duty. The city is usually divided into districts or precincts. In each of these there is a police station with a force of men assigned to it. 

At the central police headquarters there are special groups of men assigned to different kinds of work. There is usually a detective bureau whose men wear plain clothes

PROVIDENCE POLICE PISTOL RANGE SAFETY FIRST  

Police in Providence, Rhode Island, use this pistol range to improve their marksmanship. in order to conduct secret investigations of crimes. In practically all of our large cities there are women who serve as police. Policewomen are especially useful in dealing with cases involving women and children. 

Unfortunately, the police in some of our cities cannot resist the temptation to take money from persons who wish to break the law. Politicians who wield power and influence in the various city offices are sometimes responsible for the corruption in a police force. Many cities have civil service examinations in order to secure police who are free from the control of politicians. The American people have a right to expect high standards of honesty and efficiency on the part of their police forces. 

State Police.-The automobile has made it easy for the modern criminal to escape quickly from the scene of his crime and go to "hide-outs" in distant places. Our crowded highways need to be patrolled in the interests of safety. County sheriffs and local constables in rural areas are often unable to deal with these problems and are not

trained for police work. For these reasons about one-third of our states have established a state police; these officers of the law patrol the main highways in automobiles or on motorcycles and prevent and detect crime in the rural areas of the state. When serious trouble develops, such as a riot, the state police are called to assist the local police in handling it.

State police are carefully selected from a large list of applicants and are usually trained at a special school. Here they study such subjects as horsemanship, criminal law, marksmanship, investigation of crime and criminal procedure, first aid, and methods of handling individuals, crowds, and mobs. 

Like the city police force, the state police are organized in a military fashion; they are divided into troops who live in barracks or stations in different sections of the state. 

Pennsylvania and New York have large forces of state police; Michigan has a state police force whose units are all connected by a state-wide radio system. This plan has been very effective in reducing the number of serious crimes in that state. 

Federal Law Enforcement.-Most crime has to do with the breaking of state laws which are enforced by local and state police. Federal laws must be enforced by the national government. The illegal sale of drugs, smuggling goods or immigrants into the country, kidnaping across state lines, bank robberies, counterfeiting, taking stolen automobiles across state lines, are examples of federal crimes.

The United States Secret Service in the Treasury Department investigates cases of counterfeiting, forgery, and theft of government property. The Secret Service also protects the President and members of his family. 

The Federal Bureau of Investigation in the Department

of Justice has made a splendid record in its drive against bank robberies, kidnaping, and certain kinds of racketeering. These G-men, as they are called, consist of five hundred agents, three-fourths of whom are college graduates. They are highly skilled in detecting crime, and they 

Courtesy U. S. Department of the Treasury 

How to detect counterfeit money is explained by the United States 

Secret Service agents. are trained to shoot fast and straight. This Bureau has a laboratory and experts who use all the latest scientific methods for detecting crime. 

The attack on Pearl 

Harbor, December 7, 1941, found the FBI completely ready to serve its country in time of war. 

The number of trained men was greatly increased. 

Spies, saboteurs, and others who were not loyal to our country were arrested by these federal agents. 

Modern Police Equip ment.-Modern police use scientific methods for solving crime mysteries and are equipped to capture all kinds of criminals. The patrolman on his "beat" in a city can always keep in touch with headquarters by a system of telephone signal boxes. A three-way radio system makes it possible for headquarters to call all police cars and for officers in the different cars to call each other, or to send messages to headquarters. A considerable amount of science is used in the detection of crime today. Criminals are identified by the use of fingerprints and by the aid of photography. Many police head-

quarters have a rogues' gallery where pictures of criminals are kept on file. Chemical analysis of blood stains and the use of the microscope help solve crime mysteries. Experts analyze handwriting, decipher code messages, reconstruct burnt letters, and make microscopic examinations of bullet marks. 

Attitude of the Public

Toward Law Enforce ment.-Every citizen has a part in law enforcement.

In the first place, he should make sure that he obeys the laws himself. When he sees a crime being committed, he should report it to the police at once. When he is called on to be a witness in court, or to serve on a jury, he should undertake this duty willingly.

Too often people are indifferent to the problems of law enforcement. The work of the police and the courts cannot succeed without the sympathetic cooperation of the general public. 

Courtesy U. S. Department of Justice 

Laboratory experts in the Federal Bureau of Investigation help to solve crime mysteries.

Rights of Citizens in a Democracy. In a democracy all citizens have certain rights which the police and the courts must respect. These rights have been discussed in 

Chapters I and III. In a democracy, government is organized to promote the welfare of the people and laws are made by the representatives of the people for the good of everybody. They should be enforced without discrimination and with justice and fairness.

B. CRIME AND ITS CAUSES 

Our Crime Problem.-In a democracy we elect representatives to make our laws. Laws are rules and regulations which enable us to live, to work, and to play without interfering with the rights of others. To play the game of life squarely, everybody should obey the rules, but unfortunately there are some people who do not. Any violation of the law is known as a crime. Crimes are classified as felonies and misdemeanors. Felonies are serious crimes, such as murder, arson, and burglary, and are punishable by death or imprisonment in a penitentiary. Misdemeanors are less serious crimes, such as drunkenness, petty thefts, and violation of traffic laws, punishable by fines or imprisonment in local jails.

The estimated cost of crime is close to fifteen billion dollars a year, twice the amount the federal government spends for its regular services. J. Edgar Hoover, head of the Federal Bureau of Investigation, estimates that there are four million criminals loose in the United States today. 

It is obvious that the control of crime is one of our most serious national problems. 

Organized Crime.-One of our most serious forms of lawlessness is organized crime. Thomas E. Dewey, former district attorney of New York, who made such a conspicuous success in dealing with the racketeers and organized criminals of his state, says that thirty years ago there was no organized crime, but that today crime has grown into a national industry. Since World War I, gangsters, racketeers, and kidnapers have come to be a serious menace to public welfare. With their warfare, murders, bombings, and holdups, these gangsters have become a serious threat to life and property in many of our large cities. Racketeering, which preys upon almost every form
