The majority of our criminals are young. Today about fifty per cent of the arrests reported to the federal government are for persons under thirty years of age, and twenty per cent are for those under twenty-one years. of business, is the most highly organized form of crime. By threatening destruction of property or personal injury, these criminals make respectable citizens pay for so-called "protection."

The courageous efforts of the G-men and the untiring work of district attorneys and other law enforcement officials, are now beginning to show results and the power of these public enemies has greatly decreased. 

Who Commit Crimes?-Most of our crimes are committed by men. Eleven times as many men as women were sent to prison in a recent year. The majority of our criminals are young. In 1938 more persons twenty-one years of age were arrested and fingerprinted than of any other single age. Many people think that foreigners commit more crimes in this country than native Americans. This is not true, as has been shown over and over again by the official records. However, the children of immigrants usually do not have so good a record in this

respect as their parents. Because Negroes often live in the slums and under the worst kind of conditions, their crime rate is high.

The Effect of Crime on the Individual.-Some people have the idea that gangsters and other criminals are brave persons whose lives are filled with adventure and glamour. Nothing could be farther from the truth. A criminal may sometimes get "easy money" for the time being in one way or another, but he always pays a terrific price for his wrongdoing. He is never safe and never at peace. He must carry with him a burden of worry and the constant fear of being caught by the police. Even though he may not be brought to trial, he can never escape the punishment which he thus imposes upon himself. 

Many criminals are caught and sentenced to jails and penitentiaries where they have to spend the best years of their lives. Criminals also cause a great deal of suffering and trouble for their friends and families. If criminals could be made aware beforehand of the sorrow which their acts may bring to their families, it is likely that many of them would not commit crime. 

Causes of Crime.-Crime cannot be explained on the basis of any one single cause. A number of different factors in the individual and in his surroundings may be responsible for his lawbreaking. For convenience, we sometimes classify the causes of crime as individual and group. 

Individual Causes. Crime itself is not inherited, but a person may inherit some weakness, such as insanity or feeblemindedness, which may be the reason for his wrongdoing. Bad habits may cause crime. Many crimes are committed by persons under the influence of liquor or narcotics.

Group Causes. Many of the causes of crime may be traced to living conditions. Some areas of our cities have

Both photos:  

Gangs with no playgrounds except city streets are likely to get into trouble. Provision for wholesome recreation would remove one of the causes of juvenile crime. more criminals than others. You can take a large map of a city and make a dot at the street address of every person who has been found guilty of a crime, and the dots will cluster in the areas known as slums. If children are surrounded by bad influences in early childhood, it is easy for them to become criminals. Many of our young of-

fenders come from homes where their training in the right ways of living has been sadly neglected. In school they never became interested in studying; they were often truant and disorderly in conduct. In Chapter XVII we studied the importance of leisure time activities. Some young people in the slums of our cities do not have opportunities for wholesome play and recreation. They soon drift into small groups or gangs looking for something exciting to do. Frequently they get into trouble with the police and may thus get started on careers of crime. 

Unemployment and poverty are usually listed among the social causes of crime. If a man cannot find work, or if his earnings are too small to provide enough food and clothing for his family, he may steal or commit some other offense to obtain these necessities. 

Some of the social causes of crime may be found in our system of law enforcement. Police sometimes allow acts of lawlessness to be carried on without punishment. Delays in court procedure sometimes make it possible for offenders to “get off easy" or to escape punishment entirely.

The Prevention of Crime.-In order to prevent crime we must attack the problem by the same methods that were used in eliminating many diseases. By removing the causes of typhoid fever, many communities have practically wiped out this serious sickness. In a similar manner, we can greatly reduce the amount of crime by removing its causes.

Many things need to be done in order to remove the causes of crime. The problem of unemployment must be solved and people must be given the opportunity to earn enough income to maintain a proper standard of living. 

Many of our criminals come from poor homes. Since the family is so important in the training of our youth, any effort to improve the quality of our home life will help

to prevent crime. Communities should provide better programs of public recreation; there should be adequate and attractive neighborhood playgrounds. Children should be able to get their fun from games, dramatics, music and hobbies, instead of breaking into stores and stealing automobiles. There should be programs of slum clearance which will bring better housing to these areas which are now the greatest breeding places of crime. 

One of the surest ways to prevent crime is to improve our system of law enforcement. We must insist upon strict honesty on the part of all public officials. The swift and certain arrest and punishment of all criminals will help to prevent crime. A reform or improvement in our prisons will also help. Some people claim that at present our prisons are the worst places for breeding crime. 

C. PUNISHMENT AND PREVENTION OF CRIME 

Our Attitude Toward Punishment.-Three different principles have been used as a basis for punishing criminals. 

1. The Principle of Revenge. Among primitive people the purpose of punishment was to "get back" at the person who committed the crime. "An eye for an eye, and a tooth for a tooth" illustrates this principle. Punishment was planned so that the lawbreaker had to suffer in payment for the crime he committed. Methods of punishment were often very cruel. The death penalty was frequently used and various forms of torture, such as flogging or branding, were inflicted. 

2. The Principle of Repression. Under this theory punishment was made so severe that people who might be tempted to commit crime would not do so on account of the fear of the punishment that would follow. Based on this theory, the death penalty was at one time inflicted

4C

for over two hundred different offenses in England. But severity of punishment alone will not deter people from committing crime. It is related that in the days when pickpockets were hanged in public as an example, other pickpockets plied their trade among the crowds of people gathered for the occasion. We know now that it is the certainty and not the severity of the punishment which may prevent crime. 

3. The Principle of Reformation. The best thinkers today hold that whenever possible criminals should be reformed. Prisoners are looked upon as human beings who went wrong because of certain definite causes. They are to be removed from society for treatment which will cure them from their tendency to commit crime. Wrongdoers should be more law-abiding when they come out of prison than when they went in. Many young offenders can be reformed if they are properly educated and the causes for delinquency removed.

Treatment of the Criminal.-In our treatment of the lawbreaker we should make a distinction between the habitual criminal who cannot be reformed and the first offender who can be made into a useful citizen again. In our more progressive communities every prisoner is carefully studied. His past history, the record of his crime, his physical condition, and his mental ability, are all carefully analyzed. On the basis of this examination the authorities decide what steps are to be taken. They may decide whether he is to be sent to prison or some other institution, or to be given another chance through probation. Hardened criminals are kept separate from first offenders. 

Young boys and girls who have committed crime may be sent to reform schools where they are taught a trade, or they may be put on probation.

Under probation the convicted person is not sent to

prison. Instead, the judge suspends or holds back the sentence, giving the offender another chance to make good.

For a definite length of time a person on probation must observe certain rules. He must work steadily; avoid bad companions; and report regularly to the probation officer. 

Boys and girls who are under probation are usually required to attend school regularly, and to stay off the streets at night.

In many states the judge is permitted to impose an indeterminate sentence. In this case the exact length of imprisonment will depend upon the conduct of the prisoner. He may be sentenced to serve from one to five years. At the end of the first year his case comes before a parole board which decides whether he should be kept in prison or be released. If the convict is then released, he is said to be on parole for the balance of the time for which he was sentenced. During the time when he is under parole the offender must behave himself and report regularly to the parole officer, otherwise he will have to go back to prison to finish his sentence. Our officials should be more careful about releasing prisoners on parole because some serious crimes have been committed by paroled prisoners. 

Prisons.-The Old. Treatment of convicts in many prisons in the past has been disgraceful. These institutions were damp and dark. Sometimes confinement in dark dungeons was used as a special form of punishment. 

Prisoners were herded in gangs and clothed in stripes; in some institutions all conversation among the prisoners was forbidden. Prisons of this type are not well adapted to reforming offenders.

Penal institutions have improved but we still have many that are poorly planned and mismanaged. 

The New. In contrast with the old type of prison, modern penal institutions have plenty of fresh air and

Both photos: Ewing Galloway

The interior of a modern prison is shown at the left. This picture of a prison school in session was taken at the state penitentiary at 

Sing Sing, New York. sunshine. They provide different kinds of work for the prisoners, wholesome recreation, reading, and opportunities for schooling. In some, the prisoners have their own organization which provides for self-government and discipline. In return for good behavior they are given extra privileges. Such institutions are designed not only to punish the prisoner but also to teach him better ways of living. Some people, however, feel that there is a serious danger of making life too easy for our prisoners. 

Dealing with Young Offenders.-Boys and girls under eighteen years of age who commit crimes are usually kept separate from adult criminals in courts and in prisons. 

Juvenile courts have been established for the hearing of such cases. The procedure in juvenile courts is more

Ewing Galloway

Juvenile courts attempt to discover the causes of crime and to treat. young offenders in such a way that they will grow up to be law-abiding. informal than in regular trials. Usually the judge, the police official, and the parents are included in a sort of round table conference. When a child is brought before a juvenile court, the judge may order a complete investigation of his background in order to determine what should be done. Juvenile courts make extensive use of probation. 

In extreme cases they may send boys and girls to reform schools where efforts are made to teach them useful trades and better ways of living. Because the inmates in these institutions learn many wrong ideas and evil practices from each other, the value of reform schools is often considered doubtful.

UNIT SEVEN LIVING AND WORKING IN THE AMERICAN DEMOCRACY 

Most people spend much of their time in working to make a living and many of the activities of government are concerned with problems which grow out of man's work. Unit Seven describes our modes of living and working in our American democracy. 

Chapter Twenty shows why it is necessary for man to work; it describes the changes that have taken place in the methods of work; and it suggests ways by which the pupil can find his place in the world of work.

Chapter Twenty-One describes how wealth is produced and how business is organized. It discusses the relations between government and business.

The goods which are produced must be distributed to the people who are going to use them. Chapter Twenty-Two shows the importance of buying and selling; it discusses the use of money and describes the services of banks. 

Chapter Twenty-Three explains the importance of transportation and communication and describes the methods that are being used and the problems that have to be faced.

Chapter XX  A. HUMAN WANTS AND THEIR SATISFACTION 

Human Wants.-In order to live, civilized human beings must be able to satisfy certain basic wants. They must have food, clothing, and shelter. We are accustomed to a considerable variety of food, a balanced diet with all the necessary vitamins, calories, and the like. We must also have clothing to protect us from the cold of winter and the heat of summer; we also like to have a number of different articles of clothing so that we can achieve some variety in dress. Our next great need is shelter. Civilized man needs the protection of buildings of all kindsdwelling houses and apartments, and other buildings for stores, offices, and factories. 

In addition to food, clothing, and shelter, human beings have learned to want many other things. In fact, there is no limit to the things that people may wish to have and enjoy. We want automobiles, radios, books, music, and thousands of other things which help to make modern living comfortable, convenient, and pleasant. Those activities of man which are chiefly concerned with making a living and satisfying human wants are called economic activities. These include farming, manufacturing, mining, transportation, commerce, and finance.

Cooperation to Satisfy Human Wants.—In order to satisfy our numerous and varied human wants, cooperation 411

and teamwork are an essential part of our economic activities. We are likely to sit down to a good meal, enjoy a comfortable home, and wear suitable clothing without realizing how many different persons in various parts of the world have worked to make these things possible. 

People in Cuba and Hawaii work in order that we may have sugar on our table; laborers in far-away India and 

China toil so that we may have silk stockings and silk ties; native workers in South America gather and refine the sap from trees in order that we may have rubber tires on our automobiles and trucks. Farmers all over this country keep us supplied with many articles of food for our table -butter from Wisconsin, bread made from Minnesota wheat, potatoes from Maine, beef from Montana, and oranges from California and Florida.

Most of the things that we use have been changed from the raw materials into the finished product by many different kinds of workers. In order to produce a cotton dress or shirt, it is necessary first for the farmer in the South to raise and pick the cotton; then many different kinds of factory workers make the raw cotton into cloth and finally into the finished product. Wholesale and retail stores are needed to bring the goods to the consumer. Transportation agencies, ships, trains, and trucks, which carry goods from one section of our country to the other are an important part of our economic system.

The Place of Work in Human Life. In order to produce an abundance of things to satisfy human wants, work is necessary. Each person should do his share in helping to produce and distribute the wealth of the world. 

It is by intelligent work that the resources and forces of nature have been developed. Through his own efforts man has risen from the conditions of his primitive existence to the comforts and luxuries of present-day living.

For the individual man or woman, work is any activity which is directed toward earning a living. Each person who is able to do so, must perform some useful task so that he may receive money or other forms of income to provide food, clothing, shelter, and other needs. "He that will not work shall not eat," was the just command of Captain 

John Smith in the Jamestown colony. Fortunately, many people find happiness in their work. They work "for the joy of working." The artist, the musician, the actor, and the inventor get an inner satisfaction out of their work so that it becomes pleasure. Many people work because they wish to render service to others. This may be especially true of the church worker, the nurse, the physician, the social worker, or the teacher.

B. THE DEVELOPMENT OF MAN'S WORK Methods of Work and Living Have Changed.- 

Man has not always lived as he does today. Looking back over the history of mankind, one finds a gradual process of change and development in the way people have worked and lived. The wants of primitive people were few and simple and their methods of work were very different from ours. As time went on, man began to make more discoveries and inventions, his wants increased, and his methods of work changed. Because of the development of modern science and invention, the greatest changes in methods of work and living have taken place during the past century. 

How Primitive Man Lived. We do not know a great deal about the life of primitive man, but there is considerable evidence that he did not live very differently from the creatures of the fields and the forests. Getting food was one of his greatest problems, for he raised no crops and had no domesticated animals. He had to depend largely on nuts, wild fruits, honey, and probably on such game as

he could capture. For centuries, he had no way of cooking food and did not realize the value of fire. What little clothing he used probably consisted of the skins of wild animals. His shelter might consist of a cave or perhaps a big tree.

However, man has gradually learned how to make and use tools. The first tools used by primitive man were clubs or sticks made from the branches of trees. Stones came to be used as weapons to kill animals. Stone spearheads, arrowheads, and axes enabled man to kill the fiercest and strongest beasts of the field and forest. The discovery of fire enabled man to smelt ore and cast metals into definite shapes. After this he could use bronze, copper, and iron to make tools and implements. The discovery of fire was extremely important to early man. 

Stages in the Development of Man's Work. In the progress of man's work from primitive times down to the present there are certain stages or periods of development. 

These stages, however, are not separated by any clear-cut lines; one stage of development did not suddenly stop while another one began. 

First was the hunting and fishing stage. Food was obtained largely from these two activities. Some American 

Indians were still in this stage when the white men came to this country. Today hunting and fishing are usually considered as recreation, but to primitive people they were their chief means of existence. 

The pastoral stage developed when man began to domesticate animals. The dog was probably the first domesticated animal, but certain early tribes raised sheep and goats, which provided milk, wool, and meat. These people moved about from one spot to another seeking grazing lands for their animals. 

Next came the agricultural stage when man gradually

learned how to plant, cultivate, and harvest crops. Wheat and barley were among the first crops raised; the American Indians were cultivating corn when the Pilgrims came to America. With the raising of crops, man began to settle down; community life and the beginnings of government developed.

Up until a few hundred years ago most products were made by hand. In this process only small tools and implements were used. This period is known as the handicraft stage in the development of man's work. In one sense, this was real manufacturing, since that word in Latin means "making by hand." For many centuries men made goods by hand in about the same way. The farmers of 

Washington's time plowed with crude implements, sowed their grain by hand, harvested it with a sickle, and thrashed it with a flail. Traveling in the days of George 

Washington was no easier than during the time of Julius Caesar. In the field of science and invention mankind had made practically no progress for many centuries. But in the latter part of the eighteenth century great changes, known as the industrial revolution, brought in the machine age.

The Industrial Revolution.-A series of remarkable inventions, starting late in the eighteenth century and continuing to the present time, have completely changed our ways of working and living. We usually refer to these great changes in the making of goods as the industrial revolution. About the same time a number of important changes took place in farming, and this development is referred to as the agricultural revolution. If we would understand our modern world of work, we must know something about these great changes brought about by invention and discovery.

The first group of important inventions came in Eng-

land in connection with the spinning and the weaving of cloth. Both of these processes had been done by hand for many centuries. One of the first inventors was Hargreaves who made a machine, called the "spinning jenny," which could spin several threads at once. Other inventors soon improved on this machine and the spinning of thread from raw cotton or wool became much easier and faster. 

In 1785 Cartwright invented a power loom for weaving the thread into cloth. This machine could use either steam or water power. Previously all weaving looms had been operated by hand. Faster methods of weaving cloth now made it possible for the weavers to keep up with the spinners who were using improved machinery. In 1793 came one of the most important inventions of all time when Eli Whitney, an American, made the first cotton gin which separated the seeds from the cotton fiber. This led to a great increase in the production of raw cotton to be used by the spinners and weavers.

The new machines needed power. Water wheels were already in common use for this purpose, but often there was no water power available in the location where people wished to use the new machinery. In 1785 James Watt perfected the steam engine which had previously been used for pumping water out of mines. Steam gradually replaced water power in the factories; it also came to be used for locomotives and ships and caused great changes in transportation.

With the development of new machinery and the use of steam engines for power, there came a great need for iron. 

This important metal had been known for many centuries, but it was scarce and expensive. Charcoal, made out of wood, had been used for smelting iron ore but this process was expensive and the trees for making charcoal were rapidly disappearing in England. It was fortunate, there-

ALWAYS BE CAREFUL 

Hundreds of thousands of workers are now employed in factories. fore, that men discovered that coal could be transformed into coke and used in blast furnaces in place of charcoal. 

This newer knowledge made it possible first for England, and later for America and the countries of western Europe, to use their vast resources of coal and iron ore to produce iron and steel machinery. 

Men's Lives Changed by the Industrial Revolution. 

-No spectacular wars or bloody revolutions have ever affected men's lives more than the industrial revolution which has brought us the machine age and the factory system. Before the industrial revolution most people lived in the country and engaged in farming. The home was the center of industry. The worker owned his own tools. and made goods in his own household. There were here and there small factories in which a few journeymen workers and apprentices worked for the owner. 

The new machines were too large and too expensive to be placed in homes. Wealthy men and enterprising men

purchased this machinery and started large factories. 

We refer to this as the factory system of production. The workers no longer owned their own machines but became employees in factories. A separation of interests between the employer and the employees often led to much bitterness. The development of the factories caused more and more people to move into the cities where they could be near their work. The rapid growth of cities often gave rise to serious problems of overcrowding and bad housing. 

New machines were constantly being invented and put to use. As a result workers sometimes lost their jobs. In a few cases attempts were made to destroy the new machinery.

As a result of the industrial revolution, manufactured articles can be sold much cheaper than handmade products. More people buy more goods. Production has increased to meet the new demands. Thus, the standard of living, judged on the basis of what people buy and use, is today very much higher than formerly. 

The increased production of manufactured goods has given employment to millions of workers in related industries. Greater transportation facilities, roads, ships, and railroads have developed in order to move raw materials to the factories and manufactured products to the consumers.

The Machine Age.-Ours is truly a machine age. 

Today there is scarcely any article that we use that is not made by machinery. The development of new machinery is continually going on. Thousands of trained research workers and inventors are continually at work improving old machines and inventing new ones.

Because of the great development in the manufacture and transportation of electric power, some people refer to our times as the "power age." We are no longer entirely

Courtesy American Telephone and 

Telegraph Company

Courtesy International Business Machines Corporation 

Machines that perform complicated clerical operations are a part of the change that has taken place in methods of work. The busy scene at an overseas switchboard in a telephone office illustrates another phase of modern business activity. dependent on steam or gasoline engines. Centralized electric power plants can supply the motive force for running machinery in hundreds of factories. However, we shall probably always need steam or water power to generate the electricity. 

The machine age has brought mankind many benefits.

It has decreased the hours of work; increased the leisure time of the worker; and produced a greater variety and a larger amount of merchandise. With it have come many problems-chiefly unemployment, labor problems, and living conditions in crowded cities. 

Specialization. The work in modern factories is organized in such a way that each person does only a small part in the manufacture of the finished product. A worker in an automobile factory stands at the assembly line and

adds a new part or puts something in place as the partly finished cars pass by; he performs the same operation in the same manner hour after hour and day after day. An automobile, as a finished product, is the result of the work 

Courtesy U. S. Department of Labor 

Operators in this assembly line work from a moving belt. They are assembling telephone parts. of hundreds of skilled specialists, each of whom performs only one small part in its manufacture. 

Specialization has a number of advantages. 

Each worker can learn to do his job exceptionally well. Since many operations do not require much skill, the worker can learn his job in a short time. 

Long periods of apprenticeship training are no longer necessary. Specialization makes for efficient and speedy production and thus shortens the hours of work. But all the results of specialization are not ben- eficial. Doing the same thing over and over again in the same manner becomes monotonous to the worker, although it is claimed that some people like this type of work. Besides, the workman who performs only one small task cannot have the same feeling of satisfaction that the craftsman once had when he alone produced a complete article.

Specialization is found in other fields as well as in factory work. In the profession of medicine there are some doctors who specialize in the treatment of the eyes, ears,

Surgery is a highly specialized phase of the medical profession. nose, or other parts of the body. Some lawyers will accept only certain kinds of cases. In our high schools today there are usually specially trained teachers for each subject, such as English or history. Specialization in the professions has advantages as well as disadvantages. 

The Agricultural Revolution.-While industry was being changed by the use of machinery, new inventions also revolutionized farming. In 1797 the first cast-iron plow was invented. The invention of the reaper by Cyrus 

McCormick in 1831 enabled farmers to harvest grain by machinery. During the nineteenth century there were other important inventions, such as the mowing machine, the self-binder, the threshing machine, seed sowers, and cultivators. Recent years have seen the invention of the combine which cuts and threshes grain at the same time, the mechanical corn harvester, and the cotton picker. 

Many farms of today are equipped with gasoline-powered

Courtesy McCormick-Deering

Grain in the nineteenth century was cut with a then-new invention, the mowing machine. Now a tractor-drawn harvesterthresher does the job. tractors which furnish the needed power for all kinds of work in the fields or in the barn. The use of the principles of scientific farming and the breeding of farm animals so that they will be more useful, have also improved methods of farming.

A New York Times correspondent recently described the new machinery on our Middle Western farms thus: "Cornhusking? Surely that chore at least remains true

to Western tradition: the farm wagon trundling down the corn rows, the huskers, gloved in leather, bending beside it, stripping the ears from the dry stalks and flinging them, thirty bushels an hour each man, at the bumpboard mounted above the far side of the wagon bed. But today he may listen in vain for the corn's thud on the board. "He hears instead the rumble of a gasoline engine and beholds an ominous monster approaching through the field; a grotesque thing, half caterpillar tractor and half dinosaur, with a skirt or armor reaching the ground, and with a neck and a head like a giraffe's, held high above the tallest stalks. Advancing, this queer creature devours three corn rows at a time, expels the stalks and husks on the ground, and chutes the golden ears by way of its giraffe neck into a motor truck that keeps it company. "Tractors drag the plows through the black, rich prairie loam. Combines reap, thresh, winnow, and load the wheat, all in one operation.' 

The agricultural revolution has greatly increased farm production. Fewer people are needed on the farms today, since one farmer with the help of machinery can now do the work of sixty-four farmers in the days of crude and inefficient Colonial agriculture. Since fewer workers are needed on the farms, many people from the country have moved to the cities.

C. FINDING YOUR PLACE IN THE WORLD OF WORK 

Kinds of Work.-Workers are ordinarily classified according to the amount of training their jobs require. Those workers who need no special training for their jobs and whose work is mostly a matter of physical strength and endurance are said to be unskilled. The man who works with pick and shovel and the woman who scrubs floors are unskilled workers.

The unskilled laborer who works with pick and shovel is an important and necessary part of American life. 

Next come the semi-skilled groups. These persons have to acquire some training and experience before they can do their work well. Frequently, several weeks or months of training are required. Many persons who work in factories hold jobs of a semi-skilled nature. The bus driver and the telephone operator are examples of this group. 

The skilled worker is one whose job requires considerable training over a period of several years. He must be able to adapt himself to different jobs which are not always alike and may require some originality. The training for skilled workers is acquired during a period of apprenticeship, through trade courses in school, or by both methods. The carpenter, the plumber, the bricklayer, and the machinist are examples of skilled workers. Clerical workers also need special training. Bookkeepers, stenographers, and general clerks belong to the skilled group. 

Another class of workers is known as the professional

group. For entrance into the professions, years of study and preparation are required. The government usually prescribes the standards which people must meet in order to follow a certain profession. Physicians, lawyers, engineers, and teachers have to meet the requirements of the law in order to be admitted into these professions. 

The United States Census lists workers under the following classifications: agriculture and related occupations; forestry and fishing; mining; manufacturing and mechanical industries; trade; transportation and communication; public service; professional service; clerical service; domestic and personal service. 

All Types of Useful Work Are Honorable. Our economic system is a cooperative enterprise in which each type of worker is expected to do his share. In a democracy the doors of opportunity are always open to each person in finding the type of work for which he is best suited. 

Sometimes one type of worker is inclined to look down 

Carpenters are skilled workers who have been trained for their job of building houses.

Courtesy Public Works Administration 

A different training is needed by the workers who build our great skyscrapers.

Courtesy Farm Security Administration: Photos by Lee 

The typesetter, the tailor, the diemaker are all skilled workers. 426

on another as being inferior. In a democracy all types of workers who perform some useful service are needed and respected. All useful work is honorable. America has been fortunate in that there have been few, if any, class distinctions among its workers. Those who preach and agitate class hatred are real enemies of democracy. 

Choose Your Life Work with Care.-We have often heard of people who are "square pegs in round holes,' individuals who are misfits in the kind of work that they are doing. Many people merely drift into a job. Others are too easily influenced by thoughts of quick and easy money. 

Parents often insist upon choosing children's life work for them. Still others get into the wrong vocations because they drift into "blind-alley jobs" in which there is little chance for advancement. Too many young people are anxious to get one of the so-called "white collar jobs" when they are better suited for some other type of work. 

We should remember that labor with one's hands is just as honorable as any other kind of work. A carpenter who does his work well can feel just as proud of his results as the lawyer who successfully pleads his case in court. 

In our democracy every occupation is open to anybody who has the ability and the preparation to do the work involved. Each person should choose his life work with care, because one's chief satisfaction in life will be in work well done. There is no agreement as to the proper age when a person should definitely decide on his occupation. 

High school boys and girls should get all the information possible on the subject of vocations. They should not be afraid to change their minds and revise their plans when they find that a poor choice has been made. 

How the Schools Help in Choosing One's Vocation. 

-Most high schools today try to help students in their choice of vocations. In the junior high school there is

Courtesy Bureau of Mines, Department of the Interior Two kinds of mining-hydraulic and underground-are shown here. usually a variety of "exploratory" courses which give boys and girls an opportunity to get acquainted with different kinds of vocations. Short courses in electricity, carpentry, and printing are not designed to train boys to become expert mechanics, but they do give them an opportunity to learn about different occupations. Girls may learn about different kinds of work through homemaking courses. 

Junior high school clubs help to widen interests and increase knowledge about occupations. 

One's interest and degree of success in school work and activities may furnish important clues as to what type of vocation one should follow. Certainly, nobody should want to become a journalist unless he enjoys writing, and no one should follow engineering unless he likes mathematics. 

Some schools have regular courses in "Occupations" or "Vocational Guidance." Men and women who are leaders in their occupations are invited to speak to groups of pupils and hold conferences with individuals. Some of the larger schools employ vocational counselors who have made a careful study of various occupations and their

requirements. These special teachers are usually free during part of the time to talk with individual pupils and assist them in making a selection of a vocation. 

Study Yourself.-One of the first steps in selecting your occupation is to study yourself. Find out what kind of a person you are and what kind of work you can do best. Certainly, if you have no mechanical ability, you will not wish to follow a trade. If you do not have a good voice, you will not try to become a concert singer. 

Ask a number of questions about yourself. What do you like to do best? What are your interests? What are your strong points? What are your weaknesses? What subjects in school do you like best? What are your hobbies? Are you a leader or a follower? Do you have special abilities or talent in mechanics, music, or art? Ask these and many other questions about yourself and answer them frankly. 

Talk them over with some of your friends and find out what they think about your abilities and interests. 

Study Occupations.-The second important step in choosing your life work is to study the world of occupations. Benjamin Franklin in his Autobiography tells how, when he was a boy, his father tried to make him acquainted with different kinds of occupations: "He sometimes took me to walk with him to see joiners, bricklayers, turners, braziers, etc., at their work, that he might observe my inclination, and endeavor to fix it on some trade or other on land." When Benjamin Franklin was a boy, it was easy for a father and his son to take a walk and see most of the important occupations because they were few in number. Today there are hundreds of different occupations; industrial and commercial life has become exceedingly complex. Hence, knowledge about occupations is no longer so easily obtained. 

Fortunately, books and magazines with all kinds of vo-

cational information are available today. Research organizations publish pamphlets and bulletins about the latest development in special fields of work. It is worth while for each student to know something about a great variety of occupations and the opportunities and possibilities in each one. Some of the questions that should be answered about each occupation are: What is the nature of the work? What personal qualifications are desirable? What preparation is required? What are the advantages and disadvantages? What are the opportunities for advancement? 

Is this field of work overcrowded? What possibilities are there for rendering service to others?

After a pupil becomes definitely interested in a certain vocation, it is often possible for him to get some actual experience in that work through jobs in the summer or on Saturdays. One of the best ways to get vocational information is to take trips to factories, offices, and other places where people work. Personal interviews with people who are now working should prove helpful. A large amount of vocational information can also be gathered by reading the biographies of successful men and women. 

Prepare for Your Life Work. The way to success in business, the trades, or the professions is through education and training. All occupations with a real future require a definite amount of preparation. It is the untrained worker who most frequently drifts into a "blind-alley" job. 

It is important, therefore, that you should get as much education as you can. Education pays in terms of dollars and cents. Studies have been made which prove the money value of education. The people with the greater amount of training, as a rule, hold the better-paying jobs. 

Education also increases the variety of choices in occupations. Thus, a high school graduate has wider opportunities than the person who quits school at the end of the

eighth grade, and the college graduate, as a rule, has a greater choice than the person with only a high school training. Because of new inventions and discoveries, some occupations of today may entirely disappear or become greatly changed in the future. Whenever this sort of thing happens, the person with the greatest amount of general education and training can more readily adapt himself to new situations or transfer from one occupation to another.

The important thing, however, is to find out definitely what education and training are required in the vocation of your choice. Then, aim to get this necessary training and plan your selection of courses to prepare yourself for your vocation. Pupils who plan to continue their education beyond high school must make sure that they take the courses needed to meet college entrance requirements. 

Education and training should not cease after one starts working. Wide-awake and energetic workers continue their schooling after graduation. Night schools, university extension courses, and the like, enroll thousands of students every year.

Chapter XXI  A. WEALTH AND ITS PRODUCTION 

Producing Wealth.-We have already learned that in order to satisfy his wants, man must work. The workers of the world are engaged in producing wealth and performing services which satisfy human wants. The wealth of a community consists of lands and buildings, goods on the merchants' shelves, factories, railroads, and many other things that are being used by the people or will be used in the future. The power of any material things to satisfy human wants is called utility. The production of wealth is the process of making things useful, of creating utility; it is the means by which material things are made ready and available for human use. Iron ore which is in the earth cannot be used in that form. Workers have to mine it, obtain the metal from the ore by smelting it, and finally make it into tools, implements, and the like, before it can be used by man. In this manner wealth is produced. We have three types of producers of wealth. 

First, there are those workers who change the form of goods to make them more useful. The workers in a furniture factory are producers of wealth, although the final product, furniture, is composed of the same materials that the workers started with. Manufacturing is the process of changing the form of materials to make them ready for use; it is the means by which raw materials are transformed into finished products. This kind of production is called creating form utility. 435

Second, there are those workers who transport goods to the places where they can be used. After furniture has been produced by the factory, it must be moved to a location where people, who want to use it, can buy it. Railroads, automobiles, trucks, and steamships are used to create wealth in this manner. Transporting goods is an important part of the process of producing wealth. This kind of production is called creating place utility. 

Third, there are those workers who store goods until the time they are needed. Furniture cannot all be sold immediately after it has been manufactured and transported. Some of it, at least, will have to be stored in safe and convenient places until it can be sold. The storage of goods costs time and money, and is a part of the production of wealth. This kind of production is called creating time utility.

There is another type of productive activity which does not depend on the handling or changing of material things. 

It consists of personal service which some persons render to others. The physician, the teacher, and the lawyer do not change the form, time, or place utility of material things; but in a larger sense they are real producers of wealth, because they perform useful services which satisfy human wants. We may say then that the production of wealth means providing goods and services that have utility.

Division of Labor.-Our modern system of producing and distributing wealth is based on "division of labor." 

Each worker does one small part in the general process of getting materials ready for use. It is no longer practical for one person to raise his own grain, grind it into flour, and bake it into bread, or to raise his own cotton and make it into clothing. 

Instead of one person's attempting to do a great many

things for himself, he performs only one task in the production and distribution of goods. He then exchanges his labor or what he has made for other things which he needs. 

For example, the farmer produces much more food than he can use; therefore, he sells some of it and in return buys manufactured products or other things which he and his family need. The building contractor may construct many houses, but he lives in only one. He sells the houses which he has built and in return gets money to buy food, clothing, and other things for his family. Because we have division of labor in our economic system, each person can have a greater amount and variety of goods to use. 

In the previous chapter we have seen how division of labor is carried to its extreme in our great factories where we call it specialization. There is also division of labor in the case of communities as well as of individuals. Communities often specialize in the manufacture of certain products. Thus, Pittsburgh and Birmingham have become famous as steel centers, Detroit for making automobiles, Minneapolis for producing flour, and Grand Rapids for making furniture. Favorable geographical location is usually the reason why a community specializes in the manufacture of some particular product. 

The Factors in the Production of Wealth.-There are four things required in the production of wealth: (1) Land; (2) Labor; (3) Capital; (4) Management.

Land, as we use the term in the study of economics, is any gift of nature which is used in the production of wealth. It includes all our natural resources-land, forests, mineral deposits, rivers, lakes, and seas. Our supply of natural resources is definitely limited. In some cases, as in the business blocks in large cities, land becomes extremely valuable. The gain or return which the owner of land receives for its use is called rent.

Labor is any human effort in the production of wealth. 

Nature provides the raw materials of production, but man must provide the labor with which production is carried on. This includes mental as well as physical effort. 

In this sense the man who works in an office is just as much a laborer as the man who works with pick and shovel. Labor is furnished by human beings whose welfare should be the primary concern of industry as well as government. Labor gets a compensation which we call wages.

Capital consists of the tools and materials which man has made or gained for us in production. It is any product of past industry which is used in producing wealth. Money is not necessarily capital; it may be the means by which capital can be acquired. Buildings, tools, machinery, railroads, and many other things which man has and is now using to produce more wealth are capital. The return which the owners of capital get is called interest. 

Management plans and directs the production of wealth. 

It employs labor and uses the proper quantities and types of land to produce wealth. Every successful industry, farm, or business must have good management. We usually refer to the compensation of management as profit. 

B. THE ORGANIZATION OF BUSINESS What Business Is.-Business is the production or dis tribution of goods and services with the object of making a profit. The business man engages in manufacturing, buying and selling, or some other profit-making activity. 

The farmer who sells some of the products of his farm is a business man. While business is usually conducted for profit, we must not forget that it performs a real service to the community. In our modern world where there is so much division of labor, business is extremely important

and necessary. Very few people actually produce the things which they use; they have to depend upon buying them from others. Business men have factories to make goods, railroads and trucks to transport them, and stores to sell them to us. There are many varieties of business organizations. While there are many small businesses, more than half of all the business is carried on by large concerns.

Simple Forms of Business Organization.—The simplest form of business organization is that which is owned and operated by one person. This is known as a single proprietorship. One man owns the entire business, manages it and assumes the risk of the losses. Small stores, farms, many small and moderate-sized factories, and some wholesale establishments are usually single proprietorships. This organization is best for a small business, but when a concern becomes larger numerous difficulties arise. 

One man may not have enough money to run a large business and he may find it difficult to attend to the numerous details of management. 

Sometimes two or more persons form an agreement to carry on business together. This is known as a partnership. 

Each partner contributes a certain amount of money to start the business, helps to manage it, receives a part of the profits, and assumes the risk of the losses. The advantage of the partnership form of business organization is that it enables several persons to bring their money and managing ability together into one business, thus making it possible to do everything on a larger scale. The greatest weakness is in the fact that each partner is liable for all the debts and obligations assumed by the others. 

Corporations. A corporation is a form of business organization in which a group of people unite to carry on some business activity under a charter given by the state. The

charter under which the corporation operates mentions the names of the persons obtaining it, the title of the corporation, its purposes, and the conditions under which it shall conduct its business. In the eyes of the law a corporation is the same as a person. Even though a corporation may do a nation-wide business, it must receive its charter from a state. The laws which govern the chartering of corporations are by no means the same in all the states. 

In order to get money to carry on its business a corporation may sell stock. Each person who buys stock has an interest in the business and has one vote for each share that he owns. Some of our large corporations today have millions of shares of stock. The stockholders choose a board of directors who in turn elect the officers who manage the business. Recent years have seen a tremendous increase in the amount of business conducted by corporations. This form of business organization is especially well suited for the operation of large factories, chain stores, and the like. It enables many people to invest their money and makes it possible for businesses to be conducted on a large scale. Trained managers and specialists can be employed. The stockholders, unlike the members of a partnership, are not liable for the corporation's debts. Disadvantages of corporations arise because of their great size and power. They may become strong enough to exercise undue influence upon our government. The great financial power of large corporations may give them an unfair advantage in controlling newspapers and other agencies for forming public opinion. In a large corporation, the individual stockholders have little contact with the actual management of the business. Thus, a few officials can exercise a great deal of power. Our states and the national government have passed a great many laws to regulate corporations.

Stocks and Bonds.-Shares of stock in corporations are usually issued to sell at a certain price, known as par value, but all stocks do not sell at this figure. Whether or not stocks sell above or below par depends on the prosperity or lack of it in the business of the corporation. 

Stocks are often issued in two forms, common and preferred, on both of which dividends may be paid out of the profits of the company. 

Dividends are paid on the preferred stock at a fixed rate, say 5 per cent of the par value. 

Dividends are usually paid on preferred stock first, and on common stock if there are any profits left. However, if the business of the company is very profitable, dividends on common stock may be even larger than on preferred stock. But if the 

Courtesy New York Stock Exchange: 

Photo by Gottscho

The New York Stock Exchange han dles millions of dollars' worth of securities daily. financial condition of the corporation is not good, none of the stockholders may receive any dividends.

Corporations also secure money by issuing bonds, which represent money lent to the corporation for a given amount of time at a fixed rate of interest. Interest on the bonds must be paid before any dividends can be paid on stock, but if the corporation has a succession of bad years, it may also stop paying interest on bonds. Both dividend and

interest payments, therefore, are dependent on the profits made by the corporation. 

Stocks which do not represent actual value in terms of goods and materials in the business are called watered stocks. In order to protect the buyer of stocks and bonds, our governments, both federal and state, have passed laws to prevent the sale of fraudulent securities (stocks and bonds).

The Securities and Exchange Commission, established by the federal government in 1934, exercises limited control and supervision over the sale of stocks and bonds. 

People who buy stocks and bonds mainly to receive a regular return in the form of interest and dividends are making investments. Many persons buy securities without any intention of keeping them for a long time, but with the idea of selling them at a profit when they rise in value. 

This is referred to as speculation. There is constant buying and selling of stocks and bonds through brokers who do their trading in stock exchanges.

Large-Scale Production.-One of the characteristics of our present-day business organization is production on a large scale. Recent years have seen small businesses grow larger and larger. Sometimes several small organizations unite to form a large concern. A number of our large corporations have huge factories located in different parts of the country. In some cases they own and control the production of raw materials as well as the manufacture and the marketing.

Large-scale business production results in a number of advantages. It usually makes it possible to manufacture products more cheaply and sell them at a lower price. Raw materials, purchased in large quantities, can be bought at lower prices. Specialization and division of labor in a large factory can be used to great advantage. Large business

3 13 14 19 

Courtesy Farm Security Administration: Photo by Post 

This man is shopping at a cooperative grocery, Greenbelt, Maryland. concerns have sufficient money to employ chemists, inventors, and other research workers to find new and better ways of doing things. By-products in manufacturing can be utilized in various ways. The great meat-packing concerns now boast that they use every part of the pig but the squeal. Large appropriations for advertising increase sales. One of the disadvantages of large-scale production is the power which is given to a small group of men. This power may be used for good or evil, depending entirely upon the men. Sometimes business concerns may resort to unfair methods of competition.

Cooperatives.-The cooperative form of business organization began about a hundred years ago among the weavers of Rochdale, England. These workers had just lost a strike. They had practically no money, but resolved to put aside a few cents each week until they had enough money as a group to start a store. With a total of one

hundred forty dollars, they finally opened the first cooperative store. From this small beginning the cooperative movement has grown until today it is estimated that cooperatives do about twenty billion dollars' worth of business annually in thirty-eight different countries. Outside of England and Scotland, cooperatives are strongest in the Scandinavian countries. 

It was not until after the World War that cooperatives began to develop to any great extent in the United States. 

This form of buying and selling was first used by a number of farmers in the Middle West and since 1934 it has spread rapidly.

In Europe some cooperatives are engaged in manufacturing, but in this country they are engaged mostly in buying and selling.

Many cooperatives maintain stores and filling stations. 

Farmers have found in cooperatives a good way to buy the various materials they need and to market their products. 

A cooperative is owned and controlled by its members who employ a manager to run the business. To become a member of a cooperative one has to purchase stock, but each member has only one vote regardless of the amount of capital he invests. Interest is paid to each shareholder at a fixed rate, but profits are distributed to the members in proportion to the amount which they spend for purchases. 

A member who buys a great deal at a cooperative store will receive a greater share of the profits than the one who buys only a small amount. The real purpose of the cooperative is to enable the members to save money since they share in the profits of the business. 

C. THE GOVERNMENT AND BUSINESS How the Government Aids Business.-An important aid to business is the collection of scientific and statistical

Courtesy National Bureau of Standards 

The National Bureau of Standards tests hundreds of products. The machine pictured was developed to test the comparative endurance of different shoe leathers and types of construction. information by the federal government. Competent scientists are employed by the Department of Commerce to experiment with new materials and methods of manufacture. The United States government establishes standards of weights and measures. 

When a manufacturer needs new markets for his products in foreign countries, his best source of information is the Department of State. This department stations agents in the principal commercial cities of the world to gather information for American business men who wish to market their products abroad. 

Business men who feel that their interests are harmed by unfair competition may take their complaints to the Federal Trade Commission. The commission investigates the complaints, and if it discovers unfair practices, it requires the offenders to correct them.

Our government protects useful inventions by patents. 

By applying to the Patent Office in the Department of Commerce, an inventor may secure a patent which will give him the sole right to the manufacture of his invention for a period of seventeen years. In a like manner, the author of a book is protected by a copyright which gives him the exclusive right to its publication for fifty-six years. 

The Tariff and Its Effect on Business.-One form of government aid to business is a protective tariff. A tariff is protective when the tax on imports is so high that it forces the foreign manufacturer to sell his products at a higher price than like products made in America. For example, the tariff on shoes may be fifty cents a pair. When this tax is added to the original cost of each pair imported, they will cost more than shoes produced at home. 

The tariff has been a political issue in our country for more than a century. Some people favor a low tariff (for revenue), others want a high tariff (for protection). The arguments commonly used are as follows:

In Favor of the Protective Tariff 

1. It encourages the production of a variety of goods and makes a nation less dependent on other countries. 

2. It protects industries that are just getting started. 

3. It results in higher wages and a higher standard of living by keeping out foreign goods produced by low wages.

Against the Protective Tariff 

1. It raises the prices. 

2. It develops ill-feeling between the different countries.

3. It keeps certain foreign goods out of our country, with the result that other countries will not buy things from us. Thus, we may lose a valuable export trade.

In the past the Democratic party has usually favored a lower tariff than have the Republicans. Recently reciprocal trade agreements with many foreign countries were concluded. (See Chapter XI.) Tariff rates are fixed by acts of Congress, but it is possible for the President to raise or lower rates upon recommendation by the United 

States Tariff Commission. 

Competition, Monopolies, and Government Regula tion. Our economic system is based on the idea of private ownership and competition. When two or more persons or business organizations make the same type of product and try to sell it to the same group of customers, we call it competition. By means of advertising, sales, and service, each business aims to sell its product or services to the public.

Recent years have seen the growth of many forms of business combinations. Large corporations sometimes unite by forming trusts, holding companies, or mergers. Combinations of this sort may abolish competition and establish monopoly. Monopoly in business results when one organization has exclusive control over the production or sale of a commodity so as to prevent effective competition. 

There are different kinds of monopoly. The post office is an example of a government monopoly. However, we are concerned here only with large business combinations which become so powerful as to threaten all forms of competition.

In order to restrain the activities of powerful business combinations and keep them from controlling prices, the

federal government has made a series of laws and regulations. In 1890 Congress passed the Sherman Anti-Trust 

Act to prevent monopolistic combinations of business. In 

1914 it passed the Clayton Anti-Trust Act and established the Federal Trade Commission to prevent unfair competition in business. These laws, however, have been somewhat difficult to enforce.

To what extent should the government regulate and control business? This is one of the serious controversial issues before the people today. In recent years the problem of high taxes on business has been the subject of much discussion. Too much government regulation and high taxes, say many business men, prevent real prosperity. They contend that high taxes prevent private industry from investing money in new equipment and plant expansion, and that constant agitation against business on the part of government officials prevents the necessary confidence which would restore prosperity. On the other hand, many people feel that large business combinations must be strictly regulated by the government in order to prevent high prices and protect the interests of the public. 

Government Ownership of Public Utilities.—Be cause the work of public utilities is so vital to the people, it has been urged that they be owned and operated by the government. In some European countries the government owns and operates the railroads, telephones, telegraphs, and the radio, but in the United States, government ownership has been limited chiefly to the postal service and some electric power projects. 

In recent years, the federal government has actively engaged in the production of electric power. Under the direction of the Tennessee Valley Authority huge dams, constructed at government expense, are now generating electricity and furnishing power for rural and city con-

sumers. The Grand Coulee Dam in the Columbia 

River Basin now furnishes cheap electricity for certain areas in the Northwest. The Columbia River is also spanned by the Bonneville Dam. The government's power 

Courtesy Tennessee Valley Authority 

The Norris Dam, TVA water control project, helps_control seasonal floods and aids navigation on the Tennessee River in months of low flow. It provides for the incidental production of electric power. program in the northwest also includes the Casper-Alcova Dam in Wyoming and the Fort Peck Dam in Montana. 

The federal government has also given financial assistance to local communities to construct their own power plants. 

The purpose of these activities of the federal government is to promote the wider use of electricity and to compel private companies to reduce their rates. The power-production activities of the government, however, are denounced by the private power companies and by other persons who feel that the government should not spend taxpayers' money to compete with private business.

There is much difference of opinion on the general subject of government ownership of utilities. Those who favor government ownership contend that (1) government ownership can give better service at lower cost, (2) the sources of power are natural resources and should not be used for the benefit of private corporations, (3) there are a number of examples of successful operation of utilities by the government.

Those who oppose government ownership claim that (1) private companies can give better service at lower cost, (2) under government ownership public utilities would suffer from too much politics, (3) government operation of utilities has been unsuccessful. 

D. FUNDAMENTAL ISSUES IN THE RELATION BETWEEN GOVERNMENT AND BUSINESS 

Capitalism. The system under which industry in the 

United States and in most other countries is carried on is called capitalistic. Under our system practically all the means for producing and distributing wealth are privately owned. Natural resources, factories, stores, farms, and many other things used by industry and business are in private hands. Business is carried on for the purpose of making profit. Since any individual can start a business, many businesses are started and competition results. One of the main points about capitalism is freedom-freedom for the individual with money and ability to start and develop a business.

Under capitalism, each business man cannot conduct his business entirely as he chooses. He must respect the rights of others. We have seen that some government regulation has become necessary in order to prevent unfair competition and to protect the public interest. Business men are not permitted to engage in a form of enterprise

યોગથી 

Courtesy Atchison, Topeka, and Santa Fe Railway

What are the arguments for and against government ownership of public utilities such as the railroads? whose results would be harmful to the people. We have already seen how the government regulates the sale of harmful drugs.

Socialism.-There are some critics of our economic system who think that changes should be made. Socialists say that the government should own and control all means for the production and distribution of wealth. Under socialism most forms of wealth, such as natural resources, factories, and machines, would belong to the government.

Business enterprises would not be operated for profit, but only for service to the people.

Opponents of socialism claim that it would mean the end of our democracy. They say that if all forms of business enterprise were owned and controlled by the government, the people would lose much of their freedom and might even be ruled by a dictator.

Communism.-Communists are more radical than Socialists. They insist that all forms of private property should be abolished. The word "communism" refers to a

condition under which everything is owned in common. 

While the Socialist believes in bringing in a new system of society gradually, the Communist is usually a revolutionist who believes in the overthrow of capitalism by violent methods.

Dictatorship.-In Russia since World War I there has been an attempt to try out communism on a large scale, although the leaders of that country admit that pure communism is merely an ideal towards which they are striving. Some of the results of the Russian experiment have already been referred to in Chapter I. 

During World War II the Russian people showed great loyalty to their country and their government when they fought bravely to drive the German invaders from their soil. Russian industry also played a large part in bringing victory over the Germans. In spite of great handicaps, large quantities of guns, tanks, planes, and other war materials were produced. Russian leaders, however, acknowledged the help of war materials which were sent from the United States.

In Germany and Italy, where Nazi and Fascist governments existed, private ownership of industry continued to a large extent, although there was also an increase in government-owned business. In these countries, however, all forms of private business were rigidly controlled by the government and there was very little freedom for their businessmen.

Working people suffered greatly in the Fascist and Nazi countries. Wages were fixed by law and strikes were not permitted. As a result of low wages and high prices the standard of living among the working people was steadily forced down.

America's Problem.-Today our country faces many problems as we are called upon to produce large quantities

of materials to defend democracy and to prevent starvation in many parts of the world. At home we must plan to give better food, clothing, and shelter to the one-third of our nation who are ill-fed, ill-clad, and ill-housed.

America must continue to preserve her democracy and any changes must be based on the idea of preserving our fundamental human liberties. 

The Outlook for the Future. With vast natural resources at our disposal our present form of business organization in America has brought us the highest standard of living in the world. In fact, the American standard of living has increased fourfold between 1790 and 1930.

The hours of work have been reduced from an average of about sixty a week to around forty. Geographically, the United States has only six per cent of the world's acreage and only seven per cent of its population, but it has half the world's communication facilities and electric energy, more than one-third of the world's railways; it consumes half the world's coffee and rubber; it uses threefourths of the world's silk; it consumes one-third of the world's coal and two-thirds of the world's crude oil; it produces seventy-seven per cent of the world's automobiles; it has one radio for each five persons, and one telephone for each seven persons. This is indeed a marvelous record of production on the part of American business men. 

What about the future? The National Association of 

Manufacturers lists a few developments that are in sight that may raise our standard of living still higher and make millions of jobs: (1) electrification of all homes in the 

United States, (2) the increased use of electrical appliances in the home, (3) central heating in all homes, (4) air conditioning in homes, factories, and public places, (5) road improvement for greater safety and speed in improved automobiles, (6) stabilization and improvement of aircraft, (7) modernization of railroads to provide greater comfort and greater safety, (8) further development of the food industry so that the entire American nation may have a balanced diet.

Business is the production and distribution of wealth with the object of making profit may be organized as a single proprietorship, a partnership, or a corporation is frequently conducted to produce goods on a large scale may be conducted as a cooperative 

The government aids business in various ways, including the protective tariff regulates business to insure fair competition and protect the public interests regulates public utilities has engaged to some extent in the production of electricity Our form of business organization is based on the capitalistic system is criticized by Socialists and Communists should continue to preserve our liberties has helped to create a high standard of living in our country has many possibilities for the future.

Chapter XXII  A. THE IMPORTANCE OF BUYING AND SELLING Buying and Selling Are Necessary. The buying and selling of goods and services have always been an important part in the life of civilized people. We remember how 

Columbus was trying to find a new way to the East so that the people of Europe could buy spices, silks, and tapestries from the people of that part of the world. Since the coming of the industrial revolution with its great increase in division of labor and specialization, trade and commerce have become more essential than ever before. The workers in stores, factories, offices, and even the farmers must buy most of the things they use. Each exchanges the products of his labor for the products of other men's labor. 

It is the hope of selling products to other people that causes business men to start factories; and it is the desire to buy what they need that causes the workers to take jobs.

Another reason for trade and commerce is the difference in climate and soil in different parts of the earth. Some districts of our country are suited for fruit-growing, while others are best fitted for growing rice or tobacco. On the farms of the Middle West corn and wheat are grown most successfully, while in the South cotton flourishes. Just as sections of the same country vary in their products, so different countries specialize in what they produce. Coffee from Brazil, tin from Bolivia, olives from Spain and Italy, 458

and silk from Japan, are specialized products which most other nations do not have. These countries exchange products and thus a greater abundance and variety of goods are furnished to satisfy the wants of all. 

How Trade Is Carried On.-Trade may be local, as when the farmer brings his eggs to the storekeeper who in turn sells them to his customers. It may be intersectional, as when the wheat and meat of the Middle West are shipped to the more populous centers of the East. It may be international, as when the cotton of the United States is shipped to Japan and silk is sent here from that country. 

Consumers or users rarely purchase directly from the producer. Sometimes this happens when the housewife buys fruits and vegetables directly from the farmer. More frequently, products pass through the hands of a number of different persons until they finally reach the consumer. 

The persons who handle goods in their course from the producer to the consumer are called middlemen or jobbers. 

Just as goods today are often produced on a large scale, so they are often distributed by large corporations who own a number of stores in different communities. These chain stores buy goods in carload lots from producers to supply the stores in their chain. By the purchase of goods in very large quantities and through efficient management, such stores are sometimes enabled to sell things at lower prices than individually owned stores have to charge. In some parts of the country there is considerable opposition to chain stores because people feel that the owners of these stores may become so powerful that they can establish a monopoly in the distribution of certain goods. 

International Trade.-Nations import goods from other countries that they cannot produce at home, and they export to other countries commodities that they produce in greater quantities than they need. We refer to this

ONE HUNDRED YEARS AGO THE HOME AND THE FARM SUPPLIED THE FOOD A NEW ENGLAND HOME 100 YEARS AGO PICTORAL STATERCE, INC Prepared for Cooperative Food Distributors of America type of commerce as international trade. The amount of trade that a nation carries on with other countries has an important effect on its prosperity. Even the United States, which is well supplied with most natural resources, is dependent on foreign nations for such products as tin, rubber, and silk. At the same time our prosperity is dependent to a large extent on our exports. The total production of goods for international trade gives employment to millions of workers. 

The difference between the value of the exports and imports of a country is referred to as the balance of trade. 

When a country imports more goods than it exports, the balance of trade is said to be unfavorable. When the exports are greater than the imports, there is a favorable balance of trade. However, it is important to remember that a country that desires to export a large volume of goods must also be prepared to accept imports. Over a long period of time a nation cannot expect to sell more goods to the rest of the world than it buys from it.
