How Are Prices Determined?-In discussing buying and selling we have to deal with prices. The price of any article is its value expressed in terms of money. Three factors determine price. (1) Prices may rise or fall in accordance with the total amount of money and credit in circulation. If there is a large amount of money and credit in circulation, prices become high, and if the supply of money and credit decreases, prices become low. When the total supply of dollars is increased, there will be more of them available for purchasing goods. When there is so much money and credit in circulation that all prices become exorbitantly high, we call it inflation. (2) Supply and demand also help to determine prices. 

By supply we mean the total amount of goods offered for sale at a given time. During a year when the wheat crop is heavy, prices go down; during another year when the yield is not so good, the price is likely to be much higher. By demand we mean the desire and ability which people have

for buying a certain article. During the months immediately before Christmas many people go shopping for gifts, and during this time the prices of many articles are higher than during the following month of January. (3) The cost of producing and marketing a product determines its selling price. The marketing costs include packaging, advertising, and distribution. The original cost of making the materials in a twenty-five cent tube of tooth paste may be only a few cents, but the cost of packing, advertising, transporting, and selling must be included in the retail price.

B. MONEY AND ITS USES 

The Services of Money.-The simplest form of exchange is barter. When John gives Jim a pocket knife for a flashlight we call it barter. When the early settlers first came to this country, they traded with the Indians, giving them knives and trinkets in exchange for valuable furs. 

The earliest forms of exchange were conducted in this manner.

But trade by barter is clumsy and inconvenient. If a farmer wants an automobile, it would be inconvenient for him to exchange the products of his farm directly for a new car. Something that represents value, a medium of exchange, is needed. This is the service of money; it is material which people will universally accept in place of goods. The farmer sells his wheat and gets money, and with the money he can buy a new automobile. Money is also a measure of value. When the farmer buys a new car, he does not figure directly that the car is worth one hundred fifty bushels of wheat; he thinks of the value of both articles in terms of dollars and cents. Money also makes savings for the future possible. A farmer may not wish to use all the proceeds from the sale of his wheat crop this

year or the next; he may wish to save some of his wealth so that he may be able to live on it during his old age. 

He can sell his farm products, and keep or invest the money for future use.

Kinds of Money.-Many things used for money in the past are no longer used for that purpose today. Beads, salt, skins of animals, and tea have been used as money. Among pastoral people, cattle and sheep were considered so valuable that they were used as a medium of exchange. During the early days of the Jamestown colony, tobacco was used as money. Metals have long been used for this purpose. The first money was probably iron. Bronze and gold were also used in early times. In order to make sure just how much each piece was worth, metals were put into the form of coins with the value and the name of the country or the picture of the ruler that issued them stamped upon them. In order to make for uniformity and prevent dishonesty, the coinage and regulation of the value of money has to be a function of the government. 

Of all the commodities which have served as money in different parts of the world, gold and silver have been the most useful. In order to be suitable for money, a commodity should be: (1) durable-not wear out easily (2) divisible-easily divided into parts (3) recognizable-so that everyone can distinguish real money from counterfeit (4) portable-easy to carry (5) valuable-must be something that people wish to possess; the total amount produced should not vary much from year to year. 

Gold and silver possess these qualities more than any other commodities and have, therefore, become the foundation of most of the money systems of the world.

In actual practice most countries use much more paper money than gold or silver coins. This is because paper is more convenient to carry and to handle. People are willing to accept paper money because they know that the government can back it up with gold and silver. This is true even though not all of our paper money has actual gold and silver in the United States Treasury to back it up. 

Sometimes governments have issued too much paper money and put it into circulation to pay their debts. When this happens, prices become extremely high and a condition of inflation prevails. This is what happened with our Continental paper money during and after the American Revolution and with the German mark after the 

World War. 

Money in the United States.-The United States 

Constitution gives Congress the power "to coin money [and to] regulate the value thereof." From 1834 to 1900 generally speaking both silver and gold were the metals of our currency. By the act of 1834, the ratio of gold and silver was 16 to 1. The gold dollar consisted of 23.22 grains of pure gold, and the silver dollar contained sixteen times that amount of silver. From 1900 to 1933 the United 

States had a single standard of money, the gold dollar.

Because of the low prices during the depression, Congress in 1933 gave President Roosevelt the power to issue more paper money and to reduce the amount of gold in the dollar. In 1934 President Roosevelt devalued the gold dollar, that is, decreased it to 59.06 per cent of its former weight. There would be about 13.71 grains of gold in a gold dollar if it were coined, but all our gold, except that used for purposes other than money, is now in the hands of the government. No gold coins can lawfully get out of the United States Treasury except for export as the government may permit. The chief kinds of money in circula-

Courtesy Bureau of Engraving and Printing 

Uncut sheets of paper money are being examined by employees of the 

Bureau of Engraving and Printing, Washington, D. C. 

tion in the United States now are silver, nickel, and bronze coins, and paper money. There are four different kinds of paper money legally in circulation; some of it is issued by the United States government and some by the Federal 

Reserve System. 

All United States money is printed or coined by the Treasury department. All coins are made at the mints in 

Philadelphia, Denver, and San Francisco. Before the metal is made into coins it reaches the mint in bars, and is known as bullion. Paper money is printed by the Bureau of Engraving and Printing in Washington, D. C. The original plates from which the printing is done are made of steel and are carefully engraved. The paper on which the notes are printed is specially made by a secret process. 

It is a mixture of linen and cotton with silk fibers imbedded.

Money in Other Countries.-Each nation has its own unit of currency and monetary system. The United States has the dollar; England the pound; France the franc;

Italy the lira; Germany the mark; and Russia the ruble. 

If all these countries were on the gold standard, we would always know the value of foreign coins in terms of dollars and cents. But since the depression most of the countries have "devalued" their currencies and so today the money of each country constantly fluctuates in value when compared with the money of other countries. In order to attract tourists some European countries offer their money at special rates of exchange. The present condition makes for considerable uncertainty for people who wish to carry on international trade. Stabilized currencies in the countries of the world would bring about a more friendly feeling between nations and greater prosperity. 

Credit: Doing Business Without Money.-Credit has been referred to as the "life-blood of commerce, "" because nine-tenths of all our business transactions are carried on not with the use of money, but by means of credit. Credit is involved when goods are secured at one time and paid for at a later time. It is based on the confidence which one individual has in the honesty and integrity of another. Our federal government has always paid what it owed and its credit is good. People are, therefore, always willing to lend money to our government even though the interest rates are low. This is also true with individuals. People who always pay their bills promptly have no difficulty in opening up charge accounts or borrowing money when they need it. For an individual to build up his credit "honesty is the best policy."

If you buy a new suit at the store and have it charged, you are buying on credit. If you buy an automobile, you may pay by check which is a form of credit. If you wish to buy a house and lot, you may pay part of it in cash and give the seller a mortgage for the balance. If you wish to start a store or a factory, you may go to the bank and

borrow money on a promissory note. Checks, promissory notes, and mortgages are all forms of credit. 

Credit has many advantages. It makes it possible for people to do business without carrying around large sums of money; it makes long-distance buying and selling possible. It also enables governments, corporations, and individual business men to undertake great enterprises with the use of borrowed money. Through the sale of bonds, cities build schools, states construct roads, and the people benefit from these improvements while they are being paid for. A business man may frequently use his credit by obtaining a loan from a bank. Thus, the owner of a store who needs money in the fall of the year to finance his purchase of Christmas stock may go to the bank and borrow the money on a promissory note.

C. HOW BANKS SERVE THE PUBLIC 

The Service of Banks.-Banks are institutions which receive, exchange, and lend money and credit. Like other corporations, they are organized under a charter by stockholders who expect to receive profits on their investment in the form of dividends. Modern business could not do without the important services of banks. Let us summarize some of the important things that the banks do today: (1) They are places where people can bring valuables for safekeeping. This was one of the original purposes of banks and this service is still provided by modern banks which have safe deposit boxes for rent to their customers. (2) They receive money for deposit from their customers. A person can deposit his money in the bank for safekeeping. It is often convenient for him to pay his bills by check and he can draw money from his account as he needs it. (3) They receive money for savings. When money is

Courtesy of the National City Bank of New York

Banks receive, exchange, and lend money and credit. Modern business could not do without their services. placed in the savings department of a bank, the depositor receives a small amount of interest on it. Usually, advance notice is required before a depositor can draw out large sums from his savings account. (4) They lend money received from both checking and savings accounts. When banks loan money, they always receive interest for this service and in this manner they earn money to pay their expenses and to make a profit. Banks make a profit because on the money which they receive and handle, they pay a low rate of interest or none at all, and when they loan this money they always receive a higher rate of interest. When the owner of a business borrows $10,000 from the bank for a period of ninety days, he gives the bank a promissory note for this amount, and the interest on his loan is paid in advance. Instead of giving him the full amount of the loan, $10,000, the bank will first deduct the amount of interest. In this case, the interest at six per cent for ninety days would be $150.

Courtesy Farm Security Administration: Photo by Lee 

This bank scene was taken in San Augustine, Texas. 

Deducting the amount of the interest is called discounting the note. The borrower from a bank usually has the amount of the loan credited to his account and draws checks against it. A bank may require a borrower to deposit some security, such as stocks and bonds as collateral to guarantee the repayment of the loan. Sometimes an additional person or persons sign a borrower's promissory note and guarantee repayment if the borrower defaults. 

Banks do not loan money to a person who is not known to them or who does not have a good financial standing. (5) They usually have special departments which give advice to their customers on financial matters and help them to make safe and profitable investments. (6) In a more general way, we can say that banks increase the supply of money and credit; they have sometimes been called factories of credit. By the use of checks and drafts, by issuing paper money, and by loaning out money on the basis of promissory notes, banks perform essential services in the modern business world. They make

possible a large number of business transactions without the use of actual cash.

In addition to regular checks which depositors may draw on their accounts, some checks are certified. This means that the bank writes the word "certified" on the check and guarantees its payment. When a bank gives its own check for a certain amount of money, it is called a cashier's check. The draft is a bank check written by one bank to another. It is often used when a person wants to pay money to someone else who lives a considerable distance away. People who travel in this country or abroad use travelers' checks. These checks can be purchased from banks or other financial institutions in the form of a booklet which bears the purchaser's signature. When the check is properly signed by the owner, it can be used as money. 

Kinds of Banks.-Banks may be either state or national. A state bank gets its charter from the government of the state in which it is organized, and operates under the banking laws of that state. A national bank is chartered by the federal government and conducts its business under laws made by Congress. 

Banks sometimes specialize in one type of service, although a large bank has several departments giving different services. Most banks are commercial; they receive money from depositors, cash their checks, and make loans for short periods of time. Savings banks specialize in receiving money from depositors who are able to leave it for a longer period of time. They also make loans. Trust companies accept money to be managed or invested for other people. Often a certain sum of money is placed in a trust company so that the income from it can be used by the person to whom the money belongs. Investment banks lend money to large industries and devote themselves to selling stocks and bonds.

F. & L. Photo Service, Atlanta, Georgia 

The Federal Reserve Bank of Atlanta is one of twelve federal reserve banks in the United States. The Federal Reserve System was established in 1914.

The Federal Reserve System.-Our national banking system was organized by an act of Congress in 1863, but the Federal Reserve System was not established until 1914. All national banks must belong to this system and state banks may become members under certain conditions.

Under this system the country is divided into twelve districts, with one Federal Reserve Bank located in each. 

This bank does its business with banks rather than with individuals; it is a "bankers' bank." For the various banks which are members in its district it performs the same services as ordinary banks do for their depositors. It receives deposits, makes loans to member banks, and issues paper money.

At the head of the Federal Reserve System is a board of governors, composed of seven members, who are appointed by the President for a term of fourteen years. The Federal

Reserve Board has extensive powers over our banking

system; it regulates the supply of money and credit, and in this manner often helps business during time of greatest need. 

Safeguarding the Business of Banks.-Because of the great importance of banks in the business life of our country, the government regulates banks closely and supervises their work in considerable detail. Inspectors visit banks frequently to examine their records. Banks must also publish regular statements of their financial condition. Each state has its own banking laws to regulate state banks within its borders; the federal government exercises control over national banks and to a certain extent over state banks.

In order to protect the depositors against possible loss from dishonest bank employees, these persons are heavily bonded, which means that bonding companies have pledged themselves to make good any losses by embezzlement. In spite of these precautions, banks sometimes fail and are forced to close their doors because they do not have sufficient resources to pay back their depositors. 

Banks may fail because of bad investments, poor management, or dishonesty on the part of some of the officials. A bank only keeps in cash a moderate amount of money, not enough to pay off all its depositors at once. If somebody starts a story that a certain bank is in difficulties, hundreds of its depositors may rush at once to withdraw their money. This is called a "run" on the bank. If the bank does not have enough cash on hand to pay its depositors, it must close its doors. 

During the depression period of the early 1930's so many banks failed that the federal government set up a system of insurance for bank deposits. This work is carried on by the Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation.

About ninety per cent of the banks belong to this system

and contribute to a fund from which losses due to bank failures are paid. Under this plan, even if a bank fails, a depositor will receive one hundred per cent of his money up to $5,000 and a certain percentage of the amount over that sum. During recent years the number of bank failures has greatly decreased.

Other Financial Institutions. In a city where there are a number of banks there is usually a clearing house. 

All checks and drafts on the various member banks go through this central organization and the account of each one is balanced with that of the others. This makes it unnecessary for banks to send checks and drafts back and forth among each other.

For people who wish to find a good place to store savings, building and loan associations are important financial institutions. These corporations lend money to persons wishing to build houses. People who wish to save a certain amount of money regularly at a good rate of interest, can buy shares of stock in these institutions by paying a small amount each month. 

Buying and selling

Chapter XXIII  A. THE IMPORTANCE OF TRANSPORTATION AND COMMUNICATION How Our Communities Depend on Transportation and Communication.-Without our modern means of transportation and communication-railroads, automobiles, the telegraph, the telephone, the postal service, airplanes, and the radio-our business world would soon be at a standstill. Suppose there was a general strike of railroad workers and truck drivers. There would be no mail and no newspapers. The machinery of our factories. would soon stop running from lack of power and raw materials. The supply of available food would soon be exhausted. In such a case, the people of our cities would starve within a short time.

As transportation and communication have developed, we have become more dependent upon other people and foreign nations. Because of division of labor and specialization in work, different sections of our country and the countries of the world must constantly exchange their products. The farmers of the Middle West and the South ship many of their products to the large cities of the East, and our factories send their finished products long distances to find markets. For raw materials many of our industries are dependent on rubber and tin which are brought in ships and on trains from distant continents. 

Over four million people in the United States make their living in some activity connected with transportation. 477

A hundred years ago it was seriously argued in Congress that Oregon should not become a part of the United States because it was too far away from the nation's capital, that it would take a member of Congress from that state six months to reach Washington and six months to return home. Today modern means of transportation and communication bind our great country together and make a unified nation possible. Through these modern devices the people of different sections of our country have come to know each other better, and we are almost next-door neighbors to the world. 

The Importance of Modern Means of Communica tion in a Democracy. In a democracy where the people are the rulers, the newspapers, the radio, and other means of spreading information are especially important. The problems of our country must be kept before the people so that they may decide wisely on what shall be done about them. When two or more rival candidates run for public office, the people are usually dependent on the newspapers and the radio to get the facts about each one. The importance of public opinion has already been discussed in 

Chapter X. 

In those countries where dictators have come into power, the government has established a rigid control over newspapers, radio, and other means of communication. Today the people of these countries learn only the propaganda which their leaders want them to know. The welfare of modern democracy, therefore, requires that the means of communication must always remain free from government control. At the same time selfish private interests should never be permitted to monopolize the radio or the newspapers to spread false ideas. The constitutional provisions for freedom of speech and press must be jealously guarded.

B. METHODS AND PROBLEMS OF TRANSPORTATION The Development of Early Forms of Transporta tion.-Early man was his own beast of burden. He carried his goods in his hands, on his head, or on poles over his shoulder. Later there were also rude sledges for dragging things over the ground. About four or five thousand years ago came one of the most significant steps toward civilization-the invention of the wheel. At first this was only a circular piece of wood cut from the trunk of a tree, but it greatly increased the load that could be carried and the speed of travel. By this time man had tamed the ox and the donkey, and these animals were made to carry loads and to draw wagons. The horse appeared later and was at first used chiefly in war. The camel became the "ship of the desert" in very ancient times and long caravans plodded their way over hundreds of miles between centers of population. 

Long before man had invented wheels or used animals as beasts of burden, he had made boats for transportation on water. It was first discovered that a hollowed out tree trunk or the inflated skin of an animal could be pushed through the water by a flat piece of wood. In time men improved on these early forms of water transportation and built boats and ships of some size and fitted them with sails. The earliest civilizations developed along bodies of water where transportation by boats and ships came into regular use. Sea power has been the basis on which most great nations have been built. 

Water Transportation.-Picture Columbus' flagship, the Santa Maria, alongsidę a modern ocean liner and you will realize the progress that has been made in water transportation over a period of four or five hundred years. The 

Santa Maria was about the size of a small house, while a

modern ocean liner is several blocks long. The Santa Maria was equipped with sails and required weeks or even months for a single crossing of the Atlantic; the modern ocean liner is powered with oil-burning turbine engines and can make a trip to Europe in five days. The liner offers the comforts and the conveniences of a modern hotel and it may carry as many as three thousand persons at one time, compared with fifty sailors on Columbus' little ship. 

It was the development of the commercially practicable steamboat by Robert Fulton in 1807 that made possible much faster water transportation. In cur own country it stimulated the westward movement of our people by rivers and lakes.

To make water travel easier and more direct, canals were built. In 1825 New York completed the Erie Canal connecting the Great Lakes with the Hudson River. Other states also built many miles of canals. Most of these are no longer in use, as they were made obsolete by the development of railroads. Some canals, however, are of great use and importance today. The "Soo" canal from Lake 

Superior to Lake Huron does an enormous amount of business every year. The Barge Canal, financed by the state of New York, now provides water transportation directly from the Middle West through the Great Lakes to the ocean. A complete system of direct water transportation from the Great Lakes to the Atlantic by way of the St. Lawrence River has been proposed as a joint project between the United States and Canada. The most famous modern canal, the Panama Canal, was constructed by the United States and opened for traffic in 1914. The Suez 

Canal was built by an international commission and was opened in 1869. The federal government encourages the use of inland waterways by dredging rivers, improving harbors, and building dams to deepen channels. Transpor-

Courtesy United States Lines 

The S. S. “America," new seventeen-million dollar ocean liner of the 

United States Lines, is the largest and fastest vessel ever to be constructed in the United States. The picture is from a painting by 

Fred Hoertz. tation by water costs less than any other kind, but it is much slower and, therefore, not satisfactory for perishable goods unless the ships are equipped with refrigeration plants. 

The merchant marine of a nation consists of its ships engaged in commerce, whether publicly or privately owned. A large merchant marine is a great help to a country. It is especially important in time of war. It should always be a profitable business, giving employment to sailors, to shipbuilders, and to business men who own and control the ships. The federal government provides that only American ships may engage in coastwise commerce. 

During the second World War there was a need for many ships to carry men, guns, tanks, and airplanes to every far-flung battle front of a global war. The United

States Maritime Commission, consisting of five members appointed by the President, was authorized to carry on a huge ship-building program. Builders, faced at the outset by a shortage of trained workers and by inability to obtain materials, overcame these difficulties and American shipyards soon established a production record far greater than the emergency effort of the first World War. 

The Development of Railroads.—It is a little more than a hundred years since railroads came into use in this country. This form of transportation had its humble origin in the coal mines of England where horses pulled wagons over tracks made of timbers laid lengthwise. 

George Stephenson in England was the inventor of the first practical locomotive in 1825. In America the first railroad to carry passengers and freight was the Baltimore and Ohio, which first began to use a locomotive in 1830. 

Today railroads are found on every continent of the globe. Our country is well equipped with railway lines. No other country has so many miles of tracks. Modern developments include air-conditioned coaches and streamlined trains which are usually powered by diesel engines. 

On some lines transportation is made faster and more comfortable by the use of electric power. 

Railroads are ribbons of steel which have done much to unify our country whose vast area spreads across an entire continent. Farming, manufacturing, and mining have developed in many areas because railroads have developed and thus made the transportation and exchange of goods possible. The importance of the railroads for our national prosperity can hardly be overemphasized. 

Every year railroads consume about one-fourth of all the bituminous coal mined in the nation, about one-fifth of the cut timber, as well as vast quantities of electric power and petroleum. More than a million people in the United

Courtesy Union Pacific Railroad Company 

The old and the new in railroad transportation present a great contrast. 

The coal-burning locomotive at the left was in use in 1869. Modern trains are usually powered by diesel engines or run by electric power. 

Courtesy The Milwaukee Road 

Clean, air-conditioned coaches, designed for the comfort of the passengers, have added to the pleasure of modern railway travel.

States have invested their money in the railroads by purchasing stocks and bonds of the various companies which operate the lines.

Problems of Railroads. During the depression years of the 1930's few railroads made a profit; many operated at a loss. Of the total mileage, fully one third was either in bankruptcy, or in the hands of receivers. Private automobiles and buses, as well as airplanes, caused railroad passenger traffic to decline 73 per cent during these years. 

The motor truck had cut into railroad freight revenue. 

Thousands of miles of pipe lines between the oil-producing areas and the refineries on the eastern seaboard had taken away some of the business of handling petroleum and gasoline, while long-distance power transmission had cut the traffic in soft coal.

World War II and the national defense program, however, brought a great increase in business and prosperity for the railroads. The volume of traffic, both passenger and freight, became greater than in any previous time in our history. The shortage of oil tankers made it necessary for the railroads to carry more oil in tank cars to the Atlantic Coast states. The rationing of gasoline and the shortage of tires, as well as the inability to purchase new equipment, handicapped trucks, buses, and private automobiles, and a large part of their tonnage and passengers sought the railroads. Millions of men in the armed services had to be transported from one part of the country to another and passenger trains were crowded to capacity. A record-breaking performance of the railroads was made even though there was a decrease in the total amount of equipment.

Railroad rates are regulated by the Interstate Commerce Commission which consists of eleven men appointed by the President with the consent of the Senate.

NEW YORK Courtesy Greyhound Lines 

Buses have been responsible for a part of the decline in railroad passenger traffic.

The Automobile.-The automobile powered by the gasoline engine was first invented during the 1890's. In the United States in 1896 there were only four automobiles. Today there are more than 28,000,000 registered motor vehicles in our country, more than one for every five inhabitants. Between four and five million cars are manufactured annually in the United States alone. An endless stream of cars, trucks, and buses passes over our main highways every day. Bus routes carry twice as many passengers as the railroad.

No invention has more rapidly changed human life than has the automobile. It has made extensive travel possible to many people who formerly were unable to go more than a few miles from their homes. It has broadened their knowledge of their country and made them acquainted with people and customs in distant places. School buses help to make the consolidation of rural schools possible. 

The automobile has brought the farmer closer to town; the advantages of the town are now within a few minutes

Courtesy California Toll Bridge Authority 

Great bridges, such as the San Francisco-Oakland Bay Bridge pictured here, are an important part of our transportation system. of riding from the country home. With the use of modern farm machinery, operated by the gas engine, much of the old drudgery of the farm has been eliminated, and production has been vastly increased. The use of automobiles has made oil-refining one of our great industries, and is responsible for much of the growth of the steel and rubber industries. It has caused the building of millions of miles of hard-surfaced streets and highways. Directly or indirectly the automobile industry furnishes employment to millions of people. We have already referred in Chapter XVII to the effect that the automobile has had on our recreational activities.

The coming of the automobile has also brought a number of problems. We have discussed the problem of automobile accidents in Chapter XVIII. Most of our cities were laid out when the few vehicles used were drawn by horses. 

In these earlier days narrow streets could easily take care of the traffic. But today with thousands of automobiles,

Courtesy Public Roads Administration 

The Henry Hudson Parkway in New York is an example of a modern road built to handle maximum loads of traffic safely. Note the elimination of grade crossings. trucks, and taxicabs, traffic control on our city streets often becomes a serious matter. Practically every community has its parking problem. Many cities now have numerous one-way streets and place severe restrictions on parking in business sections. In some cases streets are being made wider and elevated highways are built for through traffic. 

Building Good Roads.-The ancient Romans built good roads which were surfaced with large blocks of stone. 

During the early history of our country, however, roads were often not much more than cart tracks, with ruts so deep in bad weather as to make travel almost impossible. 

Early in the nineteenth century improved roads were built. They were privately owned and operated and toll had to be paid for their use. 

The use of automobiles has created a demand for good roads, so that today we find hard-surfaced roads in most

parts of the country. The cost of these roads is met principally by taxes on motor vehicles, registration fees, license fees, and taxes on gasoline. 

City, county, and state governments engage in road building. States usually have highway departments or commissions which are responsible for the building and upkeep of the roads. Since 1916 the federal government has also appropriated money to the states for road building. Each state which receives such funds is required to appropriate an amount equal to that advanced by the federal government for use in the state. 

There are many benefits from good roads. They make it easier for the farmer to market his produce and to secure needed goods from the city. Farm land immediately increases in value wherever good roads are built. In towns which are served by good roads, business activity increases because people can come there more easily to make purchases. Good roads are now a vital part of our transportation system which makes our complex modern business life possible.

Traffic in the Air.-The first experiments in air travel were made with balloons. In 1783 two Frenchmen remained afloat for twenty-five minutes while sailing over most of Paris. Lighter-than-air craft, known as dirigibles, were used by Germany during the World War. After the 

War a number of dirigibles were built by our country but most of them have met disaster. Germany has made considerable progress in establishing commercial flying by dirigibles, although the explosion of the Hindenburg in 1937 slowed the progress of lighter-than-air transportation.

Most of the progress in aviation has been due to the development of the heavier-than-air machine the airplane. The successful flight of the Wright brothers on the

Courtesy The Glenn L. Martin Company

Air traffic in the United States is increasing each year. In addition to the continental routes, there are regular air line schedules across the 

Pacific and across the Atlantic. coast of North Carolina in 1903 was the real beginning of modern aviation. Since that time inventors and scientists from many countries have contributed to the improvement of this new means of transportation. Safety devices to overcome the menace of fog and storm are constantly being perfected. A number of adventurous men and women have pioneered in the development of longdistance aviation. The first successful flight across the 

Atlantic was made by two Englishmen who crossed to Newfoundland in 1919. In 1927 came Colonel Charles A. 

Lindbergh's epic flight from New York to Paris. In 1933 

Wiley Post flew around the world in less than eight days, and in 1938 Howard Hughes in less than four.

Today aviation is a vital link in the transportation system of the world. In both Europe and America important cities are connected with a network of air lines which carry passengers, mail, and merchandise. Passenger traffic is increasing each year. The timetables

issued by the various air lines in our country are similar to those issued by the railroads. The cost of travel is approximately six cents a mile, about the same as railroad Pullman rates. Passenger and air mail service has been

This interior view of a modern air liner shows the passengers comfortably reading, knitting, and talking. Some planes have sleeping accommodations. in operation across the Pacific for several years and was started across the Atlantic during the summer of 1939. 

In order to make air transportation generally available many cities have constructed airports. The federal government regulates air transportation through the Civil Aeronautics Administration which licenses pilots, as well as planes, and enforces rules for safety in the air. 

C. METHODS AND PROBLEMS OF COMMUNICATION The Development of Communication.-Early man knew no way of communication except by means of gestures. Languages have changed and developed as man has

become more civilized. The language of a less civilized people includes a few hundred words, but today we can find 700,000 words in a large English dictionary. Some 

5,000 languages and dialects are spoken in the world. 

Even the most primitive people always had some system of signaling by which they could transmit information over long distances. Beacon lights, such as fires and torches, as well as smoke columns were used. Among some primitive tribes the beating of drums was a signal calling people together for warfare. 

The use of writing was an important step forward in the development of communication. Picture writing came into use so long ago that its beginnings have been lost to us. People in different parts of the world finally came to develop their own alphabets. 

The earliest books were written by hand on skins or parchment which made them very expensive to make. Two thousand years ago the Chinese learned to print by carving the characters for a whole page on a block of wood Printing from movable type was first invented by Johannes Gutenberg in Germany about 1440. When the 

American colonists came here they brought the knowledge of printing with them and set up the first printing press at Cambridge, Mass., in 1638.

Freedom of the Press Triumphs.-Newspapers have come to be one of our most important means of communication. The first newspaper in America was published in 

1689 in Boston, but it soon had to be abandoned because the governor of the colony objected to it. In 1704 the first regular newspaper, the Boston News-Letter, was founded.

Between 1733 and 1735 events occurred in New York which were of great importance for the future of our democracy. The New York Weekly Journal, published by

John Peter Zenger, severely criticized Governor Cosby for dishonesty in his conduct of public affairs. Zenger was arrested and thrown into jail. After several months of imprisonment he was tried under the accusation of "false, scandalous, malicious, and seditious libel." The trial soon became a center of interest and attention throughout the colonies. After only a few minutes of discussion the colonial jury declared Zenger not guilty. The decision was greeted with cheers and speeches, and was rightfully hailed as a great victory for the freedom of the press. 

Newspapers Today. The newspapers of the early period of our history would look very odd to us today. 

There were no large headlines, no comics, and few illustrations. Advertising regarding land sales, merchandise for sale, and rewards for runaway slaves was prominent. Letters from travelers or from those who had been away were frequent, but there was little real news. 

As a result of the invention of the linotype and monotype (typesetting machines) and the improvement of the printing press, newspapers of today are printed at high speed and in large quantities.

In 1917 the number of daily papers had reached 2,514, but since then the number of different papers has been somewhat decreased because some of the smaller papers have been consolidated with larger ones. The average circulation of daily newspapers has passed 40,000,000 copies. 

The ordinary newspaper which sells for two or three cents contains reading matter equivalent to that of a fair-sized novel. The income of American newspapers for a typical year has exceeded one billion dollars; almost three-fourths of this amount comes from advertising, and the remainder from circulation.

The telephone, the telegraph, the teletype, and the radio make it possible to assemble the news of the world

overnight. For local news a newspaper depends on its staff of reporters who visit the police station, the city hall, the courthouse, and other places where news can be secured. 

Accidents, crimes, fires, and social affairs, all help to make local news. News from other cities and other countries is usually obtained through press associations. These organizations have hundreds of representatives in all parts of the world who collect news items and report them to a central office from which they are sent out to all the newspapers belonging to the association. 

The United Press, the 

Associated Press, and the International News 

Service are the three leading associations of this kind. Some of our larger papers maintain correspondents in the important centers of 

Europe and of Asia.

Courtesy Farm Security Administration: 

Photo by Lee 

This linotype operator is setting type in a newspaper office in San Augustine, Texas. Most newspapers are now set on linotype machines. 

The Services of a Newspaper in a Democracy.— 

The effect of the newspaper on public opinion has been discussed in Chapter X. We have already learned that democracy cannot exist unless the people are able to get reliable information about public questions. In a large country, such as ours, the people are largely dependent on the newspaper and the radio for getting information. This

494. 

puts a serious responsibility upon our newspapers. They should at all times discuss the issues in our elections, and give information about the candidates for office. The activities of our government should be adequately and truthfully reported so that the people may be able to decide issues fairly on the basis of facts. Fortunately, American newspapers are the best in the world; most of them are fair and honest, and do not use their columns to arouse prejudice and to mislead the people. But we must remember that most newspapers are partisan and take sides in political contests. For this reason it becomes necessary for the intelligent citizen to read papers presenting different points of view. The intelligent newspaper reader will always read more than the headlines; he will always note the difference between real facts and mere opinions and rumors.

The Postal Service. Our system of communication is aided by the government which has established the postal service, making it possible to send written messages from place to place at a small cost. The development of our postal service goes back to 1753 when Benjamin Franklin was made deputy Postmaster General of the colonies. In 1775 he became the first Postmaster General of the United States with authority to establish a line of post offices from Falmouth, Me., to Savannah, Ga. Since that time the postal service has developed in a marvelous manner.

In 1789 there were only seventy-five post offices; today there are about 46,000. The United States postal service has come to be the largest business concern in the world, employing more than 250,000 persons. Letters, newspapers, and magazines can be quickly sent from one part of the country to the other. All mail matter is divided into four classes. The first class consists of sealed letters,

postcards, and other sealed matter. The second class includes newspapers and all other periodicals issued regularly. The third class consists of circulars and other printed matter, weighing eight ounces or less. The fourth class is the parcel post and includes all matter (except first and second class) weighing more than eight ounces. At a slight extra cost almost any mailable matter may be sent by air mail. 

The International 

Postal Union makes it possible for us to send mail to practically any part of the world. Our postal service also makes it possible to send money from one person to another by means of the money order. Valuable parcels or letters may be insured or registered. 

Postal savings banks receive deposits of money up to $2,500. 

Courtesy American Telephone and 

Telegraph Company

These frames contain a very small fraction of the equipment required for a single dial central telephone office. 

Our postal service is under the control of the Postmaster General, who is a member of the President's Cabinet. A large number of positions in the postal service are filled through civil service examinations. 

The Telegraph and the Telephone.-The postal service is not fast enough for some of the needs of our modern business world. The two methods of communication, the telegraph and the telephone, make it possible to send messages over wires. The first of these inventions, the

The United States leads all countries in communication facilities. There is one telephone for every seven persons in the 

United States; one for every seventy-seven persons in Italy. telegraph, was made by Samuel F. B. Morse, who first succeeded in sending messages by wire over a long distance in 1844. The first words sent over this line between 

Washington and Baltimore were: "What hath God wrought!" Today the telegraph is a convenient method for sending short written messages quickly. Undersea cables also make it possible to communicate by telegraph with all parts of the world. 

The telephone, invented by Alexander Graham Bell, was first exhibited at the Philadelphia Centennial Ex-

position in 1876 and created a great sensation. Today the telephone has come to be one of the greatest single factors in our business and social life. It has changed our method of doing business and it has made neighbors of people living miles apart. Business men a thousand miles apart can now talk over the terms of a deal without leaving their offices. By means of the telephone we can talk to persons in every part of our country and in foreign countries as well. The United States has more telephones than the rest of the world put together. 

Both the telegraph and the telephone are public utilities and as such are regulated by the Federal Communications Commission.

The Radio.-Practical use of the radio was first made at sea when a ship in distress would send out a call and other ships would go immediately to help. The first radio broadcast was made by Station KDKA, Pittsburgh, in 1920, when the results of the Harding-Cox presidential election were sent out over the air. The use of radio equipment on ships has been greatly extended, and aviation is also made safer by the use of radio. Today we have come to look upon the radio as a great source of entertainment and news. To a certain extent the radio has come to be a competitor of the newspaper in furnishing news. Important international events during September and October of 1938 proved that radio is a force to be reckoned with in shaping modern history. For the first time great numbers of people could follow minute-to-minute news of a world crisis. The course of world history depends to some extent upon how radio is used and by whom. The freedom of the air, as well as freedom of the press, must be preserved in order to maintain democracy. 

The effect of the radio on public opinion has been discussed in Chapter X, and on recreation in Chapter XVII.

UNIT EIGHT IMPROVING WORK AND LIVING IN THE AMERICAN DEMOCRACY 

Since democracy exists only for the good of its citizens, it constantly seeks to improve conditions under which people work and live. How to improve living in our American democracy is the theme of Unit Eight.

Our country has had an abundant supply of wealth in the form of natural resources. Chapter Twenty-Four shows the need for conservation of our natural resources and describes ways by which this is being done.

Cooperation and teamwork among employers and employees are necessary for the success of our democracy. Chapter Twenty-Five describes the beginnings of organized labor and discusses the relations of employers and employees today.

In a democracy work as well as government should promote the welfare of the people. Chapter Twenty-Six shows some of the problems of workers; it describes the efforts of the government to find a solution for these problems.

Chapter Twenty-Seven shows some of the problems of farmers and discusses the efforts of the government to solve these problems. 

Chapter Twenty-Eight describes standards of living. It also discusses the problems of poverty and suggests ways by which a person can manage his income wisely.

Chapter XXIV  A. THE IMPORTANCE OF CONSERVATION 

Natural Resources in the United States.-In the production of wealth, natural resources, consisting of soil, forests, water, and minerals, are extremely important. 

National prosperity and material civilization are based on them.

Our country has been blessed with a great abundance and variety of natural resources. Stuart Chase in his book, Rich Land, Poor Land, describes the American continent as it was when the white man first found it. It is estimated that in those days almost half the acreage of the country was in forest, including a variety of useful timber unequaled anywhere on earth. An almost unbroken area of forest land stretched from the Atlantic to well beyond the Mississippi. Forests covered the three great Western mountain ranges up to the timber line; both California and the Pacific Northwest had stands of magnificent trees. 

The waving grasses of the prairies covered one-sixth of all the country.

Most of the continent had a thick layer of rich soil. 

Only two and one half per cent of the country was outright desert. Beneath the forests and grasses lay rich deposits of coal, iron, petroleum, natural gas, copper, zinc, lead, gold, silver, and other minerals. Wild life in the form of buffaloes, deer, elk, moose, bear, beavers, and all kinds of fish, was found in great abundance. 503

ROSENFIELD'S  

Courtesy Farm Security Administration: Photo by Mydans 

Floods, dust storms, and soil erosion have wasted our natural resources. 504

The Need for Conservation of Natural Resources.- 

Like spendthrifts who inherit a large fortune and squander their money recklessly, the American people have acquired wasteful habits in the use of their natural resources. We have been called the most wasteful people on earth. Twothirds of the great forests of our land have been cleared away, without sufficient provision for their replacement.

The cutting down of our forests has been followed by great floods resulting in the loss of hundreds of lives and the destruction of millions of dollars worth of property. 

The constant erosion of the hillsides and the great dust storms of recent years have taken millions of tons of good soil from the farms of America. Improper methods of mining have brought about a needless waste of coal and mineral resources. In one field in 

Texas alone a billion cubic feet of natural gas is daily blown into the air. This is enough, says the Natural Resources Board, "to supply every householder in the United States now consuming either natural or manufactured gas.' 

Although our resources are by no means all used, they are rapidly diminishing. Some of them, such as coal, oil, iron, and silver can never be replaced. Conservation means the intelligent and economical use of our natural resources. By wise methods of conservation our resources can be made useful to us and can also be preserved so that a sufficient amount will be available for many generations in the future.

The Conservation Movement. The conservation movement in America had its birth in the effort to save our forests. As early as 1891 an act was passed by Congress authorizing the President to withdraw forest areas from public sale to be set apart for forest reserves. 

After 1902 President Theodore Roosevelt and Gifford

Pinchot, who became Chief Forester, did a great deal to arouse public interest in conservation. During President Theodore Roosevelt's administration more than 234,000,000 acres of government land, containing valuable resources, were withdrawn from the possibility of private ownership. Each President since Roosevelt has done his part in conserving the nation's natural resources, in spite of strong hostility of selfish individuals and corporations eager to secure quick profits by gaining control of power sites, mineral deposits, and forest lands. 

In 1934 President Franklin D. Roosevelt appointed the 

National Resources Committee to prepare and submit to the President a program for the conservation of the land, water, mineral, and other resources of the United States. 

B. METHODS AND PROBLEMS OF CONSERVATION 

Conservation of Forests.-Forests are among our most valuable natural resources. We use an enormous amount of wood each year for such purposes as building materials, furniture, poles, railroad ties, and pulp for the manufacture of paper. As a result of the development of the cellulose industry there are almost four thousand different uses for wood or wood products. But forests are also valuable for other purposes than providing a supply of timber. When it rains the forests act as a sponge, holding some of the moisture in a matting of roots and dead leaves. 

In this way the flow of water in our streams is somewhat equalized the year round and floods are prevented. The roots of the trees on steep hillsides also help to hold back the valuable top soil and keep it from being washed away. 

Forests are also valuable as a protection for our wild animal life and provide places for healthful recreation. 

The people of the United States are using up their forests four times as fast as they are being replaced by

growth. There are two ways by which we can provide for our supply of lumber in the future. We must find methods to stop the waste of forests and we must grow new trees for the future. We can decrease much of the waste of our forests by preventing forest fires. Each year fires destroy forest areas as large as the states of New 

Hampshire and Vermont combined.

Methods of preventing and fighting forest fires were discussed in Chapter XVIII. We can also conserve our forests by the prevention of waste in logging operations and in preparing lumber for use. Waste pieces of wood not used for lumber can be made into a variety of products. 

One of the best ways to conserve our forests 

Courtesy U. S. Forest Service 

These fire fighters are digging a trench around a forest fire in an attempt to control its spread. is by planting trees or by reforestation. Too often in the past lumbermen have made no effort to save the saplings when the large trees were felled. These young trees, if left standing, would have provided lumber for future generations. By careful planning and systematic work forests can be continually replaced; this has been proved by the experience of European countries which have practiced forest conservation for over a century. A number of states in our country now have nurseries that grow and distribute young trees for planting without charge or at a

low price. Most of the states also have extensive forest reserves which are protected by rangers who are constantly on the lookout for forest fires. 

The federal government today has a forest reserve of 160,000,000 acres, located in thirty-two states and in 

Courtesy Civilian Conservation Corps 

This CCC field crew is helping to conserve our forests by planting young trees to replace those cut or destroyed annually. 

Alaska. Our national forests cover an area as large as the two states of Montana and Wyoming. These reserves are held in trust by the government for the welfare of the people. They are under the supervision of the Forest 

Service in the Department of Agriculture which regulates the cutting of timber, issues grazing permits for stockmen, builds and supervises camps for tourists, and establishes patrols as protection against fires.

The Conservation of the Soil.-The most vital of all our natural resources is the land itself. The fertility of the land depends largely upon the character of the top soil. 

It is estimated that five hundred years of weathering and

Each block represents 135 million acres of soil. plant growth are required to make one inch of good top soil. But hundreds of millions of tons of our valuable soil are carried away every year by erosion-the work of wind and water. One-sixth of our farm land-as much land as there is in all of Illinois, Ohio, North Carolina, and Maryland-has already lost the greater part of its top soil, leaving nothing but rock and sand. Another sixth of our farm land is already beginning to suffer from erosion. 

Under natural conditions the roots of the grass and trees hold the soil against the wash of rain water and force of the wind. But when the land is plowed, loose earth is exposed and erosion by water on hillsides becomes very rapid. In this manner billions of tons of soil are washed out of our fields and pastures every year. 

On the western plains wind rather than rain is responsible for soil erosion. Shortly after 1870 wheat farmers began moving westward and plowed up the sod on new farms. As long as there was moisture wheat grew, but when the rain-which was always scanty-practically failed, great dust storms carried soil from farms in Kansas, Oklahoma, Texas, New Mexico, Colorado, Nebraska,

Both photos: Courtesy Soil Conservation Service 

The Soil Conservation Service has worked out various methods of soil conservation. Two are shown here. Strip rotation of cotton and small grain followed by annual lespedeza is the means employed to combat soil erosion on the South Carolina farm shown above. On the Minnesota field in the lower picture contour strips and crop rotation are used to check erosion. The strips are separated by a contour fence.

and the Dakotas. Some of this soil was carried as far east as the Atlantic Ocean. Dust sometimes killed cattle and covered fields; it buried fence posts and obliterated roads; it sifted under doors and windows and made life miserable for the people of the "dust bowl."

In some parts of the country farmers have deserted their farms because the land is no longer worth cultivating. To deal with this serious problem of erosion the Soil 

Conservation Service in the United States Department of Agriculture was created in 1933. Its experts develop various methods of soil conservation and advise farmers how to check the loss of soil on their land. Wind erosion is being checked by restoring some of the land to grass and by planting belts of fast-growing trees which break the sweep of the winds. Steep hillsides which should never have been plowed are again being planted with trees. Soil losses on slopes can be checked by plowing the land around the hill rather than up and down. Some of our hillsides can be "terraced" with ridges of soil one to three feet high on the sides of the hills. Sometimes strips of grass which hold the soil are planted between rows of corn and cotton. Small dams made of logs and brush have been thrown across hundreds of gullies to check erosion on pasture land by slowing down the flow of water. Some crops, such as clover, beans, peas, and alfalfa, help to check erosion and build up the fertility of the soil as well. 

Farmers who cooperate by increasing their acreage of soil conservation crops receive loans and benefit payments from the government.

Conserving Our Water Resources.-The water in our streams can be put to many worth-while uses. It can be purified and made available for use in the homes and industries of our cities. It can be stored in huge dams and by irrigation systems it can be used to reclaim arid or

semi-arid waste lands. During the spring of the year when our streams are swollen from melting snow and heavy rainfall, water can be held back in dams to control floods. 

During the summer months of the year when rainfall is light, water can be released to improve navigation on our rivers. The power of water can be harnessed to huge turbines which drive generators to make electric power for farms, homes, and industries. In Chapter XX we described some of the large dams constructed by the government to generate electric power, to provide means of irrigation, to promote navigation, and to prevent floods. 

The Boulder Dam is built across the Colorado River at Black Canyon on the boundary line of Arizona and Nevada. It is an attempt to bring the river under control, and to make use of its tremendous power for generating electricity. It stores the water to prevent floods; it supplies vast quantities of water for the largest irrigation project in the world. The great lake created by Boulder Dam has already become a recreational center. 

Another giant undertaking was the construction of the Grand Coulee Dam across the Columbia River about ninety miles from Spokane, Washington. The lake formed by this dam will be used to irrigate one and one-quarter million acres of land. Its power plant will be the largest source of electrical energy in the world. This great project was constructed by the Bureau of Reclamation in the Department of the Interior; water spilled over the dam for the first time in June, 1942.

The Tennessee Valley Authority, created in 1933, is a vast conservation project in the watershed of the Tennessee River which drains land in seven southern states. The program undertaken includes flood control, reforestation, the manufacture of fertilizers, the improvement of navigation, and the generation of electric power. It is an experi-

Courtesy Bureau of Reclamation, Department of the Interior 

The lake formed by the Grand Coulee Dam across the Columbia River I will be used to irrigate one and one-quarter million acres of land. One method of ditch irrigation now in use is shown below.

ment in planning for the conservation of natural and human resources in an entire geographical region. A series of dams, some of them completed, others now under construction or proposed for the future, are a part of a unified system for the control of floods, the improvement of navigation on the Tennessee River, and the production of inexpensive electric power. In this area the land had once been fertile, but the plowing of the hillsides for corn, tobacco, and cotton, has caused much soil erosion. Today large numbers of farm families can scarcely make a living from their worn-out soil. Through the efforts of the TVA the farmer is now being shown how to save his land by terracing and crop rotation. Some of the land which has been exhausted by years of farming is being reforested or planted in grass. In order to increase the income of the people the TVA also hopes to bring small, electrically operated, private industries into the valley. 

Conserving Our Underground Wealth.-Petroleum has become an everyday necessity in our machine age. In the form of gasoline it propels our automobiles, tractors, airplanes, and motorboats; as fuel oil it moves our large ocean liners, our battleships, and some of our locomotives. 

Our supply of petroleum is large, but experts estimate that if the present rate of consumption continues, our known resources may be exhausted before very many years. Our present methods of taking petroleum from the ground are often very wasteful. Petroleum exists underground in the form of so-called "pools" which may extend over a considerable area. Whoever owns surface and mineral rights where this pool of petroleum exists may drill a well and take out as much of it as he can get through pipes. But the same pool of petroleum may extend underneath the land of a number of different persons. Therefore, all the land owners over the same pool must drill

Courtesy Bureau of Mines, Department of the Interior Oil is one of our most valuable natural resources and should be conserved for future use. Present methods of taking petroleum from the ground are often very wasteful. wells at the same time, otherwise they will not get their share of the oil. This situation has resulted in oil production greatly beyond our present needs even though our general supply is definitely limited. Natural gas associated with the production of oil has also been allowed to escape in vast amounts. There is need, therefore, for methods of extraction which will eliminate waste and conserve our supply for future use.

Our resources of coal are sufficient to last for centuries, but in many cases our richest and most accessible veins are being exhausted. In the future the cost of mining coal will become greater.

All our mineral resources are a gift from the past. Unlike our forests, they cannot be replaced. They should be so used that future generations will not be robbed of their just share.

Workmen from a government fish hatchery are dumping cans of fingerling size trout into one of the government rearing ponds. Fish from these ponds are used for stocking rivers and lakes. 

Conservation of Wild Life.-Millions of acres of our national forests have been set aside as refuges for birds and other forms of wild life. Each state has laws to protect the birds, game, and fish within its borders. These laws provide that, in certain seasons of the year, game may be killed or fish taken from the streams. Licenses paid by hunters and fishermen usually go into a special fund and the proceeds are used to increase the supply of game and fish. Many states, as well as the federal government, conduct fish hatcheries from which the stock of fish in rivers and lakes may be replenished.

The Work of the CCC-In 1933 Congress passed the Emergency Conservation Act which gave the President authority to organize and establish camps for young men between the ages of eighteen and twenty-five. Thus, the Civilian Conservation Corps, or CCC, was organized.

Under the guidance and supervision of trained foremen, the major portion of its activities were directed toward the protection of our vast forest areas. In addition to the building of fire breaks and the removing of dead trees and underbrush, many miles of roadways were improved. 

Telephone lines were laid to stations for mobilization against fires. One of the major activities in the South and 

Midwest was the work done to check soil erosion and to restore fertility to millions of acres of worn-out farms. 

More than 1,400,000 erosion-control check dams were built. Planting of trees and grass was encouraged. 

World War II, which greatly increased employment for everyone, including young men who normally would have been eligible for CCC camps, brought about the closing of the CCC.

Chapter XXV IMPROVING RELATIONS BETWEEN EMPLOYERS AND EMPLOYEES A. THE BEGINNINGS OF ORGANIZED LABOR The Effect of the Industrial Revolution Upon the 

Worker.-In the days of the spinning wheel and the hand loom there were no disputes between employers and employees such as we have today. Before the industrial revolution manufacturing was done by hand in the home or in small shops. The workers usually owned their own tools and equipment and worked in their own homes or shops. 

But the inventions of the industrial revolution led to the development of expensive machinery and equipment. Only people with money could buy this machinery and start factories. The workers now left their homes and small shops to labor in factories. They no longer owned their tools and equipment; they now received wages for their work in the factories. 

Under these conditions the interests of the employers and the employees tended to drift apart. The employer became most concerned about making profits and the workers became interested in receiving as high wages as possible. As factories grew in size and large-scale production developed, many industries passed into the hands of large corporations. The employers and the employees no longer had an opportunity for knowing each other personally, and, therefore, misunderstandings frequently arose. 520

The decline of home industries and the growth of manufacturing created new industrial problems. Workers soon began to organize in an attempt to improve their working conditions under the factory system.

The Development of Labor Unions.-Working con ditions during the early days of the factory system were often bad. There was much danger to the health and safety of the workers on account of the use of dangerous machinery and because of unsanitary conditions in many mines and factories. Men, women, and children worked from twelve to fifteen hours a day and wages were low. 

There were always some factory owners who treated their workers well, but there were many who did not. 

People soon began to ask for laws to improve working conditions. The workers began to organize themselves into labor unions in order to obtain higher wages and gain other advantages. When workers are organized in unions, they are much more able to get what they want than when they act as individuals. In dealing with the employer the union can often make a better bargain and get higher wages than when one worker acts alone. When workers unite in a union which makes an agreement with the employer concerning wages and working conditions for all of the members, we call it collective bargaining. 

B. RELATIONS OF EMPLOYERS AND EMPLOYEES TODAY The Development of Labor Unions in the United 

States. Labor organizations in the United States at first were local trade unions, made up of workers in a single trade and usually within the same community. By 1833 

Philadelphia alone had twenty-two trade unions, and in 1835 a general strike was called in protest against the "sunrise to sunset" working day; a standard labor day of ten hours was demanded by the unions. But it was not until after the War between the States that unions became strong. Then the Knights of Labor opened its membership to factory workers and unskilled laborers and launched a campaign for the eight-hour day.
