In 1883 trade union membership was 200,000; today it is estimated at more than eight million. Yet there are still millions of workers who are not members of any labor union. 

This was followed by the American Federation of Labor which was founded in 1886. It is composed of more than one hundred national or international trade unions, such as the bricklayers' union, the machinists' union, and the carpenters' union. These workers are organized on the basis of trades or crafts. It is the type of work performed rather than the industry in which a worker is employed which determines the union to which he belongs. Thus, the bricklayers' union may be made up of men who work for a number of different employers. From its very beginning the American Federation of Labor has been made up of unions whose members were mostly skilled workers,

organized in craft unions. It represent a total membership of more than five million workers. Some labor organizations, such as the four powerful railway brotherhoods, have never been members of the American Federation of Labor. There are also millions of workers in our country who are not members of any labor union. 

Because the workers in many big industries, such as steel, automobile, and rubber, had not been in any union at all, a new national labor organization was started in 

1935 by John L. Lewis, head of the United Mine Workers.

He founded the Committee for Industrial Organization, now called the Congress of Industrial Organizations. This new labor movement organizes unions which take in all workers in a particular industry or factory in the same union, no matter what their work may be. It organizes one union for all automobile workers, one for steel, and one for rubber, regardless of the type of work performed by the members. In other words, it puts the emphasis on industrial unions, rather than on craft unions. The CIO has been bitterly opposed by the AFL and today organized labor is seriously divided into two quarreling groups. 

Causes of Industrial Conflict.-There are a number of causes for conflict between employers and employees. 

The employer may want to keep wages as low as possible, because low wages will mean more profit for him. But when wages are too low, workers become dissatisfied and demand increased pay. Bad working conditions, long hours, or the use of dangerous machinery and harmful materials may be causes for complaint on the part of the workers. Employees sometimes demand the closed shop, which means that the employer is allowed to hire only members of the union. The employer may wish to have an open shop, to reserve the right to hire either union or non-union workers. The regular collection of dues from

RELATIONS BETWEEN EMPLOYERS AND EMPLOYEES 525 

all the members is a serious problem for many unions. 

Therefore, they want the employers to use the "checkoff," by which union dues are taken from the workers' pay envelopes and turned over directly to the union. Many employers do not like this system and do not wish to adopt it. Sometimes trouble arises over the discharge of a worker. The employer may say that the worker is being discharged because of inefficiency or disobedience, while the union may claim that the man has been dismissed because of his union activities. Disagreements and petty regulations among different kinds of unions sometimes are a source of annoyance to employers. For example, a bricklayer is forbidden to drive a nail and a carpenter must not handle a trowel.

What the Unions Do to Gain Advantage.-Some times open conflicts arise between workers and employers. 

The workers may make some new demand, such as an increase in wages, which the employer refuses to grant, or they may decide to oppose some new policy of the employer, such as a reduction of wages. When the difficulties between the workers and employer cannot be settled by any other method, the union may call a strike. In a strike the employees quit work. If there are no other workers to take the place of the striking employees, the business or factory must close. A strike may become an endurance contest to see which side can hold out the longest. Workers may be able to hold out during a strike as long as the union can pay them strike benefits with which they can keep their families clothed and fed. The employer stands to lose a great deal by a strike because he cannot fill his orders. He may even lose his customers. 

If nobody tries to go to work in a factory during a strike, the whole affair may be quite peaceful. But usually there are some workers who wish to work in spite of the

strike. In order to keep employees from working, the union resorts to picketing. Lines of workers are stationed around the factory to persuade others from going in to take the strikers' jobs. If the strike concerns a store, pickets try to persuade people not to patronize that store. 

By carrying signs pickets may be able to gain public sympathy. As long as the pickets observe certain conditions peaceful picketing is legal.

A union may declare a sympathetic strike when it has no direct grievance of its own, but wishes to help the workers in some other factory who are on strike. When a large number of workers in different industries strike at the same time, it is referred to as a general strike. During the sit-down strikes used extensively in 1936, some of the strikers took possession of factories and refused to allow the owners or other workers to enter. This form of strike has been declared illegal. 

During a strike or at other times union members may refuse to buy the products of a certain employer whose labor policies they dislike. This is known as boycott. Organized labor wants the public to buy goods marked with a union label, which means that it has been produced by union labor.

What the Employer Does to Gain Advantage.- 

Although most employers use every effort to deal fairly with their employees, a few use undesirable methods in dealing with the union. Some hire spies in order to find out about the plans and activities of the union. Sometimes during a strike an employer may hire guards and build riot fences in order to break up picketing. There are a few organizations from which the employer can hire strikebreakers to work during a strike. These practices on the part of the employer often result in violence. 

When an employer is in a position to resist the demands

RELATIONS BETWEEN EMPLOYERS AND EMPLOYEES 527 

PAYZ ANTERS RIKE NERAL RIKE PAINTERS PAINTERS PAINTERS GENERAL GENER STRIKE STRIKE GENERAL STRIKE 

Ewing Galloway

These strikers are advertising their cause with signs and posters to gain public sympathy. of the union, he may use the lockout. This is the opposite of the strike. Instead of the men quitting work, the employer dismisses them and closes the doors of his factory. 

Employers used to discharge certain workers who were active in the union, but under the National Labor Relations Act this practice is forbidden. Sometimes employers have refused to employ certain men because they were known to be union leaders or labor agitators. The names of such workers appeared on a blacklist which was placed in the hands of a number of employers. 

When the employer is afraid that strikers may destroy some of his property, he may ask the judge in a court to issue an injunction. An injunction is an order of a court which forbids strikers from doing certain things which might result in the destruction of life and property. The violation of an injunction is punishable by fine and imprisonment. Organized labor has always been opposed to

the use of the injunction; the federal government and some of the states now limit the power of judges to grant injunctions in labor disputes. 

The Interests of the Public.-Strikes are costly methods of settling disputes between workers and employers. Every year both capital and labor lose millions of dollars on account of industrial strife. Persons who are not directly concerned with strikes may be affected. Sometimes property is destroyed and bystanders endangered or even killed by the violence and terror resulting from a strike. Business is generally retarded by a strike. The workers lose wages and the employers cannot fill orders. 

Eventually an entire community may be affected. The general public throughout the country may be greatly inconvenienced by labor troubles in certain vital forms of business and industry. It is easy to see how a strike of truck drivers or railroad employees endangers the food supply of our cities. A strike of coal miners may put out the fires in our homes and stop the wheels of industry in all parts of the country. 

Methods of Settling Labor Disputes.-On many occasions representatives of workers and employers settle their disputes in a friendly way by talking over differences and coming to an agreement which is accepted by both sides. When the negotiations between workers and employers are carried on with the aid of an outside person, there is said to be mediation. When both sides in a controversy agree to submit their dispute to a neutral person or board, and agree to abide by the final decision, there is arbitration. Nearly all our states now have boards or commissions who offer their services to settle labor disputes by mediation or conciliation. Sometimes the governor of a state or the mayor of a city may help to settle a labor dispute through mediation. The National Mediation Board

RELATIONS BETWEEN EMPLOYERS AND EMPLOYEES 529 

is especially concerned with the settlement of disputes between railroads and their employees. The Division of 

Conciliation in the United States Department of Labor handles more than a thousand cases of industrial disputes every year.

The Federal Government Guarantees the Right of Collective Bargaining.—In 1935 Congress passed the 

National Labor Relations Act, which guarantees to workers the right of collective bargaining. It applies only to industries and forms of business whose operations are affected by interstate commerce. Under its provisions employers are forbidden to interfere in any way with the right of their employees to organize unions. This law is enforced by the National Labor Relations Board, consisting of three members appointed by the President with the consent of the Senate. The board holds hearings and issues orders when it finds that the employer has interfered with the rights of the workers to organize. In some cases it orders the employees in a factory to hold an election to decide what labor union shall represent the workers. Workers who have been discharged for union activities have been ordered reinstated with back pay. Company unions, organized among their workers by some employers, have been forbidden.

C. IMPROVING RELATIONS OF WORKERS AND EMPLOYERS Plans for Promoting Better Understanding Be tween Workers and Employers.-Hundreds of employers never have any labor troubles at all. It should be noted that many employers are sincerely anxious to improve relations between the workers and themselves. Some of them provide libraries, gymnasiums, baseball fields, and other forms of recreation for their employees. Picnics and other social affairs are held for the workers. Some

employers build attractive homes for their workers to live in, although some company houses are nothing more than cheap shacks or cabins.

Some employers share the profits of the business with the workers. According to a report of a United States 

Senate committee which has investigated the subject, there are fifty different plans for profit-sharing now in use in our country. Among the earliest plans of real significance were those established by the Procter and 

Gamble Company of Cincinnati, the N. O. Nelson Manufacturing Company of St. Louis, the Ballard and Ballard 

Company of Louisville, and the Pillsbury Flour Mills of

Minneapolis. Among other outstanding plans are those of the Westinghouse Electric and Manufacturing Company, the Endicott-Johnson Shoe Company, and the Eastman Kodak Company. Most of these companies distribute profits in the form of cash, although a few of them give shares of stock to their employees. Profit-sharing plans are often started during times of prosperity and abandoned during periods of depression. Organized labor has, in the main, opposed profit-sharing in industry, claiming that employers should pay higher wages rather than share profits with employees.

In order to induce workers to take a greater interest in the business, some firms have given employees a share in the management of the plant. This is called industrial democracy. Under this system the workers elect committees or representatives to meet with the employer. 

Working conditions, wages, hours, and shop rules are democratically discussed between these representatives and their employers. In some firms the committee of workers merely makes suggestions to the employer for improvements in the business, but in others they have a real part in the management. Workers sometimes have

RELATIONS BETWEEN EMPLOYERS AND EMPLOYEES 531 

PROCTER GAMBE 

Courtesy Procter and Gamble

Better relations between employers and employees may be promoted through recreation and other activities for the employees. representatives on the board of directors which controls the industry.

The Responsibilities of Workers and Employers in a Democracy.-No subject is of greater importance for the future of American democracy than the relationship of workers and employers. There is much prejudice on both sides and it is often difficult to know which side is in the right.

Teamwork is essential to success in business and industrial life, just as it is in football or basketball. Workers and employers must pull together in order to serve the best interests of our whole country. Each side in an industrial dispute should be willing to hear the other's point of view; both sides should be willing to sit down together and use the democratic method of settling disputes. The use of force and violence have no place in a democracy.

Shootings, beatings, and the destruction of property are foreign to the ideals of American democracy. In all our labor controversies the police should be neutral, but they should never hesitate to act when violence is attempted. 

Strikers, strikebreakers, and employers have certain fundamental rights as American citizens which must be preserved at all times. 

Employers should be willing to consider the welfare of their employees as seriously as they consider their own welfare. This spirit is illustrated by the case of a leading shoe manufacturer who says his main business is to make better men and women out of his employees, and next to make better shoes than other manufacturers do. Employees should be willing to contribute their best to the development of a worthy product, and they should not make unreasonable demands upon their employers.

Chapter XXVI  A. THE NEED FOR SECURITY Work Should Promote the Welfare of the People. 

-In Chapter XX we have studied the importance of making a living. Most people have jobs or positions working for employers, or they manage farms and businesses of their own. Why do people work? For most of them the job means getting a living, providing food, clothing, shelter, and recreation.

We have already seen that in a democracy government exists for the welfare and happiness of the people. Workwhether it is farming, manufacturing, or other form of business should also promote the welfare and happiness of the people. Under our system of capitalism, production is carried on for profit, but the desire for profits should never be allowed to interfere with human welfare. This means that the health and safety of the worker must at all times be protected; accidents and illness brought about through working conditions must be decreased to a minimum. Consideration for human welfare also means that children should not be permitted to work in factories or mines. Workers should be paid high enough wages that they may buy sufficient food, clothing, shelter, and recreation for health and comfort. Finally, all persons who are willing to work must be given jobs, for unemployment does not promote human welfare. 535

The Lack of Security Today.-There has always been some hardship and danger connected with the business of getting a living. In the days of our pioneer forefathers when most people lived on farms, long hours of backbreaking toil sometimes caused bodily injuries and a shorter life. But our modern industrial system has brought about new threats to the safety and well-being of the people. From the very beginnings of our factory system there have been periods of unemployment, but until 1900 many unemployed workers could move westward and find new ways of making a living by settling on free land. Today, however, the fear of "being laid off," is constantly in the minds of many workers and the opportunities for work in other communities are definitely limited. Before 1890 most people expected to continue work until they were sixty or seventy years of age. Today, however, we speak of the "old-age deadline" as being closer to fifty than sixty-five, and some factories do not like to employ men over forty-five. This is because employers are finding that after a man becomes older he begins to slow down in his movements and can no longer keep up with the speed of the machines. Thus, the fear of becoming too old to hold one's job is another worry of the modern worker. In addition to the fear of unemployment, workers may be concerned about accidents which may cause injuries, or they may be worried about working conditions which may lead to ill-health.

B. PROMOTING THE WELFARE OF THE WORKERS Eliminating Child Labor.-With the coming of the factory system it was found that boys and girls could operate the new machines as well as adults, and for less pay. Soon thousands of children were employed in factories and mills, working twelve to fourteen hours a day.

1900 1910 1920 

KKKK -XX 1930 Each symbol represents about 2 percent of children. 

O Nat. Educ. Assn.

The proportion of children ten to fifteen years of age gainfully employed in the United States has greatly decreased in the last twenty years, as shown in the above chart. 

Today child labor has been greatly decreased, but it is still a problem in some sections of our country, and in some kinds of work. According to the 1940 Census, 255,336 boys and girls between the ages of ten and fifteen were employed with pay outside of their homes. The largest number were employed in agriculture. They were not doing the ordinary chores on a farm, but they were doing hard work for long hours at such tasks as picking cotton, picking berries or fruit, or hoeing and weeding large beet and onion fields. Some of these children receive only a few months' schooling during the year. Until recently thousands of boys and girls were employed in our

textile mills, where they sometimes worked in unhealthful surroundings. In some of our cities children are employed in sweatshops, either in dingy tenement rooms or in small shops, making artificial flowers, cheap embroidery, and stringing toy beads. The canning industry frequently employs child labor.

Working after school hours or on Saturdays is usually not considered injurious child labor. In fact, some jobs of this kind may be helpful to a boy or girl because they develop self-reliance, character, and a better understanding of the world of work. But there are many reasons why the regular employment of children at steady jobs is undesirable. It may interfere with the education of boys and girls, stunt their growth, and weaken their bodies so that they will never become strong and healthy men and women. The low wages of child laborers tend to pull down the wages of men and women. 

Public opinion has become aroused against child labor and most states now prohibit the work of children under fourteen years of age. Compulsory education laws help to keep boys and girls from being employed. The hours of labor of young workers are usually limited to eight a day. 

Because some of our states have been slow to make and enforce such laws, Congress has passed two acts against child labor. Both of these laws have been declared unconstitutional by the United States Supreme Court on the grounds that only states had the right to regulate working conditions. The new federal Fair Labor Standards Act, however, prohibits the employment of children under sixteen years of age in all industries which do business in more than one state.

An amendment to the United States Constitution which would give Congress power to regulate the employment of persons under eighteen years of age was submitted to the

states in 1924. But by 1940 only twenty-eight states had ratified it.

Regulating the Work of Women.-Before the indus trial revolution women worked mostly in the home, raising children and doing the many household tasks, which then included even the making of the clothing for the family. Then came the factory system and soon women as well as men went into the factories to work. Today more than ten million women are employed with pay outside the home. They are engaged in many different kinds of work such as domestic service, factory work, clerical jobs, working in stores; many of them are also in the professions such as teaching, social work, and nursing. 

The work of women in factories, especially, has caused some serious problems. In some cases both homes and children have been neglected. Long hours of monotonous labor in some factories, excessive heat, and dampness in such industries as laundering and canning, seriously affect the health of women workers. Women and children, more than other workers, suffer from the unhealthy conditions and the low wages found in sweatshops. Most states now have laws which provide that women shall not be employed more than a certain number of hours per week. 

Working conditions are also regulated. In some occupations where the work is especially dangerous or heavy the employment of women is forbidden. 

One of the chief problems of women workers is the low pay which they frequently receive for their work. In one study of women workers made in New Jersey it was found that eighty per cent of them got less than seventeen dollars a week. Eight per cent of them received only five dollars a week or less. Low wages for women tend to pull down the general level of wages for all workers. In 1912 

Massachusetts passed the first minimum wage law for

Courtesy Y.M.C.A., Coatesville, Pennsylvania

Many industries enforce rigid safety rules and conduct safety campaigns among the workers. A demonstration is being given here of what to do in case of industrial accidents. women. A number of states and the District of Columbia now have similar laws. 

Solving the Problem of Industrial Accidents and 

Ill-Health.-Work in factories and mills brings certain dangers to all workers. An accident may result in a serious injury or certain working conditions may cause ill health. 

In 1942 there were eighteen thousand five hundred deaths and more than a million injuries as a result of accidents while people were at work. These figures take into account all kinds of work, including farming which alone totals twenty-six per cent of all the deaths from accidents of employed people.

The employer and the employee are both responsible for our high accident rate. The worker may be careless, indifferent, or ignorant. The employer may be negligent in providing safety devices. Unprotected gears, wheels, and belts, dark stairways, and open elevator shafts cause many

Courtesy Women's Bureau, U. S. Department of Labor 

Guarded machinery protects the laundry worker at the left. Good conditions in the hosiery plant at the right include properly adjusted chairs, good lighting, and a drinking fountain conveniently placed. 

This miner is protected by a silicosis mask.

industrial accidents. All states now have laws to protect the health and safety of the worker. These laws require the screening and guarding of dangerous machinery, and the regular inspection of the factory to see that the regulations for safety are carried out. Most industries now enforce rigid safety rules and conduct safety campaigns among the workers. As a result of this safety education the number of accidents in industries is steadily decreasing. In most states when a worker is injured a workmen's compensation law requires that the employee shall be paid a part of his wages until he recovers. Hospital and medical bills for injuries are also paid for in this manner. 

Other state laws aim to protect the health of the worker. 

Adequate ventilation and other sanitary conditions in shops and factories are required. The work in some occupations causes occupational diseases such as poisoning from the use of lead or phosphorus, flesh burns from X-rays or radium, or miner's asthma. The laws of twentyone states protect the worker who has contracted a disease because of his work, as well as the worker who suffers from an accident. These laws provide for the payment of part of the medical and hospital bills. 

The Federal Government Provides Greater Secur ity for the Worker.-Most laws for the benefit of labor and the improvement of working conditions have been passed by the legislatures of the various states, but in recent years the federal government has taken a direct interest in providing greater security for the worker. For this purpose Congress has passed the Social Security Act 

(1935) and the Fair Labor Standards Act (1938). 

The Social Security Act.-From the standpoint of the worker the most important provisions of this act deal with old-age pensions, old-age insurance, and unemployment insurance.

Prepared for the Social Security Board 

The social security program is administered by the Social Security Board, the Children's Bureau, the Public Health Service, and the Office of Education in cooperation with state agencies.

The federal government pays money to the states for old-age pensions to needy persons who are sixty-five or over. The states also pay a part of the cost of old-age pensions. The federal government provides a sum of money equal to that paid by the state, provided, however,

that its share does not exceed twenty dollars a month for each person receiving aid. All states now have old-age pension laws so that they are able to receive this federal aid to help the needy. 

The Social Security Act also sets up a system of old-age insurance for all persons who come under the terms of the act. Both employer and employee are required to contribute to the fund from which benefits will be paid. During the first six years the employer pays one per cent of his pay roll, and the employee one per cent of his wages. 

These percentages may be increased gradually in future years, when they will reach a maximum of three per cent for each. The federal government will pay benefits to persons who have reached sixty-five years of age and are not employed. The amount of money which a person will receive each month will depend on the amount of wages he has earned between January 1, 1937, and the time when he reaches sixty-five years of age. Payments to insured workers began in 1940. 

When you start your first job you will receive a social security account number and become part of this insurance system, provided you are working in an occupation covered by the act. The social security number you receive is the number of your account in the social security headquarters. This account will be kept for you from the day of your first job until the day of your death. No matter where you move or how many times you change your employer, you will always have the same number. 

The unemployment insurance features of the Social Security Act will be described in a later section of this chapter. 

The Fair Labor Standards Act.-This law is de signed to be a floor under wages and a ceiling over hours for the workers in all industries that do business in more

than one state. The law starts with a minimum wage of twenty-five cents an hour and a maximum week of fortyfour hours. These standards shall be gradually raised. By 

1945 all industries affected by the law must have a minimum wage of forty cents an hour and the maximum hours will be reduced to forty a week. The purpose of the law is to raise the wages and reduce the working hours of our lowest paid workers. 

C. THE PROBLEM OF UNEMPLOYMENT The Problem of Un employment. One of the greatest problems which threatens the security of the worker is unemployment. The fear of "being laid off" and of not being able to find another job is a serious matter for many 

Courtesy Social Security Board 

A card punched with account numworker under Social Security is kept ber, name, and earnings of every at Social Security headquarters. 

workers. Even during a time of prosperity such as the 

1920's several million workers were out of jobs. 

Unemployment during the 1930's reached staggering figures. In 1933 more than thirteen million people were out of work, and even in 1937 when business was improving, there were still eight million jobless. When workers are without jobs everybody suffers. Perhaps the family of the unemployed worker no longer gets the right kind of food and the proper amount of clothing. Probably they have to move into a much smaller house where the rent is

cheaper. Medical attention and hospital care for the family are neglected. A person who is out of work for a long time may lose his ambition and become inefficient. In some cases unemployed workers manage to get a small income from work created by the government, such as WPA. In 

These men, out-of-work, are reading want ads. Millions of able, experienced workers cannot find jobs today in private industry. other cases, families go on direct relief and draw small payments of money from the government. 

Hard times not only affect the unemployed, but the whole country suffers and everybody has to pay higher taxes to pay for government work and relief. 

Causes of Unemployment.-I. Personal Causes. Some unemploy ment may be due to causes in the individual himself.

Some people are too old to work, others may suffer from serious illness which prevents them from holding a steady job. Then there are those who lose their jobs on account of intemperance, and there are others who are too lazy to work or unwilling to accept certain kinds of jobs. 

2. Seasonal Slackness. In many industries there is much work at certain seasons of the year and much unemployment at other times. The canning industry is busy only during the harvest season in the summer and fall months. Work in the building trades often depends upon the weather. The busiest season for the mining of coal is

1920 1921 1923 1925 1927 1929 1931 1933

Unemployment is one of our most serious problems. during the fall and winter. When the busy season for such industries closes, thousands of workers have no jobs and have to "kill" time until the season reopens. Some farsighted employers have found ways of spreading work and providing employment throughout the year. 

3. Periods of Depression. The greatest single cause of unemployment is found in the periods of depression in our business and manufacturing activities. Business ac-

tivity throughout our history has been running through alternate periods of prosperity and hard times. During times of prosperity factories and mills are running full blast and stores are full of shoppers. Everybody has money to spend and we hear little about unemployment. During periods of hard times factories are shut down or running part time and workers have little money. Millions of people are without jobs and the government and private charity are forced to come to their aid. Beginning about 1930 until World War II the United States suffered from a severe industrial depression which resulted in the largest amount of unemployment in our history. 

The causes of these business cycles, with alternate periods of prosperity and depression, are difficult to find. 

When times are good, factories and mills seem to produce goods more rapidly than they can be consumed. Thus, a surplus is piled up, factories shut down or run only part time, and unemployment follows. Merchants find themselves with a stock of goods which they cannot sell and the whole country begins to suffer from hard times. Some forms of unwise investment have caused business depressions. In 1929 people were speculating in stocks, and many securities were selling far beyond their real value. The great stock market crash of October, 1929, was the beginning of the great depression of the 1930's. 

4. Inventions. Have you ever seen a group of people standing around and watching a huge steam shovel at work? Here is a good illustration of one of the chief causes of unemployment-the invention of new machinery. Since the beginning of the industrial revolution new machines have thrown men out of work. 

It is also true that by the use of machines prices of goods have been lowered, sales have been increased, and many new jobs have been created. This has been notably

true of the automobile which has been responsible for the creation of many new jobs in manufacturing cars, providing gasoline and oil, servicing and repairing cars, and the building of streets and highways. The electrical industries have also resulted in a large number of new jobs. 

WO 

PROG

WI The man at the desk is the millionth man taught to read and write by WPA.

Both photos:  

Workers on WPA canning projects have prepared over forty-eight million pounds of food for distribution to needy families. 

But in recent years the invention of power-driven machines has gone forward more rapidly than new jobs can be created. In many industries production has been greatly increased, and yet there has been a decrease in the number of workers required. In the entire construction of an automobile frame there are over five hundred operations which are done by machines. Office machinery eliminates the need for many bookkeepers and clerks. The increased use of the combine which both cuts and threshes wheat, the mechanical corn harvester, and the new machine for picking cotton-all decrease the number of workers needed on farms.

D. PROTECTING THE WORKERS AGAINST DEPRESSION AND UNEMPLOYMENT 

Unemployment Insurance.-One of the important features of the Social Security Act is its provision for unemployment insurance. To provide the money for this purpose, employers are required to pay a tax of three per cent on their pay rolls. This money goes into a fund from which employees are paid when they lose their jobs through no fault of their own. Weekly payments usually amount to about one-half of the worker's wages. These payments are usually given after the second or third week of unemployment and continue for about twenty weeks. When the insured worker loses his job, he must register with the nearest public employment office and he must keep in touch with this office regularly during the time of his unemployment. Each state has its own unemployment insurance law, but the whole system is carried out in cooperation with the federal government. 

Other Government Efforts to Help the Unemployed. The United States Employment Service was established in 1933. This agency co-operates with the states and has established a nation-wide system of employment exchanges to help find jobs for persons who are out of work. Offices are located in all important towns and cities. 

During the depression years of the 1930's national, state, and local governments appropriated tremendous sums of money for direct relief for the unemployed and for providing work. The total cost was in the neighborhood of 26,000,000,000 dollars. The Federal Emergency

Administration of Public Works (PWA) was set up in 

1933 and discontinued ten years later. This government agency loaned or granted money to state and local governments for the construction of public works, such as

schools, power plants, water works, bridges, sewage disposal plants, streets, and highways.

The Works Progress Administration (WPA) was established in 1935 to provide direct work relief at the expense of the federal government in cooperation with state and local governments. WPA labor has constructed and improved many public buildings, streets and highways, airplane landing fields, athletic fields, and water systems. 

Employment has been provided for non-manual workers, such as actors, artists, teachers, writers, and musicians. 

The Work Projects Administration was created July 1, 1939, to absorb and continue the program of the Works 

Progress Administration. 

Although most of the jobs after World War II will be provided by private industry, public works will be important as a means of assuring jobs to those not in private employment. There is much on every side of us that needs to be done. Towns, cities, and states have let both new projects and repairs slide for the duration. New roads will have to be built, old ones repaired, and many new public buildings will be needed. There is an especial need for public housing.

Chapter XXVII  A. THE IMPORTANCE OF THE FARMER What the Farmer Does for the Nation.-Farming is of fundamental importance to the welfare of the nation. 

Our prosperity depends to a large extent upon the work of the farmer. We are all dependent upon the soil for our food and clothing. Our industries need many raw materials produced on farms. The farmer produces cotton and wool for the textile mills, grain for the flour mills, meat for the packers, hides for the tanneries and shoe factories, vegetables and fruits for our canneries. 

New uses for farm products are continually being found. Cotton, corn, soybeans, molasses, lard, mohair, wool, linseed oil, and turpentine are some of the farm products used in the manufacture of automobiles. 

The railroads get much of their business by transporting goods from the farms to the cities, and taking manufactured products from the cities back to the farms. 

About one-fourth of our population lives on farms. 

These families are important buyers of manufactured products. They need all kinds of supplies for their homes, and they also buy many automobiles, trucks, tractors, and other farm machinery. When the farmer is not prosperous, many other forms of business suffer. 

The products of American farms make up a large part of our trade with foreign countries. Cotton and tobacco. from the South, grain and meats from the Middle West, 555

This cornfield in rich Lancaster County, Pennsylvania, is in a general farming area. fruits from both the East and West, are sent to other countries. Farmers also buy a larger number of imported products.

Kinds of Farming.-About sixty per cent of the farmers in the United States, especially in the East, engage in what is known as general farming. They raise a variety of crops, such as corn, wheat, oats, hay, and some vegetables and fruit. They usually have a flock of poultry and some cattle. Over a period of time general farming with its rotation of crops is best for the conservation of the soil.

Some farmers engage in specialized farming. They raise a single crop or produce one product in order to get as much cash income as possible. The Middle West, which has been for the last half century the most prosperous farm section in the world, specializes in the production of corn, pork, beef, and wheat. Iowa is the center of corn

These Vermont boys have learned in their 4-H club work how to feed and care for their cows to obtain maximum milk production. raising. In the corn belt, farmers usually combine corn growing with hog raising. In the Dakotas, Minnesota, and 

Wisconsin, corn gives way to spring wheat; in Kansas and 

Oklahoma it yields to winter wheat. In the southern states cotton and tobacco are the chief products of specialized farming.

Other types of specialized farming include market gardening or truck farming. Truck farms raise a variety of vegetables and fruits for city markets and are usually found near our large cities. However, in the South and 

Southwest vegetables are grown for northern markets during the winter and early spring months. The growing of flowers and nursery stock is still another type of specialized farming. 

The great plains of some of our western states, where grass is plentiful, are best adapted to cattle raising. In

Courtesy Farm Security Administration: Photo by Post 

Horses still play an important role on many farms, despite the increasing use of the tractor. This picture of a county agent discussing horses with a farmer was taken in Pike County, Alabama. other parts of the country, especially in New York, Wisconsin, Vermont, and New Hampshire, dairy farming is important. Dairy farms no longer need to be located near large cities in order to have a ready market for their products. By modern methods of refrigeration and sanitation, milk can be shipped long distances from farms to the consumers in the cities. Large quantities of milk are also condensed and dried by evaporation. Butter, cheese, and ice cream are products manufactured from milk. 

Since there is a steady demand for fresh eggs and dressed poultry, another type of specialized farming in the form of poultry raising has developed rapidly in certain parts of the country. Large quantities of eggs and poultry are shipped to markets daily. The use of incubators, heated brooders, and scientific methods of feeding

and housing chickens, have helped to make poultry raising a huge business.

What the Government Has Done to Help the 

Farmer. For many years the government has taken a direct interest in helping the farmers. The United States 

Department of Agriculture was organized in 1862. Agents of this department have gone all over the world to find new varieties of crops that are suitable for American farms. Seeds and plants have been brought from many other countries and millions of dollars have been added to the value of 

American farm crops.

The Department of 

Agriculture has also established quarantines to prevent the spread of insect pests, such as the Hessian fly, cotton boll weevil, gypsy and brown-tail moths, European corn borer, and 

Japanese beetle. These pests were brought here 

Courtesy Farm Security Administration: 

Photo by Rothstein

Constant warfare against insects must be waged in order to have an apple crop like this one from a Camden County, New Jersey, orchard. with imported plants or products from other countries. 

Methods of fighting these enemies of the farmer have been discovered and are now being put to use through the cooperation of the national and state departments of agriculture with the farmers.

Texas fever, foot and mouth disease, and tuberculosis are diseases which afflict cattle, while hogs may die from cholera. These diseases among farm animals are being eliminated or controlled.

Through the study of soils, experts have been able to find out what kinds of crops are best suited to certain regions and what types of fertilizers are needed. 

In order to bring the benefits of the newer knowledge of farming directly to the farmers the federal government appropriates money for an extension service carried on by local field agents who visit farm homes to give demonstrations and advice. These county agents organize the 

4-H clubs for boys and girls, whose members engage in friendly rivalry in growing field and garden crops, raising stock and poultry, and in canning and preserving vegetables. 

Most states also have a commissioner, a director, or a board of agriculture that cooperates with the federal Department of Agriculture and carries on similar activities. 

Improving Farm Life. The agricultural revolution which followed the industrial revolution has done much to improve living conditions on our farms. The use of modern farm machinery and labor-saving devices has eliminated some of the heavy work and drudgery in the fields and in the home. The radio, telephone, and the automobile along with better roads, have helped break down the isolation and remove the loneliness of farm life. Today mail is delivered on the remotest country road. A prosperous farmer can equip his home with all the comforts and conveniences which are common in city homes. Unfortunately, many farmers have not been sufficiently prosperous to obtain these advantages for themselves and their families. 

The Problem of Security on the Farm.-In 1862 

Congress passed an important law known as the Home-

Courtesy Extension Division, University of Tennessee 

This Tennessee farmer is terracing his field to prevent erosion. stead Act. Under the provisions of this law any citizen of the United States who was the head of a family could become the owner of one hundred sixty acres of public land at practically no cost, by settling on it and making certain improvements. After the War between the States hundreds of thousands of Americans settled on this free land and soon became owners of farms. Thus, free land became the means whereby the unemployed and the dissatisfied workers could find security and opportunity. Free land, along with the development of the free public school system, made democratic equality of opportunity possible. 

Although the work was hard and the hours were long, the farmer and his family were reasonably sure of a good living. Even though they did not get much actual cash, the farmers lived quite as well as the average city resident. 

Farmers never had any fear of losing their jobs. When times were hard, they could always raise enough food for their own use and they could find fuel on their wood lots.

But the period of free land has come to an end and it has become more difficult to make a living on the farm. 

During and after World War I, when there was a great demand for American farm products abroad, there was a brief period of prosperity for the farmer. Then depression settled on American agriculture. From the early 1920's until World War II there was widespread poverty and hard times in many of our farming communities. The prices of many farm products dropped far below the cost of production. Thousands of farmers found themselves hopelessly in debt and became tenants instead of owners. 

Other farmers found themselves on land where much of the soil had been carried away by erosion or had become exhausted from years of single-crop farming. Investigations showed that there were slums in our rural areas. 

B. SOLVING THE PROBLEM OF PRODUCTION 

The Importance of Prosperity for the Farmer.- 

When the farmer receives little or nothing for his crops, his family suffers because of a lower standard of living. Community activities, such as the schools and the churches, suffer from lack of funds. In the past hundreds of rural schools have been forced to close for part of the term because there was no money to pay the teachers. 

When prices sag the farmers find it difficult to pay taxes and interest, to buy machinery and other equipment. 

When farmers have little money to spend, business will be poor in the towns and cities. Manufacturers suffer from a loss in business and workers in our factories and mills lose their jobs. No part of our population can suffer heavy loss without affecting the whole nation.

Government Aid for the Farmer.-Since the farmers' problems are so serious that they affect the entire nation, our government has taken steps to solve them.

The Farm Credit Administration, with headquarters at Washington, D. C., makes it easier for farmers to borrow money at low rates of interest. Co-operative marketing associations are encouraged. By this method some of the profits of the middlemen are eliminated and the farmer gets higher prices for his products. The export of farm products has been somewhat increased by the reciprocal trade treaties with foreign nations. 

The primary job of the Agricultural Adjustment Administration (AAA) is to guide and assist farmers in producing the right products in the right amounts each year. It also helps farmers to make sure that the nation will continue to have good soil for growing crops in the future. 

To carry out these purposes the AAA pays farmers to control the acreage of such crops as corn, wheat, tobacco, rice, and cotton. The Department of Agriculture decides how much land each farmer shall plant in these crops. The land not used for these regular crops is planted in clover, alfalfa, or other soil conservation crops. For controlling his acreage of some crops and for his efforts in soil conservation, the farmer receives cash from the government. 

The government also lends money to farmers in order to help store any surplus crops that are to be carried over from one year to another. Growers of wheat also have the privilege of insuring their crops so that they will receive benefit payments during the years when the yield is poor. 

The whole problem of crop control is complicated and some of the plans for helping the farmer are still in the experimental stage. Many leading economists do not believe that controlling production is the best way to solve this problem.

World War II and the Farmer.-During World 

War II there was a great increase in the demand for food

and other farm products in this country and abroad. It was soon found that food was an important weapon in the fight against the Axis powers. The average soldier consumes about 25 per cent more food than the average civilian. Under wartime conditions civilians eat more meats, dairy products, fruits, and vegetables than most people do in peacetime. Through lend-lease exports a portion of 

American farm products was sent to our fighting allies and to the people in occupied territories. These foods consisted largely of dairy products and eggs, meat, fish, fats and oils. 

An interesting development has been the increasing importance of dehydrated foods. Most of our foods are high in water content. Eggs and meat are three-fourths water and some vegetables contain over 90 per cent. Dehydrated foods save shipping space, transportation costs, and much tin; they also reduce losses from spoilage. Eggs have been prominent in our shipping abroad. But whole eggs take up much shipping space and need refrigeration; so now we are sending dehydrated eggs. It is possible to pack the food value of a dozen fresh eggs in a small five-ounce container. Sending canned meat abroad requires much tin and much cargo space. The dehydrated product will keep and is only about one third as heavy as fresh meat. 

The Department of Agriculture urged farmers to increase the production of meat, eggs, chickens, dried beans and peas. Great emphasis was placed upon increases in such products as peanuts and soybeans. Millions of victory gardens were planted and earnestly cultivated. 

World War II has brought about a vast expansion in the production of concentrated foods. Many people feel that the demands of peace will be as great as the demands of war. For some time in the future the farmers of the 

United States will have to furnish a large part of the food for people whose countries have been devastated by war.

Courtesy Farm Security Administration: Upper photo by Lee; lower photo by Lange 

Despite the development of many new industries in the South, cotton remains one of its chief products. Much of the cotton is still picked by hand, as the worker in the top photograph is doing.

C. SOLVING THE PROBLEM OF FARM TENANCY 

The Increase of Farm Tenancy.-We have already seen how low prices for farm products forced some farmers to sell their land and become tenants rather than owners. The number of farm tenants, however, has been increasing at an alarming rate since 1880. Between 1880 and 1935 the number of tenant farmers has increased one hundred eighty per cent. Today forty-two per cent of all farmers rent the land which they till. Two out of every five farms are worked by persons who do not own them. 

Tenant Farmers.-Tenant farmers are of two principal types. The largest number are renters. The renter pays a cash rent or a share of the crop to the owner of the land at the end of the year. He usually owns his own livestock and other farm equipment needed. 

The second type of tenant is known as the sharecropper. 

He lives generally on a cotton farm in the South. The landowner furnishes him a cabin, rations, and supplies for growing cotton. At the end of the cotton season, the crop is sold and settlement made with the landlord. Too frequently, however, the sharecropper has little or no cash coming to him because it has taken his share of the crop to pay his part of the cost of supplies. Sometimes he even finds himself in debt to the landlord at the end of the season. Because of a lack of income, the family of the sharecropper often must do without essential foods and as a result they often suffer from malnutrition and from dietary diseases.

The owners of many southern plantations are now running the farms themselves and hiring men to work for them by the day. Thus, some of the former sharecroppers have become day laborers.

Not all farm tenants are necessarily worse off than

FARM TENANCY 

1880 1935 

TENANTS OWNERS 

Each symbol represents 1 million men 

From Farmers Without Land, Public Affairs Committee, Inc. 

Farm tenancy has increased at an alarming rate since 1880. Today two out of every five farms are worked by tenants. farm owners. Tenant farming sometimes is a step by which the farm laborer may eventually come to have a farm of his own. A number of problems may develop when farmers do not own the land on which they live. Tenants generally do not take much interest in conserving the soil. 

They often get all out of the land they can and then move on to another farm.

Farm Tenancy and Democracy.-The great increase in farm tenancy does not fit in with the ideals of our democracy. Thomas Jefferson once said, "The small landowners are the most precious portion of the State." He also said that the government should provide "by every possible means that as few as possible shall be without a little portion of land." He believed that a free and prosperous farming class was the foundation of democracy. He wanted

the American farmer to remain out of the peasant class and not to sink to the level of poverty and serfdom of the farmers in Europe.

What the Government Is Doing to Solve the Prob lem of Farm Tenancy.-In 1937 Congress passed the 

Bankhead-Jones Act which, even though it is only a small beginning, is expected to open a new era for the farmer and to stimulate a desire for farm ownership. It is also designed to prevent the farm owner in times of financial difficulty from slipping back into tenancy. Although the money appropriated for this service is inadequate, it is the beginning of a move for greater security for the farmer. It is an effort to establish a policy like that used in Ireland and Denmark where farm tenancy has been replaced by ownership.

This work is being carried on by the Farm Security Administration in the Department of Agriculture. Money is loaned to tenants for the purchase of farms. Individuals who borrow this money are carefully selected by a county committee.

The average loan is about $5500. It draws a low rate of interest, three per cent, and may be paid back over a period of forty years. However, a unique feature is the "variable payment" plan which enables a farmer to pay back more in good crop years and less in poor years. With several good seasons behind them by 1943, many FSA farm ownership borrowers already had paid for their farms in as little as five years just from the farm earnings without the aid of off-farm work. In addition to these realestate loans, FSA also makes small operating loansknown as rehabilitation loans-to owners or renters who can't borrow from other private or governmental credit agencies. The average "rehab" loan, since the FSA program started, has been approximately $450, used to buy

livestock, feed, fertilizer, seed or household equipment. 

The interest rate is 5 per cent. 

Farmers who receive Farm Security loans are taught better systems of farm management to prevent erosion and conserve the fertility of the soil. They are taught how to raise gardens, preserve foods, and to keep family budgets. Many of these people have been found to be suffering from pellagra or other diseases caused by improper diet. 

Upon receiving proper food and medical care their health improved to such an extent that they were able to face their work with new vitality and hope. Some families that became stranded in worn-out farming areas, cut over forests, or exhausted mining regions have been loaned money with which to make a new start on better land. 

It has been found in many places that a single-farm family, operating a forty- or fifty-acre tract, cannot afford to use the most modern and efficient farming methods, including use of a tractor and other heavy equipment. 

Therefore, Farm Security has helped neighboring onefamily farmers organize co-operatives to share the use of such equipment. Where five neighbors, for instance, each need a tractor one fifth of the time, they might take turns using it. This keeps the tractor occupied nearer fulltime, and reduces the overhead cost for each co-operator. 

In other types of co-ops assisted by FSA, small farmers have bred their livestock with better sires, have purchased seed and other supplies cheaper and more efficiently through pooled orders, have set up marketing plans to sell their food or canneries to process it. Such measures cut the expense and increase the income of small farms-the traditional family-type operation on which American agriculture was founded.

Chapter XXVIII  A. STANDARDS OF LIVING What Is a Standard of Living?—A standard of liv ing refers to the way we live. It includes the kind of house we live in, the kind of clothing we wear, the quality of the food we eat, the amount of medical and hospital care we receive during illness, the kinds of recreation we can afford, as well as many other things which we use and enjoy. 

Some people's standard of living is said to be high. The 

Jones family lives in an attractively furnished house with six rooms and a bath, located away from the noise of the city in a good neighborhood. They are able to afford plenty of healthful, appetizing food which includes fresh fruits and vegetables at all times of the year. The members of the family are always comfortably clothed. When a member of the family becomes ill, the doctor is called immediately. The family goes to the theater frequently. Mr. 

Jones drives a car; he and his family go away for a vacation every summer. We might say that the Jones family enjoys a reasonably high standard of living. 

Some people are said to have a low standard of living. 

The Smith family lives in two rooms of a tenement house in the city slums. Their dwelling place may be a shelter from rain and to some extent from cold, but there is nothing attractive or comfortable about it. Because the mother cannot afford to buy enough milk for the growing children, they are undernourished. Their clothing is worn thread- 574

bare and is never in style. When sickness comes to some member of the family, home remedies are tried first and the doctor is called only as a last resort. A day's outing in a city park and an occasional movie are the only forms of recreation which the members of this family can ever afford. We might say that the Smith family has a low standard of living.

The standards of living of the Jones and Smith families are determined by the houses in which they live, the food that they eat, the clothing that they wear, the kind of medical care that they receive, and their opportunities for education, recreation, and travel. 

Why Are Some Standards of Living High and 

Others Low?-Why do some families have high standards of living while others just manage to get the bare necessities of life? The amount of income which a family receives determines its standard of living. Money is required not only to buy the necessities of life-food, clothing, and shelter, but also for many of the extras that make life worth living-books, travel, and entertainment. Of course, income may include much more than money. The farmer who can raise some of his food on his own land must figure that as part of his income. 

Incomes in the United States are by no means equal. 

In fact, there is a wide gap between the few wealthiest families at the top and the many who are poor. In 1940 single persons with incomes of eight hundred dollars a year or more and married men who got two thousand dollars a year or more were required to pay federal income taxes after certain deductions had been made. Only a small percentage of the people in the United States, however, had a large enough income to require them to pay this tax.

According to a report made by the National Re-

sources Board, in 1935-36 two out of every three families in the United States had incomes of less than fifteen hundred dollars a year. The largest number of American families have an income of twenty-two dollars a week, or eleven hundred sixty dollars a year. The income of fifteen out of every one hundred families is so low that they require help to get along. Incomes, of course, vary in different occupations and in different sections of the country. 

Education, personal tastes, and ideals of life have a great deal to do with standards of living. Some people, as we have just said, are easily satisfied and are content to live in accordance with low standards; others are constantly anxious to better their condition. Different families will use their incomes for different purposes. One family may insist on buying a new automobile, while another may use that same amount of money to help pay the oldest son's expenses at college. Later in this chapter you will find suggestions for managing an income. 

What Are Desirable Standards of Living?—Taken as a whole, the people of the United States have the highest standard of living in the world. This has been made possible because we have a great abundance of natural resources. Many experts believe, however, that the standards of living of the American people can be raised much higher than they are today. Our farms and factories can produce much more goods than the American people are now consuming.

Nobody seriously thinks that the wealth of our country should be divided equally, but some persons feel that in a democracy, where equality of opportunity is the ideal, we should find ways of raising the incomes of a large number of families. Many families do not get sufficient to eat or to wear and are poorly housed. If these people earned higher wages, they could enjoy a better standard of living.
